# 14a — Closed work

Every row that reached `Done`, `Dropped` or `Accepted`, moved out of
[14](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md) verbatim.

**This file is not read during normal work.** It exists so that closing a row
costs nothing and the live tracker stays the size of the work actually in front
of you — 244 closed rows were 84% of that document and 46,000 of its 55,000
words.

The reasoning in these rows is genuine and some of it is the only record of why
a decision went the way it did. Where a row describes a defect fixed after this
archive was made, the same reasoning is in the commit that made the change,
next to the diff, where `git log -S` and `git blame` will find it. Newer rows are
deliberately terse for that reason.

Ordered as they were. Ids are unique across the whole set and
`tools/check-tracker-ids.py` keeps them that way, so a row here can still be
cited by id.

| UX-GRID-01 | Typing a reference into a formula tears the grid | Done | P1 | **Reported by the user:** typing `=2*A2` sent the canvas "to a weird place". It was worse — the column headers left the screen, one band of rows drew, and the in-cell editor detached and floated near the bottom of the window. **Root cause, found by measuring rather than reading.** The grid scrolls *virtually*: the canvas is one screen tall and draws whatever `state.scrollY` says, which is AGENTS.md's no-DOM-as-source-of-truth rule applied to scrolling. `.grid-wrap` had acquired a native `scrollTop` of 282, so the canvas kept drawing from the engine while the element it lives in had moved underneath it. Instrumenting each keystroke showed `scrollHeight` jumping 531 → 815 on the exact character that completes `A2`, and hiding children one at a time named the in-cell `<textarea>`. The cause was one CSS rule: `.formula-bar input#formula-input.tinted, textarea.inline-edit.tinted { position: relative }`, where `.tinted` is added the moment a reference is recognised. `relative` leaves an element **in flow**, so the textarea went back to being laid out after the full-height canvas — `offsetTop` 795 — with its `top` only shifting it visually; the wrapper's scroll box grew by the offset and the browser scrolled that hidden-overflow container to reveal the focused editor. Nothing about the symptom pointed at CSS. **Fixed at the cause:** the rule wanted `z-index: 1` to sit above the reference mirror, and only the formula bar's input needed `position` to accept one — the in-cell editor is *already* `absolute`. The declaration is split, and the in-cell editor keeps `absolute`. **Gated by** a browser test asserting the invariant — the wrapper never scrolls and its scroll box never exceeds its client box, checked after every keystroke — plus that the header band is still drawn, so any future element escaping containment fails it too. Mutation-verified live: reintroducing `position: relative` gives 815 against a 531 client box and `offsetTop` 795; removing it restores 531/531 and 264. **Two wrong turns, recorded because they cost time:** `focus({ preventScroll: true })` was applied first and did not fix it (kept, since it is correct on its own terms); and a `scroll` listener that reset `scrollTop` **froze the renderer** — assigning scroll position inside a scroll handler loops — and was withdrawn. Both were patches over a cause that had not yet been found. |
| UX-SCROLL-01 | Scrolling leaves a partial column (and row) against the leading edge | Done | P1 | **Reported by the user** from real use on a table: *"as scroll right, rows width shrink; on moving left it expand again; based a column; these behaviour is not constant."* **Reproduced in Chrome** against a 60-column table before anything was changed, and the reproduction is what identified it: arrowing to `AE` left a sliver of column J — the tail of one glyph — jammed between the row header and column K, with K's header label sitting over a column whose left edge was cut off. `ensureVisible` scrolled by the **bare minimum**: `state.scrollX = cL + cW - viewW` puts the target's right edge exactly on the viewport's right edge, which leaves the *leading* edge wherever it happens to fall inside another column. The leftover is a different size for every target, so the first visible column appears to change width as the selection moves — and scrolling back makes it whole again. That is precisely "not constant": there is no single wrong width to notice, only a width that never settles. Excel never shows a partial leading column. **Both axes, because it is one defect.** The row branch is the same arithmetic and leaves a partial row against the top; fixing only the axis that was reported would leave the identical bug behind the identical mechanism. **Snapping *up* to the next boundary**, never down: it only ever scrolls further in the direction already being travelled, so the cell that prompted the scroll stays on screen. The cell's own leading edge is the limit and the answer when it is wider or taller than the viewport — there no aligned offset can show it, and showing it wins over aligning. Rows go through `rowAtPx`/`rowOffsetPx` rather than the engine directly, since a row grown by wrapped or rotated text is taller than the engine believes and those two are the pair that knows it. Body-space against absolute-sheet-pixel coordinates is converted explicitly, because getting it wrong would misalign **only** on a sheet with a frozen pane — the kind of thing that survives a demo. Wheel and drag scrolling are untouched and stay smooth, which is what Excel and Sheets both do; snapping belongs to navigation. **Gated by** a browser test that drives the editor's own `select` and asserts `scrollX`/`scrollY` are members of the engine's actual boundary lists, over six targets forward and back — one jump could land on a boundary by luck. Column widths and row heights are deliberately **uneven** (`40 + (c*17)%60`), so an implementation that snapped to `round(px/width)*width` fails; and each target is also asserted still visible, since an "aligned" offset that hid the cell would satisfy alignment and defeat the point. Two new test-only exports (`scrollStateForTest`, `selectForTest`) because the grid is a canvas: a pixel test would be a test of what a column boundary is guessed to look like, and `selectForTest` routes through the real `select` rather than reimplementing it. **Mutation-verified:** restoring `scrollX = cL + cW - viewW` fails with `scrollX 951 after going to r80c30 is not a column edge` against edges `[0, 40, 97, 171, 262, 310, 375, 457, 556, 612, …]`; and the same revert reproduces the sliver in Chrome by eye. |
| VER-01 | The browser bundle is re-verified after the engine round | Done | P2 | `webapp/pkg` is not committed and is built by CI, so a local one goes stale silently — ci.yml carries a comment about a stale `pkg` once shipping 25 of 222 exports with every Rust gate green. Rebuilt after eight engine changes had landed behind it (`int_keys`, four `numfmt` fixes, the font-size bound, the axis clamps, the grid bounds, the content types) and re-checked in a real browser through WebAssembly rather than by re-running the Rust suite: `mmss.0` on 0.00358 gives `0509.3`; `0.00" %"` gives `0.50 %` while `0.00%` still gives `50.00%`; `00000` on 42 gives `00042`; `0.00` on 0.005 gives `0.01`; `"1"=1` is FALSE; `DATE(1900,2,29)` is 60; `SUM(TRUE,1)` is 2; a column resize applies; and a save/reopen round trip keeps `SUM(A2:A3)`. Worth doing as its own step: every one of those is a *displayed* value, and the Rust tests assert the function while the bundle is what a user meets. |
| FID-19 | An external relationship is dropped on open→save, and not reported | Done | P1 | `retain_unmodelled` skipped a relationship when `rel.external || <type is modelled>`. `/hyperlink` is already in `MODELLED_REL_SUFFIXES`, so the second clause alone handled the case the `external` clause was presumably written for — which left the first clause doing one thing only: **dropping every external relationship whose type nothing models.** The one that matters is `externalLink`, a reference to another workbook. It was neither retained nor recorded, so a workbook whose formulas read `[1]Sheet1!A1` came back from a save with `<externalReference r:id="rId9"/>` still in `workbook.xml` and nothing behind it — the `Omitted` + `NotRetained`-without-a-count case [34](34-SPREADSHEETML-FIDELITY-ARCHITECTURE.md) says can never be silent. **Retained, not reported**, because nothing is lost: `RetainedRel` gains `external`, the importer pushes the relationship and stops there, and the writer re-emits `TargetMode="External"`. **A URL is not a path**, so the external branch returns before `resolve_part`, before `package.contains`, and before `read_part`: `file:///other.xlsx` under `xl/workbook.xml` resolves to `xl/file:/other.xlsx`, and the package truthfully saying it has no such part would have reported a loss that never happened. `retained_drawing` in the writer gained the same guard for the same reason. Every `.rels` this writer emits now goes through one `retained_rel_xml`, because the attribute that is easiest to forget is the one that changes what the target *means*. **Additive under ADR-010 and `SCHEMA_VERSION` does not move**: `#[serde(default)]` reads a snapshot written before the field existed, `skip_serializing_if` keeps a workbook with no external relationship byte-identical — both pinned by a test, not assumed. **Gated by** a round trip of a package declaring an external `externalLink` on `workbook.xml` and an external `oleObject` on a sheet (two different `.rels` writers), asserting id, type and mode survive import→write→import; plus a hyperlink test asserting it is still *not* retained, so a fix that keeps everything fails. |
| FID-18 | A reference past the grid maximum becomes a corrupt saved package | Done | P1 | `parse_a1` accepted anything that fit in a `u32`, nothing range-checked the resulting `CellRef`, and `Workbook::validate` looks only at schema version, sheet ids and dangling handles. So `<c r="ZZZZ4294967295">` imported cleanly to `row=4294967294 col=475253` with an **empty report**, and the writer re-emitted it: a bad input turned into a corrupt output with no diagnostic, and a 475,254-column x 4-billion-row merge landed in `sheet.merges` for the layout to walk. [21](21-PARSER-LIMITS.md) lists 2^20 x 2^14 as a non-bypassable admission limit and **no such constant existed anywhere in `crates/`**. **Dropped and reported, never clamped, never refused.** [34](34-SPREADSHEETML-FIDELITY-ARCHITECTURE.md) names exactly one way for data to leave — `Omitted` + `NotRetained`, counted and named — and an unrepresentable address is what that taxonomy is for. Refusing the package was rejected because import is loss-*aware* rather than all-or-nothing, and docs/21's fail-closed rule governs *resource* bounds, which one bad address does not threaten. Clamping was rejected hardest: it yields a valid file whose `7` now sits in a cell nobody put it in, reported as `Mapped` — silent corruption dressed as success. A merge is refused whole rather than trimmed, since keeping the in-grid corner of `A1:ZZZZ4294967295` gives a merge over the entire sheet; a `<col>` span that merely *ends* past the last column is clipped, which moves no value because the columns beyond the grid are not columns. **Two escape routes the brief did not name, found by asking what else is written verbatim:** a formula — `SUM(A1:ZZZZ4294967295)` goes straight into `<f>`, so it now keeps its cached value and loses the expression, checked *after* the shared-formula shift since a shift can carry an in-grid master out; and `sheet.carried`, where `<dimension>`, `<selection>`, `<ignoredError>` and `<protectedRange>` are re-emitted attribute-for-attribute and had walked past every parsed path. Carried verbatim cannot mean exempt from the grid when verbatim is what gets written. `GRID_MAX_ROW`/`GRID_MAX_COL` now live beside `CellRef` in the model, with a test asserting they equal `casual_calc_formula::MAX_ROW`/`MAX_COL` so the two names for one number cannot drift. **Gated by** a walk over every reference in the *written* package rather than a named cell; reverting the one grid check in `a1.rs` fails it with the 4-billion-row merge. **Orchestrator correction:** the worker put that gate in `casual-calc-import` and added `casual-calc-export` as a dev-dependency to reach the writer — but export already dev-depends on import, so that made the two mutually dev-dependent. The gate moved to `casual-calc-export/tests/grid_bounds.rs`, where the edge already existed, and the manifest is unchanged: the crate DAG is fixed by ADR-003, not by whichever direction a test was written in first. |
| FID-20 | A `/hyperlink` from a part other than a worksheet is dropped silently | Done | P2 | Found by the FID-19 work, pre-existing and one hop out from it. `MODELLED_REL_SUFFIXES` matched on relationship *type* alone, so the hyperlink hanging off a **drawing** — the web address behind a clickable picture — was skipped as "modelled" even though the model only carries *cell* hyperlinks (`Sheet::hyperlinks`, resolved from the worksheet's own rels and re-minted on write). Same `Omitted` + `NotRetained`-without-a-count shape [34](34-SPREADSHEETML-FIDELITY-ARCHITECTURE.md) forbids — and sharper than most, because **the drawing's bytes are retained either way**: the `<a:hlinkClick r:id="rId3"/>` inside it survived the save while the relationship it named did not, leaving a dangling `r:id` in a part this writer emits verbatim, and a picture that had quietly stopped being a link. **A type does not say whether the model carries a relationship — the part it hangs off decides.** So the list became pairs: a new `PartKind` (`Root`/`Workbook`/`Worksheet`/`Other`) classifies each source as the retention walk pops it, and `is_modelled` matches type *and* kind. `Other` — every drawing, chart, image and customXml the walk reaches — models nothing, which is the safe default: a relationship kept twice is a bug visible in the file, one dropped is visible only to whoever opens it next. The whole list was source-sensitive, not just `/hyperlink`; `/officeDocument` is now root-only, the workbook's five workbook-only, and `/vmlDrawing`, `/table` and the comment types worksheet-only. **Gated by** a round trip of sheet→drawing→external `/hyperlink` asserting the relationship is retained against the drawing as its source and re-emitted with its id, mode and target, plus no `Omitted`+`NotRetained` entry. The existing "a hyperlink is modelled and so is not retained" test is the other jaw: **removing `/hyperlink` from the worksheet list turns it red while the new test stays green**, so the pairing is pinned in both directions and not just the new half. **Corpus: a verified negative.** Retention over all 16 real files in `fixtures/corpus` is byte-for-byte identical before and after — none of them puts a hyperlink on a drawing — so the change carries no regression risk on real files *and* the corpus does not cover the fix. Recorded as what it is rather than reported as corpus verification. (The same probe showed `58325_lt.xlsx` carrying `oleObject`, `image` and `drawing` relationships on one sheet, which is what makes FID-21's worksheet half an ordinary file rather than a contrived one.) |
| FID-21 | A retained relationship id can collide with a minted one | Done | P1 | `workbook_rels` emitted `rId1..rIdN` for the sheets, then `rId{N+1}…` for styles, sharedStrings, theme and persons, and appended retained workbook relationships **verbatim** afterwards. A file whose `externalLink` is `rId1` therefore yielded two `Id="rId1"` in one `.rels`, and `Id` is an `xsd:ID` — Excel repairs the package, and repairing it drops relationships. `root_rels` already solved exactly this by stepping the workbook's own id aside (FID-16); `workbook_rels` had no equivalent. Pre-existing for **every** retained workbook relationship — the existing test uses `rId9` and so never tripped it — and made easier to reach by FID-19, since external relationships are now retained rather than dropped. **The retained id is the one that cannot move**: it came from the file and the content naming it — `<externalReference r:id="rId1"/>` — is re-emitted verbatim, so a renumbered relationship would leave that element pointing at nothing. The minted ids are named by nothing but their own part, so they give way. One `RelIdMinter` now holds that rule, and `root_rels`'s open-coded loop was replaced by a call to it. **The same defect on worksheets, found while fixing this one and fixed with it** rather than left as a second row: `sheet_rels` mints a bare `rId1`/`rId2`/`rId3` for the VML, comments and threaded-comments parts behind a note, and appends retained sheet relationships beside them — so an ordinary sheet with **a picture and a note** collides. Worse than a duplicate id: `<legacyDrawing r:id="rId1"/>` in the worksheet names the VML that draws the note markers, and with two `rId1` it resolves to whichever Excel reaches first, leaving a note that is in the file with no marker to click. Tables, hyperlinks and new drawings are unaffected because their ids are deliberately prefixed (`rIdTbl1`, `rIdHl1`, `rIdDrawing1`) and cannot collide with a producer's `rId{n}`. **Minted once and handed to both writers** (`WorkbookRelIds`, `SheetRelIds`) rather than derived twice, because `workbook.xml` names each sheet by id while `workbook.xml.rels` defines it: a disagreement is a sheet Excel cannot open, and for the VML it is a silent missing marker. Ids are allocated for the optional parts whether or not they are written, so the numbering does not shift depending on whether a workbook happened to need a theme; the gaps that leaves are legal and `root_rels` already produced them. **Gated by** two round trips — two sheets against two colliding retained ids, so an allocator that merely starts one higher fails; and a note beside a retained external `oleObject` at `rId1` — each asserting no repeated `Id`, the retained ids unmoved, and every `r:id` **resolving to the right relationship**. Four mutations checked: both collisions reappear without the minter, and re-hardcoding either `<sheet r:id>` or `<legacyDrawing r:id>` is caught by the resolution half. |
| FID-17 | A retained part typed by a `<Default Extension>` is written with no content type | Done | P1 | Import recorded `RetainedPart::content_type` from `<Override>` only, and the writer emitted Overrides only — its own Defaults are `rels`, `xml` and `vml`. So any retained part typed by a `<Default Extension>` was written **undeclared**, and Excel refuses such a package or repairs it, and repairing drops the parts. Fourteen across the corpus: seven `printerSettings*.bin` in `45540_classic_Header.xlsx`, three in `sample.xlsx`, one in `WithMoreVariousData.xlsx`, `image1.jpeg` in `47668.xlsx`, and `attachment1.cbn` + `image1.emf` in `58325_lt.xlsx`. **Carried, never inferred.** `casual-calc-ooxml` now exposes both halves of `[Content_Types].xml` behind a `resolve(part)` applying the OPC precedence rule — Override naming the part, else Default for its extension, else `None` — folding case on both sides, since OPC compares part names case-insensitively and a file whose rels say `image1.emf` while its content types say `/xl/media/Image1.EMF` is well-formed. No extension-to-type table exists anywhere in the change: the type comes out of the file being read, or the part is honestly recorded as undeclared. **Written back as a per-part `<Override>`,** which is the deliberate half of the design. A `<Default Extension="bin">` is what a real producer writes, and would look more like the file we read — but one extension carries exactly one type per package, and `.bin` is printer settings in one workbook and an OLE object in the next. Grouping by extension would force the writer to pick a winner among types that disagree, and the loser would be *declared as something it is not* — strictly worse than the undeclared part being fixed, because the package would then open and misread it. **Gated by** a test that walks **every** part of every written corpus package and asserts each resolves against that package's own content types, plus a second save to prove the property is a fixed point; reverting `resolve` to Overrides-only names all fourteen. |
| FID-16 | Every part attached at the package root is dropped on open→save | Done | P0 | `retain_unmodelled` seeded its retention walk with the workbook part and the sheets only, so the package root was never queued, `_rels/.rels` was never read, and no root-attached relationship was followed. Excel hangs four there on **every** file it writes — officeDocument, core-properties, extended-properties, customXml — and none is reachable from `workbook.xml`. So opening an ordinary workbook and saving it dropped its author, its title, its company and every customXml payload, with an empty report to say so: the `Omitted`+`NotRetained`-without-a-count case [34](34-SPREADSHEETML-FIDELITY-ARCHITECTURE.md) says can never be silent. **Three parts to the fix.** Import queues the root, and `/officeDocument` joins the modelled suffixes, since the workbook part is regenerated and retaining it would write a stale copy beside the fresh one. A relationship whose target the package does not carry is now recorded rather than dropped, keyed by path and capped with an overflow bucket because those names come from the file. Export re-emits root-sourced retained relationships beside the workbook's own — without which the parts sit in the zip with nothing pointing at them, which Excel reports as needing repair — and `workbook_rels` now writes only what the *workbook part* declared, which also fixed a sheet drawing rel that was being written twice, once dangling. Third, found while checking: nothing numbers the root relationships, so a producer may already have used `rId1`; the writer's hard-coded `rId1` for the workbook then emitted two `Id="rId1"` in one `.rels`, and `Id` is an xsd:ID. The workbook's id now steps aside, the retained one never moves. **Survived the suite** because every export test built its package from a shared `ROOT_RELS` holding exactly one relationship, and the retention tests hang their parts off `workbook.xml` and a worksheet — the two seeds the walk did have. The corpus tests read real files that *do* carry root docProps, but assert only on cell content and that the file opens, never on what came back out of a write: both ends of the round trip were this codebase, agreeing with each other about a file that had quietly lost its author. **Verified independently** by round-tripping the 12 corpus files that carry docProps or customXml: 12 kept everything, 0 lost anything. |
| FID-14 | An absurd column width or row height overflows or goes negative | Done | P1 | `(px as i64) * 15` was unguarded, and `px` comes from an arbitrary `xsd:double` on `<col width>` or `<sheetFormatPr defaultColWidth>`. The float-to-int cast **saturates**, so `width="1e300"` gave `i64::MAX` and the multiply panicked with "attempt to multiply with overflow" under the dev/test profile — a crafted `<cols>` aborting the host process instead of returning `Err` — while the shipped release profile wrapped, and `width="-1e300"` handed layout a *negative* column width. `row_height_to_twips` had no multiply and no bound either. Both now clamp to Excel's own ceilings (255 characters, 409.5 points) **before** the multiply. Survived the suite because every existing width/height test used realistic values and every corpus fixture was written by a real producer, so nothing had ever fed the importer an absurd double. |
| FID-15 | A font size saturates to two billion points | Done | P1 | Found while checking FID-14 against the "no patches" bar: bounding two converters left the *source* of unbounded doubles unexamined. `<sz val>` does not go through `read_f64_attr` at all — it is `(v * 2.0).round() as u32`, and `as` saturates, so `<sz val="1e300"/>` reached the model as `u32::MAX` half-points, about two billion points of font, and `NaN` reached it as 0. A size travels to row autofit, text measurement and the rasteriser, none of which is prepared to be asked for a glyph that tall. Now bounded to Excel's 409-point ceiling, and a size outside the range is **dropped rather than clamped** — `None` means the font states no size, which every caller already handles, and a silently resized font is a worse answer than an unstyled one. `read_f64_attr` also now refuses non-finite values at the boundary, which is defence in depth rather than a fix: its four consumers are all width/height and already clamped. `to_micro`, which takes the tint, was already guarded. |
| FMT-01 | A time code with fractional seconds renders as its own format code | Done | P1 | `format_sections` asked about digit placeholders before dates, and `mmss.0` has a real `0`, so every fractional-seconds time went down the numeric path where `split_literal_runs` turns the field letters into a literal prefix. The cell showed `mmss0.0` rather than a wrong time — and `numFmtId="47"` is one of Excel's **built-ins**, so no unusual workbook was needed. Reordering exposed that `is_date_or_time` matched a field letter *anywhere*, including the `d` in `[Red]` and the `m` in `"Amount"`, so it now skips quotes, escapes and bracket tokens while still admitting the elapsed-time forms `[h]`/`[m]`/`[s]`. **Fractional seconds were then not implemented at all**: the group is taken out of the pattern before tokenizing, the time of day is rounded to the width it asks for, and the fraction is re-attached to the seconds field. |
| FMT-02 | A quoted `%` multiplies the displayed value by a hundred | Done | P1 | Percent scaling was decided by `section.contains('%')`, a raw scan, while every other scanner in the module carefully skips quoted literals, escapes and bracket tokens. In SpreadsheetML only an **unquoted** `%` scales; `"%"` and `\%` are text. So `0.00" %"` — the European convention for a value already in percent units with a decorative sign — displayed 0.5 as `50.00 %`, and the grid, the PNG render and the autofit width were all computed from a number a hundred times too large. |
| FMT-03 | Leading-zero placeholders are ignored | Done | P2 | Only the fractional digit count was read from the pattern, so the `0`s left of the decimal — the ones that force padding, and the whole difference between `#####` and `00000` — did nothing. A ZIP code stored as a number with the standard `00000` format displayed `42` instead of `00042`. |
| FMT-04 | Rounding is half-to-even where a spreadsheet rounds half away from zero | Done | P1 | Found while fixing FMT-02: Rust's `{:.*}` rounds half to even, which is statistically unbiased and not what Excel, LibreOffice or a column of money does. `0%` on 0.125 gave 12% against everyone else's 13%, `0.00` on 0.005 gave `0.00` against `0.01`. Values landing exactly on a half at the rounding position are what prices and percentages are made of, so this was wrong on ordinary data rather than on an edge case. Rounded to the target precision with `f64::round` before formatting, which makes the format a no-op. |
| COL-39 | Integer-keyed maps are undeliverable on the wire | Done | P0 | **Found by driving two browsers against the real server, after the engine tests said filters relayed fine.** `ClientMessage` is `#[serde(tag = "type")]`; serde reads an internally-tagged enum by buffering into its private `Content` type and re-deserializing the variant from the buffer — a pass that never reaches `serde_json` and so has none of its string-key-to-integer parsing. Every field typed `BTreeMap<u32, _>` came back `invalid type: string "0", expected u32` and **the whole message was unreadable**. That is autofilter rules, every column width, every row height, every outline level, and rich-text runs: applied locally, believed sent, never parsed, never seen by a peer. The client was told `CannotMerge`, naming the transform — the one part that worked. Observed live: Ada's column 150, Grace's 64, Grace's revision never moving. **Fixed** by `casual_calc_model::int_keys`, a `deserialize_with` that accepts a key written as a string or as an integer; **not a byte on the wire changes**, so no protocol bump and old and new clients still read each other. **Gated by** a test that round-trips a populated `Submit` through `serde_json::to_string` and back and names each map individually — restoring any one guard fails it with the production error string verbatim. The sibling test above it had closed the same class for `StyleId`/`StringId` and did not generalize; every other test on both sides constructed the message instead of parsing one. |
| CALC-01 | `MoveSheet` leaves the precedent graph keyed to the old order | Done | P0 | The kept graph is keyed by sheet **index**, and `MoveSheet` renumbers by removing and re-inserting, but it was classified `RecalcPlan::Skip`. After a tab drag every later edit to that sheet stopped propagating — silently, permanently, into the saved file. `SHEET()` also returns a sheet's position, so the operation changes values outright; the `Skip` comment was wrong on both of its claims. **Two more in the same path:** `SetSheetMetadata` was `Skip` while `SUBTOTAL`'s 101–111 codes and `AGGREGATE` read `is_row_hidden`, whose union includes the set an autofilter hides — so **applying a filter never recomputed the subtotal under it**; and manual calculation mode returned before invalidating, so a deferred recalculation ran against a stale graph. **Gated by** three tests asserting the kept graph equals a full recalculation, `SHEET()` after a drag, and a subtotal under a filter. Verified in the browser: `B1 = 200` after a drag where it froze at 4, and a filter moving a subtotal 6 → 4. |
| CALC-02 | Incremental recalc is not deterministic | Done | P0 | `apply_dirty` walked the dirty set straight out of a `HashSet`, whose iteration order is seeded per process, and `next_random` draws from a counter advanced in evaluation order — so which cell received which `RAND()` value changed run to run for identical input, and with it the saved bytes. Priority 2 in AGENTS.md. Volatiles are unconditionally always-dirty, so this fired on every incremental pass, which is the path every edit takes. **Fixed** by walking in sheet/row/column order, the order `recalculate_once` already uses. **Gated by** a differential test pinning the incremental assignment to the full-recalc one over thirty volatile cells. Confirmed by running the same program in eight processes: one distinct result, against six orderings in six runs before. |
| CALC-03 | Logicals in a range are counted | Done | P1 | `flatten_numbers` coerced `Value::Bool` to 1/0 in its two *reference* branches, so a column with a `TRUE` in it corrupted every aggregate over it — `AVERAGE` twice over, inflating sum and divisor together. Text was already skipped by the very next arm. Excel's rule is about how the value arrived: written as an argument a logical counts, read out of a reference it does not. The `A`-suffixed functions, which do count them, go through `stat_over_a` and are untouched. **Gated by** a test asserting both halves. |
| CALC-04 | `=`, `<`, `>` disagree with Excel and with this engine's own matcher | Done | P1 | `comparison()` tried `as_number()` on both operands first, and that parses text — so `="1"=1` and `=TRUE=1` were both TRUE where Excel says FALSE. The fallback compared UTF-8 bytes, making text comparison case- and code-point-sensitive. Meanwhile `loose_cmp` and `criterion_matches` already did it the Excel way while claiming in a doc comment to "match the engine's comparison rules" — so `COUNTIF(range,"yes")` and `SUM(IF(range="yes",…))` disagreed about the same data. **Designed** in [70](70-COMPARISON-SEMANTICS.md), including the locale-collation limit named rather than left to be found. **Gated by** the full type-ordering matrix and a test that the two halves of the engine now agree. |
| CALC-05 | The 1900 leap-year bug is not reproduced | Done | P2 | Serials were a proleptic Gregorian offset, omitting the phantom 1900-02-29 that Lotus invented, Excel reproduced deliberately and every spreadsheet since has reproduced from Excel. `DATE(1900,1,1)` was 2 against Excel's 1 and `DAY(59)` was 27 against 28. Blast radius is serials 1–60, but a workbook holding one reported the wrong day and a `DATE()` written back was off by one against every other spreadsheet. The correction lives only in the two conversions, so anything derived by subtracting serials inherits the quirk in the same direction, which is why `days_in_month(1900, 2)` now reports 29 — wrong about history, right about Excel. |
| SRV-01 | Cluster append counts batches while revisions count operations | Done | P0 | `order()` called `append(after = revision - 1)`, but a revision advances once per **operation** while the log holds one entry per **append**. The two agree only for a chunk of exactly one operation — and the editor batches everything typed inside a flush window into one. A two-operation chunk asked an empty log to accept `after = 1`, was refused `Stale`, and `order()` returned *after* `commit` had already applied both operations to the leader's own copy: diverged from the log permanently, its client never acknowledged, no peer ever told, every later append refused for the same reason. Every test here and in `net/tests.rs` submitted exactly one op. **Fixed** by passing both ends and gating on the last entry's recorded revision. **Gated by** a contract test run against both coordinators, which fails on either backend if the entry-count gate returns. |
| SRV-04 | An unauthenticated connection is bounded by nothing | Done | P1 | A WebSocket upgrade cannot require a token — the token arrives in the first frame — so anyone who could reach the port could open sockets and never speak. Each completed the upgrade, spawned a task, and parked in `authorise`'s `recv().await` with **no deadline**: the heartbeat and idle timers only start after a successful join, and every limit in `Limits` is per-document or per-message, so a pending connection was attributed to nothing and counted against nothing. They accumulate to the process's descriptor limit, at which point real joins fail at accept — while `/healthz` goes on answering "ok", so the load balancer keeps sending traffic to a node that can no longer take any. **Fixed** with a semaphore (`OPENCALC_MAX_PENDING_CONNECTIONS`) and a join deadline (`OPENCALC_JOIN_TIMEOUT_MS`), the permit released the moment the join succeeds — holding it for the session would be a second connection cap nobody configured. **Gated by** a socket that says nothing and must be closed, and a second connection over the cap that must be refused rather than parked; removing either bound fails them with `Elapsed`. |
| SRV-08 | Standalone broadcasts after awaiting the acknowledgement | Done | P1 | The cluster path holds an ordering lock from commit through publish and says why; the standalone path broadcast *after* `await`ing the sender's `Ack`, which is a suspension point. Two connections committing concurrently could fan out in the opposite order to their revisions, and a third participant would apply the later one first and set its revision backwards — undetected, since the client applies whatever arrives. More certainly: a failed acknowledgement returned early and the broadcast never happened at all, so an operation committed into the server's document reached nobody. **Fixed** by holding the same ordering lock across commit and broadcast, with the acknowledgement sent outside it. |
| SEC-004 | The zip-bomb bound is computed from the attacker's own headers | Done | P0 | `Package::open` sums `entry.size()` and compares ratios, and every one of those numbers is the central directory's — attacker input. `read_part` then capped a single part at the *whole package's* ceiling and never compared output to the declaration. Patching one four-byte field turned a 1 MB file correctly rejected as a 1028:1 bomb into one admitted and expanded to 600 MiB; unmodelled parts are retained in the model, so the bytes stayed resident. docs/47 called these bounds non-bypassable. **Fixed** by holding each part to what it declared and charging the package ceiling cumulatively against bytes that actually exist. **Gated by** a test that forges the header exactly as the attack does. |
| SEC-005 | A workbook colour executes script through the Print window | Done | P0 | `html_cell_css` interpolated `font_color`/`fill_color` verbatim into `style="…"`, and `session_print_html` hands that to `document.write` in a window inheriting the editor's origin with a live `window.opener`. The importer preserves the attribute verbatim, as it must, so the string is fully workbook-controlled. The SEC-001 CI check could not see it: it greps `webapp/*.js` and the host's HTML, and this markup is assembled in Rust. **Fixed by validating rather than escaping** — a colour is hex or it is not a colour, and one that is not is dropped. |
| WASM-03 | Case-insensitive replace traps the module on ordinary non-ASCII text | Done | P0 | `ci_replace` lowercased the haystack once and indexed it with offsets from the original, which only works if lowercasing preserves byte length. `İ` grows to two characters and the Kelvin sign shrinks to one byte. A panic here is not a catchable error: on wasm32 it traps, aborts the module, leaves the `RefCell` borrow held across it locked forever and takes the open workbook with it. A Turkish column heading was enough, through the default Find bar path — match case is off by default. **Also fixed:** `color_ref` sliced `s[2..]` on eight arbitrary *bytes*, so `<color rgb="aé12345"/>` trapped the engine on open. |
| FID-13 | A sheet name that is not an identifier is written unquoted | Done | P0 | Sheet names were quoted only when they contained a character outside `[A-Za-z0-9_]`, which `2024` passes — so it was emitted as `2024!A1`, text neither this parser nor Excel reads as a reference. On reopen the parse failed, the stale cached value was kept and the formula dropped: a hard-coded constant that never recalculates again, in violation of docs/36's fixed point. **Fixed** by asking the right question — a plain identifier that is not itself a cell reference in either notation — and erring toward quoting, which is always valid. **Gated by** a print-and-read-back round trip over the failing shapes plus the ordinary names, which must stay unquoted. |
| CI-006 | The Redis half of the suite passes while skipping | Done | P1 | `redis_store` ended in `.ok()`, so in CI — where `OPENCALC_TEST_REDIS` is always set, pointing at the service container — a missing, misconfigured or not-yet-ready container produced a **green** Redis half. That is exactly the "a suite that always skips half of itself reports on half of itself while looking green" failure the `services:` block in ci.yml was added to prevent, reintroduced one line below it. Unset still skips, for a developer without a server; set and unreachable now fails and says which. Verified all three ways. |
| DEV-01 | The dev server cannot serve two editors at once | Done | P1 | `webapp/serve.py` used `http.server.HTTPServer`, which handles one connection at a time, and browsers hold connections open with keep-alive. The **second** editor tab could not load its modules while the first had a socket open: its dynamic `import("./collab.js")` never resolved, with no error anywhere — indistinguishable from a collaboration bug, and found while chasing one. Two editors at once is the whole point of the collaboration demo and of the browser gate that drives two pages through this same server. Now `ThreadingHTTPServer`. |
| COL-35 | In-progress typing is invisible to peers | Done | P1 | Reported by the user: *"while editing in co-editing i can only see edit when it's done, not while typing."* A participant saw another's change only once the cell editor closed and the edit committed, so two people could be filling the same cell and neither knew until one of them lost. **The text travels, not merely the cell.** The cheaper design broadcasts only *where* somebody is editing — a real feature, and a lock indicator rather than co-editing. Watching a colleague fill a column is what people mean by co-editing a spreadsheet, so a `Draft { at, text }` rides the **presence** channel: ADR-011 keeps it off the operation wire, because a half-typed cell has no inverse, nothing depends on its history, and losing it must cost nothing. Bounded at 256 characters and truncated on a **character** boundary — `String::truncate` panics mid-codepoint, so a byte-counted bound would be a crash triggerable by typing an accent — and re-bounded on arrival, since a peer that does not bound its own does not get to decide how much text everybody else's grid draws. Drawn to the canvas with `fillText` and never into markup (SEC-001). **`PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays at 5**, argued rather than assumed: `editing` is optional on a message neither side reads with `deny_unknown_fields`, so an old peer skips it and a new peer reading a message without it concludes "not typing" — nobody is misled, and a bump would cost every unupgraded tab its session to add a cursor decoration. Gated by `a_peer_that_has_never_heard_of_drafts_still_reads_a_message_carrying_one`, both directions. **An abandoned draft clears by construction**: a participant owns one presence entry and replaces it wholesale, so there is no separate "stopped typing" message for a dropped socket to lose, and a disconnect clears it by the entry going with its author. **Verified in two real browsers**: one participant's uncommitted text appeared in the other's grid in the sender's server-issued colour with their name, then became an ordinary cell value on commit while the draft disappeared from the roster. Thirteen tests, including that a draft never reaches the document, the history or the applied log; reverting the engine's draft construction fails two of them. **Note on process:** the worker died on its final report after writing all seven files, so there was no `how_to_revert` and no claims to check — the mutation was found and run by the orchestrator instead, and the worker had never reached the gates, leaving two clippy errors and a rustdoc error that are fixed here. |
| COL-36 | No gate for the unresumed-reconnect data loss | Done | P1 | The fix — `collab.js` asks `collab_unacknowledged()` and announces *before* the snapshot replaces the document, and the transport withholds "live" until an edit made **after** the loss is acknowledged — was **verified by reading only**, which is the state this row existed to end. **The hard part was making the loss reachable at all.** An unrecognised resume key produces no refusal: the server simply sends a `welcome`, so from the client's side a restart, a rebalance to another node, an eviction and a key aged out of a bounded map are the same event. A resume key belongs to its document, so **evicting the document is how a server comes to not recognise one** — and eviction is the only one of the four that can be provoked in a few hundred milliseconds. **A second server instance rather than a shorter eviction on the shared one.** Eviction is global to a server: tuning the existing instance down to sub-second would have every other test in the file racing a timer it does not care about, and a gate that makes twenty unrelated tests flaky to make one possible is a bad trade. The new instance differs in exactly one setting (`OPENCALC_IDLE_EVICTION_MS=500`) and is used by exactly one test. **Two preconditions that are easy to get wrong and silently turn the test into the happy path.** The participant must be **alone**, or a second one holds the roster non-empty and there is nothing to reconnect into. And **nothing may be edited before the disconnect**: `evict_if_idle` refuses to let go of a document with unsaved work — correctly, since the host has to get it back — so a single early edit keeps the document resident, the resume key with it, and the test would quietly exercise resumption while appearing to pass. Both are stated in the test rather than left to be rediscovered. **Asserted: that the loss is real, not merely announced.** The cell typed while offline is checked to be gone and the document's reason checked to be `welcome`-shaped rather than `resumed` — an announcement that fires when nothing was lost trains people to ignore the one that matters. **Verification, stated honestly.** The gate passes against the fix (6.6s) and the same assertions were exercised repeatedly. The **mutation run could not be completed locally**: this machine suspends network I/O during long Playwright runs — `net::ERR_NETWORK_IO_SUSPENDED` and `ERR_ABORTED; maybe frame was detached?`, which is also what produced 19 spurious failures across every spec file in one full-suite run, including "the editor boots". What *is* established statically is that `status("lost", …)` has **exactly one emitter** (`webapp/collab.js:215`), inside the branch this fix introduced, so nulling the branch cannot leave the assertion satisfiable. That is an argument, not a run, and it is recorded as one — CI executes the gate on every push. Then that "live" does **not** reappear after the notice, which is the half that stops a data-loss message being erased a moment later by a status line saying everything is fine; then that it *does* return once an edit made after the loss is acknowledged. |
| COL-37 | A refused session cannot be rejoined without a reload | Done | P1 | Observed live, driving the real editor against the real server. `stopped` is terminal — the transport has closed the socket and will not reconnect, since reconnecting would be refused for the same reason — but only an explicit `stopCollaborating()` cleared the editor's `collabSession`. So after any refusal the editor believed it was still in a session that no longer existed, and the next `collaborate()` threw "already in a collaborative session". A token that expired could therefore only be recovered from by **reloading the page**, which throws away whatever the user had locally. Now a terminal state clears the session, roster and announcement state. **Gated by** a browser test that refuses a join with an unreadable token, then joins properly on the same page and edits a cell — which threw before the fix. Not run locally: the browser gate needs loopback HTTP the sandbox drops, so this is verified in CI.
| SRV-02 | The JWKS is frozen at boot | Done | P1 | `read_verifier` fetched once and moved the `KeySet` into the configuration for the life of the process, while ADR-014 and [59](59-COLLABORATION-SERVICE-STACK.md) state the opposite as decided — *"the server picks it up at the next fetch, and no coordinated restart is needed"*. A scheduled rotation therefore locked **every** user out of **every** document until an operator restarted every node, reported to the client as the same `NotAuthorised` a bad token gets; and revoking a compromised key had no effect on a running node at all. `select` refusing an unknown `kid` outright — which is correct — is what makes a stale set fatal rather than merely stale. **Fixed as two paths, because rotation fails in two directions.** A key being *added* announces itself: a token naming an unknown `kid` prompts one re-read and a retry, throttled by `OPENCALC_JWKS_MIN_REFRESH_MS` because that trigger is attacker-reachable and would otherwise turn every connection attempt into a request to somebody else's endpoint. A key being *withdrawn* announces nothing — nobody presents a token for a key being revoked — so a timer re-reads every `OPENCALC_JWKS_REFRESH_MS`, and that interval is the bound on how long a revoked key keeps working. A failed fetch keeps the cached set, per docs/59: a key server going down must not evict everybody. **Gated by** a test that installs a rotated set and asserts the new key is accepted and a withdrawn one stops working, plus a throttle test; freezing `install` fails it. docs/59 now says which half is which.
| FID-23 | A structural insert or delete never moved a table | Done | P0 | **[verified]** Found by the [73](73-EXCEL-UX-PARITY-AUDIT.md) audit and confirmed against the source. `structural.rs` shifted merges, the sheet's `auto_filter`, `filter_hidden`, sizing, hidden lines, the freeze boundary and outline levels — and **`sheet.tables` appeared nowhere in the file**; the only `tables.iter_mut()` in the workspace were the table operations themselves. So inserting one row above a table left its range, banding, filter buttons and column list on the old row numbers, and `SUM(Table1[Amount])` read the header text row and dropped the last record. **A wrong number in a saved file, with no error and no compatibility-report entry** — the module's own header comment lists what must move "or a merge, custom height, or freeze silently" breaks, and tables were never added to that list or its tests. **Fixed in the trait, not at the call sites.** `Positional` gains `tables_mut`, so `Sheet` and `SheetMetadata` both get it from the one macro — the bundle needs the same shift when a pending metadata edit is rebased past someone else's insert (ADR-011), and a shift implemented twice is a shift that disagrees with itself the first time a field is added. A table's range moves exactly like a merge; its **own** `auto_filter` range moves too, since a table filters independently of the sheet and leaving it behind puts the buttons on the wrong columns; and a delete clamps the range, drops the table when the band takes all of it, and removes the `TableColumn` entries **by position** — keyed by where each column *was*, because when the band only partly overlaps the table the survivors are the ones outside it, not the first or last N. **Widening sideways mints a column as well as the room for it.** `columns` is indexed left to right and every structured reference resolves through it, so a range one wider than the list silently shifts every column's meaning by one; ids stay unique because a filter rule refers to a column by id, and the generated `Column{n}` name is checked against the existing ones because two columns sharing a name make `Table[Column2]` ambiguous. Only an insert **strictly inside** widens — one at the table's own first column pushes the whole table right, which is what `at > start` distinguishes. **Gated by** five tests: a table moved wholly down, a table grown from the inside, a column insert that must produce a matching `TableColumn` with a unique id and name, a column delete that must keep the survivors outside the band, and a table deleted out of existence. **Mutation-verified:** removing the table handling from both paths fails all five, the first with the table still on rows 3..6 where it should be on 5..8. **Left open:** `header_row_count`/`totals_row_count` are not adjusted when a delete removes the header or totals row itself. |
| UX-NAV-01 | Arrowing out of a merged cell did nothing | Done | P1 | `select` snaps any coordinate inside a merge back to its top-left anchor, and the arrow step was ±1 from `state.sel` — so ArrowRight on `B2:D2` computed (1,2), snapped back to (1,1), and the cursor never moved. Left and up worked (the anchor *is* the top-left), so it failed asymmetrically and read as a frozen keyboard. One `stepFrom` leaves from the edge facing the way of travel; Enter and Tab use it too. **Gate:** both axes, both directions, plus an unmerged cell still stepping by one. **Mutation-verified in Chrome** — without it the cursor stays at col 1 instead of reaching col 4. |
| UX-NAV-02 | Ctrl+Shift+End/Home threw the selection away | Done | P1 | Both branches called `select` — which collapses to one cell — and never tested `shiftKey`, while the sibling handlers either side (Ctrl+arrow, plain Home/End) already did. **Gate** asserts the rectangle, not the active cell: the defect is a selection collapsing while the active cell still looks right. Mutation-verified. |
| BLD-01 | Release builds wrapped instead of panicking on overflow | Done | P1 | `[profile.release]` never set `overflow-checks`, and Cargo defaults it **off** — so arithmetic that panics in a debug test wrapped silently in the binary people run, and the tests proved a property the artefact did not have. This engine's arithmetic runs on indices, lengths and serials taken from untrusted files. Flagged as S3-5 in [60](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md) and tracked nowhere until now. **Gate:** the `repository-policy` job greps for the setting. 998 tests pass in release with it on, so nothing was relying on wrapping. |
| UX-CUT-01 | Cut then paste rewrote the moved formula's references | Done | P0 | The paste path shifted references by the per-cell delta whether the operation was a copy or a cut, and the `cut` flag only decided whether the source was cleared. Cutting `=A1+1` from B1 to D5 rewrote it to `=C4+1` — a formula pointing somewhere it had never referred to. A **copy** shifts (that is what a copy means); a **cut moves the cell**, so its formula travels verbatim. **Gate** asserts both halves in one test, so a fix that stopped shifting altogether fails. Mutation-verified: `left: "=C4+1", right: "=A1+1"`. |
| UX-CUT-02 | Esc cancelled the marquee but not the cut | Done | P0 | `stopMarch` touched no engine state and no `session_clip_clear` existed, so Esc — then a click elsewhere, then paste — still **moved** the data and emptied the source. The visible signal said cancelled and the state said otherwise, which is the worst pairing for an action that deletes. Mutation-verified. |
| DOC-026 | Tracker ids named more than one thing | Done | P2 | `docs/14` and `docs/52` ran independent `FID-01..FID-25` series for different work, colliding across **18** numbers — `FID-13` was both a sheet-quoting defect and a `sheetView` construct, so `git log --grep FID-13` returned two unrelated changes. Within `docs/14`, `UX-GRID-02`, `UX-CLIP-01`, `UX-B04` and `UX-B05` each named two defects, while the same file says ids are never reused; three of those clashes were created by rows appended without reading the existing id space. 52's series is OOXML construct coverage and became `OOX-`; the newer duplicates moved, since the older ids are the ones other docs cite. **Gate:** `tools/check-tracker-ids.py` in `repository-policy` — review cannot catch this, because a diff shows the row being added and not the one 300 lines up. |
| SEC-007 | Two high-severity advisories in the XLSX parser, unseen because nothing scanned | Done | P0 | `cargo deny` ran `bans licenses sources` and omitted **`advisories`**, and `cargo audit` was never installed or run — while [07](07-QUALITY-SECURITY-AND-COMPATIBILITY.md) and [15](15-CI-AND-RELEASE-GATES.md) both stated it gated the supply chain. First run found `quick-xml` 0.37.5 carrying RUSTSEC-2026-0194/0195, both **7.5 high**: unbounded namespace allocation and quadratic attribute checking, i.e. memory and CPU exhaustion reachable from any uploaded `.xlsx` — exactly this engine's threat model. Upgraded to 0.41. **Gate:** `cargo audit` in `dependency-policy`, plus `.cargo/audit.toml` listing the three *unmaintained* crates as dated decisions so a **new** one still fails. |
| SEC-008 | Entity references were dropped by the parser upgrade | Done | P0 | quick-xml 0.41 stopped inlining entity references in `Event::Text` and emits each as its own `Event::GeneralRef`. Nothing failed to compile: every `Text`-only arm kept working and silently dropped `&amp;`, `&lt;` and `&#65;` — `"Needs <review> & sign-off"` arrived as `"Needs  sign-off"`. Ten arms across four files, one found only by a clippy lint pointing at a line the first sweep missed. The worksheet arm routes text to seven destinations, so `Text` and `GeneralRef` share one resolved string rather than duplicating it. **Gate:** named and numeric entities through a shared string, inline text and a formula operator; mutation gives `"inline  here"`. |
| SEC-009 | CI actions were floating tags, and no scheduled security run existed | Done | P1 | [15](15-CI-AND-RELEASE-GATES.md) claimed SHA pinning and a weekly `cargo audit` + fuzz campaign; every `uses:` was a floating major tag and **no workflow carried a `schedule:` trigger at all**. A floating `@v4` resolves to whatever that tag points at today, so a retagged action runs with the workflow's permissions and nothing in the repository changed. 23 uses pinned to commit SHAs; `security.yml` added on a weekly cron. **Gate:** `repository-policy` fails on any floating `uses:`. Part of CI-005. |
| SEC-010 | The compatibility report grew without bound on a hostile file | Done | P0 | `CompatibilityReport.entries` was an unbounded `BTreeMap<String, _>` **keyed by strings from the document** — a cell's `t=` attribute reaches `record` verbatim (`import/src/lib.rs:1380`) — so a workbook with many distinct type strings inserted unbounded `String` keys. The reader's other admission limits do not help: each cell is legal, and the growth is in the report *about* them. [34](34-SPREADSHEETML-FIDELITY-ARCHITECTURE.md) already stated the report was bounded with an `(overflow)` bucket; it was not. Cap lives **inside `record`**, because there are 23 call sites and exactly one had bounded anything — a limit each caller opts into holds until somebody adds the 24th. Excess is **counted**, not dropped, per docs/34. **Mutation:** "the report grew to 2048 entries". |
| DEP-01 | TLS is configured, logged as on, and never wired | Done | P0 | **[verified]** `tls_config()` built a full rustls `ServerConfig` including a mutual-TLS client CA (`net.rs:500-560`) and had **no caller outside `net/tests.rs`**; `serve()` bound a plain `tokio::net::TcpListener` and handed it to `axum::serve`, while `axum-server` sat in `Cargo.toml` unmentioned by `src/`. An operator who set `OPENCALC_TLS_CERT`/`_KEY` got **plaintext WebSockets carrying document contents and bearer tokens**. **What made it P0 rather than a missing feature is that both things that should have revealed it agreed with the mistake**: startup logged `tls = exposure.public.is_tls()` — true because the *configuration* said so — and `--healthcheck` **downgraded to a bare TCP connect** whenever a certificate was set, so neither could tell plaintext from TLS. A deployment reported itself secure while it was not. The existing test said as much in its own comment (*"`serve` ignored all of it — so the TLS choice was documentation"*) and asserted only that a certificate parses: a test written to what the code did. **Fixed by carrying the built configuration, not the paths.** `ServiceConfig` gains `tls: Option<Arc<rustls::ServerConfig>>`, built in `main` *before* the listener opens, so a certificate that cannot be read or parsed refuses to start the process rather than failing per-connection on a node that is up, healthy and unusable. `serve_on_with_shutdown` branches: plain keeps `axum::serve`; TLS uses `axum_server::from_tcp_rustls` over `listener.into_std()` — the port is already open, and rebinding would lose every connection accepted in between. `axum-server`'s graceful shutdown is a `Handle` rather than a future, so the signal is bridged to it on the same `drain_timeout_ms` budget the rest of shutdown uses. **Both corroborating lies fixed too**, because the fix is worth little if the next mistake is equally invisible: the startup line now reports what was *built*, and the healthcheck fetches `/healthz` over **https** with verification off — deliberately, since a certificate is issued for the operator's hostname and this connects to loopback, so verification could only ever fail, while a completed handshake plus a served response is exactly what a bare TCP connect could not establish. **Gated** from the outside in both directions: a plaintext request must **fail** — the symptom the defect produced, and the only assertion that separates a fixed server from a broken one — and a TLS request must succeed, so a server that merely refuses everything cannot pass. **Mutation-verified:** making `serve` ignore the configured certificate fails with *"the endpoint answered a plaintext request: Ok(Response { status: 200 })"*. Known as S3-2 in [60](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md); no row had closed it. **Still open and split out: `OPENCALC_INTERNAL_BIND` is parsed and validated (`src/main.rs:205-212`) and never bound** — the internal endpoint does not exist as a listener, which is where mutual TLS was meant to live. |
| DEP-02 | In a cluster every replica saves the document, not just the leader | Done | P0 | **[verified]** `leads(...)` had exactly two callers — the submit path and the inbox — so **ordering was leadership-gated and saving was not**. Every node holding the document ran the same save cadence: assemble the whole workbook, POST it to the integrator. N times the CPU and N times the callback traffic, and worse than either, **a lost-update path** — a node momentarily behind delivers an older package *after* the leader delivered a newer one, and a host that writes what arrives keeps the older. Invisible standalone, where one process leads everything by definition, which is why it survived. **`may_save` gates the sweeper**, and skips the **whole lifecycle** rather than the delivery alone: `tick` is not a query — it sets `in_flight` when it hands out a `Save` — so taking the action and dropping it would leave a replica's lifecycle believing a save was running and never asking again. **The drain too**, because a replica's "unsaved" means only "not saved *by me*" while the leader holds the same revisions, and shutdown is exactly when a stale copy is most likely to arrive last; `outstanding` is counted the same way it is drained, so the log line is not a different claim from the work. **And `adopt` passed a literal `0`** as the edit time, so the quiesce test `now - last_edit >= quiesce_ms` was true on the very next tick — a replica believed the document had been idle since the epoch. Leadership now decides whether a save happens at all, but the wrong timestamp is what produces the *next* defect: a replica promoted to leader would inherit it and save on a cadence nobody chose. `leads` now takes a node id rather than the whole `Membership`, since the decision depends on a lease and a name and not on the store — a predicate needing a live Redis connection cannot be tested without one. **Gated by** a two-node test asserting **exactly one** node delivers — never "node-one", because which node leads is not knowable from the test and must not matter. Its first run failed with *"nobody saved the document at all, so this proves nothing about who did"*, catching that the tokens carried no callback; that guard earned its place immediately. **Mutation-verified:** removing the gate fails with *"both nodes returned the document to the host: 1 and 1"*. Run against a real Redis locally, and CI supplies one, so it executes rather than skipping. |
| COL-27 | Remote cursor/selection and participant identity | Done | P1 | **Observed:** transport sent `{client,name,color,sheet,selection}`, the editor stored it in `collabRoster`, and nothing ever read it — co-editing looked exactly like editing alone. **Landed:** the grid paints same-sheet remote ranges with a wash, a 2px border and a name label in the server-issued colour, per pane; the server now sends the current roster to a joiner, without which whoever arrived second saw nobody until somebody moved; the host offers an editable display name instead of only inventing `Guest NN`. **Two defects only running found:** the palette is bare hex (`0891B2`), and an invalid `strokeStyle` is *silently ignored* by canvas, so every cursor would have inherited the accent colour and looked like a working feature — colours are normalised and validated now. And `ctx.textAlign` leaks from cell drawing, so the first label rendered as "st 21" with the rest painted white-on-white outside its tag; nothing in the code said so, only the screenshot. **Gated by** a two-browser test that reads the participant's colour from the roster rather than assuming a palette entry, finds those pixels on the canvas, follows them down the sheet, and requires them gone after departure; plus a server test that a joiner is told who is already here, at their *current* position. **Remaining:** proof across scroll, zoom, merges and frozen panes; the participant dot still shows only an initial. | **Finished here.** The cursor half had landed; what was missing was any way to see *who* is here — `grep -c "participant\|roster" webapp/editor.html webapp/editor.css` returned 0 and 0, so the only sign of another person was the coloured label beside their cell. Now a face stack in the **menu bar**, deliberately not the app header, which folds away via `hdr-collapse` — the people you are working with should not fold away with it. Hidden entirely until a session exists, since the editor is single-player most of the time. Degrades properly: "Only you" with a disabled row saying so, because an empty `role="menu"` reads as nothing to a screen reader; three faces plus `+n` past that, the list scrolling at `min(52vh, 320px)`. Each row carries colour, name, `Sheet!D8`, and "typing" with a pulsing dot when they hold a draft — following the *draft* cell rather than the selection, because a formula author's selection walks off to pick references. Clicking moves **the view only**, never your own selection: your active cell is where your next keystroke lands. **A participant name is untrusted text** — it comes from a token an integrator minted, and the name inside it is somebody's input. It reaches the DOM only via `textContent`; the colour goes through `participantColor()` and is assigned through CSSOM, which drops what it cannot parse; a client id is compared in a loop rather than interpolated into a `querySelector`. A participant named `<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>` produced zero `img` elements and the literal string as text. CI's sink gate, re-run locally: 0 unmarked sinks. **Verification is partial, and the gap is named rather than glossed:** Playwright cannot run in this environment — the sandbox drops loopback HTTP — so the three-test spec is written for CI and has **not** run against the real server. What did run: `node --check`, the SEC-001 sink gate, and the real editor with the real WebAssembly engine in Chromium, served by request interception with only the socket mocked, exercising every locator the spec uses.
| SEC-001 | Workbook-controlled DOM injection | Done | P0 | **Demonstrated, not inferred.** A workbook whose defined-name `refersTo` is `<img src=x onerror="window.__pwned=1">` puts a live `<img>` in the page the moment somebody opens the Name Manager on it, and `window.__pwned` becomes 1 — script from a file you were sent, running in the origin that holds the document, the session token and the collaboration socket. **Fixed at three sinks that took untrusted text:** the Name Manager (the finding's own), the import summary (which quotes the file's sheet and function names, and re-parsed `bar.textContent` as markup on top of it), and the host admin page's document list — a title is the **uploaded filename**, so `<img src=x onerror=…>.xlsx` ran on the page of whoever holds the admin token. All three build elements and set `textContent`. **Thirteen sinks remain and are all static** — literal SVG icons, menu scaffolding, the print page whose every workbook string is escaped in Rust — each now carrying an `oc-safe-html:` comment saying why, enforced by a CI check so a new one must be argued for rather than merely typed. **The check was wrong twice before it was right**, which is the point of testing a gate: `git grep -E` is POSIX ERE where `\s` matches nothing, so it saw 1 sink of 13 and reported success; and a three-line marker window let an unrelated nearby comment exempt a planted sink. It now matches `[[:space:]]`, requires the marker on the line directly above, and was verified to fail on a deliberately unmarked sink. **Gate:** `fixtures/generated/hostile-names.xlsx` carries the payload in a defined name's target, a sheet name and a cell value; the browser suite opens it, opens the Name Manager and the formula bar, and asserts no script ran, no `<img>` exists, no element acquired an inline handler and nothing was fetched — while the text is still *displayed*, since escaping it into oblivion would be a different bug. Reverting the Name Manager fix fails it with `__pwned === 1`. **Not attempted:** refusing hostile text on import. The file is allowed to say anything; not building DOM from it is the defence. Trusted Types remains open and is now worth considering, since the sinks it would have blessed are gone. |
| COL-29 | Record only successful local operations | Done | P0 | **Confirmed:** `WorkbookSession::edit` narrowed the operation and pushed it to the outgoing log *before* `History::apply` had said whether the edit was possible, so a locally refused edit was already queued — the next flush sent the server, and through it every peer, an operation this client had itself rejected. Nothing downstream can catch that: the operation is well formed, it simply is not what happened here. **Fixed** by keeping the narrowing where it was — it has to run against the pre-edit workbook, since afterwards the state it was written against is gone — and moving only the *recording* after success. **Gate:** one refusal per operation class (value, style, geometry, structural, batch), each asserting the outgoing log holds only the edit that actually applied, the workbook is unchanged, the refusal added no history step, and the saved bytes are identical. Restoring the pre-apply append fails it, naming the sheet-99 operation that would have gone to peers. **Also fixed here**, one method down and listed under SDK-008: `apply_raw` cleared `source` *before* deciding whether to refuse, throwing away the untouched-original guarantee on behalf of an edit that never happened. That test needed strengthening before it could see anything — asserted against our own output it passed either way, because re-serialising what this crate wrote can be byte-identical; it now uses the LibreOffice fixture, which our exporter does not reproduce byte-for-byte, and the mutation fails it. |

| E-003 | Selection model (`casual-calc-selection`) + fill/paste | Done | **Fill and paste shipped** — `session_fill`, `session_fill_mode`, `session_paste_tsv`, paste-special with transpose and formula reference adjustment. The `casual-calc-selection` crate stays a four-line skeleton **by decision, not omission**: a selection is a property of a *view*, not of the document, so the editor owns it and the engine is handed ranges rather than a cursor. The crate is a reserved boundary in case a second host needs shared selection algebra. Carried as Partial for a missing half that was never going to be built; if nothing depends on it at 1.0 it should be deleted rather than filled in. |
| P2-001 | Evaluator + full recalc (`casual-calc-eval`) | Done | Memoized recursive evaluation with circular detection; arithmetic/comparison/concat, unary, cell + range refs (same/cross-sheet), defined names; functions SUM/AVERAGE/MIN/MAX/COUNT/IF/ABS/ROUND; `recalculate(workbook)` full recalc; deterministic. Nothing depends on `-eval` except (future) host bridges. 7 tests |
| P2-003 | Broader function library + oracle diff (LibreOffice Calc) | Done | **376 functions**, and now an oracle that is not us. `tools/casual-calc-fidelity` builds a workbook from a committed corpus, hands it to `soffice --convert-to csv` — which loads, **recalculates** and writes what it computed — and diffs the two. It exists because every other test of the evaluator was written from the specification by whoever wrote the code it tests: that catches mistakes but not **misreadings**, where the test agrees with the bug. **Its first run found one, immediately.** Nine of 190 formulas came back wrong, and five of those were a single real defect: SpreadsheetML requires a function it postdates to be written `_xlfn.CONCAT`, and this writer emitted the bare name — so a workbook saved here containing `CONCAT`, `TEXTJOIN`, `SWITCH`, `IFNA`, `UNICHAR`, `XLOOKUP`, `LET`, `LAMBDA` or thirty-five others **opened in Excel with `#NAME?` in every one of those cells**, with nothing left to tell the user what the formula had said. No test in this repository could have caught it: both ends of every round-trip were this codebase, which agreed with itself perfectly while producing a file Excel would not read. Fixed at the format boundary in one place each way — the writer qualifies, the reader strips — with the table of names stated once so the two cannot drift. After the fix: 186 agree, 0 unexpected. Two limits are recorded rather than glossed. The oracle is LibreOffice and the **target is Excel**, so a case where those two legitimately differ is marked `@differs: <reason>` in the corpus and the run fails if the difference ever *disappears*, which is what stops an excuse rotting. And LibreOffice's CSV writes its own error codes (`Err:502`), which the format has no token for, so where both sides error but spell it differently the run reports **error-class agreement** instead of pretending to have adjudicated `#NUM!` against `#VALUE!` — four formulas sit there today. Still open, and now visible: `LAMBDA`'s parameters need their own `_xlpm.` prefix, which is not yet written. |
| P2-004 | Volatile functions, spill/dynamic arrays, iterative calc | Done | All three. **Volatile functions** need the host to supply a clock before anything volatile evaluates — an engine that reads the wall clock cannot be reproduced or agreed on by two hosts computing the same workbook. **Spill / dynamic arrays** refuse rather than overwrite, which is what `#SPILL!` is for. **Iterative calculation** was the one still missing, and its absence was not a missing feature but a broken file: a workbook whose author enabled iteration — the only way some financial models can be written, where a balance depends on the interest it accrues — opened here as a sheet of `#REF!`. `docs/29` had predicted exactly this, noting that `<calcPr>` is "inert while the calc engine is held back, and becomes load-bearing the moment it lands". The settings are **read from the carried attributes** rather than stored again, so the verbatim round-trip stays the single source of truth. With iteration on, a re-entrant cell returns its **previous pass's value** instead of `#REF!`, which is what turns the loop from an error into a sequence that can converge; the outer pass then repeats until nothing moves by more than `iterateDelta` or `iterateCount` passes have run. **Both stopping conditions are load-bearing** — convergence is the one that matters and a divergent or oscillating model has none, so the count is what guarantees termination. Excel's defaults (100, 0.001) apply when a file enables iteration without saying how much, and a malformed limit falls back to them rather than disabling the loop: the author asked for iteration, and refusing over a detail they cannot see would turn a working model into errors. Off by default and costing one map lookup when off, which is almost every workbook. 6 tests. |

Per-glyph coverage fallback for missing glyphs landed separately. **Corrected from "Not started" during CI-003.** |

Incremental repaint is the host's for the same reason (the canvas coalesces to one rAF, UX-P02). |

Invariant gated: viewport == full restricted to window. Reads cached values only (no calc). 8 tests |

Deferred: negative/zero/text sections, currency/literals, token-exact date layout |

Round-trip gated by the semantic fixed point. Verified in-browser: wide/narrow columns + tall rows render. Editor adds **interactive drag-to-resize** (undoable `SetColumnWidth`/`SetRowHeight` ops, live preview, double-click-to-reset) and **fluid pixel scrolling** (absolute `scrollX/scrollY`, sub-cell offsets, clipped body/headers). Hidden/outline still pending |

Semantic fixed-point gated (import→write→import equal); import pre-interns cellXfs in order for canonical style round-trip. 3 round-trip tests |
| P1B-002 | Byte-identical repackager (retention mode) | Done | **A file opened and not edited now saves as itself, byte for byte** — verified against packages this engine did not write, including one LibreOffice produced. The guarantee lives in the **session**, not the model, and that is the design decision: `WorkbookSession` keeps the bytes it opened and returns them from `save` while nothing has been edited. Putting the original in the `Workbook` would have doubled every model snapshot for a property that is about *this opening of this file*, not about the document. Why it matters: the semantic writer reconstructs canonical OOXML, so anything unmodelled is rebuilt from what **is** modelled and a merely-retained part comes back in a package that is no longer the author's. Opening a workbook to look at it should not rewrite it. The source is dropped by any edit, by undo and redo, and by `workbook_mut` — that last one conservatively, whether or not the caller writes a byte, because handing out `&mut Workbook` is handing out the right to change anything and the alternative failure is silently returning a package that is not what the session holds. A refused edit in a read-only session keeps the guarantee, which is the same rule the history follows. Two limits stated rather than glossed: **undoing back to the opening state does not restore it** (the workbook is equal, but that does not prove the package would be, and guessing wrong hands back a wrong file), and an unedited save returns the file's **own** cached values rather than the ones computed on open — if those disagree the file was already inconsistent, and reproducing it exactly is the more conservative answer, since this engine does not model every construct that might explain the difference. 7 tests. |
| P1B-003 | Opens without repair in LibreOffice Calc | Done | `casual-calc-fidelity --validate-package`, riding on the harness P2-003 built. The method is a **full re-save**, not an open: `soffice` is asked to convert our `.xlsx` back to `.xlsx`, which drives its reader over every part and then writes out what it understood, and re-importing that catches what an "it opened" check cannot — a part skipped, a merge never seen, a freeze lost. The input is a workbook carrying the structure the format has to hold: values of all four kinds, formulas, a future function, a merged banner, a freeze on both axes, a hidden row, a resized column, two sheets, a cross-sheet formula and a defined name. **26 of 29 properties survive identically**; the three that do not are LibreOffice rewriting boolean and error *literals* into formulas on its own way out, which is a spelling change and not a loss. Those three are listed with reasons rather than normalised away, and the allowance rules are deliberately narrow — a flipped boolean, a different error, a value silently becoming a formula and a changed range are each asserted **not** to be forgiven, because the risk with an allowance list is that it grows into "close enough". The honest limit is stated in the tool and in [15](15-CI-AND-RELEASE-GATES.md): this is **LibreOffice's** acceptance, not Excel's, and Excel's repair prompt cannot be tested without Excel. |

Font/fill/border deferred to P1A-003b. 18 tests (model 10 + import 8) |

Borders + export of fonts/fills are follow-ups. Import test gates it |

Editor draws borders (thickness/dash by token) + **All-borders toggle** (`Ctrl+Shift+7`, one undo step); border-only empty cells now render. PNG-render borders pending (display-list contract). Also: editor chrome split into header + toolbar bars |
