| SDK-007 | npm packaging and the tag-scoped release | Done | Three packages assembled from the repository rather than developed as one, so the editor stays as loose files under `webapp/` served straight off disk — a build step between typing and seeing is a tax paid on every iteration. `sdk/build-packages.mjs` is the only place that knows the layout the element expects at runtime (everything flat beside `embed.js`), and it asserts rather than assumes: three versions agreeing, `@opencalc/react`'s exact pin on `@opencalc/sheet` matching, and the engine exporting four sentinel functions from this release. **The sentinel check earned itself immediately** — `crates/casual-calc-wasm/pkg/` held an August 5 build with 25 of the 222 exports, and a size floor would not have caught it because an optimised build is *smaller*. The version check [docs/55](55-SDK-EMBEDDING-AND-INTEGRATION-DESIGN.md) promised now exists: `opencalc-assets` writes a manifest, the element reads it before loading anything, and a mismatch throws naming the command that fixes it — while a *missing* manifest only warns, because absence is not evidence of a skew. Releases fire on a **component-scoped tag** (`sdk-v0.0.0`, not `v0.0.0`): a desktop app and a collaboration server are coming, and two release workflows watching one tag namespace means tagging either fires both. Credentials sit on the `release` environment; `PKG_USERNAME`/`PKG_PASS` are exchanged for a registry token, which cannot work once 2FA is required for publishing and says so rather than failing opaquely. Verified end to end in-browser from the packaged `dist/` — boots, hoists fonts, 189 commands — and the skew path verified by mutating a copied manifest. |
| DOC-024 | The deployed site was missing the pages it was being blamed for | Done | `pages.yml` copied a hand-written list of files that had drifted: `docs.html`, `embed.html`, `embed.js` and the whole `fonts/` directory were never deployed. So the SDK guide **404'd on the live site** while working perfectly on localhost, and every cell fell back to a system font. Invisible locally, where the same files are served straight off disk — which is exactly why the fix is to copy the directory and then *assert* the result contains what the site needs, rather than to add three more filenames to the list. |
| COL-01 | Concurrency model for shared editing: OT vs CRDT | Accepted | [ADR-011](08-ADR-REGISTER.md) / [docs/56](56-COLLABORATION-CONCURRENCY-DESIGN.md), closing the decision [24](24-TRANSACTION-AND-EDIT-SEMANTICS.md) left open. **Server-mediated operational transform over the existing op set, not a CRDT.** Decided principally by two things neither of which was chosen with collaboration in mind, which is what makes them evidence rather than rationalisation. First, the **per-cell byte ceiling** (ADR-004, [23](23-CELL-STORE-REPRESENTATION.md)) exists so a million cells fit a bounded budget; CRDT elements carry an id, causal ordering and tombstones — 16–32 bytes per *character* for text CRDTs — and even a coarse map-of-registers over cells spends that in every session, including the overwhelming majority with one editor. OT's concurrency state is per *session*: a revision number and a short queue, zero when nobody else is connected. Second, **our documents are files, not histories**: an `.xlsx` is a snapshot with no causal ancestor, so two people opening the same file produce replicas whose merge is meaningless; making CRDT work would promote its state to source of truth and demote the file to import/export, contradicting ADR-007 where retained bytes are authoritative. Two supporting reasons: a server is assumed anyway, which removes the TP2 obligation that is most of OT's difficulty and that virtually every shipped OT system sidesteps the same way; and structural ops already have reference-rewriting in `casual-calc-transaction`, so `transform(op, InsertRows)` is that arithmetic applied to a pending op rather than the model — whereas a CRDT needs row identity to stop being an integer index, which reaches into the model, layout, reference algebra and writer. **Costs stated rather than hidden**: no true offline-first (bounded offline, with a client further behind than the retained history reloading and being *told* its unsent ops were dropped), no serverless deployment, and transform correctness being the standard way OT ships broken — mitigated by a property-based TP1 convergence test rather than by care. **Accepted** after the two questions that could have changed the answer were settled: bounded offline is acceptable, and peer-to-peer co-editing is never required — so the CRDT question is closed for the document model. Three things were added on acceptance. **The snapshot model**: a document at revision *N* is a snapshot at *M* plus the ops since, which is nearly free because ADR-010 already gives a deterministic byte-stable snapshot, and which bounds log growth, makes a late joiner cost the same as a fresh one, and defines the offline edge precisely (a client older than the retained window reloads and is *told* its unsent ops went, rather than losing them quietly). It also buys an integrity check for free — replaying the log from an earlier snapshot must produce bytes identical to the stored later one, verifiable with the existing golden machinery. **Serverless at both ends**: one editor contacts nothing, and a session is a single-writer actor per document, which is exactly a Durable Object rather than a compromise on one; the snapshot cadence therefore becomes a latency knob and not only a storage one. **A hybrid, but partitioned by domain, not layered**: presence is ephemeral last-writer-wins with a TTL, never transformed, never persisted, never in a snapshot — the document stays under one reconciliation system, because two convergence arguments over the same cells compose into something nobody can reason about and reinstate the per-cell cost that decided this in the first place. **Where recalculation runs is also settled**: wherever the authoritative model is — the browser when there is no server, both ends in a session, native Rust on the desktop. Determinism is what makes that a free choice rather than a trade-off, and it has two consequences worth stating. The wire carries **ops, not values**: every participant applies the same ordered ops to the same base snapshot and arrives at the same cells, so the collaboration protocol is the op set and nothing more. And the **environment becomes server-issued and stamped per revision** — `TODAY()`, `NOW()`, `RAND()` read `volatile_now`/`volatile_seed`, which the engine already never samples from a clock, so in a session those stop being the client's to choose or two clients recomputing the same revision disagree. The honest edge, recorded rather than smoothed over: a client evaluating its own unacknowledged ops has no stamp yet, so a volatile cell can change value when the revision lands. Confined to volatiles, transient, and better than round-tripping before showing the user anything. **The scoping items are now answered, and one of them turned up a prerequisite.** *Presence*: in the first cut — cheap, and its absence reads as broken rather than minimal; Yjs's awareness shape, a `Map<clientId, state>` each client overwrites wholesale, dropped after ~30s without a refresh (their number, roughly three missed heartbeats), never persisted. *Snapshot cadence*: **200 ops or on quiesce, retaining two intervals** — an order of magnitude under ShareDB's 1,000-version default, because our ops are coarse (a `Batch` paste is one op carrying a hundred thousand cells) and our snapshots are cheap; and because a Durable Object hibernates after **10 seconds**, cold start is not a rare event to amortise but the normal case for an intermittently-edited document, so the cadence is tuned for wake latency rather than storage. Starting points to benchmark, not derived. *Retained bytes*: server-owned and never transformed — retention is not in the op set, invalidation is a side effect of ops that cause it and is idempotent, and the CHT-04 anchor stripper runs server-side at save so there is exactly one writer of the drawing bytes. *Comments*: the document, not presence — they persist and round-trip. |
| COL-02 | `SetSheetMetadata` granularity — prerequisite for COL-01 | Done | Found while researching COL-01, and it is the thing that would have made collaboration quietly lossy. `SetSheetMetadata` installs a **23-field whole-sheet bundle** — merges, sizing, hidden lines, view, autofilter, outlines, validations, conditional formats, comments, visibility, protection, hyperlinks, tables, charts, pivots, print setup, sort state. It exists for a sound single-user reason: a structural delete drops merges and freeze bands that re-inserting an empty band cannot recover, so the inverse carries a pre-mutation snapshot. Under OT it is a **collision magnet** — one client resizes a column while another adds a comment, and the later op silently discards the earlier one's work. Cell editing would work beautifully while every *non-cell* edit in the product quietly lost data, which is the worst available failure shape for a project whose first principle is no silent data loss. Two stages, both **before** collaboration rather than during. **(1) A change mask**: the op records which fields it intends to change, so transform merges disjoint field sets and conflicts only on the same field; the single-user path is untouched, and this alone removes the large majority of collisions because concurrent editors usually touch different things. **(2) Identity for collection elements**: `Table` and `PivotTable` already carry a stable `id`, but charts, comments, validations, conditional formats and hyperlinks are **positional** — a `Vec` index — and index-keyed collections are exactly what breaks under concurrency, since two concurrent inserts both claim index 2. Stable ids turn each into a keyed set where inserts merge and only same-element edits conflict. Also corrected a line in [56](56-COLLABORATION-CONCURRENCY-DESIGN.md) that this exposed as sloppy: needing an op the single-user editor lacks is evidence against the ADR only for new *semantics*; refining an existing op's **granularity** adds no operation and no semantics, it makes an op say what it already meant. **Both stages built.** Stage 1: `apply` narrows a declared bundle to the fields that actually differ and the inverse inherits the narrowed set, so callers keep the ergonomic "capture, change one field, submit" path — by the time they submit they genuinely do not know what changed, having handed a mutable bundle to whatever edited it, and `apply` is the only place holding both states. Mask, diff and masked install are generated from one field list by a macro, because the bundle has grown from six fields to twenty-three and a field missing from the diff is an edit that neither merges nor conflicts but simply vanishes. Stage 2 **contradicted its own plan, for the better**: the intent was ids on all five positional collections, but four already have a natural key — a comment by its cell, a hyperlink and a validation by their range, a conditional format by its OOXML `priority`, which is unique per sheet and is already the ranking. Inventing ids for those would have added a second identity to keep in step with the anchor and a field in every snapshot. Only `ChartView` genuinely lacked one, because two charts may cover the same cells; it is assigned on import in document order so a file always numbers identically, skipped on write when unassigned so an older snapshot round-trips byte-identically (ADR-010), and exposed on the wire so the host can stop keying by index. Ten tests. Verified in-browser: two charts over identical cells get distinct ids that survive undo and redo, and undoing a comment leaves an earlier gridline change intact. |
| COL-03 | `transform` and TP1 as a property | Done | The concurrency primitive from [ADR-011](56-COLLABORATION-CONCURRENCY-DESIGN.md), and nothing above it — pure functions over the op set, no server, no protocol, testable alone. `transform(subject, against, side)` rebases an op written against one state onto a state where another has landed. Covers cell writes, axis sizing, row/column structure against each other and against everything else, and metadata bundles resolved per field. **Refuses rather than guesses** on three pairs: anything renumbering sheet indices (a second transform axis), a metadata bundle meeting a structural op on its own sheet (the positional fields inside the bundle need the same shift the sheet got), and a `SetCell` carrying a formula ordered before a concurrent style change (`SetValue` is the only content-without-style op and it carries a value, so the formula has nowhere to go — refusing beats quietly turning it into its last result). An untransformed op applied to a state it was not written against diverges the replicas *silently*, which is the one outcome this layer must not produce. **The property test earned its keep twice, on the first run.** (1) Transforming a `Batch`'s members against the same unchanged `against` is the obvious implementation and it is wrong: member two was written against the state member one produced, so it lands a member's worth of shift out of place; `against` has to advance past each member as the member is consumed. (2) Same-cell contention needed a tiebreak both replicas compute identically — "whoever is transforming" is the one fact they disagree about — so the settled order is passed in as `Side`. That in turn exposed a third thing: "the earlier op yields entirely" is too blunt, because `SetStyle` keeps the value and `SetValue` keeps the style, so bolding a cell while someone types into it would have destroyed the typing. A cell is written in two aspects and the earlier op yields only the ones the later one overwrites. Also fixed the boundary that makes insert-inside-delete convergent: an insert at a deleted band's *first* line sits before it and survives, pushing the delete along; strictly inside, the delete widens and the insert becomes a no-op. Those two rules are only correct as a pair. TP1 asserted over 3,000+ generated pairs plus 12 named cases. |
| UX-B03 | A structural insert or delete left the outline behind | Done | Found while generalising the metadata shift for COL-01's transform, not by a failing test. `shift_metadata_insert`/`_delete` move merges, the autofilter, axis sizing, hidden lines and the freeze band — and never touched `row_outline_levels`, `col_outline_levels`, `collapsed_rows` or `collapsed_cols`, all four of which are keyed by line index. Insert three rows above a group and its levels and collapse flags stayed on the rows the group used to occupy: the outline silently detached from its own rows, and the grouping controls then operated on the wrong ones. Both directions fixed through the same axis accessor the other fields already use, so the next positional field added is one line in one place rather than four. Two tests, including the round-trip back through the inverse. |
| COL-04 | Rebase a pending metadata edit across a structural op | Done | The first of COL-03's two refusals, answered. A bundle carries positional state of its own — merges, axis sizing, hidden lines, the freeze band, the outline — so a pending resize written before someone else's inserted column has to move with the sheet or it lands on the wrong column. `shift_metadata_insert`/`_delete` now work through a `Positional` trait implemented for both `Sheet` and `SheetMetadata`, so the rebase reuses the shift `apply` performs rather than growing a second copy that disagrees with it the first time a field is added. Generalising it is what surfaced [UX-B03](#). Two further findings, both from the property test rather than from review. **Ops must be narrowed before they enter the protocol**, not inside `apply`: `transform` reads the mask and never sees a workbook, so an op still declaring `ALL` reads as contending with every concurrent metadata edit and one of them is discarded for nothing. `Operation::narrowed` does it against the state the op was written on, which is the only moment both states exist — by the time two ops meet, that state is gone. **A bundle and a single-line resize write the same field at different granularities**, so neither "the earlier yields the field" nor "these do not collide" converges: the bundle is the whole sizing map and the resize is one key in it. The earlier one now concedes exactly that key, and `sheet_fields` reports `COLUMNS`/`ROWS` for the resize ops so the collision is visible at all — reporting `NONE` said a column resize could not collide with a bundle replacing every column width, which is exactly backwards. |
| COL-05 | Rebase across sheet renumbering | Done | COL-03's second refusal, mostly answered. `InsertSheet`, `RemoveSheet` and `MoveSheet` renumber every `sheet` index in the workbook, which is a second transform axis on top of the row/column one. An ordinary edit now follows its sheet across all three — and becomes a no-op when the sheet it named was removed, which is the same shape as a cell inside a deleted band. A move is mapped as the remove-then-insert `apply` actually performs, rather than as a formula derived separately that could drift from it. **Still refused: a `MoveSheet` rebased across another sheet-level op.** A move is defined by the sheets it lands *between*, and a bare pair of indices does not record that, so two concurrent reorderings have no answer that is obviously the intended one; it is also rare, so refusing costs little. The property test found one more bug on the way: `same_target` treated **any** two `RenameSheet` operations as contending regardless of index, because `sheet_of` does not see a rename's index — so renaming two different sheets concurrently silently dropped one of them. The generator now runs on a two-sheet workbook with sheet-level ops in the candidate set, and the comparison keys sheets by **name rather than position**, since comparing position by position would have called two different orderings equal. 7,897 pairs converge, 24 refused, all of them concurrent reordering. |
| COL-06 | The session protocol: client and server state machines | Done | Two state machines over `transform` and nothing else — no transport, no storage, no clock — so the whole protocol runs in a test with several clients and no network, which is the only way its interleavings get exercised at all. The client applies its own edits immediately (waiting for a round trip would put the server between a keystroke and the character appearing), holds them until acknowledged, and keeps **one chunk in flight** — Wave's rule, and what makes a single server order sufficient. An arriving operation is ordered *before* a client's unacknowledged edits, so it rebases as `Earlier` while they rebase as `Later`: the two halves of the diamond TP1 describes. Ops are narrowed on the way in, never later, because the transform reads the mask and never sees a workbook. The server refuses a base outside its retained history with the range it needed — the bounded-offline edge from ADR-011, where the client is *told* rather than having its work dropped — and rebases a whole chunk before applying any of it, so a refusal leaves the document untouched instead of half-committed. **Mutation testing is what made the tests real.** Eleven tests passed on the first run, which after five iterations on the transform was not believable; deliberately breaking four things showed two of the breaks went undetected. Writing the tests that caught them then found a genuine bug in the unmutated server: it pushed a chunk's own operations into the concurrent history, so the second operation in a chunk was rebased against the first — which the client had already written it against — and landed two rows out. All four mutations are now caught: the client's side convention, the client rebasing its own buffer, the history advancing past each chunk operation, and the one-chunk-in-flight rule. |
| UX-B04 | Scrolling redrew synchronously on every wheel event | Done | Reported as "scroll became lagging". The wheel handler called `draw()` inline, and a trackpad delivers wheel events at 100–120Hz — so the main thread was asked for two or three full repaints inside one frame, of which the browser can only ever show the last. The surplus is not merely wasted: the handler runs long enough to push the frame past its budget, so what is drawn arrives late, which is what "heavy" feels like. Measured before the fix at **6.7 ms median and 13.3 ms worst per event**, with zero engine calls in it — the cost was entirely canvas painting, not the model. Redraws for the two high-frequency scroll paths (the wheel, and dragging a scrollbar thumb, which fires one per `mousemove`) are now coalesced to one per animation frame; moving the offsets stays synchronous, because it is cheap and the scrollbar geometry reads it. After: **0.0 ms median, 0.1 ms worst**, sixty events costing 0.6 ms in total against roughly 400 ms before. Not a regression from recent work — the handler had always drawn inline — which the measurement showed by finding no engine calls on the path at all. |
| UX-B05 | Toolbar tooltips could not be translated | Done | Found while investigating UX-B04. The custom tooltip layer moves `title` onto `data-tip` to suppress the native bubble, and it runs *before* the pass that registers tooltips for translation — which looked only at `[title]`. By then just thirteen nodes still had one, so **64 of the 75 tooltips were never registered**, and `tip.*` — a documented part of the SDK's localization surface, in the guide and in the package README — silently did nothing for almost all of them. Registration now reads `data-tip` as well, and `relabel` writes the translation back to `data-tip` and `aria-label` rather than to a `title` that no longer exists and would have resurrected the native bubble beside our own. 84 tooltips registered, up from 11; verified end to end by switching to a German catalogue and back. |
| UX-P02 | A collapsed toolbar group is a menu, so it looks like one | Done | The flyout a collapsed group opens into was a mini-toolbar: a row of bare icons in a floating box. In the toolbar an icon is fine — it sits among known neighbours and hovering for a tooltip costs nothing, because the thing is already on screen. Once the group has collapsed, three unlabelled glyphs in a popup are a guessing game: you have to hover each one to learn what you just opened, which is why "I can't open Tools from the toolbar" was a fair description of something that opened perfectly well. Every other menu in the application is a labelled list, and a collapsed group *is* a menu. Now laid out as one: full-width rows, icons at a fixed offset so the labels align and the list scans. The label is drawn from the button's own tooltip via `content: attr(data-tip)`, so there is no second copy of the string to translate and none to fall out of step — the i18n pass rewrites `data-tip` and the label follows, which is only true because [UX-B05](#) made those translatable in the first place. Controls that are not icon buttons — the font box, the size field, a colour swatch — keep their own shape and take a row each. Verified on Tools (three buttons) and Font (two selects plus two buttons). |
| COL-07 | The snapshot store: cadence, retention, and verification | Done | ADR-011's snapshot model, and nearly free to build because ADR-010 already gives a deterministic byte-stable serialization — a `Snapshot` is those bytes plus a revision number, not a format anyone had to design. Four things fall out of it. **The log stops growing**, compacted behind each snapshot. **A late joiner costs the same as a fresh one**, getting a snapshot and a tail rather than a year of history. **Cold start is a read, not a replay**, which is what makes serverless compute viable at all: a Durable Object hibernates after ten seconds and discards its memory, so for an intermittently-edited document a cold start is not a rare event to amortise but the normal case. **And bounded offline gets a defined edge** — compaction deliberately keeps a margin of `every × retain_intervals` operations behind the snapshot, so a client away across a single boundary is still rebasable rather than reloaded, and one past it is refused *with the range it needed*. The cadence defaults to 200 revisions, an order of magnitude under ShareDB's thousand, because our operations are coarse (a `Batch` paste is one operation carrying a hundred thousand cells) and our snapshots are cheap; both numbers are starting points to benchmark against time-to-first-paint, and are documented as such rather than presented as derived. The property worth having on purpose: a stored snapshot can be **verified rather than trusted** — replaying the log from an earlier one must reproduce it byte for byte, and a mismatch means the document and its history have diverged, which is corruption however it arose and is surfaced rather than repaired. Seven tests including a simulated hibernate-and-wake. Mutation-tested before being believed: verification always succeeding, the cadence off by one, compaction dropping its margin, and the retained-window bound ignored are all caught. |
| COL-08 | Collaboration server boundary | Accepted | [ADR-012](08-ADR-REGISTER.md) / [docs/57](57-COLLABORATION-SERVER-BOUNDARY.md). A server that is **an editing session, not a filing system**: it coordinates co-editing and carries auth, admin, telemetry and configuration — the concerns that only exist once a deployment does, which is why they live here and not in the SDK — while the integrator keeps the document of record and receives finished bytes through a webhook callback. **The two prior arts named are not interchangeable, and only one fits.** Excalidraw's server relays *encrypted* messages and does no centralized coordination; a relay that sees ciphertext cannot transform anything, which is exactly why Excalidraw reconciles on the client. Taking that path would reopen ADR-011 rather than deploy it. OnlyOffice's shape is what already exists here: `ServerSession` + `Snapshot` *is* a document server, and its storage model — the host downloads the finished file through a callback — is the part the request was actually about. **The finding worth having is a fidelity trap only this path could walk into**: ADR-007 makes retained bytes authoritative for everything the model does not represent, and that table is built by importing the *file*. A session started from a model-only snapshot would converge perfectly and write back a document with every unrecognised chart, VBA part and `customXml` silently removed — the exact failure the whole fidelity effort exists to prevent, reachable only through collaboration because it is the only path that reconstructs a document from something other than the bytes it arrived as. So the session's durable state is original bytes + snapshot + log, and a session that cannot obtain the original bytes refuses to start. Webhooks now, WOPI kept unforeclosed behind an interface — it is roughly ten times the work for a payoff that is admission to the SharePoint ecosystem, which is a customer-driven reason and not an architectural one. The server is a **host, not a layer** (AGENTS.md: the engine computes, the host decides I/O): it lives under `server/`, nothing in `crates/` may depend on it, and it releases on its own `server-v*` tag. **Then the hard requirement arrived — horizontal scale, no sticky sessions — which looks like it contradicts ADR-011** and is the most useful thing in this row. OT needs one serialization point per document; the naive way to get it is an owner node, which is exactly the stickiness being rejected. The resolution is to notice that the point must be *one*, not one **node**: make it the **datastore**. Every node runs the same `commit`, and the append is *conditional* on the document still being at the revision the node read — the database decides the order, so any node may accept any connection for any document. A failed compare-and-swap is not a special case, it is `transform` against the handful of operations that won, which is what this layer does anyway, and it doubles as a cache-invalidation signal naming how far behind the node is. Nodes hold only caches — workbook, revision and the original bytes the retention path needs — rehydrated from snapshot-plus-tail, which is what COL-07 exists to make cheap. **The genuine price is fan-out**: without affinity a document's participants are spread across nodes, so a committing node must reach nodes it does not know about, which needs per-document pub/sub. Worth paying — fan-out is a well-understood horizontally scalable problem, whereas affinity is an operational tax on every deploy, restart and rebalance forever. Rejected alternatives: a **per-document lease** is affinity wearing a different hat, buying lease-expiry tuning and split-brain to avoid a conditional write; a **single-writer actor** (Durable Objects) is a good fit and the platform supplies the affinity rather than the operator, but binds to one vendor — and the compare-and-swap design does not foreclose it, since the object simply becomes a very effective cache in front of the same conditional append; a **partitioned log** is correct and is a broker to operate for one conditional write per commit. To be measured before it is believed: commit latency under contention and the retry rate as concurrent editors on one document rise. Five open questions; not Accepted, no code. **Refined to a cluster with a leader per document.** A client connects to any node — that is the requirement, met at the load balancer, which stays dumb — while inside the cluster one node **leads a given document** and the rest relay to it, so a commit is the in-process `ServerSession::commit` that already exists rather than a storage round trip per operation. That matters because operations are small and frequent: paying a database write on each one puts the datastore on the typing path. This is not sticky sessions reintroduced: stickiness is affinity a *client* is subject to, and leadership is internal, cluster-assigned, and moves on its own. **The synthesis worth keeping is that leadership is an optimisation for latency while the conditional append is what makes divergence impossible** — the leader is not trusted to be unique, so it still appends conditionally on the revision and a stale leader is told *no* by the store and steps down. Neither half suffices alone: election without the guard is correct only while the election is, and the guard without a leader puts a round trip on every keystroke. It also means election can be a cheap TTL lease rather than a consensus protocol, because getting it briefly wrong costs a rejected append, not a corrupted document. Failover needs nothing from the client — `ClientSession` already holds its chunk until acknowledged, which falls out of the one-chunk-in-flight rule rather than being designed for this. **Refined again to Kafka's per-partition topology**, which is a restatement rather than an analogy: a document *is* a partition and COL-07 already built its log and snapshots. The leader replicates each committed operation to **in-sync replicas**, so failover is a promotion in place rather than a cold rehydrate. Three rules, each one a place Kafka has been bitten. **Acknowledge after replication, never before** — acking then dying tells a client its work is saved when it is not. **Refuse writes when redundancy is gone**: the subtle one, because `acks=all` with the in-sync set collapsed to just the leader still acknowledges — "all" means "all of the ones still here", which is one — so the document sits a single crash from losing acknowledged edits while reporting perfect health; below the minimum it becomes read-only *with a stated reason*. **Never promote an out-of-sync replica**: Kafka makes this configurable and defaults it off because enabling it loses committed messages permanently; we do not make it configurable, and recover from the snapshot instead — slower, and it loses nothing that was ever acknowledged. Zombie leaders are fenced by a **leader epoch** carried on every append and refused by replicas that have seen a newer one, which is what makes a sloppy TTL lease acceptable: the lease says who *probably* leads, the epoch says who *does*. **Placement is separated from election** — capacity, latency and locality propose a candidate and are free to be wrong; the epoch settles the outcome, because merging them lets a load metric decide correctness. Replication factor is a dial, not a constant: each copy costs the workbook *and* the original bytes retention needs. **Deployment is tiered and standalone needs nothing at all** — one process, leader by definition, no election, no fan-out, snapshots to disk; a cluster adds a lease and per-document pub/sub, with **Redis for liveness and the epoch for correctness**, since a Redis lease can be violated and that is survivable only because the fence is elsewhere. One binary, clustering as configuration, the coordination surface a small interface with an in-process implementation — so the standalone path is the same code rather than a special case that rots. **Accepted, with the five open questions answered.** *Clients get the model, the server keeps the bytes*: a participant never fetches the document itself — it would arrive at revision zero while the session is at five hundred — so the server hands out the model snapshot and its revision on join, and the original bytes stay server-side where retention needs them. That has an SDK-visible consequence recorded rather than discovered later: **a client in a session cannot save**, because it does not hold the retained parts and anything it wrote would be the lossy copy this whole design exists to prevent. *Save cadence* is four triggers — quiesce at 5s, the revision interval reusing `SnapshotPolicy::every` so there is one dial rather than two that drift, immediately when the last participant leaves, and a **60s ceiling**, which is the one easy to omit: sustained editing never quiesces, so without it the loss window grows without bound exactly when the most work is at risk. *The token is the whole integration contract* — a host-signed JWT carrying the user, the document, the permission, the presence identity, **and where to fetch and send back** — so the server holds a signing secret per tenant and no per-document state whatsoever: nothing to provision, nothing to migrate, multi-tenant by construction, with the capability a URL-in-a-token represents bounded by `exp`. *Presence identity comes from the token, never the client*, since presence is exactly the surface where a claimed identity would be believed; an absent colour is derived deterministically from the subject so a person looks the same in every session. *A failing callback stops the session rather than hiding*: participants are told on the **first** failure and not the last — a warning is only useful while there is still time to copy the work out — and when retries are exhausted the document goes read-only with the reason shown, because continuing to accept edits that provably cannot be persisted is silent loss dressed as availability. What is left is measurement, not design. |
| COL-09 | Submissions need an idempotency key | Done | Exposed by COL-08's failover analysis, and required before the server runs rather than optional hardening. A leader can commit a chunk and die **before acknowledging it**; the client, having had no acknowledgement, resends, and the new leader cannot tell a lost chunk from a duplicate — so it applies it twice. Typing a value twice is invisible; inserting a row twice is not. `Submission` needs a client id and a per-client sequence number, with the server recording the last sequence accepted from each client and treating anything at or below it as already committed. Small, on a type that is already written. **Built.** `Submission` carries a `ClientId` — assigned by the host, since the engine never invents identity for the same reason it never reads a clock — and a per-client sequence, with `resend()` reusing the sequence while following the client's *current* base, because remote operations arriving meanwhile have already rebased the chunk. `commit` returns `Applied` or `Duplicate`, and two details in it are load-bearing rather than incidental. **`Duplicate` reports where the chunk landed originally, not the server's current revision** — acknowledging at a later one would have the client skip everything committed in between and never receive it. And **the duplicate check runs before the base is validated**, because a resend arriving late can name a revision compaction has since dropped, and refusing it would tell a client its committed work was lost. The acceptance record is durable session state, not a cache: a promotion that fails to carry it double-applies the first resend it sees, which one test demonstrates directly. Six tests, including a repeated row insert — the case that makes this required rather than tidy, since a repeated value is invisible but a repeated insert moves every row below it. Five mutations all caught: the check removed, the comparison off by one, the record never written, `Duplicate` reporting the current revision, and the key ignoring the client. |
| COL-10 | The server crate, its boundary, and the session lifecycle | Done | The first increment of ADR-012, and deliberately the part with no network in it. **The boundary comes first because it is the thing that erodes**: `server/casual-calc-collab-server` may depend on the engine, nothing under `crates/` may depend on it, and CI now *checks* that rather than the docs merely asserting it — a documented boundary nothing enforces is a boundary that lasts until the first convenient exception. It is what keeps an async runtime and an HTTP stack out of a library that has neither. **The lifecycle is a state machine over supplied milliseconds**, with no clock, no socket and no `await`, for the same reason the engine works that way: a save cadence and a retry policy are precisely the things whose bugs live in rare timing, and they are only testable when time is an argument. Implements ADR-012's four save triggers — quiesce, revision count, last-participant-leave, and the **ceiling** that is easy to omit and matters most under load, because sustained editing never quiesces and without it the loss window grows without bound exactly when the most work is at risk. The callback policy warns on the **first** failure rather than the last (a warning is only useful while there is still time to copy the work out), backs off exponentially with saturation so a long outage cannot fold the delay back to nothing, and on exhausting its attempts makes the document read-only rather than continuing to accept edits it provably cannot persist. One action per `tick`, not a queue: an action changes the state it was derived from, and handing out several at once invites acting on a stale one. Twelve tests including continuous editing that never quiesces, work done *while* a save was in flight, and a stale acknowledgement that must not move the saved mark backwards. Six mutations all caught. |
| COL-11 | Correction: retention survives a snapshot; what it costs is size | Done | COL-08 asserted that a session restoring from a model snapshot would silently drop every unrecognised chart, VBA part and `customXml`, called it the finding worth having, and built a rule on it — *a session must start from the original bytes or refuse to start*. **It is wrong.** `Workbook::retained_parts` is serialized like every other field, so a snapshot carries the retention table with the bytes intact and restoring from one is faithful. The claim came from reading the import path instead of testing the snapshot, which is the whole difference. Two tests now pin it, so it cannot drift back into doubt. What *is* real is a cost with a number: **a retained part occupies about four times its size in a snapshot**, because `serde_json` writes `Vec<u8>` as an array of decimal numbers — measured at 4,181 bytes for 1 KiB, not estimated. Unremarkable for a golden fixture, expensive for something written every two hundred revisions and read on every cold start: 20 MB of embedded media makes each snapshot around 80 MB, and on hibernating compute cold start is the *normal* case rather than a rare one. The consequence is small because retained parts are **inert** — the model's own documentation says a retained part is never parsed and never edited — so they cannot change during a session and do not belong in something written repeatedly: stored once with the document, referenced by the periodic snapshot, rejoined on restore. The original file is still worth keeping, but for that reason rather than for fidelity. |
| COL-12 | Operations carry replica-local handles, so formulas do not travel | Done | Found building the document session, and it undoes an assumption ADR-011 rested on. `Workbook::store_formula` allocates `FormulaHandle(self.formulas.len())` — a **positional index into the sending workbook's arena**, not content-addressed — so an `Operation::SetCell` carrying a formula means something different on every replica. `StyleId` is interned into a per-workbook table and has the same shape of problem. ADR-011 called the op set "closed, serializable, invertible" and therefore ready to transmit; it is serializable, but a handle inside it is not *portable*, which is a different property and the one that matters on a wire. Demonstrated rather than argued: a chunk carrying a handle valid on its sender is committed by the server, and the cell **vanishes from the written file entirely** — the writer finds a handle indexing nothing and drops the whole cell, not merely its formula. Silent, and worse than losing the formula alone. The fix is a wire-format concern rather than a model one: an operation must carry the expression (and the style) **by value**, with the receiving replica interning locally, so the model keeps its handles and the protocol stops depending on them. **Fixed by making the operation carry its meanings rather than by mirroring the op set.** The obvious repair is a parallel portable tree with expressions and styles by value; it is also a second definition of every operation kept in step by hand, for a problem living in exactly one place — `Cell` is the only type in the model holding a handle, since even `DefinedName` carries its expression by value. So `WireOperation` is an operation *plus* the formulas and styles its handles refer to; the receiver interns those into its own tables and rewrites the handles. One type, one transform matrix, one `apply`. Interning is **by value**, so two replicas that reach the same formula converge on whatever their tables already hold rather than growing a duplicate per crossing. A handle the *sender* could not resolve is dropped rather than carried, because carrying it would index the receiver's arena and silently name a different formula — a wrong answer where the sender had none. The walk covers `SetStyle`'s **bare** style id as well as the ones inside cells: missing that is how a style crosses meaning something else, and it did until the walk was restructured to cover both slots. `Submission` and `Commit` now carry wire operations, and `receive` localises before comparing anything, so the protocol has no path that transmits a bare handle. Nine tests, including a formula and a style crossing between two clients whose arenas are deliberately out of step, and an unpackaged handle proving `carries_handles`/`dangling_handle` let a server refuse rather than commit-and-lose. |
| COL-13 | The document session | Done | Composes the three pieces that already existed — `ServerSession` for order, a `Workbook` for the document, `SessionLifecycle` for the cadence — and adds the rules that only make sense together. A joining participant is given **the model snapshot and its revision, never the file**: everyone must start from the same revision, and a client fetching the document itself would arrive at zero while the session was at five hundred. Editing **stops** once the callback has failed its way to read-only, which is ADR-012's rule enforced rather than described. **Recalculation is deliberately off the commit path** — commits happen at typing speed and saves do not — so values are brought up to date at the two moments they are read, assembling and snapshotting. Resuming from a snapshot is faithful including retained parts, which COL-11 established by test after the design had claimed otherwise. Seven tests, several round-tripping through a real `.xlsx` rather than trusting the in-memory model. **Mutation testing caught a hollow test of my own**: "values are current in the assembled file" used two literals, which need no recalculation at all, so it passed with recalculation removed entirely; rewritten around a formula, it fails as it should. |
| COL-14 | The wire format: serialization and the protocol messages | Done | Until now none of the protocol types could actually be sent — `Operation`, `Submission` and `Commit` had no serialization at all, so "the wire carries ops, not values" was a design statement with nothing behind it. They serialize now, and the messages the two ends exchange are defined. **In the engine rather than the server crate**, because both ends speak it: the browser client is compiled to WebAssembly from these same crates, and a protocol defined only on the server is one the client reimplements and keeps in step by hand. **No transport here** — a WebSocket, a long poll and a test harness passing values between two structs should all be moving the same things, which is what makes the protocol testable before any of them exist. `PROTOCOL_VERSION` is checked on the first message so mismatched peers stop at once rather than proceeding until a missing field produces something more confusing; it is separate from the model's schema version, since a document format can change without the conversation changing and the reverse. Two details are deliberate. A `NotAuthorised` refusal **says only that** — which of expired, malformed, wrong-document or missing it was is useful to an operator in a log and useful to an attacker in a response. And `ReadOnlyAccess` is distinct from `NotSaving`: one is about who you are and the other about what is wrong, and a client should say something different for each. Six tests, including a formula surviving JSON *and still localising* on the far side, so the two halves of COL-12 are checked together rather than separately. |
| COL-15 | Presence | Done | Deliberately the shallowest thing in the server, and that is the design rather than a shortcut: presence is **not part of the document** — never transformed, never persisted, never in a snapshot — and losing all of it costs nothing, which is precisely why it may take the cheap path the document may not. Yjs's awareness shape: a map keyed by participant, each owning exactly one entry it overwrites wholesale, dropped after silence. No merge, therefore no transform. TTL is Yjs's **30 seconds** — about three missed heartbeats at ten — adopted rather than invented, because the number has to tolerate a slow network without leaving ghosts on screen for a minute and this one is known to. **Identity comes from the token and cannot come from anywhere else**: name and colour are fixed at join, a movement update carries only *where*, and `ClientMessage::Presence` has no field a client could put a name in — presence being the one surface where a claimed identity would be believed without question. An update for somebody who never joined is ignored rather than inventing them. An absent colour is derived from the id alone, so a person is the same colour in every session and among any set of others: a colour that changes per join identifies a cursor, not a person. Expiry **returns who went** rather than merely removing them, because the others have to be told — a cursor that stops moving and never disappears is worse than one that never appeared, since it reads as somebody watching. Eight tests; five mutations caught, and a sixth **caught the test rather than the code**: the stability check originally compared two rosters of identical shape, so a colour depending on join order would have passed it. Rewritten to differ in every way a colour might accidentally depend on — when they joined, who else was there, in what order — it now fails as it should. |
| PERF-01 | The 1M-cell memory gate that two documents said existed | Done | [23](23-CELL-STORE-REPRESENTATION.md) and [30](30-PERFORMANCE-AND-CAPACITY-TARGETS.md) both stated that the per-cell byte ceiling "is asserted by the memory benchmark". **It was asserted nowhere.** The benchmark harness measures no memory at all and the CI job validates its report's *shape* with `jq`, so the project's headline capacity claim — 1,000,000+ cells within a bounded budget, on the README and the marketing site — rested on nothing that could fail. The gate now exists and bites: adding 32 bytes to `Cell` fails it with a message naming the cost. **Measuring it turned up a discrepancy between the documented design and the code.** A cell is **80 bytes**, and three quarters of that is two id types: `CellValue` is 32 and `Option<StyleId>` another 32, because `StringId` and `StyleId` both wrap a **128-bit `Id`** where doc 23 describes an interned *index* and says in as many words "a cell holds a 32-bit id, not text". `FormulaHandle` is a real 32-bit index and its option costs 8 — which is what the other two were meant to be. Narrowing them would cut a cell by roughly 40%, or 40 MB at the target, and is not an edit: it changes the ids' snapshot encoding, so it wants an ADR. The ceiling therefore records what a cell costs **today** rather than the design's intent, because a gate that already fails teaches nothing. Also stated rather than glossed: this measures the per-cell *payload*, not the allocator's resident footprint — that needs a counting global allocator and therefore `unsafe`, which the workspace forbids, and the rule is worth more than the more precise number. |
| PERF-02 | Interned ids narrowed: a cell goes from 80 bytes to 32 | Done | [ADR-013](58-INTERNED-ID-WIDTH.md), which PERF-01's gate made possible by measuring what a cell actually costs. The finding was not that these ids *should* be indices but that they **already were**: `id_for` packed a `u32` into a `u128` with a constant namespace tag, so ninety-six of the hundred and twenty-eight bits were a constant. The tag re-checked at runtime what distinct newtypes already prevent at compile time — and, as COL-12 established, could not tell *this* workbook's style 7 from *another* workbook's, which is the confusion that actually happens. Twelve bytes per cell for a check the compiler makes for free. `NonZeroU32` rather than `u32` for the **niche**: `Option<StyleId>` costs four bytes rather than eight, and the tables already numbered from one so nothing changed about how they count. **Measured: 80 → 32 bytes per cell, better than the 32–40 estimated**, because narrowing `StringId` shrank `CellValue` from 32 to 16 as well; 88 → 40 per stored cell, and **40 MB rather than 88 MB** of payload at the 1M-cell target. The cost is real and paid deliberately: an id serializes `transparent`, so the snapshot goes from a hex string to a number, which is not additive — `SCHEMA_VERSION` 0 → 1, and the byte-stable golden caught it, which is what that test is for. Verified in-browser after the change: shared and unique interned strings resolve, a formula computes, and a save produces a valid package. |
| RND-02 | Frozen panes in the headless PNG backend | Done | A gap the support matrix (since deleted) named in two places and [42](42-GRID-LAYOUT-AND-RENDERING-ARCHITECTURE.md) had already designed: the editor canvas split frozen panes from the start, the PNG backend drew one unbroken window, so an exported image lost the pinned header the screen was still showing — visible in a side-by-side render of the same scroll position, where the frozen version keeps its `Q5…Q12` headings and its row labels and the other is bare numbers. The split lives in **layout**, not in either host, precisely because both needed it and only one had it: `panes(geometry, viewport, Freeze)` returns up to four `Pane`s, each a plain `Viewport` plus where it sits in the image, so `layout_viewport` and the display list work on a pane unchanged and only the composition is new. `render_panes` renders each and copies it into place. Three decisions worth recording. The scrolling panes **clamp to the first unfrozen line**, because scrolling back past the freeze would show the pinned lines twice, beside themselves. A freeze wider than the image **keeps the frozen band and drops the body** rather than producing a negative size — those are the lines the author asked to always see. And composition is a **row copy rather than `draw_pixmap`**: the panes are opaque and land on whole pixels, so there is nothing to blend, and a copy is exact where a shader is only very close, which matters when the goldens compare bytes. The compatibility property is asserted on bytes rather than trusted — an unfrozen sheet composes to *exactly* what the unsplit path produced — because every existing caller was moved onto the split path, including the WASM `render_xlsx`, which had the same gap and now shares one `render_sheet_png`. **Mutation testing found two gaps in my own tests**: swapping the two freeze fields at the SDK still produced a frozen-looking render, so the test now pins the axes against a hand-built composition; and the clip in `blit` looked defensive but is load-bearing, since each pane's pixel size rounds up independently and a boundary at 64.6 px starts the body at pixel 65 with 101 pixels to put in a 165-pixel image — a case no test reached until one was built for it. 16 tests. |
| CI-002 | `browser-smoke`: the gate that runs the editor | Done | The last job in [15](15-CI-AND-RELEASE-GATES.md)'s contract table that did not exist, and the only one that runs the editor as a person does. Playwright against `webapp/editor.html`, on an engine **built by the job** — `webapp/pkg/` is not committed, and a gate that would accept a stale build cannot catch one. Asserts through real user surfaces only: the name box, the formula bar, and the accessibility mirror of the visible cells; no test-only hooks, so nothing has to be kept in step and a failure in the mirror is one a screen-reader user would hit. No retries, because a retry turns an intermittent failure into a pass. **Its first run failed, and found four defects that had shipped**, every one of them invisible to 600-odd Rust tests: see UX-B04, UX-B05, UX-B06 and FID-05. That is the argument for the gate, made by the gate. |
| UX-B14 | Undo was one press behind, for every edit | Done | Type a value, press Ctrl+Z, watch nothing happen. Found by CI-002 in the browser; root-caused to `History::apply` recording an inverse **unconditionally**. The editor calls `session_table_autoexpand` after every cell commit — a whole-sheet metadata bundle that, with no table near the cell, differs in nothing — and each one landed on the undo stack, so the first press popped a phantom and the second did the work. A user who presses undo and sees no change does not press it again; they conclude undo is broken, which it was. The fix is one guard and the machinery for it already existed: COL-02's change mask means `apply` hands back an inverse narrowed to the fields that actually differed, so an empty mask **is** a proof of no-op — no workbook comparison needed. Deliberately conservative: setting a cell to the value it already holds is also a no-op and this does not claim so, because proving it needs state that is gone by then, and silently dropping a real edit is far worse than keeping a redundant one. Two bugs for one fix — a no-op also **cleared the redo stack**, which is how the history says "a new edit happened"; nothing happened, so a redo the user was one keystroke from was being thrown away. The principle was already written down in `WorkbookSession::edit`, for the refused-edit case: "must leave no trace, or undo has an entry that undoes nothing." It just was not kept for the case that changed nothing. 6 tests. |
| UX-B15 | Ctrl+Z walked out of the document it opened | Done | Undo was enabled on a freshly opened document, and holding Ctrl+Z took the seeded sheet apart cell by cell into an empty grid. Found by CI-002 while investigating UX-B04 — the two look identical from the outside and are not related. The editor seeds through the same edit path a user types on, which is right (those writes must recalculate and stay consistent) and which also means they are undoable. Opening a file is not an edit, so `History::clear`, `WorkbookSession::clear_history` and `session_clear_history` give a host the one thing it was missing: a way to say *this is where the document starts*. Not editor-only — any embedder restoring a document from its own store had the same defect, and `open` never did, because it builds the workbook by import rather than by editing. 2 tests. |
| UX-B06 | The undo tooltip never said what it would undo | Done | `refreshFormulaBar` has always asked the engine what undo would reverse and written "Undo cell edit (Ctrl+Z)" to `title`. Nobody ever saw it. The tooltip layer runs at boot, moves `title` onto `data-tip` and **deletes the attribute**, so every later assignment to `title` did two wrong things at once: the custom tooltip went on showing the text captured at boot, and the native browser bubble — the thing `tipify` exists to suppress — came back underneath it. The same class of bug as the i18n one in UX-P01, and for the same reason: two mechanisms for one label, with one of them silently authoritative. Fixed with a `setTip` helper that writes through whichever surface the node is using. Undo and Redo are the only tooltips in the editor that change after boot, which is exactly why they were the only ones that could show this. |
| FID-05 | A typed formula came back rewritten | Done | Type `=1+2*3`, select the cell again, and the formula bar shows `=(1+(2*3))`. Found by CI-002. A cell does not store the text that was typed — it stores the parsed tree, and the printer emits it back — so the printer **is** the formula as far as anyone editing it is concerned, and it bracketed every operator. Not a display quirk: the same text goes into the saved `.xlsx`, so a workbook written here and opened in Excel shows the rewritten formula permanently. A spreadsheet that quietly edits your formulas is not a viable alternative to one that does not. This was a **documented deferral** rather than an oversight — `print.rs` said "canonical, not minimal; a minimal printer can replace it later" — and nothing had made the cost visible until a test typed a formula and read it back. The replacement mirrors the parser's own binding powers, so "minimal" is defined against the actual grammar: a left child is bracketed when it binds no tighter on its right than its parent does on its left (which keeps `(2^3)^2`, since `^` is right-associative), a right child when it binds looser on its left than its parent does on its right (which keeps `1-(2-3)` and lets `1+2*3` go bare). The existing `parse(print(e)) == e` gate is what makes those rules correct rather than plausible; its corpus grew from 17 inputs to 34, and a new gate asserts the stronger property that now holds — `print(parse(s)) == s` for text already minimally bracketed, which is what a user experiences. Eight expectations elsewhere in the workspace changed from `"(A1*2)"` to `"A1*2"`, each one a place the old output was reaching a file or a formula bar. |
| CI-003 | Reconcile this tracker against the code | Done | Asked for the product's current state, this file could not give it: **seven rows said "Not started" for work that had shipped** — structural ops with reference rewriting (E-002), glyph text (P1D-002), part discovery through content-types and rels (P1A-005), retention and opaque parts (P1A-006) — and three more were part-done in ways nobody could see from a status column. UX-G01 appeared twice, once Open and once Done, and the support matrix (since deleted) still said the 1M-cell ceiling was unasserted three days after PERF-01 asserted it. Every row was re-checked against the code and now carries the evidence that settles it, so the next person can disagree with a claim rather than merely doubt it. Three rows moved to a status the tracker had no word for, because "not started" was hiding a **decision** rather than a gap: `casual-calc-selection` is a four-line skeleton and should stay one (a selection belongs to a view, not a document, so the editor owns it and the engine is handed ranges); pixel→cell hit-testing is the host's, since it needs the device pixel ratio, scroll position and header sizes, none of which the document knows; and incremental repaint likewise. If those are still true at 1.0, the selection crate should be deleted rather than filled in. **The reconciliation also found a live defect** — the support matrix (since deleted) claimed Render ● for merged ranges against a rule in its own header ("if a row says ● and you cannot point at the test that would fail when it regresses, the row is wrong"), and there is no such test because there is no such code: see RND-03. A tracker that reports shipped work as unstarted does not merely go stale, it stops being usable for the one question it exists to answer. |
| RND-03 | Merged cells in the headless PNG backend | Done | Found by CI-003 checking the support matrix (since deleted)'s Render ● against the rule in its own header. `casual-calc-layout` and `casual-calc-render` contained **no reference to a merge at all**, so the headless renderer drew a merged range as separate cells, each ruled apart and the anchor's text in the wrong box. It survived because every surface a person looks at was right — the editor canvas handles merges and they round-trip through the model — and nothing looked at the PNG. Layout now resolves the merges **intersecting** the window before walking the cells: intersecting rather than contained, because a wide block's anchor is off screen precisely *because* it is wide, and dropping it would make a merged band vanish exactly when it is widest. Cells the range covers are skipped but keep their values, which is the distinction that matters — Excel stores them and so does the writer, so a merge hides cells rather than emptying them. The display list gained a `MergedRegion` item **carrying the anchor's fill**, which is not decoration: a backend paints ground-then-outline across the range to erase the interior grid, and a separate background item following it painted the outline away — two adjacent merged headers came out looking like one band twice as wide, and that is now gated. Six mutations tried, six caught; the two that survived a first pass were about *intersecting* vs *contained* and about sorting the merges, both of which the tests had assumed rather than asserted. Verified by eye on a title band and two group headers. 13 tests. |
| CI-004 | The core editing paths, in a browser | Done | A second suite beside the smoke gate (CI-002), asking not "does the editor work at all" but "do the things people do with a spreadsheet all day work": copy and paste with reference adjustment, cut, fill down and right, insert and delete lines, bold, a second sheet and a cross-sheet reference, keyboard navigation, and saving a real `.xlsx` (asserted down to the `PK` of the package, because a download that arrives and is not openable is the failure that looks most like success). Fourteen tests, ten green on the first run; two of the four failures were my own helper over-asserting — after a *range* is typed into the name box it shows the active cell, not the range, which is what a spreadsheet does — and one was a genuine defect, UX-NV5. Same discipline as CI-002: real user surfaces only, no hooks. |
| UX-NV5 | Extending a selection moved the active cell with it | Done | Select H2, press Shift+Down twice, type: the value landed in **H4**. Excel and Sheets both keep the active cell where the selection began and travel the far corner, and typing replaces the active cell — so the value belonged in H2, the cell the grid was still highlighting. Anyone selecting a range with the keyboard and typing put their data in the wrong cell. Found by CI-004 while writing a fill test, from a helper that noticed the name box reading `F4` after `F2:F4` was typed into it. The fix is one function: `extend` moves `state.anchor` rather than `state.sel`, which reads backwards only until you remember [UX-NV4](73-EXCEL-UX-PARITY-AUDIT.md)'s finding that **`state.sel` is the active cell everywhere in the file** — so the corner that travels has to be the other one. `selRect` takes the min and max of the pair, so the block is identical either way; only where the active cell ends up changes, which is the whole bug. Three callers then had to follow, because they had been measuring the next step from the active cell: Shift+Arrow, Ctrl+Shift+Arrow (or a second jump-extend would re-measure from the stationary origin and land in the same place twice) and Shift+Home/End. Verified by eye as well as by test — the white active cell now sits at the top of a downward selection, and the status bar's Sum/Count agree with the block. |
| FID-06 | `_xlfn.`: the prefix that decides whether Excel can read a formula | Done | Found by P2-003's oracle on its first run. The file format's function set was fixed in 2007 and everything since is written prefixed, so that a reader which does not know the function still knows it is *a function it does not know* and says `#NAME?` rather than mistaking it for a defined name. This writer emitted the bare name, which is wrong in the worst available way: the file opens, looks complete, and shows `#NAME?` in every cell that used one of the forty-three affected functions — irrecoverably, since nothing left says what the formula was. The reader had the matching hole and would have failed on any file Excel wrote. Fixed as a pair at the format boundary: the writer qualifies on the way out, the reader strips on the way in, and the table of names lives in one place because a list kept twice is a list that drifts. Both defined names and cell formulas go through it — the second is the one easy to forget. Structural rather than textual: `_xlfn.CONCAT` lexes as a single word, so the prefix is added and removed on the `Expr`, and no string surgery can catch a quoted literal by accident. 14 tests. |
| FID-07 | Rewrites walked past a first-class call | Done | Noticed while adding the `_xlfn.` walker (FID-06) and confirmed by removing the fix and watching the new tests fail. `LAMBDA(x,…)(A1)` parses as `Call { callee, args }`, and **both** `shift_references` and `rename_sheet_references` fell through to their catch-all arm on it. So filling such a formula down a column gave every row the first row's references — quietly, with plausible numbers — and renaming a sheet left a stale name inside the call, which becomes `#REF!` on the next recalculation somewhere the user never edited. Narrow, because first-class function values are new here (LET/LAMBDA), and exactly the kind of arm a walker acquires late and forgets. Both now recurse through the callee as well as the arguments, since the callee is an expression too. |
| FID-08 | `_xlpm.`: the second prefix, without which a LAMBDA is still unreadable | Done | The other half of [FID-06](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md), which that row named as still open. The names `LAMBDA` and `LET` **bind** carry their own `_xlpm.` prefix in the file, for the same reason the function names do: without it a reader cannot tell a bound parameter from a defined name, and looks `x` up in the workbook's names and finds nothing. A `LAMBDA` with its function name qualified and its parameters bare is still a formula Excel will not evaluate. The pass has to be **scope-aware**, which the function-name pass did not: a bare name is a *defined name* unless something binds it, and prefixing one of those points the formula at a name no workbook contains. So the walk carries a scope stack and prefixes only a reference to a binding it can see — `LAMBDA(x,x*TaxRate)` qualifies `x` and leaves `TaxRate`. Two details follow the language rather than convenience: `LET` binds **sequentially**, so each value is qualified against the bindings *before* it and not against its own name (`LET(x,x,x)` has a bound `x` and a free one), and an inner binding shadows an outer one. **A test caught a real bug in the first version**: Excel writes these prefixes in lower case, and stripping was case-sensitive, so `_xlpm.x` came back untouched. Function names had survived the same compare only by accident — the parser upper-cases them, and a bound name is not a function name. Verified by the oracle rather than by assertion alone: `LET(x,5,x*2)`, `LET(x,5,y,x+1,x*y)`, `REDUCE` and `SUM(MAP(...))` all now agree with LibreOffice, which they could not have before. 11 tests. **The `@differs` mechanism proved itself here too**: I marked `SUM(MAP(…))` as a known LibreOffice gap, the run reported the marker as stale because the two agreed, and the excuse came out rather than sitting there being wrong. |
| FID-09 | Strict OOXML is read as an empty workbook, silently | Done | **Checked, and the defect does not exist.** Recorded on the strength of a grep — 22 matches for the transitional namespace, none for the strict one — and the grep was right about the counts and wrong about the conclusion. The readers match element **local names** rather than namespace URIs, in forty-nine places, so a Strict Open XML workbook parses without anything special. Verified by building strict versions of the corpus and importing them: identical modelled content, file for file. Now gated, because the thing that was true by design was true unchecked for however long — a reader tightened later to match on a namespace would be a reasonable-looking change that silently stops opening files Excel produces. Confirmed the gate bites: making one reader namespace-sensitive fails it. |
| FID-10 | Deliberately malformed packages are barely covered | Done | Seven broken packages, generated by `fixtures/tools/malformed.py` rather than committed as opaque bytes, each violating exactly one thing so a failure names the violation: a relationship pointing at a part that is not there, a cell referencing a shared string past the end, `dimension` claiming `A1:XFD1048576`, a wrong content type, a part truncated mid-tag, a part name climbing out of the package, and no `[Content_Types].xml` at all. **All seven terminate with an answer** — three refused (traversal, truncation, missing part), four opened tolerantly, none panicked or hung. The test asserts the contract and not the verdict: which files are refused is a judgement that should be free to change, and pinning today's choice would make improving tolerance look like a regression. What is pinned is that traversal and truncation are refused, because those are not things to be lenient about — confirmed by removing the traversal guard and watching refusals fall from three to two. **One fixture was wrong and the check caught it:** the shared-string case injected a nested `<c>` inside another, which is invalid in a second way, and the file opened with zero cells for a reason that had nothing to do with the index. Fixed to a sibling cell; it now opens with the good cell kept and the bad reference dropped, which is the behaviour worth having. |
| FID-11 | Every corpus file comes from one upstream project | Done | A **LibreOffice-written** file joins the corpus, generated by `fixtures/tools/libreoffice.sh` rather than sourced, so there is no licence question and the producer is recorded exactly. It carries formulas *and the values LibreOffice computed for them* — which is the independent oracle the corpus exists for: every other fidelity assertion here compares this engine against itself, our importer against our exporter, our evaluator against our own expectations. Here a different implementation wrote both the question and its answer. The test recalculates from the formulas alone and compares; confirmed it bites by making multiplication off by one, which fails with `we say 14.5, LibreOffice said 13.5`. **Still open:** two producers is not the long tail — Google Sheets, `xlsxwriter`, `openpyxl` and SheetJS each have their own quirks, and the generator pattern extends to any of them that can be run headless. **Closed with two more producers, chosen for what they get wrong rather than for the count.** `fixtures/tools/producers.py` generates the same spreadsheet through **openpyxl**, which writes `<f>` with no `<v>` at all, and **XlsxWriter**, which writes `<v>0</v>` — a cached value that is present, well-formed and wrong. Every file already in the corpus, Excel's and LibreOffice's alike, carries a *correct* cached value, so nothing here had ever exercised the case the rest of the ecosystem actually emits. Because all three files are the same spreadsheet, LibreOffice's computed values are the oracle for all of them. Confirmed it bites: keeping a formula only when the file also cached a value for it — the shortcut that turns every openpyxl document into blanks — fails this test and **passes all six of the others**, which is the gap stated as a number. The generator also asserts each quirk is still in the bytes it just wrote, so a library changing its default leaves a failing generator rather than a fixture that opens, passes, and tests nothing. Google Sheets and SheetJS remain unsourced: the first needs an account, the second is no longer distributed through the registry this repository could pin. |
| FID-12 | Odd-but-valid documents are asserted only against fixtures we wrote | Done | 1904 dates, inline strings versus the shared table, shared and array formulas, and a `dimension` of `A1:XFD1048576` are all handled, and all of them are checked against documents this project produced — which is the circularity the corpus exists to break. Assert them against real files instead. **Done, and it found a real one.** Surveying the corpus first — the FID-09 lesson — showed shared formulas and inline strings were already present in Excel-written files and merely unasserted, while 1904 and array formulas were absent. *Shared formulas*: `45540_classic_Header.xlsx` expands one `C10/543` across `ref="B10:B31"` and caches Excel's own answer in all 22 cells, so the file states the question and the answer; 60+ cells compared, and shifting a follower by one row fails it. *1904*: an XlsxWriter file with `date1904="1"`; 2020-02-29 is serial 42428 there and 43890 under 1900, and ignoring the flag renders 2016-02-28 — asserted on the **displayed date**, not the serial. *Array formulas*: an XlsxWriter `t="array"` file where `{=SUM(A1:A3*B1:B3)}` must give 140, and scalar coercion gives 10. **The defect**: `56278.xlsx` — real, Excel-written — uses `<c t="inlineStr">` with no `r`, inside `<row>` with no `r`, in a sheet with no `<dimension>`. All three are legal (`r` is optional in ECMA-376; position is implied by ordinal) and we required `r`, so the file imported as **ten sheets and zero cells**. It opened, so the open-rate guard passed, and the corpus-wide cell count passed on other files' cells: an entire real document silently dropped. Fixed by tracking position, and **0 → 588 cells**. One combination no real producer emits — a row mixing explicit and implicit positions — is covered by unit tests, because the first mutation there passed every corpus file. An oversized `dimension` stays asserted against our own fixture: no third-party producer here writes one, and `56278.xlsx` writing *none* covers the same premise, that `dimension` is a hint rather than a contract. |
| PERF-04 | Incremental recalculation is linear in sheet size, not in dirty set | Done | **The measurement for [P2-002](#)'s remaining half, kept as its own row because it is a number and that row is a plan.** Editing one cell in a sheet of independent formulas scaled **9.2x for 10x the sheet** (898 µs → 8.3 ms): the dirty set is one cell either way, so all of that was the precedent graph being rebuilt per pass. Extrapolated, a million cells was ~830 ms against the 50 ms target of [30](30-PERFORMANCE-AND-CAPACITY-TARGETS.md) — about 16x over. **Closed by P2-002 step 3: a kept graph makes the same edit 375 ns at ten thousand cells and 375 ns at a hundred thousand — 1.00x, flat, measured a decade up rather than extrapolated again.** The rebuild path is unchanged at ~9x and is still what a caller without a `Recalculator` gets. One caveat recorded because it nearly went the other way: the first kept-graph result read 19x and looked like a failure, and was the benchmark timing its own `cells.iter().count()` return value — about 3% of a 2.4 ms rebuild and ~100% of a 2 µs kept-graph edit. An optimisation that succeeds enough makes the harness the thing being measured. |
| RND-04 | Which scripts the bundled build covers is now a host's decision | Done | Fonts are ingested at runtime (`register_font`), so Arabic, Devanagari, Thai and CJK are supplied rather than embedded — the bundle carries Latin so something works unconfigured. What is missing is the other half of making that usable: the demo host does not offer a font, nothing documents which faces a deployment needs for which scripts, and a document in a script with no face registered renders as boxes with nothing saying why. A one-line note in the deployment guide and a registered face in the demo would close it. **Closed on all three halves.** *Which faces*: [65](65-RUNNING-IT.md) now has a script-to-face table and says plainly that Latin is bundled and everything else is a file drop; `.env.example` and both compose stacks carry `OPENCALC_FONT_DIR`. *A face in the demo*: the host serves a font directory, lists it at `/api/fonts` as **URLs rather than names** so a deployment can serve faces from anywhere, and `/admin` shows the directory and what is in it. No face is committed — a CJK font alone is tens of megabytes and which one is a regional decision, so `fonts/` holds a README and is gitignored. *Nothing saying why*: `missing_scripts` names the scripts a document contains that no available face can draw, with a sample character, exposed as `WorkbookSession::missing_font_coverage()` and `missing_font_scripts(text)` in the browser — enough to write "this sheet contains Thai and no face covering it is installed" instead of showing boxes. **Two things only running found.** The first version probed `/api/fonts` on every boot; `fetch` swallows a 404 but the browser logs it, so every deployment without a font service would have had a console error on every session — it is opt-in via `?fonts=` now, and the browser suite asserts a clean boot without it. The second: an earlier "verification" passed against a stray Python server holding the port while the host had failed to bind, and coincidentally served the same font path. Verified properly afterwards — two faces registered in a real browser against the real host, Thai reported, Latin silent, no console errors. |
| COL-16 | The collaboration service's stack, decided | Done | [ADR-014](59-COLLABORATION-SERVICE-STACK.md). [ADR-011](56-COLLABORATION-CONCURRENCY-DESIGN.md) chose OT and [ADR-012](57-COLLABORATION-SERVER-BOUNDARY.md) drew the boundary; both are about spreadsheets.
