This records the four choices that are about **infrastructure**, which are the ones an operator lives with and the ones that are expensive to reverse once a deployment exists — which is why they were taken to the person who owns them rather than guessed. **WebSocket over `axum`**: the protocol is already message-shaped, and a WebSocket pins a *connection* rather than a client identity, so it is not the sticky session ADR-012 rules out. **Host-signed JWT verified against JWKS**: the server can verify a token and cannot mint one, so a compromised node cannot issue itself access to a document; rotation is the integrator's business alone, and `kid` lets old and new keys coexist. HS256 is kept for standalone and development and documented as the weaker option rather than offered as an equal. **Redis, and only in a cluster**: one dependency doing three jobs with three primitives — `SET NX PX` leases fenced by the epoch, pub/sub for relay, Streams for the log — while standalone uses none of it, as a first-class mode rather than a degraded one. **Append before acknowledging**: the alternative is faster and loses edits that every participant's screen still shows, which is silent data loss with a receipt; `Commit::Applied` is a promise, and a promise that is sometimes false is worse than a slower one that is always true. `acks=all` stays configurable with its trap recorded — if the in-sync set collapses to the leader alone it is satisfied by one replica and the guarantee evaporates silently, so it needs a minimum in-sync count enforced separately. |
| COL-17 | The token: identity, permissions, file info, callbacks | Done | ADR-012 said the token is the whole integration contract; this is that contract written out as a type, and the consequence is that **the server holds no per-document state at all** — no table of who may edit what, where each file came from, or where it goes back to. It is told per join by a party that already knows, and cannot be persuaded otherwise because the claims are signed. Carries the participant (id, name, email, avatar, group, cursor colour), the document (session key, file id, title, version, owner, fetch URL), the permissions, and the callback — either an OnlyOffice-style URL or a **WOPI** `src`+`token`, tagged rather than guessed at from the shape of a string. Four things in it are decisions rather than fields. **`document.key` is not `document.id`**: the key identifies an editing *session*, so a host restoring an old version must issue a **new** key or its clients join the session still holding the content it just replaced. **`access` is a mode, not a label** — `comment` refuses a cell edit at the *operation* level, including one hidden inside a batch or a metadata bundle naming comments among other fields, because a permission that is transported and then ignored reads like a guarantee in the integrator's code and is a suggestion in ours; the match is deny-by-default, so an operation added later is refused below `edit` until somebody decides which side of the line it is on. **The absent default is the least**: a token omitting `permissions` grants `view`. And **`copy` is honestly client-side** — the bytes are on the participant's machine by then, and a system claiming otherwise is describing a screenshot it cannot prevent. **The URLs are the dangerous part**: a token names addresses this server will connect to, which makes a leaked one a request-forgery primitive aimed at whatever the deployment can reach from the inside. The host signed it, which is not enough, so there is a host allow-list and an `https` requirement — and the check parses the authority rather than looking for a substring, because `https://host.example@attacker.example/` contains the allowed host and is not it. No I/O: expiry, audience, document match and URL policy are all decidable from the claims, the policy and a supplied clock, which is what makes the rare cases testable. 22 tests; six mutations tried on the security-critical paths, six caught. |
| COL-18 | Verifying the token | Done | Turning a signed string into [COL-17](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md)'s claims, per ADR-014: the integrator signs, this server verifies, and never holds a signing key. **Fetching is not verifying** — a JWKS document arrives over the network and checking a signature against it does not, so `KeySet::from_jwks` parses bytes the caller obtained however it likes and `Verifier` is then a pure function of the token, the keys, the policy and a supplied clock. Every rule worth getting right is on the pure side. Shaped around the two attacks a JWT verifier exists to survive. **Algorithm confusion**: a token's header names its own algorithm, and a verifier that believes it can be handed `alg: none`, or handed `alg: HS256` and tricked into using a published RSA *public* key as an HMAC secret — so the accepted algorithms are **configuration, not input**, and the header is checked against them rather than consulted. (`alg: none` turns out to be refused twice over, since the library will not parse it into an algorithm at all; the test asserts the *property* rather than which layer caught it, because pinning the layer would make a defence-in-depth change look like a regression.) **Key confusion**: an unknown `kid` is a refusal rather than an invitation to try the others, because "try everything until one works" is how a key retired for being compromised goes on working. Three smaller decisions: keys the server cannot use are **skipped** rather than failing the whole set, since a real JWKS carries encryption keys beside the signing one; a set with *nothing* usable is an error rather than an empty verifier, because silently refusing every token reads as a client problem and is a configuration one; and `KeySet`'s `Debug` never prints a key, since an HS256 "key" is a secret and a debug line is the most casual way for one to reach a log. Every failure tells the client the same thing — a caller who can tell malformed from wrong-key from bad-signature from expired has an oracle. 12 tests. |
| COL-19 | The network service: standalone, end to end | Done | ADR-014's transport, wired to everything the crate already had. `axum` + WebSocket, a `/collab` upgrade keyed by the document the client asks for, `/healthz` for a load balancer. **This is the standalone shape** — one process, leader of every document by definition, log in memory — which ADR-012 is explicit is a *first-class mode* and not a degraded one: it is what most deployments will run, and requiring a cluster for it would be requiring infrastructure to solve a problem the operator does not have. **Fetching is injected** rather than an HTTP client being built in: an integrator may need mutual TLS, a proxy or a signed request this crate should not have opinions about, and a test can answer without a network — which is what makes the join path testable at all. **No lock is ever held across an `await`**, which is not a style preference: a guard over a suspension point makes the future non-`Send` (the compiler said so) and, worse, parks every other participant on that document behind one slow socket. The document registry resolves a race by letting the *other* opener win — two sessions over one document is the one outcome that must not happen, and a wasted fetch is the cheaper mistake. **The end-to-end tests found two real bugs.** The broadcast went to every subscriber including the sender, so a client would have received its own operations back and applied them a second time — the fan-out now carries who caused each message so a connection skips its own, and the comment in the code that claimed this already worked is now true. And the protocol-mismatch reply reported the *server's* version as the client's, so the message said the two agreed while refusing them. 12 tests over a real listener and real sockets, including two participants seeing each other's edits, a viewer and a commenter refused at the socket rather than by a hidden toolbar, and presence carrying the name from the token. |
| COL-20 | Exposure: TLS, plain, reverse proxies, and node identity | Done | All of it configuration, because every deployment answers differently and none of the answers is a default worth imposing: an evaluation wants plain HTTP on one port, a Kubernetes deployment terminates TLS at an ingress and runs plain behind it, a regulated one wants TLS to the process *and* between its own nodes. A node has a **public** endpoint and, in a cluster, a separate **internal** one, each independently plain or TLS — different security problems (browsers over the internet, versus known peers on a network the operator controls) deserving different answers. **The dangerous part is the forwarded headers, not the certificates.** `X-Forwarded-For` is a header, so anyone who can reach the port can write one, and a server that believes it has no idea who its clients are. Believed **only from a configured proxy**, defaulting to nobody; and the chain is walked **right to left**, because each hop appends — the rightmost entries came from the proxies nearest this server and the leftmost from whoever spoke first, which includes the client. Taking the leftmost is the common implementation and is exactly backwards. An unreadable entry *ends* the walk rather than being stepped over. **A test caught a real flaw in the first version**: `trust_any_peer` was also treating every address *in the chain* as a proxy, so a deployment saying "I am only reachable through a proxy" got the proxy's own address as every client's — the exact value the walk exists to avoid returning. The two questions are now separate, and IPv4-mapped IPv6 peers are normalised, because a dual-stack listener reports `::ffff:10.0.0.7` for a proxy configured as `10.0.0.7` and a list that does not match is a list that silently trusts nobody. **Node identity** settles discovery: advertising is *not* binding, and deriving one from the other is what makes a cluster look configured and never form — `0.0.0.0` accepts from everywhere and can be dialled from nowhere. `NodeIdentity::problems` refuses an unspecified address, loopback, port zero and an empty id when the configuration is read, rather than letting them present later as an unexplained absence of peers. `Exposure::warnings` says the probably-wrong combinations out loud at startup, because none of them will ever *fail*. 23 tests. |
| COL-21 | Two address spaces: the proxy's, and the cluster's | Done | A clarification that turned out to be structural. A client reaches a node **through** whatever is in front of it, and often the node does not know that address at all because the proxy owns it; relay and replication do **not** go that way, but directly on the cluster network at the address found in Redis. Routing peer traffic back out through the public proxy would be slower and would make cluster traffic arrive looking like a client. Three consequences are now enforced rather than left implicit. The advertised address is checked against the **internal** endpoint's port, so a node advertising its public port is called out instead of quietly sending every peer through the proxy. Proxy trust is documented and scoped as **public-only** — a peer is not a proxy, there is no hop between two nodes for a header to describe, and honouring one on the cluster port would let anything that can reach it claim to be anything. And the internal endpoint gained a **client CA**, because that is where mutual TLS belongs: its peers are a known, small, operator-controlled set, which is exactly what client certificates are good at and exactly what a browser is not. TLS there without a client CA proves the traffic is private and not that the peer is one of yours, and that combination is now called out — **an existing test named `a_fully_secured_deployment_has_nothing_to_say` was failing it**, which is how the check earned its place: the fixture had TLS on both endpoints and no client CA, and by the stricter and more accurate definition it was not fully secured. 5 further tests. |
| PROD-01 | Production-readiness audit of the whole application | Done | [60](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md). Sixteen findings across four severities, each marked **Verified** (made to happen) or **Observed** (read out of the code) — a distinction this repository has earned the hard way, since claims from reading have repeatedly been wrong here and claims from running have held. The two that matter most were both invisible to 767 passing tests. |
| PROD-02 | A nested formula aborts the process | Done | Two bounds, both **measured rather than chosen** — and the measuring is the story. `MAX_DEPTH` stops the parser's own recursion; `MAX_CHAIN` stops something subtler, a left-associative chain like `1+1+1+…` which the parser handles in a *loop* so no counter rises, and which builds a left spine that `Expr`'s recursive `Drop` and `Display` then walk off the stack. It parsed cleanly and aborted on the way out of scope. **The first values for both were wrong, and each was caught by a test rather than by reasoning.** `MAX_CHAIN` began at 4,096, argued from the format's 8,192-character limit; it passed on the main thread's eight megabytes and aborted the moment a *test* thread ran it with two. `MAX_DEPTH` began at 256 to match the evaluator's, and died at a megabyte — measuring each shape **in its own process** (a stack overflow aborts rather than unwinding, so it cannot be observed from inside) showed function nesting failing between 64 and 128, brackets between 128 and 192, unary past 256. Function nesting sets the bound because it costs the most frames per level. The final numbers are 65 expression levels — **exactly Excel's sixty-four levels of nesting plus the outermost expression**, an off-by-one a test also caught — and a 512-term chain, which is below what the format permits and is a deliberate trade: a clear error where there was `SIGABRT`. Two things verified rather than assumed along the way: the evaluator's `MAX_DEPTH` is a *different* limit (it bounds a chain of cell references, which no formula's shape constrains) so forcing them equal was wrong; and `serde_json`'s own 128-deep recursion limit already protects the snapshot and wire paths, which could otherwise have built a deep `Expr` without going near the parser. A **formula fuzz target** now exists — the missing one, since the only target covered ZIP admission — and it parses, prints, re-parses and *drops*, because the chain bug happened on the way out. 3.1 million runs, no crashes. 5 tests. |
| PROD-03 | The collaboration server never saves | Done | A sweeper now drives every document's clock — one task for all of them, because a thousand idle timers is a thousand things to cancel correctly on eviction. Each tick expires silent participants, asks the lifecycle what to do, and lets go of documents nobody is in. `Deliver` is injected beside `Fetch` for the same reasons: an integrator may need mutual TLS or a signed request, and a test must be able to watch a save without a network. Assembly happens **under** the document lock and delivery **outside** it, so a slow or unreachable integrator cannot stop everyone editing. A token with no callback records the save as accepted rather than retrying forever against nothing — that is a preview, not a failure. The follow-up actions reach the participants: `WarnNotSaving` on the *first* failure, because a warning is only useful while there is still time to copy the work out, and `GoReadOnly` when continuing would be silent loss dressed as availability. |
| PROD-04 | A panic while holding a lock breaks a document forever | Done | Every `lock().expect(…)` in the service replaced by a helper that recovers from poisoning. The data behind these locks is a document and a roster, not an invariant a half-finished mutation ruins beyond use, so recovering and carrying on is strictly better than refusing forever — and refusing forever was the behaviour, silently, while the process kept serving and no health check noticed. Worth a loud line in the log; not worth an outage. |
| PROD-05 | Nothing is ever evicted or bounded | Done | Documents are evicted once empty, idle past a window and with **nothing outstanding** — never with unsaved work, since that loses exactly what the lifecycle exists to deliver. The idle window is deliberately not zero: somebody reloading the page is gone for a second, and rebuilding the session from the origin for that is wasteful. Presence expiry is now driven, so `Roster::expire` — written, tested and never called — finally runs. And there are caps on documents per node, participants per document, and message size, the last applied at the upgrade before a byte is read. **Three of five mutations survived the first attempt at these tests**, including a hollow eviction test that asserted rejoining works, which it does whether or not anything was evicted. Fixed by adding `/stats`, which is real observability rather than a test hook: counts, never identities, because a document key names a customer's file. |
| PROD-06 | The service cannot be deployed or operated | Done | **Graceful shutdown done.** `SIGTERM` and Ctrl-C stop the listener, stop the **sweeper first**, and give every document with work outstanding one bounded final save. Stopping the sweeper first is not tidiness: both it and the drain assemble and deliver, so a sweeper still ticking during shutdown races the final save and can hand the host the same document twice. The bound is configurable and deliberate — the host is often being restarted at the same moment, and a node that refuses to exit is a worse failure than one that exits having tried; what it must not do is exit *without* trying, which was the behaviour. **The mutation pass rewrote the tests rather than the code**: two of three survived because they waited on the *port* closing, which happens when `serve` returns and says nothing about the drain that runs after it. They now await the service task. The third — sweeper racing drain — is prevented by construction and is not gated, because a deterministic test needs a deliberately slow deliverer and precise ordering, and a timing-dependent test that usually passes is worse than saying so. `/stats` also landed (counts, never identities). **The HTTP transport is done too**: a real `Fetch` and `Deliver` over `reqwest`, with both callback shapes — an OnlyOffice-style POST, and a WOPI `PUT {src}/contents` carrying `X-WOPI-Override: PUT`, without which a WOPI server answers 404 rather than saving. Bounded everywhere a request to somebody else's server can hang or grow: a timeout, a response ceiling checked both against a declared `Content-Length` *and* while streaming (a missing or lying length is exactly what an endless body has), and **redirects deliberately not followed**, since one would take the request somewhere the token's allow-list never approved. A document title is stripped before it becomes a header, because it comes from the token and is the field most likely to hold whatever a user typed — a newline in it would inject another header, and that is gated. **Trust comes from the system store, not a bundled root list**, which `cargo deny` prompted and correctness settled: an integrator inside a company sits behind that company's own CA, and a bundled public list rejects it correctly and uselessly. The cost is a deployment requirement (`ca-certificates` in the image), which fails loudly at the first request rather than permanently. 10 tests against a real origin on a real socket. **Tracing** replaced every `eprintln!`: one span per connection carrying user, document id and access level, with events for saves, callback failures, read-only transitions and evictions. The document *key* is deliberately absent from all of it — it names a customer's file, and a log is the easiest place for that to leak. **TLS is wired**, so the choice `Exposure` models is no longer documentation: certificates and keys are read into a `rustls` configuration, and a **client CA builds a real verifier** — the half that is easy to configure and forget, since TLS on the internal endpoint proves the traffic is private and only a client CA proves the peer is one of yours. Every failure names the file rather than being guessed at, including the more confusing case of a file that exists and holds nothing useful, and a client CA with no certificate in it is refused rather than bringing the endpoint up demanding certificates it can never accept. 5 tests, on a certificate generated at test time rather than committed — a checked-in private key is one somebody eventually uses somewhere real. |
| PROD-07 | The cluster is designed and not implemented | Done | **The Redis coordinator is built and held to the same rules as the in-memory one.** Every cluster rule is now written once against `&dyn Coordinator` and run against **both** backends — that is the whole point of the trait, since a second implementation tested by a second set of tests is two specifications that happen to share a name. Redis runs get a fresh key namespace per test, because a real database persists and a suite that only passes on an empty one fails on the second run. Verified by mutating the Lua: removing the fence, bumping the epoch on renewal, and dropping the conditional append each fail the Redis half. **The trait had to become fallible.** It was written for an in-memory store that cannot fail, and against a network store `claim` returning a `Lease` unconditionally is a node inventing leadership it never obtained — so every method returns `Result` and `Unavailable` is deliberately distinct from every protocol "no": *somebody else leads* is an answer, *I could not ask* is not. Claim and append are **Lua scripts** because both are read-then-write and a gap between the halves is two leaders. A `LIST` rather than Streams, diverging from ADR-014 and saying so: the trait speaks in revisions and a list index *is* one, where a stream id would need a parallel mapping; Streams' consumer groups are unused because pub/sub does the fan-out. Key namespace is configurable, since one Redis is routinely shared between staging and production and two deployments sharing a prefix share leases. Startup refuses when a node is configured for a cluster it cannot reach — verified by running it — because such a node takes leadership of everything it is asked about and is wrong about all of it. **The relay is designed ([ADR-017](63-COLLABORATION-RELAY.md)) and its two halves are built and tested:** the pub/sub transport on the Redis coordinator — subscriptions get their own connection, because a Redis connection in subscribe mode accepts nothing else and sharing the multiplexed one would take the coordinator offline the moment anything subscribed — and the gap decision, kept as a pure function so it is testable as a decision rather than as a side effect. That decision is the part that is easy to get subtly wrong and impossible to notice: applying a batch out of order does not fail, it diverges. Both verified by mutation — dropping the document from the channel name, and treating a gap as applicable, each fail. The engine half a relay needs is built too: `ServerSession::adopt` applies an **already-ordered** batch without transforming it again (doing so would rebase it past the very operations it was already rebased past — which does not fail, it produces a document that quietly disagrees with the leader's), refuses a batch that does not follow directly, and applies nothing when it refuses; and `note_accepted`, so a relay records what it saw ordered and a client reconnecting to it still gets duplicate suppression. Writing ADR-017 also turned up a flaw in ADR-017: an earlier draft said every node applies from the channel *including the leader*, which is not implementable — ordering and applying are the same step, so a leader waiting for its own publication would transform the next submission against a document missing the previous one. The record is corrected; the fan-out to clients stays one path with two feeds. **Wired, and two nodes against one Redis now serve one document.** Building it found four bugs, none of which one node could show: (1) a **`ClientId` is a per-node counter**, so the first participant on every node was client 1 — duplicate suppression discarded a second person's first chunk as a redelivery of the first person's, and the broadcast acknowledged one node's writer to another node's reader while withholding the edit from whoever should have got it; the node's name now goes in the high half. (2) A committed batch was attributed to the node that **ordered** it rather than the one holding its **author**, so a relayed edit was announced as the leader's. (3) `commit` assigns a revision under a lock and the append that records it happened outside — two concurrent orders could reach the log out of order, and the conditional append then dropped one **after it had already been applied locally**. (4) Subscriptions were established asynchronously, leaving a window in which a node served clients before it was listening; Redis pub/sub has no history, so a submission forwarded into that window vanished and its client waited forever. Subscribing is now awaited while the document opens, and a node that cannot subscribe refuses the document rather than serving one it cannot hear about. **The reconciliation ADR-017 named as unbuilt is built**: every node reads the log from where it is on each lease renewal, so a node that missed a publication on a document which then goes quiet is corrected by time rather than by the next edit — and a node taking over a document catches up before ordering anything, rather than building on a revision the log has moved past. The channel is now only ever a prompt; everything reaching a client comes from the log or from a batch directly following what the node had. Gated by a test that writes a batch straight into the log and never publishes it, which is what a leader dying between its append and its publish looks like from every other node; confirmed by mutation (removing the reconciliation leaves the client waiting the full fifteen seconds). **Node registration is wired**: a node announces itself on a timer, so `peers` returns something and `elect` has a list to pick from — without it both were permanently empty, and the cluster worked only because leadership is a lease that needs no discovery, which is a real gap wearing the appearance of working. It is a heartbeat in the only sense this design has one: a node announcing *itself*, never an opinion about anybody else, and a node that stops is forgotten on the next read rather than judged. The advertised address is the **internal** one, since a client is given an address by a load balancer and a node is not. The announced load is this node's **document count**, read fresh each round, gated by a test that opens a document and waits for the count to appear. PROD-07 is complete. Superseded: wiring them into `serve` — claiming leadership on a timer per document, subscribing a document's channels when it is opened, routing a submission to the inbox when this node does not lead, and the two-process integration test that is the only arrangement where a relay exists at all. |
| PROD-09 | Client inactivity, and the ghost connection it left | Done | Presence expired a quiet participant and **nothing closed their socket**, which is the worse half: a connection nobody was on, still holding a slot, a broadcast subscription and a place in the participant cap — and still able to submit edits while belonging to nobody as far as presence was concerned. The two now agree. The server **pings a quiet connection**, which any live client answers without the page doing anything, so liveness does not depend on the application protocol being spoken — and it is the only thing that notices a **half-open** socket, where a laptop slept or a network vanished and the connection looks open with nobody on the far end. *Anything* from the far end, the automatic pong included, refreshes both the idle clock and the roster, so a participant who is reading rather than typing keeps their cursor instead of being expired while plainly present. The idle limit is deliberately longer than the presence TTL and several times the ping interval: closing a connection because its user went to lunch is worse than holding it. Three mutations tried, three caught, including the one that matters most — removing the roster refresh, which restores exactly the inconsistency this closes. 3 tests. |
| COL-26 | Submissions were stop-and-wait, costing a round trip per chunk | Done | [ADR-016](62-COLLABORATION-PIPELINING.md). `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 5. A client sent one chunk and would not send another until it was acknowledged, so on a 200 ms link it could submit about five times a second however fast anyone typed — and the delay was not only the typist's: the other participants did not see the second batch until the first was acknowledged, because it had never been sent. The obstacle was real, though: the second chunk is written on top of the first *locally*, before the first has been ordered, so naming the same base has the server rebase it against a chunk it already contains — transformed twice, landing wrong, and silently, since there is no error to raise, only two documents that no longer agree. The client cannot name the right base because the right base is wherever the first chunk landed. Resolved the way TCP does: a sender does not need to know the receiver's position. `Submission::base` is now `Revision(n)` or `Chained`, and the server resolves `Chained` from the `accepted` table it already keeps for duplicate suppression — sound because one client's chunks travel one connection and arrive in order. A chained chunk from a client with nothing accepted is **refused**, since inventing a base is how divergence starts. `Ack` is cumulative (`through`), so a lost acknowledgement heals on the next rather than stranding a chunk. Outstanding chunks are bounded at 32, degrading to stop-and-wait under pressure — which is a good thing to degrade to, being what it replaced. The interleaving harness had to learn that one client's chunks cannot overtake each other while keeping cross-client reordering free, which is where the interesting cases are. |
| COL-25 | Opening a document blocked the join, and happened once per arrival | Done | `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 4 adds `Opening`, sent as soon as the token checks out and **before** the document is fetched. Fetching means asking the integrator's server, which can take as long as the configured HTTP timeout; until now the client saw an open socket and silence during that, which is exactly what a hung server looks like — and which a user answers by reloading, starting a second wait beside the first. The client's ping timer starts at `Opening` rather than at the welcome, because that wait is the longest stretch of a session with no traffic on it and so the likeliest place for a connection to die unnoticed; it is also the one state where "slow" and "broken" used to look identical. Separately, the open is now **single-flighted** through a `OnceCell` per document key: thirty people opening one workbook at the start of a meeting was thirty simultaneous downloads of the same file, twenty-nine discarded, aimed at that server at its busiest. The old code settled the race after the fact — correct for two, badly wrong for thirty. `get_or_try_init`, so a failure is not remembered and a briefly-down origin does not poison the document until restart. The document cap is now checked before the fetch as well as after, so a full node refuses without first downloading what it cannot hold. |
| COL-24 | A client cannot tell a live connection from a half-open one | Done | `Ping`/`Pong` with a matched nonce, and `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 3. The server already pinged at the WebSocket level and a browser answers those without the page being told — which detects a dead **client** and leaves the opposite case entirely uncovered. A half-open connection (a slept laptop, a vanished network, a load balancer that dropped the flow without a FIN) looks perfectly open to the browser holding it for as long as the OS takes to give up on the socket, which is minutes. For all of them the editor works, accepts typing and sends into nothing: the socket does not notice, and neither does the flush, because `send` on a dead socket does not fail. So the client asks and starts a clock; silence past the deadline reconnects, which since [COL-23](61-COLLABORATION-RESUME.md) loses nothing. The nonce is the part that matters — without it a late answer to an earlier ping satisfies the current one and a connection failing steadily reads as healthy. One outstanding at a time, so the deadline measures the most recent ask rather than the oldest; cleared with the timers, so a ping outstanding across a reconnect cannot condemn the new connection for the old one's silence. The round trip is kept and exposed as `latency()` for a host that wants to show connection quality. A viewer may ping too: liveness is not an edit, and a read-only participant needs it more, since nothing else they do would ever draw an answer out of the server. |
| COL-23 | Reconnecting silently lost or duplicated unacknowledged edits | Done | [ADR-015](61-COLLABORATION-RESUME.md). A dropped socket had two outcomes and both were silent. The work was **lost** — on rejoin the server sent a snapshot and the client replaced its workbook with it, so anything typed that the server never received existed only in the workbook being discarded. Or it was **duplicated** — the obvious repair, resending, was worse, because `ClientId` came from a per-connection counter, so a reconnecting participant was a new client and the server's `(client, seq)` duplicate suppression did not apply to it. Fixed with a client-generated resume key, scoped to the authenticated user so a guessed key is worth nothing, mapping back to the same `ClientId`; a resumed client is sent **what it missed rather than a snapshot**, so its document stays continuous and its outstanding chunk is still expressed against a workbook it understands; and it resends with its original sequence number, which the server answers `Duplicate` to if it already had it. When the history cannot reach back far enough the client is told `TooFarBehind` **before** the snapshot lands, so the loss is audible and a host can offer to rescue the cells first. `PROTOCOL_VERSION` is 2. Verified at three levels, ending in a real browser taken offline mid-edit that reconnects and lands its work. Two smaller things fell out: `collab_unacknowledged` asked only the collaborative session, and so answered "nothing unsaved" for somebody who had been typing into a dropped connection for a minute — the work sits in the editor's applied log until the next flush, which is where a disconnected client spends all its time; and `reconnect()` was added to the transport, because a dead TCP connection can look open for minutes and a host that knows the network changed should not have to wait for the operating system to notice. |
| PROD-10 | The browser half of collaboration is unbuilt | Done | **Two browsers now edit one document through the real server binary**, gated by `tests/browser/collab.spec.mjs`. The editor owns the join (`collaborate()` in `editor.js`) rather than leaving it to the host, because the engine is imported under a cache-busting specifier and each mounted element gets its own copy — a host that imported the glue itself would get a second, uninitialised instance and every call would throw from inside the generated bindings. **Putting the halves in one room found three defects that every unit test on both sides had passed over.** (1) The WebAssembly binding returned a bare `Submission` where the protocol carries a tagged `ClientMessage::Submit`, so the server could not parse a single edit any browser ever made. (2) `WireOperation`'s three interned tables — formulas, styles, strings — are JSON maps keyed by ids that **serialize correctly and cannot be read back**: JSON keys are strings and `serde_json` parses integer keys only for the primitive integer types, which `NonZeroU32` and a transparent newtype are not. Every text edit and every style edit was affected. It was invisible because the round-trips that went through JSON carried those tables empty and the tests that filled them went through `localise` rather than serde. (3) The server **silently dropped** anything it could not parse, so a client in that state looked connected and working from both ends — socket open, heartbeat answered, present in the roster, every edit going nowhere. It now warns and refuses, naming the submission when it can. A protocol mismatch also became `Stopped` rather than `Refused`, since a client that reasonably treats a refusal as retryable loops forever against a server that can never accept it. |
| COL-22 | `StringId` is replica-local too, and text crossed replicas as the wrong text | Done | **Silent data corruption in the collaboration wire format, found by the first test that made two participants type words at each other.** COL-12 established that a `FormulaHandle` and a `StyleId` are replica-local and must be translated rather than trusted; a `StringId` comes from a table that numbers exactly as independently and was left out. So each participant interned their own word as id 1, each received the other's operation, each resolved it against their own table — and each read *their own word* where the other had typed. Nothing errored, both sides were self-consistent, and the documents differed. `WireOperation` now carries the strings its ids refer to, and `localise` re-interns them **by value**, so two replicas that already know a word converge on the receiver's existing id rather than growing the table on every arrival. One deliberate asymmetry with the handle path: an id arriving with no accompanying text is **left alone rather than dropped**, because unlike a formula handle it addresses a *value* — clearing it would erase the cell's contents rather than degrade them, and wrong-and-visible beats wrong-and-invisible. Both value shapes are walked, `SetCell` and `SetValue`, since covering one and not the other is how half the edits cross correctly. 5 tests. |
| PROD-11 | The server had no way to be run | Done | `casual-calc-collab-server` was a **library with no `main`**: state machines, a transport, a verifier, a cluster, and nothing that could start any of it. [60](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md) swept this crate and did not notice, because it read what was there rather than trying to start it — the second scope miss that audit has had to record. Configuration is twelve-factor, because the deployments this is for hand a container an environment and nothing else, and everything is checked **before the listener opens**: half a TLS pair, half a node identity, an advertised address no peer can dial, and no way to verify a token at all are each refused with a line naming the fix, while the probably-wrong combinations are warned about once. A tuning knob that will not parse takes the default and says so rather than taking the service down — a typo in a limit should not be an outage, and silence would leave an operator wondering why their setting did nothing. **Running it found a real cost**: `SIGTERM` took 2,040 ms because the drain slept twice the tick interval waiting for the sweeper to notice, which was a guess standing in for a fact. Awaiting the sweeper instead — bounded, so one wedged inside a save cannot hold the process open — took it to **4 ms**. Two seconds of dead time per node per deploy, found only by starting the thing and stopping it. |

## Closed after 2026-08-30

The second sweep of this kind. The rows above were moved when 244 closed rows
were 84% of the live tracker; these 245 had accumulated since, and were 86% of it
again — which says the rate is the normal one and this is a recurring chore
rather than a one-off tidy.

**These are verbatim, with their mechanism intact**, where the older rows above
were shortened on the ground that a commit carries the same reasoning. That
turned out to be only mostly true: several rows here were filed by one piece of
work and closed by another, so their mechanism was never in a single commit
message. Keeping the text costs a file nobody reads during normal work, and
losing it costs the reason a defect class was understood.

**No ids were merged.** Rows here are cited by id from `docs/12`, from code
comments, and from each other, and `tools/check-doc-references.py` fails on a
citation to an id no tracker defines — so merging two closed rows into one would
turn every citation of the loser into a dangling reference. Shortening is safe;
renaming is not.

| ID | Title | St | Sev | Mechanism | Gate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CI-024 | Four gates each kept a private answer to "what is a tracker row", and two guessed narrow | Done | P1 | `check-tracker-shape` and `check-tracker-freshness` re-derived the row-id pattern from the ids in front of them and landed on `[A-Z][A-Z0-9]*(?:-[A-Z]+)*-[0-9]+` — upper case, digits at the end. `check-tracker-ids`, written first, had the right one all along. **47 rows were invisible** to the narrow pair: `UX-P08a`, `UX-A11Y-01`, `BUG-FREEZEPANE`, `M11-1a`, `P1A-003b`, `REVIEW-FIXES`. Four were `Done` rows in the live tracker, which is why `DOC-050`'s sweep left them behind and freshness then printed "0 closed" over them; 43 were archive rows shape had never checked. **What made it invisible:** a gate that cannot see a row cannot fail on it, so both printed a confident green — the fault was never a wrong answer, it was a narrower question. One definition now lives in `tools/tracker_rows.py` and all four gates import it. | **`tools/check-gate-selftest.py`, new — the first test of any gate in this repo.** Asserts the shared pattern sees eight real id shapes and rejects three non-rows, that `cells()` honours `\|`, that `rows()` skips fenced examples, and that **no `check-*.py` defines a row-id pattern of its own** — the part that stops the class recurring. Seen red on the shipped narrow regex, naming all six irregular shapes; seen red on a reintroduced private copy in three different spellings |
| UX-P08a | Border palette polish | Done | P3 | Diagonals, composite placements and true double rendering shipped. Choosing a colour sets `--oc-x-border-swatch` on the toolbar button and marks the swatch; both are now pinned by a browser test, seen red when the tint is not set and when the marking is dropped. | The toolbar swatch shows the colour the next border will use |
| UX-P09a | Elapsed-time formats | Done | P3 | `[h]`, `[m]` and `[s]` shipped as fields. Locale-prefix polish remains. **Already done**: `numfmt.rs` handles `[$-409]` and carries month and weekday names for every language an `[$-lcid]` prefix can select, and its own comment records the bug that prompted it — a plain `contains('0')` reading the locale id's zero as a digit placeholder. Gated from the outside for the first time: `[$-409][h]:mm` must render as an elapsed time rather than literal text. | An elapsed-time format behind a locale prefix renders |
| UX-P13a | Sheet visibility | Done | P3 | Model, round-trip and hide/unhide UI shipped. Tab overflow, move-to and protection remain. **All three were already there** — the strip is `overflow-x: auto` with `min-width: 0`, `session_move_sheet` is wired to the drag reorder, and sheet protection landed with `UX-PROT-01`. Gated: twenty-four sheets added *through the editor's own button* must scroll rather than squash or push past the window, and a moved sheet must stay moved. | Many tabs scroll; a moved sheet stays moved |
| UX-A11Y-01 | The grid was too faint to see | Done | P1 | Same report, same session. Gridlines were `#f0f1f4`, which is **1.13:1** against the canvas — measurably fainter than Google Sheets (1.32:1) and Excel (~1.45:1), and far below the 3:1 WCAG 2.1 asks for a graphical object needed to understand the content. A spreadsheet's grid is the definition of that: it is what tells you which cell you are looking at. Raised to 1.44:1 light and 1.66:1 dark, in line with the category. **Deliberately not 3:1 by default** — a hairline at 3:1 reads as a heavy table border and fights the data for attention, which is why nothing in this category ships one. The accessible answer is not a darker default for everybody but honouring the person who has asked for more, so `prefers-contrast: more` now clears 3:1 in both schemes (3.07:1 and 3.22:1). **What made it invisible:** no test can be asked how visible a hairline is, and the value was inside every theme gate because those check that a token is *honoured*, not that it can be *seen*. This one asserts the computed contrast ratio, which is mechanical. | The computed gridline-to-canvas ratio is at least the category's own level; seen red at the shipped `#f0f1f4` as "gridlines are 1.13:1" |
| DOC-050 | The tracker had grown to seven closed rows for every open one | Done | P2 | 285 rows, of which 245 were `Done`, `Dropped` or `Accepted` \| the open work was 14% of a 382-line file and 34 of those rows were the only ones anyone needed. The rule to move closed rows to [14a](14a-ARCHIVE-CLOSED-WORK.md) was already written in 14's own preamble and had been applied once, in 2026-08; nothing enforced it afterwards, so it decayed straight back. **What made it invisible:** no gate can tell a stale row from a live one, because both are well-formed \| the signal is a ratio, and nothing was counting it. Moved verbatim rather than merged, because `check-doc-references` fails on a citation to an id no tracker defines, so merging closed rows would dangle every citation of the loser. | **`tools/check-tracker-freshness.py`, new** — fails when closed rows are over half the live tracker and at least 20, which no other gate can see because a stale row and a live row are both well-formed. Seen red against `main`'s tracker at 245-of-285, green at 0-of-40. Plus `check-tracker-ids` (ids stay unique across both files), `check-doc-references` (every citation still resolves), `check-tracker-shape` (the moved rows got a header, so its 245 rows are now checked where the 2026-08 batch is still reported unchecked) |
| COL-28 | Collaborative undo does not converge | Done | P0 | Undo applied its stored inverse untransformed. For a cell that is last-writer-wins and acceptable (docs/69); for a structural edit it deletes a band somebody has since filled, destroying work that was never there when the undo was recorded and that **no undo stack anywhere holds** — the peer's history says "typed into row 10", not what row 10 contained. Structural undo is now refused before it applies, and refused loudly. No authorship tracking was needed: the stack is LIFO, so by the time an insert is on top, everything this session did after it has already been undone and anything left in the band is somebody else's. | `undoing_an_insert_a_peer_has_filled_is_refused` + 4 siblings; `collab.spec.mjs` — two browsers, refused with a message, both documents unchanged |
| COL-30 | An imported chart's retained bytes bypassed the operation wire | Done | P0 | The row also named `sheet.tables`; that half was **stale** — tables are in the `SheetMetadata` bundle and have crossed the wire since. Retained parts were real: deleting or detaching an imported chart called `drop_chart_part`, which wrote through `workbook_mut` and so was in no operation. A replica kept the part, and whichever node saved decided what the file contained; undo restored the chart's entry with its bytes already destroyed. `apply` now **derives** which parts died from the bundle every replica already receives — no wire data forward — and carries them on the inverse in a `skip_serializing_if`-empty field, so no `PROTOCOL_VERSION` bump. | `deleting_an_imported_chart_reaches_a_replica`, `undoing_a_chart_deletion_restores_its_retained_bytes`, `the_retained_bytes_field_is_not_a_wire_break` |
| SEC-002 | No end-to-end admission budget | Done | P0 | Every limit was per part and every one was passing; nothing added them up. A package may hold 50,000 parts, so the only real ceiling on the total was the 4 GiB uncompressed cap — and 4 GiB of `<c r="A1"><v>1</v></c>` is on the order of a hundred million cells, from an upload of a few megabytes. `SpreadsheetLimits` now counts populated cells, shared strings, defined names and merged ranges **once for the document**, across every part, and fails closed. | `cells_are_counted_across_the_whole_workbook` — two sheets of 40 refused by a budget of 79, which a per-sheet check admits |
| SEC-003 | Docker secret hygiene | Done | P0 | Three holes: `.env` was in neither `.gitignore` nor `.dockerignore` while the README tells everyone to create one holding the signing secret; and compose defaulted to `dev-secret-change-me`, so a deployment that forgot got a **published** key — and a shared secret lets its holder *mint* tokens, not merely check them. Compose now has no default (missing fails loudly) and the server **refuses** every placeholder this repo publishes, plus anything under 16 bytes. | `secret_tests` reads the values out of `.env.example` and both compose files, so a new placeholder cannot slip in |
| PROD-12 | Docker collaboration endpoint is unreachable | Done | P0 | The default was `ws://127.0.0.1:8443/collab` — the *browser's* loopback, on a different port from the page — so a share link worked only on the Docker host. Now derived from the request the browser just made (same origin, `X-Forwarded-Proto` picks `wss`), and standalone gains an nginx so one origin is true. Explicit config still wins. | `endpoint_tests` (4); CI brings the stack up and upgrades `/collab` on the page's origin |
| PROD-13 | Upload → store → open → co-edit | Done | P0 | Limits landed; Docker reachability, error UX and the end-to-end gate remain. **Done.** `tools/e2e-document-lifecycle.mjs` runs the whole chain against the deployed stack in `docker-build`: upload a corpus `.xlsx` (somebody else's file, not one this project's own writer produced), follow the `303` to the new document, open the share link, take two tokens *from the host* so its own signing is under test, have one participant edit and the other receive it, then download and check what comes back is still an OOXML package. Speaks the wire protocol directly rather than through Playwright — the browser suite already proves two browsers converge; what was unproven is the same thing through the **deployed** stack, and the job that has the stack has no browser. Reads `PROTOCOL_VERSION` from the engine rather than keeping a copy, because `COL-42` was exactly that drifting. Running it found `PROD-14`. Also excluded `.claude/` from the build context: a finished agent worktree keeps its own full `target/` tree, so the directory had reached 4.4 GB and every `docker build` was shipping all of it — the existing `target/` rule does not cover a *nested* copy under a different prefix, and it is working state that has no business in a layer either way. | Upload a real `.xlsx`, share it, edit from two browsers |
| PROD-14 | A configured collaboration endpoint with no path is a dead socket | Done | P1 | The derived endpoint has always ended in `/collab`, the only path the collaboration server serves. A **configured** `OPENCALC_COLLAB_WS` was returned verbatim — so `ws://collab:8443`, which is exactly what the variable's name invites, produced an address nothing answers. It fails silently on both sides: the host did as it was told, the server was never asked, and the only symptom is an editor that never connects, with nothing in either log. **Found by running the stack, not reading it** — it is the first thing that happened when `PROD-13`'s gate was pointed at a hand-configured deployment. Fixed by giving a pathless configured endpoint the `/collab` path; one that names a real path is left alone, since a deployment behind a proxy may mount the socket anywhere and rewriting *that* would break the setup it was configured for. Gated through `collab_endpoint` rather than the helper — asserting on the helper alone passes with the call site reverted, which is the defect itself, and the first version of the test did exactly that. | The endpoint reaches the socket whether or not the path was written |
| PROD-15 | The quick start shipped a collaboration endpoint nothing listens on | Done | P0 | **Found by `PROD-13`'s gate on its first real run**, which is what it was built for. `.env.example` set `OPENCALC_COLLAB_WS=ws://127.0.0.1:8443/collab`, and the collaboration service is `expose`d to the compose network and **not published to the host** — so the browser dialled a port nothing was listening on. `cp .env.example .env && docker compose up`, the documented quick start, produced a stack whose one distinguishing feature was broken. `PROD-12` had already fixed exactly this in the code and `docs/65` records why the default was removed; the example file was left carrying the value the fix deleted, and the example file is what people copy. **Now empty**, so the host derives the endpoint from the request — same origin, through the proxy, which is what the proxy routes. `OPENCALC_COLLAB_PORT` went with it: neither compose file ever read it. Gated by `tools/check-env-example.py` — every `127.0.0.1:PORT` the file points a browser at must be published by some compose file. Seen red on the exact value that broke main, and on an unpublished port; a third mutation shows that blurring `expose` and `publish` hides it, which is why the check distinguishes them. | The quick start co-edits |
| PROD-16 | The proxy told the host a hostname with no port | Done | P1 | Caught while fixing `PROD-15`, before it cost a second CI round rather than after. With `OPENCALC_COLLAB_WS` empty the host derives the endpoint from the request — and nginx's `$host` **strips the port**, so a demo reached on `127.0.0.1:8080` would be told it was reached on `127.0.0.1` and would derive a socket on port 80, where nothing listens. The fix for `PROD-15` would have failed the same gate for a different reason. All three nginx configs now send `X-Forwarded-Host: $http_host`, which the host already prefers over `Host`. Caddy sets it by default and needed no change. | A stack behind the proxy derives an endpoint with its port |
| SEC-014 | No default recalc budget | Done | P2 | Cancellation existed but nothing bounded a recalculation for an embedder who passed no token. Now bounded by a **work** budget, counted across every pass including the iterative ones. Work rather than wall-clock deliberately: `Instant::now` panics on wasm32, and a clock would make the same workbook finish on one machine and stop half-way on a slower one — determinism ranks above security bounds here. docs/21 says so now rather than continuing to say "time". | `the_budget_spans_every_pass` — a per-pass counter lets a workbook ask for 30,000 passes |
| SEC-015 | The iterative recalc phase ignored the cancellation token | Done | P1 | Found one merge after SEC-012 shipped, by reading what the new code called rather than what it claimed. `recalculate_cancellable` checked the token inside a pass and then handed off to `iterate_to_convergence`, which took no token at all — and the pass count comes from the *workbook*, where Excel allows 32,767. So a document could ask for tens of thousands of uncancellable whole-workbook passes through an entry point named for being cancellable. | `the_iterative_phase_stops_too` — one formula cell, so only a check *between* passes can stop it |
| SEC-016 | The SEC-001 sink gate matched one sink in seven, and skipped a whole host | Done | P1 | Two independent blind spots in the same check -- the one that enforces the XSS boundary a workbook can reach (`SEC-001`). **(1) The regex never worked.** It used `\s`, and `git grep -E` is POSIX ERE, where `\s` is undefined; so the `innerHTML`/`outerHTML` half matched *nothing*. The job still looked alive because `document\.write\(` -- the one alternative with no `\s` in it -- kept matching, so the gate reported success having examined one sink out of seven. **(2) The path list named one host.** `server/casual-calc-host/src/*.html` was listed while `casual-calc-wopi/src/editor.html`, served to a browser exactly the same way, was not. **Neither was exploitable**: every existing sink turned out to be correctly marked, so the hole was latent -- but a *new* sink in either dimension would have passed the check written to stop it. Found while reviewing a WOPI change that touched that html. Fixed with `[[:space:]]` and a path *shape* rather than a list of crates. Proven by planting an unmarked sink in each dimension: the old gate is GREEN on both, the new gate is RED on both, and both return GREEN when the plant is removed. This is the third hand-maintained host list to go stale here, after the wasm excludes and the ADR-012 boundary. | A planted sink in any host's markup fails CI |
| SEC-013 | No bound on snapshot size | Done | P2 | The last row of docs/21's scale table, and the one admission path that accepted whatever it was handed — reached from a resumed collaborative session and from any host calling the model directly. The byte ceiling is checked **before** `serde_json` sees the input, because a limit applied after parsing has already paid for the allocation it exists to prevent; the cell count is checked after, at the same ceiling admission uses, so a workbook cannot enter by one door at a size the other refuses. | `an_oversized_snapshot_is_refused_before_parsing` — `OC-MDL-0005`, and not the malformed-snapshot code |
| CI-010 | Diagnostic codes and their registry could drift | Done | P1 | docs/20 is the promise a log-reader relies on, and nothing checked the code agreed with it. Both drifts had already happened: `OC-IMP-0003` sat registered for a limit nothing enforced, and `OC-RND-0001` was emitted by the renderer and registered nowhere — found by the new gate on its first run, not by review. Checked one way only: an emitted code must be registered; a registered code may be reserved for planned work. | The gate passes clean and fails on an unregistered code, verified both ways |
| SEC-011 | No populated-cell admission cap | Done | P1 | docs/21's spreadsheet-scale table listed seven limits; the grid bound and the formula depth/length bounds existed, four did not. Cells, shared strings, defined names and merged ranges are now admitted against a document-wide budget, emitting `OC-IMP-0003` — the code docs/20 reserved for exactly this and which nothing had ever emitted. Snapshot size is the one row still open, split out as SEC-013 rather than implied. | `the_refusal_is_diagnosable` — over-cap refused with `OC-IMP-0003` |
| SEC-012 | Nothing is cancellable | Done | P1 | docs/07 and docs/21 both promised admission and full recalc were "bounded *and* cancellable". They were bounded; nothing in any crate could stop one, so a workbook inside every limit and merely enormous held the browser's only thread until it finished. A cooperative `Cancel` token now exists in the model crate — cooperative because a browser offers no pre-emption, so a job can only stop by asking — and both loops ask periodically. Reachable from the SDK via `open_cancellable` and `recalculate_cancellable`. An import fails closed (`OC-IMP-0007`); a recalc keeps what it computed and *reports* that it stopped, because the document was already holding stale values and discarding fresh ones would leave it worse. | `an_import_stops_after_it_has_begun`, `a_recalculation_stops_after_it_has_begun` — both survive a check at the top of the loop and fail on it |
| FID-24 | Defined names are not shifted by an insert or delete | Done | P1 | Found by auditing docs/46 (CP-032). `structural.rs` shifted merges, sizing, hidden sets, the freeze boundary, outline levels, tables and autofilters — everything position-indexed except `defined_names`. A name pointing at `A10` still pointed at `A10` after five rows were inserted above it, so every formula using it silently read different cells: nothing marked `#REF!`, nothing refused, the number simply changed. Names now go through the same `rewrite_expr` the cells do. A workbook-scoped name is treated as having no home sheet, so an unqualified reference is left alone rather than rewritten against a sheet chosen arbitrarily — Excel writes those fully qualified anyway. | `a_name_below_an_insert_follows_its_target` + 3; the control catches a rewrite that shifts regardless of position |
| COL-41 | No version history or restore | Done | P2 | Asked for directly. There is none, and it is deliberate at the server: docs/57 says the collaboration server "is not a file store, a version history" — storage belongs to the integrator, so a WOPI host such as Nextcloud or SharePoint already provides versioning for that deployment. The gap is real for the SDK-embedded path and the demo host. Snapshot I/O exists in the model, so the missing part is retention and a restore path, not serialisation. **Built, at the host, where the row said it belongs.** A version is kept **before** each save overwrites the document, so history holds what it *was* rather than what it became — a version taken afterwards is a copy of the thing somebody is trying to undo. The timestamp is the filename, so there is no second file to keep in step and a directory listing is already in order. Bounded by `OPENCALC_MAX_VERSIONS` (default 20), because a version per save would otherwise fill the volume. Three endpoints: list, fetch one, restore. **Restoring keeps the current document first**, so the one button whose purpose is undoing a mistake is not the only one that cannot be undone; and a version that will not open is refused rather than made live, the same argument the upload path makes. A history panel on the demo document page makes it reachable rather than an API nobody can call. **Known limit, stated in the deployment page rather than hidden:** a restore lands on the stored file, not on open sessions — anyone still editing overwrites it on their next save. Reaching live sessions means the collaboration server evicting the document, which is a change to a boundary ADR-012 draws deliberately. Two flaws in my own tests were the interesting part. Millisecond-named versions collide on rapid saves and silently lose an entry; and the callback-ordering test passed with the ordering swapped because `blank_xlsx` ignores its title, so its two "different" documents were byte-identical. Seven mutations red once both were fixed. | A named revision can be listed and restored |
| COL-42 | The browser suite's protocol constant drifted from the engine's | Done | P1 | `PROTOCOL_VERSION` went to 6; `tests/browser/collab.spec.mjs` kept `const PROTOCOL = 5`. The suite speaks the wire protocol over a raw socket, so it carries its own copy, and the server refused every join at the old version -- three collaboration tests red **on main**, after the merge. The test that exists for exactly this even says `bump PROTOCOL in this file when the protocol changes`, and it still went unnoticed, because the only thing that could notice was a ten-minute `browser-smoke` job that does not run locally. **Fixed.** Constant bumped, and a grep gate added to the fast `ci` job so the same drift fails in a second instead of after a merge. The gate was exercised three ways: the drift that actually broke main, the engine moving ahead next time, and the constant being renamed so the grep matches nothing -- that last one is what stops a gate like this quietly passing forever. | Engine and suite agree, checked in `ci` rather than only in the browser |
| UX-PAGE-01 | Alt+PgUp/PgDn does not page horizontally | Done | P3 | Found by auditing docs/46 (CP-005). The horizontal-paging branch sits one nesting level too deep, inside the modifier block, so it answers to Ctrl+Alt+PgUp/Dn and Excel's plain Alt+PgDn falls through to vertical paging. A placement bug that reads as a missing feature. | Alt+PgDn pages right |
| UX-PROT-01 | Column and row resizing ignores sheet protection | Done | P1 | `edit_axis` took the sheet index as `_sheet` and dropped it, so resizing a column on a protected sheet succeeded while every other write went through a guard. The unused parameter is what made it invisible — the signature looked as though it cared. A resize is not a cell edit, so the question is not whether the cells are locked: Excel gates it behind protection's own `formatColumns`/`formatRows`, and `SheetProtection::permits` now reads them, refusing when the file is silent (the OOXML default, and the safer reading). | `resizing_obeys_sheet_protection` — asserts the *edit path* consults the rule, after a mutation showed that asserting the rule alone stays green |
| UX-SHIFT-01 | The destructive-shift confirmation fails open | Done | P1 | `session_shift_affects_formulas` is probed inside a swallowing catch whose fallback is `false` — so a probe that throws asserts the *safe* answer and the warning is skipped for a shift about to break formulas. The fix is one line (start `risky` at `true`) and was written and reverted: three attempts at a browser gate failed on locator resolution in the grid's context menu, and landing an ungated behaviour change is not how this repository works. Next session: gate it, then land it. **Done, and the gate is what took the thinking.** The three failed attempts all drove the grid's context menu and died on locator resolution in the submenu — they were testing Playwright, not the editor. The decision is now a named export, `shiftIsRisky(probe)`, taking the probe as a callback, so the browser gate imports the editor module and calls the **real** function with a probe it controls: no menu, no locators, and the failing case is reachable without a wasm module that can be made to throw on demand. Four cases: a throwing probe warns, a `true` probe warns, a `false` probe does not, and `undefined` is coerced rather than reaching the branch as a truthy-ish nothing. Seen red both ways — restoring the `false` fallback fails exactly one test, the one that owns the defect; making it always warn fails both controls, which is what stops the fix being "warn about everything" and getting trained away in a day. Full browser suite re-run: 51 passed. | A failing probe warns rather than proceeding |
| UX-END-01 | Excel's End mode does not exist | Done | P1 | `End` jumped to the last used column — which is Excel's *`End` then `Right`* — so the destination existed while the mode it belongs to did not, and the two-key sequence a spreadsheet user has in their fingers did nothing at all. It is how a keyboard user crosses a large sheet without holding a modifier. Found by auditing `docs/48` against the code rather than reading it: of 35 P0/P1 pipeline items, this was one of only two genuine gaps. **`End` now arms the mode and moves nothing**, an arrow spends it on a block jump, `End`+`Home` is the last used cell, and any other key cancels it — an armed mode that survives an unrelated keystroke fires on an arrow the user has forgotten arming. Announced in the status bar, because an invisible armed mode makes the next keystroke behave differently for no visible reason. Six tests, four mutations red: `End` back to jumping, the mode never spent, every arrow jumping, and nothing cancelling it. | End then an arrow jumps by block |
| UX-TOKEN-01 | No elevation or surface layer in the design tokens | Done | P2 | The other gap the `docs/48` audit found (`M10-1`). 24 semantic colour tokens exist — accent, border, text, selection, popover — and the chrome reads them, so the canvas and the CSS already agree about colour. What is absent is the *elevation and surface* layer the design system asks for: shadows, raised/sunken surfaces and state layers are still written inline per component, so a popover, a menu and a dialog each decide their own depth. Cosmetic rather than behavioural, which is why it is P2 — but it is the reason the chrome looks assembled rather than designed in places. **Done, and the interesting half was not cosmetic.** Chasing the elevation layer turned up two things a user can see: the focus ring and the text selection were written as `rgba(47, 109, 246, …)` — the *default accent*, spelled out — so a host that set a brand accent got brand-coloured buttons and **blue focus rings**. Invisible to us because our own accent is that blue, which is how this class always hides. And `dangerColor` sat in the **published** `THEME_TOKENS` list while `--oc-danger-color` appeared nowhere in any stylesheet: a host set it through the documented API and nothing happened, with no error to say why. Two different reds were hand-written instead. Both now derive from their tokens with `color-mix`, which already had precedent here. Elevation is two steps — `raised` is attached to the page, `overlay` floats above it — aliasing the component-named shadows rather than renaming them, because `controlShadow` and `popoverShadow` are in the published contract. Four hand-written overlay shadows became one, and are theme-aware for the first time; a fixed `rgba(0,0,0,…)` reads wrong on a dark surface. Five new tokens published and typed. Gated twice: `tools/check-theme-tokens.py` in CI asserts every advertised token is defined or referenced — red on the defect exactly as it was — and four browser tests assert the *computed* values, so `color-mix` and the token chain are exercised rather than assumed. Six mutations red in all. | Elevation and surface are tokens, and every advertised token does something |
| RND-08 | The canvas still draws its own data bar | Done | P2 | RND-07 gave the headless renderer a `DataBar` item; the editor canvas still draws its own from a per-cell `bar`/`barc` payload. They agree only because the geometry and alpha were copied across — which is the two-renderers-can-disagree shape `conditional.rs`'s own module doc argues against, reintroduced one layer up. **The stated acceptance was wrong about the size of the job, and is changed here deliberately rather than quietly.** "The canvas consumes `PaintItem::DataBar`" needs a display list across the WebAssembly boundary, and there is none — building one is an architectural change, not the P2 tidy-up this row reads as. What the row is *about* is the two renderers being able to disagree, and that is removable completely and cheaply: the inset, the alpha and the default colour were written out twice, in `casual-calc-render` and in `editor.js`, agreeing only because somebody had copied them across. `data_bar_style()` now exports them and the canvas reads them. **The canvas still paints; it no longer decides.** `default_data_bar()` parses the same exported constant, so the two blues cannot drift either. Gated where it counts: a browser test measures the *painted pixels* and compares them against what the engine exports — comparing the export against itself would pass with the canvas hardcoding whatever it liked. Proven by moving the engine to `padX = 4` while reverting the canvas to its old literal `1`: `the bar starts 1px into the cell; the engine says 4px`. Plus two Rust tests, one of which (the exported colour parsing to the drawn colour) is the one place the export cannot prevent drift by construction. **Still open, and now the honest version of the original idea:** a display list on the JS side would let the canvas and the PNG share the *drawing* as well as the numbers. That is `RND-10`. | The canvas draws from the engine's geometry, checked in pixels |
| RND-10 | No display list across the WebAssembly boundary | Done | P2 | The canvas painted charts from its own JavaScript while the PNG renderer painted them from `casual_calc_layout::chart::push_chart` — two implementations of one picture, so every fix landed twice and a divergence between them was invisible until somebody compared a screen against an export. Both arms are guarded now, checked under both feature configurations. **The legend now draws too, which closes this.** Built to the owner's decision: a **font-metrics crate below layout**, not a capability trait (ADR-019 rejected that). New `casual-calc-text` holds the bundled faces (moved from `casual-calc-render`), the substitution table (moved *down* from `casual-calc-layout`, because the faces need it and it needs nothing — it is still re-exported from layout, so `casual_calc_layout::substitute` is unchanged for every caller), and `advance_width`. Measured: 7,547,464 → 7,553,213 bytes, +0.08%, which is the legend code. **Seen red without the fix**: forcing the legend back to `None` fails 5 of 6 layout tests and the render pixel test with *"the pie still reaches where it did without a legend"*. The sixth is the too-small-frame guard, which is correctly unchanged either way. **What is honest about the result**: layout measures the *bundled* face and the canvas measures the browser's `system-ui`, so the plot rectangles differ slightly. That is the same disagreement those two have had about every other string since the PNG path existed, and it replaces an error the width of the whole legend. | Pixel assertions at named coordinates — a reversed pie sweep puts accent 4 at (250,48) where accent 1 belongs, and a doughnut with `inner_radius: 0` fails as "a doughnut with no hole is a pie"; both seen red here |
| RND-12 | Only PNG is decoded; every other picture format is named, not drawn | Done | P2 | `RND-06` decodes PNG because tiny-skia already could. jpeg, gif, bmp, tiff, webp, emf, wmf and svg are sniffed by magic number and reported as `UnsupportedFormat`, which honours the no-silent-loss rule but leaves a real gap: **the browser draws all of them**, so the canvas and the headless render disagree about the same document. Behind a `raster` feature, on by default and off for WebAssembly, the bundle is **byte-identical** to before (7,553,213). That is not the "fidelity split by deployment" the owner rejected — it is dead code removed from the one build that cannot run it. **A regression the feature gate introduced, caught by running both configurations.** The first draft moved the *format names* inside the feature along with the decoding, so a build without decoders called a JPEG "an unrecognised format" — a no-silent-loss report quietly getting worse because a codec was not compiled in. The name table is unconditional now and `a_jpeg_is_still_named_when_the_decoders_are_compiled_out` holds it there. Same class as `DOC-031` and `RND-11`: a second feature configuration is a second program, and only running it finds this. **The bomb seam.** Dimensions are read from the header *before* any allocation, as the PNG path already did, plus `image`'s own `Limits` because a header can lie — and `MAX_IMAGE_SIDE`, since 16,000,000x1 is inside `MAX_IMAGE_PIXELS` and is not a picture. **Seen red without the fix**, twice: removing the decode branch fails 3 tests; *flipping the decoded image vertically* — the classic BMP bug, since BMP stores rows bottom-up — fails the quadrant assertions with `top-left: pixel (48,15) is (0,0,255), expected (255,0,0)`. "It decoded" and "it is not blank" both pass against that. **Fixtures are third-party by design**: `sips` (Apple ImageIO) for jpeg/gif/bmp/tiff and `cwebp` (Google's reference encoder) for webp. A fixture made by the crate that decodes it proves only that the crate agrees with itself. | A JPEG in a workbook renders headlessly, or the decision to refuse it is an Accepted ADR |

Its own comment admitted the frame was a guess, on the reasonable grounds that a chart drawn a column out beats one not drawn — true for a chart, which redraws itself into whatever box it lands in, and false for a **picture**, which is scaled to fill its frame. A guessed frame is a fabricated aspect ratio, so every one-cell-anchored photograph rendered visibly squashed. `<xdr:ext>` is now read and carried to `ImageView::extent` (additive by ADR-010 — `SCHEMA_VERSION` is still 1 and verified so), and layout uses the authored size where the file states one, falling back to the measured rectangle for a two-cell anchor whose `<xdr:to>` *is* the picture's corner — including a distortion the author made by dragging a handle, which must be reproduced rather than corrected. **The second version of the guard test could not fail either:** it fed an ordinary two-cell anchor, and a real one has no `<xdr:ext>` at all, so removing the guard changed nothing. Fixed by indexing the retained parts as an `ImageSource` so `render_sheet_png` draws pictures at its existing signature, plus `render_sheet_png_with_report` and `WorkbookSession::render_png_with_report` for the half the old signature cannot carry. **`ImageReport` is folded into `CompatibilityReport`** using the `UndrawnReason::feature()` keys the render crate had already provided for exactly that, so a host gets one answer about what a document lost instead of two shapes it must merge itself. **Third instance of one pattern this round** — with `SEC-017` and `WASM-01` — and the cause is the same each time: a capability whose tests all live in the crate that implements it is fully green while reaching no caller. | PERF-11 | A filled-down column still costs N ASTs | Done | P1 | docs/40 promises more than dedup: "a filled-down column of the *same* relative formula shares one AST plus a per-cell origin". PERF-09 shares *identical* ASTs, and fill-down produces different ones — the references shift, so `A1*2` and `A2*2` are distinct trees. Sharing them needs relative references in the AST and an origin per cell, which changes the parser, the evaluator, the shifter, import's shared-formula path and export. Architectural, and deliberately not folded into a row about deduplication. **Designed in [75](75-RELATIVE-FORMULA-SHARING-DESIGN.md); not started.** Two things came out of writing it. **The per-cell origin docs/40 asks for does not need storing** — the origin *is* the cell's own address, which the sheet already knows, so this is a pure memory win with nothing added per cell. And **the relativity has to be in the type, not a convention**: after normalising, a reader holding a stored formula gets a delta where it used to get an address, and a single missed call site computes a plausible wrong answer in a spreadsheet, silently. Distinct `StoredRef`/`ResolvedRef` types make the compiler find all of them. The note also names the interaction that will break first — a cut must travel verbatim (`UX-CUT-03`), so it has to resolve at the old origin and re-store at the new one — and states what would make the work not worth doing. **Stage 1 of 4 landed: the types, unused.** `StoredRef` (relative unless anchored), `ResolvedRef` (an address) and `Origin`, with `store`/`resolve` between them and lossless conversion to the existing `CellReference` so stage 2 can move the evaluator without changing meaning. Nothing consumes them yet — that is the point of staging it this way, and it is why this stage is safe to land on its own. Eight tests; four mutations red, including the two that matter most in opposite directions: anchoring ignored (so `$A$1` drifts down a filled column) and every shape collapsing to one (which would make the memory figures excellent and the documents wrong). Off-sheet resolves to `None` rather than wrapping — one column left of A is `#REF!` in Excel, not column 4294967295. **Stage 3 started and stopped, on a blocker the design missed — which is the finding.** `Workbook::formulas: Vec<Expr>` is **serialised**, into snapshots that are durable *and* on the wire (`casual-calc-collab-server` holds one per document and hands it to joining clients). The design lists seven readers — parser, evaluator, shifter, import, export, structural rewrite, cut — and not this one.
