A relative `A1` writes today as `{"reference":{"col":0,"row":0}}`, bare `col`/`row` with the anchor flags skipped when false; the moment `Expr` holds a `StoredRef` of that shape, the same JSON reads back as **offset zero — the cell itself**. An existing `B1 = A1*2` returns as `B1 = B1*2`, a circular reference: **every relative reference in every saved document, silently**, with `ADR-010` closing the usual escape by forbidding a `SCHEMA_VERSION` bump. **Proved, not reasoned about**: adding the derive and feeding a real serialised formula back through it is two lines, and it was written before the claim was made. **Owner's decision: the format does not change.** Relativity is an in-memory representation only — resolved to absolute per cell on write, stored relative at the cell's origin on read. No migration, no version bump, mixed-version clusters keep working. The cost is stated rather than hidden: snapshot and collaboration payloads keep their present size, and **memory** — the thing this row is for — is what improves. **Two decisions locked.** The AST is one `Expr` holding `StoredRef` with entry points taking an `Origin`, not a generic `Expr<R>`: the compiler still finds every call site, since `.row` stops being a `u32` address, so the mitigation survives without a type parameter in every signature. And a **new stage 2a** — move the arena's serialisation from `Expr` up to `Workbook`, the only place that knows which origin belongs to which tree — lands before stage 3, gated on a snapshot reading back byte-identical. **One thing turned out cheaper**: `Evaluator` already carries `current`, the cell being evaluated, saved and restored per formula for `ROW()`/`COLUMN()`. The origin stage 2 was going to thread is already there and already correct. **Then measured, because the design says measuring is the cheaper thing to do and the blocker had just raised the price.** The benchmark reports an `arena` block — nodes times `size_of::<Expr>()`, counted rather than sampled, so it works on a machine with no `/proc`. A 5 000-cell filled `=A1*2` column holds **5 000 distinct trees** (confirming directly that `PERF-09` collapses none of it), 15 000 nodes, **240 arena bytes per cell** against a cell's own ~83. So three quarters of a filled column is tree, and nine tenths for a nine-node `IF(…)`. **The win is not theoretical**, and the number is now reproducible and pinned by a test that fails the moment a filled column starts sharing — which is to say when stage 3 lands. **Stage 1b landed: the compatibility story, proved.** `StoredRef` now carries `CellReference`'s **exact** encoding — same camelCase, same `skip_serializing_if`, same `deny_unknown_fields` — and `ABSOLUTE` names the origin `(0, 0)` at which storing subtracts nothing, so a stored reference's `col`/`row` *are* the address. Two tests hold the invariant the whole compatibility design rests on: a reference stored at the zero origin serialises byte-identically to the absolute one, across all six shapes that differ in encoding (plain, each axis anchored, sheet-qualified, whole-column, whole-row), and reads back meaning the same thing. **Seen red** by dropping one `skip_serializing_if`. `StoredRef::absolute`/`as_absolute`/`at` are the conversions the parser, printer and snapshot will each go through. **What that buys**: the snapshot never has to change, so there is no migration and no `SCHEMA_VERSION` move — which was the blocker found last round — and it is now a tested property rather than an argument. **What stage 3 still needs, scoped precisely.** Swapping `Expr`'s payload compiles down to three semantic decisions and about a hundred literal test constructions, not just a type change: **printing becomes origin-dependent** (the same shared tree reads `A1*2` in `B1` and `A2*2` in `B2`, so `Display` becomes the absolute case and a `print_at` carries the origin — done and reverted here, since it cannot land without the swap); **`shift_references` becomes a no-op for copy and fill**, because resolving at the new origin *is* the shift, leaving it needed only for structural inserts; and **off-sheet stops clamping**, since `resolve` answers `None` where the old `shift_ref` clamped at zero — `#REF!` is Excel's answer and the more correct one, but it is a behaviour change with tests to match. **The swap was then carried through five crates and reverted, on a second gap the design misses.** What worked: `casual-calc-formula` fully migrated (71 tests, including the new semantics — copy shifts *by being read elsewhere*, `restore_at` replaces `shift_references` for moves, off-sheet is `#REF!` rather than a clamp); `casual-calc-layout` (a chart's series reference has no holding cell, so it resolves at `ABSOLUTE`); `casual-calc-model` (`store_formula_at`); `casual-calc-eval` — evaluator, dependency graph and `functions.rs` all threading the origin, where **`Evaluator.current` turned out to already be it**; and `casual-calc-import`, where the shared-formula path becomes the *ideal* case: `<f t="shared">` means precisely "one tree, many cells", so a whole shared group now lands on one tree with no shift at all. **A defect in the landed stage-1 types, found by an existing test**: `store`/`resolve` carried over `shift_ref`'s rule for `$`-anchored axes but not its rule for **unnamed** ones, so `A:A` read six rows down came out `A6:#REF!`. Fixed and tested — and the test that caught it is one that already existed, asserting the property rather than the mechanism, which is why it survived the mechanism being replaced. **Where it stopped: `structural.rs` moves the cells and *then* rewrites the formulas.** Under absolute storage the order is irrelevant; under relative storage it decides the answer. `B5 = A2*2` with a row inserted at 3 moves to `B6`, and its offset — three rows up, measured from `B5` — resolves at `B6` to `A3`, while `A2` never moved. The rewrite exists to correct exactly that and cannot: `job.at` is the **new** address and the offsets were taken from the **old** one, which has already been discarded. Either the rewrite runs before the move, or it is given both origins. **Resolved by working the cases out, and neither candidate answer was right.** Rewriting before the move does not help: for `B5 = A2*2` with a row inserted at 3, the rewrite correctly leaves the tree alone (the *reference* is at row 2, before the insertion), and then the move to `B6` turns the untouched offset into `A3` anyway. What changed the meaning was the **move**, not the rewrite. So **the move accounts for itself**: a cell carrying a relative tree that moves is doing what a *cut* does, and `restore_at` is already the primitive — `shift_cells_insert`/`_delete` re-store any formula they move, from old origin to new. The existing address-based rewrite then runs afterwards **completely unchanged**. The two compose: the move keeps a tree's meaning invariant, the rewrite applies the change the insertion implies. `B5 = A5*2` → `A6` (shifted, 5 ≥ 3); `B5 = A2*2` → `A2` (untouched, 2 < 3); both Excel's answers. **The order the code already has is the correct one, and there is no unknown left**: `restore_at` exists and is tested, and the rewrite needs no change. **Done. `Expr` holds `StoredRef`, and a filled-down column is one tree.** Measured by the benchmark that was built to answer it: 5 000 cells, **5 000 distinct trees → 1**, 15 000 nodes → **3**, **240 arena bytes per cell → 0.05**. The pinning test written to fail at exactly this moment did, and is now inverted to assert the arena **does not grow with the column** — stronger than any per-cell figure and independent of how many cells are measured. **The format did not move**, which was the blocker's whole resolution: one tree in memory, three *absolute* references on the wire, asserted as the literal JSON text rather than against this build's own output so it cannot pass by both sides drifting together. No migration, no `SCHEMA_VERSION` change, mixed-version clusters unaffected. **Two honest costs.** `size_of::<Expr>()` is **80 → 96 bytes**, because a `StoredRef` carries `i64` offsets where a `CellReference` carried `u32` addresses — so an *unshared* formula costs 20% more, and only sharing pays that back. And the WebAssembly bundle grew **7,547,191 → 7,597,111 (+0.66%)**. **The pair inverted, exactly as `75` predicted.** Copy and fill now rewrite **nothing** — resolving a shared tree at the destination *is* the shift — while a cut, a row insert and a row sort must `restore_at`, because moving a cell changes what its offsets mean. `shift_references` is deleted. The structural rewrite is **unchanged**: callers convert to the absolute form at the boundary and back, which is where "is this reference past the insertion point" remains a question about addresses. **`<f t="shared">` became the ideal case**: the format already means "one tree, many cells", so an imported shared group now lands on one tree with no shift at all. **A defect the type change did *not* catch.** `OFFSET` computed `base.row + rows` — an **offset** used as an address. Both are integers, so it compiled; the test caught it. That is worth recording against the design's own claim that putting relativity in the type finds every site: it finds every site where the types differ, and `i64 + i64` is not one. **A side effect worth naming**: a snapshot round trip now reclaims trees no cell references, because the conversion is driven by the cells and an orphan has no origin. `store_formula`'s own comment had lamented that nothing reclaimed. Tested. **Verified**: 1 393 workspace tests, 129 browser tests, 13/13 repository gates, 5/5 compiler gates. **And it still broke `oracle-diff` on main, which is the finding.** `cargo test --workspace` is green because the fidelity tool has no tests of its own — it *is* the test, and it only runs where LibreOffice does. Two things in it stored formulas absolutely and were missed: the **corpus harness**, which places each line on its own row, so a formula on row 5 reading `C1:C10` resolved to `C6:C15` and every lookup came back empty against an oracle that had values; and `describe_cell`, which rendered `={expr}` **absolutely** in the comparison report. The second is the worse one — it is not cosmetic. Two *different* formulas can both render `#REF!` and compare equal, so the check quietly stops catching what it exists for. **The lesson is about which gates a change reaches.** `check-rust.py` runs the compiler gates and `check-all.py` the repository ones; neither runs the oracle, because it needs LibreOffice. A change to formula *semantics* is exactly the class that only the oracle sees, and the two runners gave a green that meant less than it looked. LibreOffice **is** available on this machine — `cargo run -p casual-calc-fidelity` reproduced the failure in one command, before and after. **`check-rust.py` now runs it**, so the gap closes rather than being remembered: three oracle steps after the compiler gates, **skipped loudly** when LibreOffice is absent (a gate that quietly does nothing is how four counters reported zero while being scraped — `SRV-05`) and skipped when a compiler gate already failed, since there is no point asking the oracle about a tree that does not build. Proved by reintroducing this exact regression: `FAIL oracle: corpus`, while package and ods stay green. **Seen red**: making `store_formula_at` skip the re-store fails both sharing tests *and* 82 evaluator tests.  The design says a half-migrated codebase is worse than not starting, and starting the migration before the serialisation question was settled is precisely how that happens. | N filled-down cells hold one AST |
| PERF-10 | Resident memory is still not measured | Done | P2 | The row's own reasoning was right and is what held this open: an uncalibrated gate is worse than none, no machine here has `/proc`, so the threshold could only come from the runner. It now has, from **thirteen successful CI runs**: 82.32 x2, 82.41 x2, 83.64 x4, 83.72 x5 — a spread of 1.40 bytes, or **1.7%**. The measurement is very nearly deterministic, which is what makes an absolute ceiling honest rather than a guess dressed as one. **Ceiling 88**: 4.28 bytes over the highest ever seen, so 3.1x the observed spread and it will not flake, while still catching one extra pointer per cell (+8 bytes to 91.7) — the smallest change that would actually matter. **Floor 70**, and that half is the one worth explaining: a sudden *drop* is far more likely to mean the measurement broke than that the engine got 16% smaller, and a gate with only a ceiling passes forever once it starts measuring the wrong thing. That is precisely how four counters on this project reported zero while being scraped (`SRV-05`). Verified by extracting the step's shell and running it against synthetic reports rather than by reading it. | A cell costs between 70 and 88 bytes on the runner; driven with 82.32, 83.72, 88.00, 88.01, 69.99 and 91.70 and each answered correctly |
| PERF-09 | No AST interning | Done | P1 | `store_formula` appended unconditionally. The row named the duplicate cost; the larger problem was unnamed — **nothing reclaimed**, so every edit replacing a formula orphaned the old AST and a person editing one cell back and forth grew the arena once per edit for the session's life. A thousand edits between two formulas left a thousand ASTs. Now interned by fingerprint, with equality deciding, so the index does not hold a second copy of every AST. The index is derived state and not serialised, so it is rebuilt on load — without which a round trip silently restored the old behaviour. | `editing_one_cell_does_not_grow_the_arena_forever`, `interning_survives_a_snapshot_round_trip`, `a_colliding_fingerprint_does_not_return_the_wrong_formula` |
| PERF-07 | Cold and adversarial recalc unproven | Done | P1 | Worse than the row said: the <50 ms cap was asserted **nowhere**. Every recalc case was a scaling *ratio* — how the work grows, never how long it takes — while docs/30 calls T3 a hard cap and says a PR past it fails CI. Two absolute cases now: the cold first edit, with the precedent graph built inside the clock, and the adversarial sheet docs/30 defines — a 5,000-deep chain and a 5,000-wide fan-out, edited at the chain's root, which is also what the fan reads, so one keystroke dirties depth and breadth at once. Gated on the **worst** sample, not the median: T3 says worst-case, and a median hides the tail a person actually complains about. | `recalc-cold-first-edit` 2.90 ms and `recalc-adversarial-chain-and-fanout` 3.22 ms against the 50 ms cap, release |
| PERF-08 | Memory gate measures payload, not RSS | Done | P1 | Three things, each a gate that ran and passed without gating what it described. (1) **CI measured a debug build** — the same frame is 0.13 ms in release and 5.7 ms in debug, so a "sixty frames a second" gate was applied to a binary nobody ships; the job now runs `--release` and the report carries `profile`, which CI asserts. (2) The frame ceiling was **four whole frames**, enforcing fifteen fps against a comment and a doc that both said sixty; it is now docs/30's engine-side working budget (≤8 ms of a 16.6 ms frame), 65× above the release measurement. (3) The memory gate was `size_of` × a target — arithmetic that passes unchanged if the store boxes values or cannot reach a million. A million cells are now built. RSS remains unmeasured (PERF-10). | `the_frame_budget_is_the_engine_side_working_budget`, `a_million_cells_can_actually_be_held`, and CI rejecting a `profile: debug` report |
| UX-FREEZE-01 | A freeze can only be made from a menu, never by dragging | Done | P2 | `freezeHit` matches the divider, and the divider is drawn at the freeze line — so with nothing frozen there is nothing to grab. Every drag gesture operated on a freeze somebody had already made from the menu, and no gesture made one, which is the affordance a user reaches for first. Two corner handles now start the drag; they live inside the select-all box because a grab zone on the body's leading edge would sit on column A and swallow selection clicks. | `editor.grid-handles.spec.mjs` — dragging the corner handle freezes columns |
| UX-HIDE-01 | Reported: a hidden band cannot be revealed from its handle | Dropped | P2 | Reported by the user, and **not reproduced**: `mousedown` already checks `hiddenMarkAt` before the resize boundary, and a browser gate now shows one click on the handle revealing the column. A first reading of the handler missed the existing check and nearly added a duplicate. Kept as a row because the report was real — if it recurs the gate names the case that does work, which narrows what to look for. | `editor.grid-handles.spec.mjs` — a hidden column is revealed by clicking its handle |
| UX-CUT-03 | A cut does not repoint formulas that referenced it | Done | P1 | Excel rewrites every other formula pointing at cut cells so it follows them. Only the moved cells' own formulas are handled. | A formula referencing a cut cell follows it Confirmed independently by the docs/46 audit (CP-040), which adds the reason it stays unnoticed: the visible parts — marching ants, the source cleared in one undo step, Esc disarming — all work, so a cut *looks* complete while every other formula keeps pointing at the old addresses. **Fixed.** `repointed_after_move` in `casual-calc-transaction::structural` decides which references named a moved cell, reusing the qualified/unqualified home-sheet rule the insert/delete rewrite already uses so the two cannot disagree about what "this sheet" means. The wasm paste path folds the rewrites into the cut's own op batch, ahead of the paste, so the move stays one undo step and a repointed cell the paste also lands on keeps the pasted content. `$` anchoring does not exempt a reference: it governs what a *copy* does, not whether the cell named may move. A range only partly inside the block is left alone -- moving one endpoint would silently resize somebody's range. Gated by two tests in `casual-calc-wasm`, each seen red under five mutations (repoint disabled; block bounds dropped; `$` treated as exempt; sheet-reaching forced true; qualified references ignored) with every failure landing on the assertion that owns it. |
| UX-CUT-04 | A cut does not repoint the defined names that pointed at it | Done | P1 | Sibling of `FID-24`, which made an insert or a delete shift defined names. A cut left them behind, so `Rate` went on meaning `$A$1` after `$A$1` had gone to `G6` and every `=Rate` silently read a different cell. Worse than the formula case it was found beside (`UX-CUT-03`): a name is the indirection people reach for *so that* they need not track addresses, and nothing on screen shows a name has gone stale. Found by probing the paste path for `defined_name` after fixing UX-CUT-03 and getting zero hits. **Fixed.** `defined_names_after_move` reuses the same expression rewrite and the same sheet-scoped/workbook-scoped home rule as `FID-24`, and the wasm paste path pushes the result as `Operation::SetDefinedNames` -- which inverts -- into the cut's own batch, so the move stays one undo step. | Gated in `casual-calc-wasm`, seen red under three mutations: repoint disabled, block bounds dropped (the untouched-name control), and the op applied outside the batch (the undo-step claim). |
| COL-40 | Owner cannot change access or lock a live document | Done | P1 | A session-scoped override on top of the token, never instead of it (docs/72). The safety property is that it may only ever **reduce**: the enforcement point takes the more restrictive of token and override, so a compromised client cannot promote itself and the token stays the ceiling — granting more means minting a token, which involves the system of record. Who may set it is a separate `owner` claim, not inferred from `Access::Edit`, because every editor being able to lock out every other one is a different feature and a worse one. Ephemeral by ADR-012: an evicted document returns with the token's own permissions. The affected participant is told in words rather than discovering it when a keystroke stops working. | `an_override_cannot_raise_access_above_the_token`, `an_editor_who_is_not_an_owner_cannot_reduce_anyone`, `an_owner_can_reduce_another_participants_access` |
| COL-32 | Filter sharing and personal views | Done | P1 | Built to [71](71-FILTER-SHARING-AND-VIEWS.md). A personal view lives on `WorkbookSession`, never on `Sheet`, and that placement **is** the design: `SUBTOTAL`'s 101-111 codes and `AGGREGATE` reach the sheet, so anything stored there is shared by definition, and a personal view that moved a subtotal would let two participants hold different numbers for one cell - which makes the convergence property every other part of the collaboration design assumes simply false. Two questions that look identical and are not: the layout asks the session and gets shared union personal; the evaluator asks the sheet and gets shared only. The engine computes *which* rows a value-set hides (`session_rows_hidden_by_values`) using the same helpers the shared path uses, so the two cannot disagree about the same tick-boxes - only where the answer is stored differs. The scope choice defaults to **shared**, because that is what a spreadsheet has always done and the only kind `.xlsx` can express. **Two things learned in the building:** the file format has no separate notion of a filter-hidden row - ECMA-376 stores one as `<row hidden="1">` - so a shared filter returns from a round trip in `hidden_rows`, and the first version of the save test asserted on `filter_hidden` and failed. And a view keyed by sheet index needs resequencing on insert, remove and move, or it silently goes on hiding rows on whichever sheet inherits the number, with nothing on the wire to explain it and nothing in the history to undo. Named, shareable view *definitions* remain out of scope, as 71 says: that is a document-level list, which is a new operation, which is an ADR trigger. | Browser: a personal filter hides rows and moves no value; clearing restores them; the scope choice defaults to shared. Rust: nothing relayed, nothing in the history, nothing saved, subtotal unmoved, one session's view invisible to another |
| UX-FOCUS-01 | The grid never takes the keyboard until it is clicked | Done | P1 | Reported from a running Docker stack: Cmd+A and Cmd+X "not supported". Both **were** implemented — `k === "a"` calls `ctrlA()`, `k === "x"` calls `doCut()`, and the modifier test is `e.ctrlKey \|\| e.metaKey`, so Cmd was handled everywhere. The handler is bound on `<canvas id="grid">`, and **nothing focused it at startup**: the editor opened with `document.activeElement === body`, so every grid shortcut — select-all, cut, copy, the arrows, Ctrl+Arrow, the lot — did nothing until the user happened to click a cell. On macOS Cmd+A then ran the *browser's* select-all over the page chrome, which is exactly what it looked like from outside. Fixed by focusing the canvas at the end of `main()`, and **only when nothing else holds focus** — an embed lives in somebody else's page and a component that steals focus on load moves the caret out of whatever the person was typing in. **Seen red without the fix**: `activeElement` is `BODY#`, Cmd+A leaves the selection at `{r0:0,c0:0,r1:0,c1:0}`, and Cmd+X leaves the status line reading `engine v0.0.0` instead of `cut`. **What made it invisible**: 53 `canvas.focus()` calls existed to put focus *back* after every dialog, menu and colour picker — so focus was correct in every state the tests drove, because every one of those tests clicked the grid first. The missing call was the one that puts focus there to begin with, and no test started from a keyboard. | The grid answers a shortcut on a freshly opened document, with no click first |
| FID-25 | Four more position-indexed things a row insert never moved | Done | P1 | Found by driving the editor rather than by reading it: insert a row above a validated block and the rule went on policing the rows the data had **left**. `shift_metadata_insert` moved merges, the autofilter, filter-hidden rows, sizing, hidden lines, the freeze boundary, the outline and — since the table fix — tables. The sheet also holds **validations, conditional formats, comments and hyperlinks**, every one a range or an address, and none of them moved. **The same shape as the table defect and just as quiet**: no error, no compatibility report, a saved file that looks right. The consequences are worse than losing them — the row that moved *out* keeps the constraint and the row that moved *in* is unguarded, so a person typing in the cell they just created is refused and the cell they meant is not; and a comment left behind annotates whatever moved into its row, which reads as though it belongs. **They were in the metadata bundle already**, so a delete has always restored them; what was missing was only the *shift*. That is why the fix goes in `Positional` beside merges rather than next to it — the trait exists because "a shift implemented twice is a shift that disagrees with itself the first time a field is added", and this is that field, four times. Ranges clamp-or-drop on delete exactly as a merge does; a comment names one cell and goes with its row. **Seen red before the fix**: `left: (4, 7), right: (5, 8)` — the validation on the rows its data left. Six tests, including that undo restores a validation a delete swallowed, which is the half that proves the bundle was already doing its job. **Still open, and filed rather than fixed here**: `charts` and `pivots` are position-indexed too and are *not* in the bundle — a chart's series is a reference **string**, so moving it means parsing and re-emitting rather than shifting a `CellRef`. Confirmed by probe: a chart's series stayed on `$D$2:$D$11` across an insert. `FID-26`. | A validation, highlight, comment and link move with their rows, and a delete's undo restores them |
| FID-26 | A chart's series and a pivot's ranges never move either | Done | P2 | The other half of `FID-25`. `charts`, `images` and `pivots` were the last three position-indexed collections `structural.rs` never touched. **Confirmed by probe**: insert a row above a chart's data and the series stayed on `$D$2:$D$11` and the frame did not move, so the chart plotted the wrong rows from the wrong place, silently. Three different shapes, so three mechanisms. A chart's and an image's **frame** is a range and moves like a merge. A chart's **series** is a reference *string*, so it is parsed, put through the same `rewrite_expr` cells and defined names already use, and printed back — text that will not parse is left alone, on the rule `Expr::Raw` already follows, because a reference that cannot be understood cannot be shifted without guessing and a guess here corrupts a chart. A **pivot** splits: `anchor` and `output` are on the sheet holding it, `source` is on `source_sheet` and very often another sheet entirely, so the source shift runs at workbook level where the two identities can actually be compared. Frames and pivot blocks went on the `Positional` trait, so the OT transform path gets them too; images and pivot sources could not, and the reason is recorded on the trait rather than left to be rediscovered — `SheetMetadata` has no images and no sheet identity, and a trait method one implementor cannot answer honestly is a lie. A lost `output` clears to `None` instead of dropping the pivot: the definition is still valid and a refresh will write a new extent, where dropping it would delete a report the user can still see. **Complete for an authored chart, partial for an imported one** — see `FID-27`. | `insert_rows_moves_charts_images_and_pivots`, `insert_rows_moves_a_pivot_source_and_output_independently`, `delete_rows_clamps_charts_and_images_and_breaks_lost_series`, `deleting_a_pivot_output_band_keeps_the_pivot`, plus quoting and cross-sheet controls. Insert path seen red first; every delete-path behaviour then mutated one at a time and each fails its own assertion |
| FID-27 | An imported chart shifts on screen but not in the saved file | Done | P2 | `FID-26` shifted the model; a chart read from a file is written back from its **retained part**, so the picture moved and the saved bytes did not. **The design fork this row was filed to decide turned out not to exist.** It looked like a choice between breaking the *inert retained part* invariant and calling `detach` and losing the formatting retention exists to keep. Reading the writer settled it: export only ever *reads* `retained_parts` and emits new bytes — `strip_dangling_anchors` already parses and edits retained drawing XML on the way out — so the invariant is about the **model**, not about the bytes leaving the writer. There was a third option present in the codebase the whole time. The corrected part is emitted as a *generated* part at the same path, and the writer's existing "a part we also generated wins" rule does the rest. `RetainedPart` is never touched. Only the text inside a series' `<c:f>` changes — `<c:tx>` is left alone, because a series *name* is not a position — and an unchanged chart produces no generated part at all, so it stays byte-identical. **A test that passed first time and was wrong to.** The end-to-end case asserted that `<c:serAx>` and `<c:valAx>` survive, and the doc comment claimed this proved the matcher does not match on a prefix. Mutating the matcher to do exactly that left the test green: valid OOXML orders the axes outside the series, so ordering alone spares them. The claim was corrected and the guard given tests that do fail — matching `ser` inside `serAx`, and a closing tag matching `</c:serAx>` for `<c:ser>`. | `a_retained_chart_part_is_re_emitted_with_its_shifted_series` and `an_unshifted_retained_chart_part_is_written_back_unchanged`; the fix mutated away reproduces the defect verbatim (`$D$2:$D$11` still in the saved part). `chart::tests` covers whole-name matching, each seen red under its own mutation |
| FID-29 | An imported chart's *frame* still does not move in the saved file | Done | P3 | The remainder of `FID-27`. A chart's series live in its own part, which `ChartView::part` names outright; its **frame** lives in the retained *drawing*, where nothing names the chart. The link is the anchor's `r:id`, so matching an anchor to a modelled chart means resolving that id through the drawing's own relationships to a part path and comparing it with what the chart says it came from. Images work the same way through `r:embed` and their media part. Only the four cell coordinates change; offsets stay exactly as the file had them, because a row insert moves a frame by whole rows and rewriting the offsets would snap every edge that was ever dragged between gridlines. `oneCellAnchor` has an extent rather than a `<xdr:to>`, so only its corner moves; `absoluteAnchor` names no cells and is left alone. A media part placed twice gets a queue rather than a lookup, so the nth anchor naming it takes the nth frame. | `a_retained_drawing_anchor_follows_the_frame_the_model_holds`, seen red first with the file still saying `<xdr:row>4</xdr:row>` where the model said 6 |
| FID-30 | Deleting an imported chart makes the workbook refuse to save | Done | P1 | **Found while doing `FID-29`, and much worse than it.** Not a fidelity defect — a total failure to save. Deleting an imported chart leaves a dangling anchor; stripping it rebuilds the drawing, so the sheet contributes a drawing part with **no chart parts**. Two places then write that drawing's `.rels`: the chart builder writes it for every non-empty `drawing_part`, and the retained-relationship pass writes it for every source it has not already seen — but that pass only stepped aside for a build that had *chart parts*. With none, both fired, the package got two entries at one path, and the zip writer rejected the whole file with `Duplicate filename`. Having a chart part was never what caused the rels to be written; a non-empty `drawing_part` is. The guard now says so. **What made it invisible**: the enclosing comment 30 lines above already stated that a sheet can contribute a drawing with no chart parts — the guard simply did not match its own documentation, and no test exercised the combination. It surfaced only because `FID-29` made the drawing bytes change on a path that had nothing to do with charts. | `a_drawing_rebuilt_without_chart_parts_does_not_write_its_rels_twice`, seen red on **unmodified `main`** with `Package("invalid Zip archive: Duplicate filename")` |
| FID-31 | Twice now, an end-to-end test claimed to prove whole-name matching | Done | P3 | Worth its own row because it is a *method* defect, not a code one, and it recurred. Both the `FID-27` and the `FID-29` end-to-end tests asserted that a lookalike element survives — `<c:serAx>` beside `<c:ser>`, `<xdr:colOff>` beside `<xdr:col>` — and both doc comments claimed this proved element names are matched whole rather than as a prefix. Mutating the matcher to match on a prefix left **both** green. The reason is the same each time: prefix matching only bites when the **longer name comes first**, and valid OOXML writes the shorter one first in every case that appears — `col` before `colOff`, the axes outside the series. An end-to-end case built from realistic XML therefore *cannot* tell the two matchers apart, and reads as coverage while proving nothing. Both claims were corrected to say what the assertions actually cover, and the guard is now proved by direct cases written in the biting order, each isolated so an earlier assertion cannot shield it. | `chart::tests::a_frame_coordinate_is_not_read_out_of_its_offset` and siblings; under prefix matching the column reads as `12700</xdr:colOff><xdr:col>5` |
| FID-28 | A pending metadata bundle's series and pivot source do not transform | Done | P3 | **The row said this needed a wire change and a `PROTOCOL_VERSION` bump. It does not, and that claim was never checked.** A chart's series is a reference *string* and a pivot's `source` is on another sheet, so neither can be decided from the sheet *index* an operation carries — the conclusion drawn from that was that identity had to travel on the wire. But `transform` being a **pure function** does not mean it may not take more arguments; it means it may not go and look something up. Both its callers — `receive` and `commit` in `session.rs` — already have `&mut Workbook` in scope, four lines from the call. So they pass what the indices name, the wire is untouched, and `PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays at 6. The alternative that was nearly built instead — two new fields on each of four structural operations — would have meant ~200 construction sites and a protocol break, to carry data the caller already held. | `a_pending_chart_series_follows_a_concurrent_insert`; with the identities withheld it reinstates the pre-insert `S!$D$2:$D$11` |
| UX-THEME-02 | A filled cell's text is chosen against the theme, not the fill | Done | P1 | Reported from a running editor: *"font color change on dark light mode change but not bg of cell .. making it harder to see"*. Exactly right, and the asymmetry is the bug: a cell's **fill comes from the file**, so it is the same colour in either theme, while `cellFg` fell back to `colors.fg` — a *theme* token. Switching to dark mode therefore put near-white text on an authored pale fill and the contents vanished. `contrastInk` already existed, already computed WCAG relative luminance, and was already used for column headers — it simply was not consulted for cell text. An explicit font colour still wins: that pairing is the author's, and repainting it would be second-guessing somebody's formatting. | `editor.cell-contrast.spec.mjs`; reverted, a yellow fill takes the theme's `#0b0d12` instead of `#111418`, and follows the theme into dark mode |
| UX-RESIZE-01 | A column resize does not reflow its contents until the mouse is released | Done | P1 | Reported as *"not realtime .. untill i leave it .. than content rearrange .. not fluidly"*. Half of it was already live: the client overrides the dragged line's size in its own `geo`, so the **edge** moved. The **text** did not, because display text comes from the engine and the engine still held the old width — so the edge slid out from under stationary content and everything snapped on release. The obvious fix is the wrong one. `session_set_col_width` records an undoable transaction, so calling it per mouse-move would bury the undo stack and make one Ctrl+Z step back a single pixel. `session_preview_line_size` writes straight to the sheet instead — no transaction, no history, nothing relayed to a collaborator, because a drag nobody has finished is not an edit anyone else should see. The commit path restores the size the drag *started* from before recording the real edit, so undo inverts to that rather than to the last frame. | `editor.resize-live.spec.mjs` asserts both halves — the engine sees the width mid-drag, and nothing is undoable until release. Without the preview the width stays at 64 for the whole drag |
| UX-FREEZE-02 | A frozen pane looks like it cannot be extended or removed | Done | P3 | Reported as no way to extend or remove a frozen column. Both already existed — an **Unfreeze** item in the freeze menu, and `commitFreezeDrag` maps a drag back to the header as zero — so this was discoverability, not a missing feature. `FREEZE_GRAB` was **4px** against a 1px divider: under half a fingertip, so a deliberate aim missed more often than it caught, and the only reliable route was two clicks inside a popup nobody opens looking for it. 8px is the smallest that catches an aim without stealing clicks from the cell beside it — the divider sits on a gridline, so a much wider zone starts swallowing selections. | The existing freeze-drag gate still passes at the wider target |
| UX-CLIP-02 | A paste cannot carry column widths, even when asked | Done | P2 | Reported from a running stack: pasting from LibreOffice "doesn't keep width and height". **It does not, and `68` records that as a decision** — applying them on a plain paste silently reshapes the sheet somebody pasted *into*, and Excel's own `Ctrl+V` does not do it either. What Excel has and this did not is the **explicit** option, so there was no way to ask for the thing the person actually wanted. Paste Special gains **Column widths only**. The clip captures the source columns' widths **on copy**, not on paste: by then the source may have been resized, or after a cut may not be there at all. Only columns with an *explicit* width travel — a column sitting at the sheet default has no width to carry, and writing the default onto the destination would pin a column that had been following it. Widths and not heights: a row's height belongs to the row, and a paste that set those would resize rows the copied block merely passed through. **Seen red**: disabling the mode fails with *"the column width did not travel"*, while the two guards stay green — a plain paste still reshapes nothing, and a default-width source still overwrites nothing. That is the point of having all three: the feature and the two things it must **not** become. | Paste Special carries the source columns' widths, and a plain paste still does not |
| UX-EDIT-01 | The editor has no width breakpoint at all — *scroll mechanism superseded by `UX-MOB-01`* | Done | P2 | Found by checking the one surface nothing had ever measured at a phone width. `editor.css` has seven media queries and **not one of them is about width** — they are dark mode, reduced motion and contrast. `.menubar` and `.toolbar` are unwrapped flex rows, so on a 390px phone they ran past the edge and widened the document to **779px**: the page slid sideways, and half the toolbar could only be reached by dragging the whole editor across. Same at 768px. They scroll **within themselves** rather than wrapping. Wrapping changes the editor's height and every pixel of that comes out of the grid, which is the one part of a spreadsheet nobody wants smaller — Excel's answer on a narrow window is to collapse groups and then scroll, which is the step after the existing `.tb-collapsed` mechanism runs out of groups to fold. The scrollbar is hidden because these are chrome rather than content, and a visible track costs 8–15px of a 48px bar on platforms that reserve space for one. **A mutation that passed, and why.** The first attempt to prove the test flipped `overflow-x: auto` to `visible` — and everything stayed green, because `overflow-y: hidden` is still set beside it and CSS computes the other axis back to `auto` when one is not `visible`. The mutation had changed nothing. Removing the block outright is what actually reproduces it. A mutation has to be checked for having *happened* before its result means anything. | `editor.narrow-screens.spec.mjs`; without the block the document is 779px in both a 390px and a 768px window. It also asserts the toolbar can be scrolled to its far end — 401px of controls lie past the edge, and a *clipped* toolbar would report nothing unreachable just as a scrolling one does |
| UX-SITE-03 | The site scrolls sideways on every phone | Done | P1 | 763px of document in a 390px viewport on the landing page; 706px and 756px on `deploy` and `docs`. Every phone visitor got a page that slid horizontally. **`overflow-x: auto` was already on several of the `<pre>` blocks and never engaged.** A grid and flex item defaults to `min-width: auto` — *do not shrink below your content* — so the card grew, the row grew, and the scrollbar the `<pre>` was meant to gain never had a reason to appear. `min-width: 0` is what lets the overflow rule do its job. **Three separate mistakes on the way, each caught by measuring rather than reasoning.** (1) A catch-all `html, body { overflow-x: clip }` looked like sensible insurance; a clip makes the element a scroll container and I then measured the page as unable to scroll at all. That measurement was itself wrong — `scroll-behavior: smooth` means `scrollY` is not updated synchronously — so I nearly reverted a working page on a broken check, and then nearly kept a guard that hides overflow instead of fixing it. Removed: a guard that can silently disable the page is worse than the overflow. (2) The `min-width: 0` rule enumerated six selectors and missed `.doc`, so both documentation pages stayed broken after the 'fix'. (3) The `<pre>` rules named `.step pre`, `.code-card pre`, `.contrib pre` — and the blocks actually forcing the page wide were **bare** `<pre>`s with no class, matching none of them. Both are the same error: a rule that has to list its subjects is one omission away from being wrong. Tables scroll inside themselves rather than collapsing, because a documentation table's columns *are* the content. | `site.no-sideways-scroll.spec.mjs` — 3 pages × 3 widths. It asserts the document is no wider than the window **and** that anything still wider sits in something that can scroll it into view, because clipping the page would satisfy the first check while making the content unreachable |
| UX-SITE-02 | Dark mode shipped on one page of three | Done | P2 | `UX-SITE-01` added dark mode as a token swap and I verified it on the landing page — which is the only page whose styling lives *entirely* in `style.css`. `deploy.html` and `docs.html` each carry their own `<style>` block with literal light values for inline code and the two callout tints, so both shipped painting white boxes onto a dark ground. Readable enough to miss at a glance, wrong on any dark machine. **Found by checking the pages I had not looked at**, immediately after merging — the same omission as the round before it, where three passes went into a stylesheet without once rendering the page. The habit that keeps catching things is looking; the habit that keeps missing them is assuming the one sample generalises. The literals now read `--code-bg`, `--code-fg`, `--note-bg` and `--note-warn-bg` from the shared sheet, so a page-local copy cannot drift from it. The dark values are tinted toward the accent and the warning hue at low saturation: the light tints are washes on white and become glare on black. Also removed a duplication I had introduced in the same paragraph — the three image names appeared twice in consecutive sentences. | `site.dark-mode.spec.mjs` walks **every** page: nothing of consequence may paint near-white, and the body must actually be dark, so a page that never applied the theme cannot pass by having no offenders. Restoring one literal fails it |
| UX-META-01 | A workbook's own properties are invisible, and xlsx never read or wrote them | Done | P1 | Reported: *"we don't even have a way to show file properties, metadata and others"*. True, and the missing UI was the smallest part of it. `DocumentProperties` — title, subject, description, keywords, creator, last-modified-by, created, modified, language — has been in the model throughout. **The `.xlsx` reader never parsed `docProps/core.xml` into it** (the part was *retained* as bytes and nothing more), and **the writer never emitted one**. So an Excel file's author and title were invisible to every host, and anything authored here was lost on save. `casual-calc-ods` has parsed the equivalent `meta.xml` since `ODS-03`, so the two formats disagreed about whether a document knows its own name. Reader, writer and a File ▸ Properties dialog. Five fields editable; `created`, `modified`, `lastModifiedBy` and `language` shown **read-only**, because those are what happened rather than opinions about it and a text box there invites writing a false history into a document. **Two bugs the tests caught before this shipped.** The round-trip assertion failed `Expected "Q3 Forecast", Received ""` — the dialog looked perfect and persisted nothing, which is worse than no dialog because it tells somebody their document is attributed when it is not. Then generating our own part **replaced** an imported file's `core.xml`, losing the producer extensions this model does not hold; `parts_attached_at_the_package_root_survive_a_save` caught that. The retained bytes are now edited **in place** — each element the model holds has its text replaced and everything else survives, so an unedited workbook saves the bytes it arrived with. That is `FID-27`'s rule and its method. | `editor.document-properties.spec.mjs`: properties set in the dialog survive a save and reopen; the dialog separates editable from filed; Escape abandons rather than saves |
| UX-KEY-01 | Excel's keyboard shortcuts had no gate, and an audit without one reported six false failures | Done | P1 | Raised under the migrating-user priority — *"cognitive burden while a user transfers from Excel to our app"* — whose sharpest edge is a shortcut the hands already know doing nothing. **The audit found no such shortcut. It did find that my probes were worse than the editor.** `F2`, `Alt+=`, `Ctrl+;`, `Ctrl+Space`, `Shift+Space`, `` Ctrl+` `` and `Ctrl+PageDown`/`PageUp` all work and always did. Six were reported broken and **the wrong claim reached the body of `UX-META-01`'s merged PR** before it was checked; it is corrected here rather than only there, because a false 'this is broken' costs the next reader the same hour it cost me. Three distinct probe defects, each of which agreed with the mistake: a key sent without focusing `#grid` first, which lands on `<body>` and is bound to nothing; **`session_active_sheet()` read as the displayed sheet when it reports the workbook's saved `tab_selected` flag** — the sheet a reader *opens* on, which does not move when a user switches tabs; and a whole-column selection expected to span a million rows when it spans the used range, which is the better behaviour and was scored as a failure for not being the worse one. **What made it invisible: nothing asserted any of this, so there was no red to contradict me.** An engine surface with no gate is one where a confident wrong report and the truth look identical. The gate is the fix, and it is written against observables each checked to mean what it is read as saying — the visible `.sheet-tab.active` label rather than a flag that sounds like it. | `editor.excel-shortcuts.spec.mjs`: seven shortcuts, each seen red under a mutation — `mod` forced false takes the four `Ctrl` tests, `e.altKey` forced false empties `Alt+=` to `""`, and renaming `case "F2"` leaves `#inline-edit` hidden |
| UX-KEY-02 | Three Excel chords the engine could already serve reached nothing, and the two docs describing them disagreed with the code in opposite directions | Done | P1 | Follows `UX-KEY-01`, which gated the shortcuts that work. These are the ones that did not, and each is the same shape: **the capability existed and no key reached it** — the fourth instance of that pattern after `autoSum`, `DocumentProperties` and `build_set_op`. **`Ctrl+Shift+L` left-aligned instead of toggling the filter.** Borrowed from Word. `toggleFilter` had been there all along, so the one chord an Excel user hits without looking made a silent formatting change and no filter — worse than a missing key, by the rule this repository already states at `Ctrl+9/0`: finger memory that is *wrong* costs more than finger memory that is unanswered. Left-align now has no chord rather than a made-up one; centre and right keep theirs because neither means anything in Excel and so neither can contradict it. **`Ctrl+H` opened nothing.** `#replace-input`, `#replace-one`, `#replace-all`, `session_replace_all` and `session_replace_at` all existed, reachable only by opening Find and tabbing across. `openReplace()` lands the caret in the replacement field, which is the whole point of a separate chord from `Ctrl+F`. **`Ctrl+Enter` was absent.** Now fills the selection through `session_fill`, so relative references adjust per cell — `=A1*2` across B1:B3 gives `=A2*2` in B2 — and it is **one** undo step rather than N. It routes through `commit` first, so the entry meets the same validation rule and formula guard as any typed value. **The documents were wrong in both directions, which is the finding worth keeping.** `docs/73` recorded five keyboard defects; three were real (fixed here) and two had gone stale — Alt+PageDown is not nested in the Ctrl branch (`editor.core.js:5930`, ahead of `if (mod)` on 5940) and Shift+Enter moves up, as Excel does. Meanwhile `docs/47` listed `Ctrl+X`, `Ctrl+Space`, `Shift+Space`, `F4`, `Ctrl+D/R`, `Ctrl+1` and `Ctrl+;` as **missing**, and every one of them was bound and working. **What made it invisible: prose was the only record, so it could drift in either direction and nothing went red.** Understating is not the safe direction — it invites somebody to rebuild what already exists. Both documents now defer to the spec rather than restating it. | `editor.excel-shortcuts.spec.mjs` grows to 9. Each of the three was seen red first: `Ctrl+Shift+L` gave `filter: null`, `Ctrl+H` left `#find-bar` hidden, `Ctrl+Enter` returned `["=A1*2", "", ""]` |
| UX-KEY-03 | Alt access keys were called the last keyboard gap; they were already complete | Done | P2 | A correction to `UX-KEY-02`, which I wrote, and which claimed Alt ribbon access keys were the one remaining Excel keyboard gap. They are not: `Alt+F`, `Alt+D` and `Alt+H` open File, Data and Help, holding Alt alone underlines each menu's letter as on Windows, and the menubar carries a roving tabindex so Tab passes it and arrows move within it. **The claim came from grepping the grid's keydown handler, where the binding is not** — it is registered on `document`, beside the menu construction. That is the same mistake as `UX-KEY-01`'s six false failures and `UX-KEY-02`'s `k === "d"` miss: **absence in a grep read as absence in the app**, three times, in one area, by me. The rule that would have prevented all three is the repository's own — *check means running the thing* — and the reason it kept not being applied is that a grep feels like checking. There is now no known keyboard gap against Excel. | `editor.excel-shortcuts.spec.mjs` grows to 10; the Alt case seen red by forcing the mnemonic lookup to `undefined`, which yields `Alt+f must open File / Received: null` |
| UX-MOB-01 | The chrome had no width below which it stopped, so every viewport under 791px silently clipped controls | Done | P1 | First measured work on the mobile gap. Raised because `editor.css` contained **no width-based `@media` query of any kind** — only colour-scheme, reduced-motion and contrast — which is a fair summary of how much of this had been considered. **The toolbar's progressive collapse was not the problem; running out of things to collapse was.** Fully folded (6/6 groups) the bar still measured **791px**, so it clipped on every viewport narrower than that: 401px of toolbar off-screen on a 390px phone, 471px at 320px, and **23px even on an iPad Mini in portrait**, which nobody would have filed as a mobile bug. The menu bar had no collapse at all and sliced *Help* in half at 390, taking *Tools* with it at 320. Both bars now fold their trailing end into a `⋯`, which is the trade the toolbar's design already made — one indirection for the rarely-used end, and never a scrollbar or a second row. Undo/redo is marked `tb-keep` and never moves: if one group survives a 320px screen it has to be the one that takes back a mistake. **Three defects were introduced fixing it, and every one was caught by looking rather than by the assertion.** A bare `display:` on the new flyout outranked its `hidden` attribute, so the panel was laid out *inside the toolbar* and added its own width to the measurement deciding whether anything needed to move. `overflow: hidden` on `.toolbar` then clipped that panel to the toolbar band, rendering it as two stray letters at the screen edge — while the test asserting it was *visible* passed. And laid out as a row, its full-width labelled buttons ran off the panel. **The `display`-beats-`hidden` trap is the fourth instance in this file** — `.app-header`, `.presence` and the flyout preceded it, and `.menu-top` had simply never been hidden before. **What made it invisible: a clipped control is not a broken one.** It renders perfectly up to the edge of a viewport nobody measured at, so nothing errors, nothing logs, and the only evidence is a screenshot somebody has to take. **This supersedes a decided design, which is the part that needed saying out loud.** `UX-EDIT-01` chose *collapse, then scroll* on purpose, and ruled out wrapping correctly: wrapping costs height and every pixel comes out of the grid. A `⋯` costs no height either — the panel floats — and it does the one thing that scrollbar could not, which is **say that there is more**. `UX-EDIT-01` also hides the scrollbar deliberately, because a visible track costs 8–15px of a 48px bar; a hidden scrollbar on a toolbar is not an affordance, and on a phone the drag competes with the grid's own pan. Scrolling stays wired underneath, and `editor.narrow-screens.spec.mjs` now asserts the stronger contract — nothing past the edge at all — with its reachability half intact. | `editor.chrome-reachability.spec.mjs`: six widths from 320 to 1440 assert the toolbar fits its own box, no menu is sliced, and the overflow panel is on-screen with nothing spilling out of it. Seen red at 471px/401px over, and the spill assertion re-verified by restoring `flex-direction: row` |
| UX-MOB-02 | The grid could not be scrolled by touch at all, so a phone showed the first screenful of a workbook and nothing else | Done | P0 | The larger half of the mobile gap, and it was hiding behind two things that *did* work. The grid is a canvas this editor scrolls itself; **every ancestor is `overflow: clip`, `hidden` or `visible`**, so there is nothing for a browser to scroll natively. And there were **no touch or pointer listeners anywhere in the webapp** — `grep` across every file returned nothing. A finger had no way to move the sheet. **What made it invisible: tap and double-tap worked the whole time.** The browser synthesises `click` and `dblclick` from a tap, so selecting a cell and opening the editor behaved perfectly, and the app looked fine right up until somebody tried to reach row 30. A defect that presents as *most things work* is far harder to see than one that presents as a blank screen. Touch events rather than pointer events, deliberately: every selection path here is built on mouse events, and unifying them under `pointerdown` would have rewritten the working half to fix the missing one. One finger pans 1:1 (raw pixels, no wheel damping — a finger is already 1:1 with the screen), two fingers pinch-zoom, and a second finger cancels the pan rather than fighting it, because a pinch midpoint drifts and panning to it makes the sheet lurch. An 8px slop separates a tap from a drag, and a drag suppresses the synthesised click — without that, **every swipe would also move the selection to wherever the finger came to rest**, so scrolling would silently change which cell you were on. `touch-action: none` on `#grid` stops the browser competing for the same gestures. | `editor.touch.spec.mjs`, driven by **real CDP touch input, not synthesised `TouchEvent`s** — a hand-built event proved this broken before the handlers existed and would have proved it fixed just as readily. Five cases: vertical pan, horizontal pan, clamp at the top edge, tap-still-selects-but-swipe-does-not, and pinch. Pan seen red at `scrollY: 0`; pinch seen red at `zoom: 1` by disabling the `setZoom` call |
| UX-MOB-03 | On a phone there was no cut, copy, paste or insert at all, and a swipe stopped dead | Done | P1 | The half of `UX-MOB-02` that made touch *usable* rather than merely possible. **Long press.** Every one of cut, copy, paste, insert and delete lives in the context menu, and a touch device has no right button — so on a phone none of them existed. A held finger now raises the same `contextmenu` event the right button does, rather than growing a second, thinner menu that drifts from it: that handler already knows about headers, the corner and cells, and duplicating it would mean two menus to keep in step. The synthesised click after the press is suppressed, or it lands on the document and closes the menu it just opened. **Inertia.** A flick now carries on after release and decays, instead of stopping the instant the finger leaves — the single thing that makes a touch surface feel like an application rather than a web page, and precisely the *"laggy like LibreOffice"* impression this project exists to avoid. Velocity is smoothed over the last samples because one frame's delta is noise, and the frame delta is clamped to 32ms because a backgrounded tab resumes with one enormous frame and would otherwise fling the sheet to its last row. **A mutation that proved nothing, twice.** The drag-cancels-the-press test survived removing `cancelPress()` from `touchmove`, and survived removing the timer's own `pan.moved` check — because the two guard each other. It only goes red with **both** removed, which is the honest reading: they are defence in depth, not one mechanism and one comment. A single-guard mutation would have shipped a test that asserts nothing. | `editor.touch.spec.mjs` grows to 8. Long press seen red as `#sheet-ctx` *element(s) not found*; inertia seen red at `settled 190` against `atRelease 190` — stopped dead; drag-cancels-press seen red only with both guards removed |
| UX-MOB-04 | A flick was thrown away whenever the release arrived late, which reads as lag | Done | P1 | Found by CI failing what passed locally ten times out of ten. `UX-MOB-03`'s inertia only started if the release landed within **90ms** of the last movement — a hard cut-off, and wrong in both directions. It discarded a genuine flick whenever the release arrived late (a loaded machine, a slow phone, a busy main thread), and it kept **full** speed for a finger that had paused just inside the window. A finger that pauses produces no `touchmove`, so the gap before the release is the only evidence of a pause that exists — which makes it the right signal and a cliff the wrong way to read it. Now decayed linearly over 300ms: a prompt release throws fully, a rested finger not at all, and everything between in proportion. **What made it invisible: the failure is silent and reads as the wrong thing.** The sheet stops at the exact pixel the finger left, which a user calls *laggy* — the precise impression this project exists to avoid — rather than *a threshold I missed*. Locally it never fired, because a desktop delivers the release in a frame. | `editor.touch.spec.mjs` grows to 9 with a release deliberately delayed 120ms; restoring the 90ms cut-off reproduces CI exactly — `Expected > 280, Received 270`, stopped dead at the release pixel |
| SEC-006 | Sheet protection was enforced on two write paths and ignored by nine | Done | P0 | Found by a parallel audit and confirmed by running before a line was changed. **`protection_blocks` was tested; which bindings consult it was not** — so the rule had coverage and its *use* had none, and nine write paths never asked: fill (`Ctrl+D`, `Ctrl+R`, the fill handle), replace-all, replace-at, merge and its discarding form, shift-cells, and all three pastes. **A user who protected a sheet and pressed `Ctrl+V` was told `pasted`, and the locked cells were gone.** Two of the pastes did guard — over a **1×1 anchor**. `session_paste_html` parses the block, with every `dr`/`dc` in hand, *one line above* a guard on a single cell; `session_paste_tsv` carried a comment saying the extent was unknowable before parsing, when the parse is a `split`. So a paste from Excel onto one unlocked input cell overwrote ten locked rows beneath it. Both now measure the extent they are about to write. `session_clip_paste_mode` guards its anchor even with an empty clipboard: pasting nothing changes nothing, but the user pressed `Ctrl+V` on a protected sheet and is owed the same answer either way rather than silence that depends on what they last copied. **What made it invisible is written down in this repository already.** `UX-PROT-01` records that `JsError::new` **panics off-wasm**, so a native test cannot call a binding that refuses — and that asserting the predicate alone *"tests the rule and not its use"*, having been caught by a mutation that left such a test green. That note explains precisely why this hole existed and it did not stop it: the guidance was followed for the one path it was written about. The gate is therefore a **browser** test, where a refusal is an ordinary thrown error and the bindings can be driven the way a host drives them. | `editor.protection.spec.mjs`: nine write paths refused, a paste refused over its extent while a one-cell paste into an unlocked cell still succeeds, and every path released again by unprotecting. Reverting the guards and rebuilding the wasm — the revert verified by grep before the rebuild, because a stash that silently failed had already produced one mutation proving nothing — turns **9 of 11 red**, each `Received string: "wrote"` |
| SEC-019 | Closing the tab discarded the document, and File ▸ New was the only destructive verb that did not ask | Done | P0 | Two data-loss paths from the same audit, sharing one missing fact: **nothing knew whether there was unsaved work.** The document lives in wasm memory and nowhere else — no autosave, no draft — and `grep beforeunload webapp/` returned nothing, so Ctrl+R, Ctrl+W or Back discarded an hour without a word. `File ▸ New` was worse: `session_new` replaces the session outright and `seed()` then calls `session_clear_history`, so **Ctrl+Z recovers nothing** — while merge, merge-across, delimited export and a lossy save all confirm first. `confirmModal` is used fifteen times in this editor, and the most destructive verb in it was not one of them. **The dirty check asks the engine rather than tallying in JavaScript.** A tally in the editor is a list of every mutation it can perform, and this repository has twice paid for a rule that enumerates its subjects — `UX-SITE-03` twice — where the omission is always the write path added last. `History` now counts applied edits monotonically, surfaced as `session_edits_applied()`, and the host compares it against the value it saw at the last save. **Deliberately not the undo stack's depth**, which is bounded: past the cap it stops rising, so a long-edited document would compare equal to its save point and be reported *clean while dirty*. The counter can only err the other way — undo counts as an edit, so undoing back to a save point still warns. A needless warning costs a click; the other mistake costs the document. Crosses to JavaScript as `f64` rather than `u64`, because a `u64` arrives as a `BigInt` and compares unequal to a `Number` under `!==`, which would make every document look dirty. A clean document is never warned about, so this does not become the dialog everyone clicks through without reading. | `editor.unsaved-work.spec.mjs` (6): a seeded document is clean, an edit dirties it and saving settles it, the counter rises and never falls across undo, `File ▸ New` asks and cancelling keeps the data, it stays silent when there is nothing to lose, and `beforeunload` cancels only when dirty. Forcing `isDirty()` to `false` turns **3 of 6 red** |
| SEC-020 | Paste Special abandoned the block at the first non-numeric cell, a host crash read as consent, and a typed comment was destroyed by a click | Done | P1 | Three from the silent-failure audit, each verified before it was believed — and **one of its findings refuted**. **Paste Special ▸ Add.** The arithmetic modes did `return (ops, cut, false)` on the first cell whose source or target was not a number, leaving the *whole closure* rather than the iteration. The operations gathered so far were still applied, so the paste half-happened: the cells above the label updated, everything below silently did not, and the status bar said `pasted add` either way. Plainly a slip — the comment three lines above says non-numeric cells are *left alone*, and the divide-by-zero arm in the same `match` already used `continue`. **A host handler that threw was read as consent.** `emit()` caught and logged, then carried on — so a `beforeCellsChanged` permission check that crashed meant *allowed*, and a host's own rule was bypassed by a bug inside it. A throw from a `before…` event is now a refusal. For a notification event it is not a veto — the edit has happened — but no longer silence either: a `cellsChanged` handler that fails while writing to the host's store means the change exists here and nowhere else, which is the shape of the submission this project once dropped without a word. **A half-typed comment was destroyed by moving the selection.** The reasoning in the comment was right — a draft belongs to its own thread and must not follow the cursor to someone else's — but emptying the box is not how to honour it. Drafts are parked against their cell and come back; an empty box parks nothing, so a posted reply leaves no stale text to reappear. **Refuted:** the audit called the `catch {}` around `session_validation_error` a fail-open integrity hole. It is not reachable — that binding returns `String`, not `Result`, so it has no ordinary failure to swallow. The change was written and then reverted when the return type answered it: a fix for an impossible path is not a fix, and it cannot be gated by a test that fails without it. | `editor.paste-special.spec.mjs` (3) and `editor.fails-closed.spec.mjs` (3). Paste seen red at `["11","20","30"]` against `["11","20","33"]`; the veto seen red by disabling the `before…` branch; the draft seen red by restoring the clear, which returns `""` on coming back |
| SEC-021 | Ctrl+S converted the document instead of saving it, and a refused clipboard reported the opposite of what the engine had done | Done | P1 | Two mismatches between what the editor said and what it did. **Ctrl+S was the accidental conversion the File menu warns about.** That menu's first Download entry is named *Same format as opened* and carries the reason in a comment: *"the others are conversions, and a conversion chosen by accident is how a `.csv` becomes a package under its own name"*. `Ctrl+S` — the one save nobody opens a menu for — called the raw `.xlsx` path. It ignored `session_format()`, so a `.csv` came back as `opencalc.xlsx`; it never asked `session_save_loss()`, whose binding says the sentence must be said **before** the download because afterwards the file is already on disk; it set no status, so the commonest save in the application gave no sign of having happened; and it had **no `catch`** inside an `async` listener, so a throw became an unhandled rejection — no file, no message, nothing. Now `saveAs("native")`, which is what the menu entry has always called. **A refused system clipboard reported `cut blocked` for a cut that was armed.** `clipToOS` arms the engine's clipboard and starts the marching ants *first*, then tries the system one, and reports failure only for that last step — so the ants were drawn, the cut was live, the next Ctrl+V moved the data, and the status bar said it had not happened. `stopMarch`'s own comment names this pairing from the other side: *"the visible signal said cancelled and the state said otherwise, which is the worst possible pairing for an action that deletes."* The cut is **not** undone, because it works — inside the application it pastes exactly as it should. Only the half that failed is named. | `editor.save-shortcut.spec.mjs` (4) and `editor.cut-report.spec.mjs` (3). Ctrl+S seen red as `opencalc.xlsx` for a document whose format is `csv`, and as a status still reading `engine v0.0.0`; the cut seen red as `cut blocked` while a second test moves the data with the same armed clip |
| DV-01 | The commonest kind of Excel dropdown reached nothing: a list backed by a range had no menu and no rule | Done | P0 | Excel's lists are usually a **range** — kept out of the way on another sheet and maintained on its own — not an inline CSV. The importer preserved the reference in `formula1` and **said so in a comment**: *"the rule survives even though the editor cannot offer the dropdown yet."* Both `session_validation_at` (which decides whether a chevron is drawn) and the enforcement gated on the literal `values` being non-empty, so for the commonest real dropdown there was no list **and no rule** — a user opened their workbook, the dropdowns were gone, anything could be typed into those cells, and nothing said why. Resolved **on read, not materialised at import**, because the list is live: adding a row to the source range adds an option, exactly as in Excel. The scan is bounded — a source is often written `$B$1:$B$10000` because the author does not know how long the list will grow, and reading that literally is thousands of lookups every time the grid asks whether to draw a chevron. An unreadable source returns nothing and therefore refuses nothing: never a wrong list, and never a refusal nobody can satisfy. Enforcement is confined to the range-backed case, so an inline list keeps exactly the semantics it had — this adds enforcement where there was none rather than changing enforcement that worked. The dialog now takes a range in the same field as a comma list, as Excel's own does, so the feature can be created and not only read. | `range_list_tests` (5 in the wasm crate) and `editor.range-dropdown.spec.mjs` (4). Disabling the resolver turns 4 of 5 red — `left: "null"` against `["One","Three"]` — while the inline-list test stays green, which is the proof that existing behaviour did not move |
| FID-34 | Every colour scale and data bar imported as nothing, because real files self-close `<color/>` | Done | P1 | `<color rgb="FFF8696B"/>` is how **every** writer spells it, and the handler for it sat only in the `Start` dispatch — a self-closed element arrives as `Event::Empty`. So `RawCf.colors` stayed empty, the `colors.len() >= 2` gate failed, the rule became `None`, and a sheet full of colour scales imported as a sheet with none. Data bars went the same way. The two dispatches now share one `read_cf_color`, because a second copy is the thing that drifts — and the drift here *was* the bug: the `Start` arm existed and the `Empty` one did not. | `colour_scales_and_data_bars_survive_a_self_closed_color_element`, with the XML Excel actually writes. Seen red at `left: 0` rules imported |
| CI-017 | The gate written to make that class of defect impossible could not see the arms where it lives | Done | P1 | The more valuable half. `childless_elements_are_handled_in_the_empty_dispatch` exists **precisely** to catch a childless element handled only on `Start`; its doc comment says the failure *"is invisible in review — the arm is right there — and shows up only as a construct that silently fails to import"*. It was green while exactly that defect sat in the file. Its name extractor accepted a `b"name"` only when `=>` or `\|` followed **immediately**, so every *guarded* arm was invisible — and `b"color" if cur_cf.is_some() =>` is guarded, as is every conditional-format element. A test that quietly ignores a whole syntactic form is worse than no test, because the form it ignores is where the careful code is: a guard is what somebody writes when the case is subtle. **The repaired gate found the defect by itself**, before anything was fixed, naming it: ``these childless elements are dispatched only on Start, so a self-closed one is silently ignored: ["color"]``. | Proven by reverting **both** changes: the gate passes while the behavioural test fails — which is the whole finding, a green gate over a live bug. With only the fix reverted, both go red |
| FID-33 | Conditional-format rules were dropped without a word, including `>=` and `<=` | Done | P1 | Against this project's governing rule — *anything the model cannot keep is counted and named in a compatibility report, never dropped quietly*. The CF importer matched a fixed set and let everything else fall to `_ => None` with no `report.record` anywhere in the block. **Four of the casualties were ordinary**: `greaterThanOrEqual`, `lessThanOrEqual`, `notEqual` and `notBetween` — `>= 100` is not an edge case — and those are now modelled, imported and exported rather than reported. The rest (`iconSet`, `expression`, `timePeriod`, `beginsWith`, `endsWith`, a `cellIs` whose formula is not a bare number, and a rule whose dxf paints nothing this model holds) are counted and named. **A test changed the design rather than confirming it.** With the dxf resolved before the predicate, an `iconSet` — which carries no `dxfId` — failed the fill lookup first and was reported as an unmodellable *dxf*: true of the lookup, and useless to a host that needs to be told it lost an icon set. The decision was moved above the resolution, so the reordering is load-bearing rather than tidying. The report key is an **allowlist**, not the file's `type` attribute passed through, because that attribute is attacker-controlled and the report's cap is a whole-report ceiling rather than a budget for one construct. | Five tests across model, import and export, each seen red by mutation: removing the importer arms gives `left: []` against the four operators; writing `NotBetween` as `operator="between"` fails the round-trip; removing the report call reproduces the silent drop exactly, `[]` |
| CALC-06 | Every Excel-2010 statistical name was `#NAME?`, and no dotted function could be saved at all | Done | P1 | Excel renamed its statistical functions in 2010 and has offered **only** the dotted spellings in its own UI since; the legacy ones are buried under *Compatibility*. This engine implemented only the legacy ones, so both the formulas a migrating user types and the formulas already in their files returned `#NAME?`. 31 added as true aliases; **`NORM.S.DIST` and `LOGNORM.DIST` implemented instead**, because 2010 made `cumulative` a *required* argument and the `scalar` helper reads only `args.first()` — aliasing would have returned the CDF where the density was asked for, `0.5` instead of `0.3989…`, a silently wrong number rather than a visible error. **Four deliberately skipped**: `T.DIST.RT` (2 args against `TDIST`'s 3), `BETA.DIST` (argument *positions* differ, so an alias reads `TRUE` as the lower bound and returns a plausible wrong number), `HYPGEOM.DIST` and `NEGBINOM.DIST` (both gained a `cumulative` branch that needs a summation this engine does not have). **The half that made it shippable was the export side.** `FUTURE_FUNCTIONS` — the list of implemented functions SpreadsheetML requires the `_xlfn.` prefix for — contained **no dotted name at all**, so `NETWORKDAYS.INTL`, `WORKDAY.INTL`, `ISO.CEILING` and `ECMA.CEILING` had been evaluable and *unsaveable* since they were added: written bare, Excel opens the file and shows `#NAME?` in every cell that used one. Adding 33 aliases without this would have quietly widened that. 42 → 79 entries. `ERROR.TYPE` stays undecorated, as it predates the convention. | `casual-calc-eval` 205 → 208 tests, seen red as `NORM.DIST(1.5,2,3,TRUE) gave Error(Name)`; the naive-alias risk proven separately at `NORM.S.DIST(0,FALSE) = 0.5, want 0.3989422804014327`. The export half is gated by an **invariant** rather than a list: every dotted name in the catalog must be `is_future_function()`, so a dotted function added and forgotten fails a test instead of a user's file — seen red by removing `STDEV.S`, which reports ``add them to FUTURE_FUNCTIONS: ["STDEV.S"]`` |
| FID-32 | Conditional formatting lost its text colour in both directions, and two rules sharing a fill shared one format | Done | P1 | Excel's single most-used conditional format is *Light Red Fill with **Dark Red Text***. The fill survived; the text colour did not, at either end. **Import** hardcoded `font_color: None, bold: false` and `styles.rs` parsed only `dxf_fills` from `<dxfs>` — no dxf `<font>` was read at all — so the *Red Text* preset, which has no fill, hit a `None => continue` and vanished entirely. **Export** was worse than the brief assumed, and the worker refuted the brief rather than accepting it: `collect_dxfs` returned fills only, and `dxf_id` was `position(\|f\| *f == cf.fill)` — so two rules sharing a colour shared one `<dxf>` and the second silently took the first's text colour, while a fill-less rule matched nothing and fell to `unwrap_or(0)`, which is some other rule's format. A `<dxf>` is now the whole differential format, keyed on all three parts, and is written with `<font>` before `<fill>` because OOXML's `CT_Dxf` sequence is ordered and Excel rejects it otherwise. A self-closed `<font/>` also had to be handled: without it the *following* `<border>`'s colour was read as the font's. | `casual-calc-import` seen red at *a dxf that states a font but no fill is still a rule: left 1, right 2*; a round-trip in `casual-calc-export` seen red at *all three rules survive: left 2, right 3*, and now asserts a rule sharing a fill keeps its own text colour |
| UX-SITE-01 | The landing page is light-only, and its features are a 3×2 table | Done | P2 | Two things, found by **screenshotting the page** instead of reading its stylesheet — which is also how `UX-EMBED-01` was found, after three rounds of editing colours that were never the problem. **No dark mode at all.** The site had zero `prefers-color-scheme` rules while the editor has had nine throughout — and the hero *embeds that editor*, so on a dark machine the page rendered white with a dark application sitting inside it. Done as a token swap: every rule already reads the tokens, so there is no second stylesheet to keep in step.
