Not an inversion — `--panel` is *lighter* than the page in dark and darker in light, because a raised surface catches more light than what it sits on, and flipping the values literally would turn every card into a hole. `--ink` deliberately does not flip: that band is meant to be dark in both themes, so it becomes a *lifted* panel in dark rather than a darker hole. **Six features in three equal columns** is two tidy rows of three — the shape that says every claim carries identical weight, so none of them lead. A six-track grid with each row opening wider: 3+3 is a table, 4+2 has a subject and a footnote. Spans are confined to a `min-width` query, so the single-column layout is untouched. | Rendered and inspected in both schemes at 1440×900; the CTA band measured `rgb(11,13,18)` in light and `rgb(28,36,48)` in dark, which is the non-inversion behaving as intended |
| UX-EMBED-01 | The landing page loses its hero every time it loads | Done | P1 | Reported as the site looking wrong, and it was — but not in the stylesheet, which is where three rounds of looking went first. `index.html` embeds the live editor in an iframe below the fold, and **two independent things inside that editor scrolled the host document as it booted**. Between them the page arrived scrolled ~440px down: badge, headline and opening paragraph all pushed off the top. Every visitor saw a landing page with no headline on it. **One.** The boot focus (`UX-FOCUS-01`) guarded itself with `document.activeElement === document.body` and a comment saying an embed must not steal focus from the page around it. Inside an iframe that check asks about the **iframe's own** document, where nothing is focused — so it passed every time, and focusing the canvas made the parent scroll the frame into view. The intent was written down and never implemented. Now `window.top === window`, with `preventScroll` behind it. **Two.** `renderTabs` kept the active sheet tab visible with `scrollIntoView`, which walks **every** scrollable ancestor — and when embedded, the outermost is somebody else's page. `block: "nearest"` bounds how far each ancestor moves, not *which* ancestors move. Replaced with arithmetic on the strip's own `scrollLeft`, which cannot reach outside the element. **What made it invisible**: it looks exactly like a CSS problem, so that is where you look. It also cannot be seen from the editor's own page, where there is no host to scroll — and the earlier fix for it was reasoned about rather than run. Found by screenshotting the page and seeing the `<h1>` sitting at `y: -220`. | `editor.embed-does-not-scroll-host.spec.mjs`; each cause restored on its own scrolls the host 757px and 653px. Asserts `toBeInViewport()`, not presence — the element was in the DOM throughout the bug |
| UX-CLIP-01 | Cross-application rich HTML paste | Done | P1 | The inbound path: values, merges, fonts, colours, alignment and number formats all land. **Borders were the one thing left, deferred on a reason that did not survive being looked at.** `68` recorded them as unmappable because the CSS shorthand "collapses several of those into one declaration whose meaning depends on the table's border-collapse". `border-collapse` decides which of two *competing* declarations paints a **shared** edge — and the model has no shared edges: every cell carries its own four. Mapping what a cell declares for itself is exact, not approximate, and the property is simply never consulted. The captures agree: of the three, only LibreOffice carries a border at all, and it is a clean per-edge longhand. Weight maps to `thin`/`medium`/`thick`; `double`, `dashed` and `dotted` keep their style; `groove`, `ridge`, `inset`, `outset` become a solid line of the same weight, because Excel has no such styles and a line where a line was asked for is closer than nothing; `none`, `hidden` and a zero width are no edge. A per-edge longhand beats the shorthand, and an explicit `border-top: none` beats it too, so **presence** of the declaration is tested rather than what it parsed to. An edge the clipboard said nothing about is left alone, the same rule every other pasted property follows. The line-style token is checked against the OOXML list on the Rust side. Nothing is rejected today — the parser emits from a fixed set — but the token is written into the file verbatim, and a value that reaches the writer unchecked is one a clipboard could choose. Borders had no read path either: `session_range_bordered` answers only "are all four present across this whole range", which cannot see a cell carrying a bottom rule and nothing else. `session_cell_format` now reports the four edges, which the border picker can use as well. | Both tests seen red: without the mapping, `Expected "thin", Received undefined`. Then each branch mutated on its own — shorthand precedence (`none` lost to `thin`) and zero width (a line where there should be none) |
| WOPI-01 | No WOPI discovery, so no host can register OpenCalc | Done | P0 | Nextcloud, ownCloud, SharePoint, Moodle and Alfresco all integrate an editor by reading `/hosting/discovery`. Nothing served one, so none of them could be pointed at OpenCalc at all. Now served by `casual-calc-wopi`, advertising edit and view actions for every format the save leg can write back unchanged — a format it would rewrite is silent data loss with an administrator's blessing. That was `xlsx` alone until `WOPI-05`, and is `xlsx, csv, tsv, psv` since. | `an_edit_action_is_advertised_and_takes_parameters`, `only_formats_that_round_trip_are_advertised`, `discovery_is_served`, CI `docker-build` parses the XML |
| WOPI-02 | No WOPI action URL, so a host cannot open a file | Done | P0 | A host opens an editor by sending a browser to `<action>?WOPISrc=..&access_token=..`, which OpenCalc had no entry point for. `/wopi/edit` and `/wopi/view` now check the file, take a lock, mint the ordinary collaboration claims and serve the editor — so the collaboration server needed no changes to gain a WOPI integration. | `a_file_opens_edits_and_saves_back_to_the_host`, `an_unlisted_host_is_never_contacted` |
| WOPI-03 | No `CheckFileInfo`, so the file's name and rights are unknown | Done | P0 | WOPI carries the filename, rights and version in `CheckFileInfo`, not in the token. Calling it is also what *validates* the access token — we hold no key that could check somebody else's credential. `UserCanWrite` and `SupportsUpdate` are both required for a writable session: a host can claim the first without the second, and finding out at the save is the worst moment. | `write_permission_is_not_assumed`, `writing_needs_both_permission_and_a_host_that_accepts_it`, `read_only_is_enforced_in_the_token` |
| WOPI-04 | No WOPI locking, so SharePoint cannot save | Done | P1 | `PutFile` against a locked file is a 409 without a matching `X-WOPI-Lock`, and SharePoint locks on open. Lock, RefreshLock and Unlock are implemented in the adapter, refreshed on a timer rather than on activity — a document left open over lunch is exactly the one whose lock must survive — and released on close, on expiry and on shutdown. | `the_lock_operations_are_named_on_the_wire`, `a_save_is_addressed_the_way_wopi_specifies`, `locks_come_due_on_a_timer`, `a_file_locked_elsewhere_still_opens` |
| WOPI-05 | A save always emits xlsx, so only xlsx can be advertised | Done | P1 | The save leg wrote an OOXML package whatever it opened, so a host handed `books.csv` got a zip back under that name and lost the original — and discovery had to advertise `xlsx` alone to stay honest. A session now **remembers the format it opened** (`SessionFormat`, set where the bytes are read, because by save time nothing on a `Workbook` says how it arrived) and `save_as` writes any format from any session. The WOPI adapter converts on both legs — it is the only process that holds the host's format *and* talks to a collaboration server that reads packages and only packages — so `EDITABLE` is now `xlsx, csv, tsv, psv`, held to `SessionFormat::for_extension` by a test rather than to a comment. A filename whose extension that table does not know is **refused before the file is locked**, where the old code assumed `.xlsx`. What CSV cannot carry is counted and named — sheets, formulas, formatting, merges, panes, validations, comments, charts — in the compatibility report, logged per save and shown in the session chrome; `record_n(…, 0)` records nothing, so a clean CSV does not cry wolf. **What made it invisible:** every layer round-tripped perfectly *in its own format*, and no test ever asked one layer for another layer's format. `session_save()` in the WASM bridge was deliberately left xlsx-always for the same reason — the editor's "Save as Excel" names its download `.xlsx`, and making that call format-native would have reintroduced the identical defect pointing the other way (`session_save_native` is the one that follows the format). | `a_csv_opens_as_a_package_and_saves_back_as_a_csv`, `a_csv_save_names_the_sheets_and_formulas_it_cannot_carry`, `a_format_this_service_cannot_write_is_never_opened`, `only_formats_that_round_trip_are_advertised`, `a_filename_names_the_format_and_an_unknown_one_names_nothing`, `a_save_is_addressed_the_way_wopi_specifies`, `an_edited_csv_session_saves_csv_that_reads_back_with_the_edit`, `converting_an_untouched_session_does_not_return_the_original_bytes`; mutations give `assertion failed: the host's .csv was overwritten with something else: expected "Item,Qty\r\nWidget,7\r\n"`, `a second sheet and a formula were dropped and nothing said so`, and `` `csv` is advertised, and a save would rewrite it as something else `` |
| WOPI-07 | `.ods` is still not advertised, because nothing reads or writes one | Done | P2 | The row text was stale twice over. `casual-calc-ods` already had a reader **and** a writer; what was missing was that `SessionFormat` had no `Ods`, so nothing could *remember* a session had come from one — the same defect `WOPI-05` closed for `.csv`, one format later. `SessionFormat::Ods` now records the format at open (with the untouched-saves-byte-for-byte guarantee), `save_as` writes ODF, `loss_writing` delegates to a new `export_loss`, and `.ods` is in `EDITABLE`, so both actions appear in discovery. **Advertising it first required fixing five defects in the converter, four of them silent corruption.** (1) `export_ods` **wrote no formulas at all** — a `.ods` opened and saved came back a table of constants, every formula frozen to its last value; main's whole crate contained one mention of `table:formula` and it was in the *reader*. (2) The reader could not parse ODF's `;` argument separator — the one `of:` actually uses — so every multi-argument function in every real LibreOffice file was dropped and reported. (3) A self-closing `<table:table-row number-rows-repeated="N"/>`, which is exactly how LibreOffice writes the gap between two blocks of data, never advanced the row cursor, so the second block landed on top of the first. (4) `MAX_REPEAT` was clamped on the cursor rather than on materialisation, shifting everything after a gap of more than 4,096. (5) XML entities were never unescaped, so `Ada & Co` grew four characters on every save and any formula containing `&` stopped parsing. **What made all five invisible: every one produces a file that opens without complaint.** They were found by round-tripping a real LibreOffice fixture and asserting on the *bytes*. **What the format still costs, named not silent:** the writer keeps values, formulas and sheets only — formatting, merges, widths, validation, conditional formats, comments, hyperlinks, print setup, charts, images and defined names are counted by `export_loss` and logged on the WOPI save leg; the reader models less still, and that loss is now logged on the read leg too. Error values are written as text and reported `Degraded`. Function-name mapping to OpenFormula is not attempted, so a function ODF spells differently will show `#NAME?` in LibreOffice. | A LibreOffice `.ods` opens through WOPI and saves back as ODF with its formulas intact; `.ods` appears in `EDITABLE` and in discovery. Nine tests seen red first, six mutations caught |
| WASM-01 | The browser editor cannot open anything but `.xlsx` | Done | P1 | The bridge hard-coded `SessionFormat::Xlsx` and the page kept a **second** extension table, so a `.csv` was openable through the WOPI service and not in the editor that almost everyone uses. There were in fact **five** copies of "which formats we support" — the bridge, `openBytes`, the picker's `accept`, and two doc strings — all drifting independently, and each individually plausible. Only `.tab`, which `casual-calc-io` has always read and the picker never offered, showed they had diverged. All of it now delegates to `SessionFormat`, with a catch-all match arm so a format the SDK grows needs no change here. An extension the engine does not claim is **refused by name** rather than handed to the package importer, because the SDK answers `None` precisely so a file is never opened as one thing and saved back as another. Download gained "Same format as opened", reaching four engine functions that existed and were called by nothing, and the delimited export now takes its MIME type from the engine — it hard-coded `text/csv` for all three, which is right for one. | Four browser tests; seen red by guessing an unclaimed extension as a package — "a .xls was opened by guessing it was really a package" |
| ODS-01 | `casual-calc-io` is not the format registry the design says it is | Done | P2 | `19` places `casual-calc-ods` "under the `-io` registry" and `36` repeats it. `casual-calc-io` handles delimited text only, and the SDK now depends on `casual-calc-ods` **directly**, exactly as it does on `casual-calc-export`. So either the document or the crate DAG is wrong, and this is precisely the case where editing the prose is the failure mode — the registry was a structural decision about where format knowledge lives, and quietly restating it as whatever the code grew into would erase the decision without anybody making it. **Done, by ADR-022, to the owner's decision: detection below, dispatch above.** `-io` was worse than the row said — no identity, no detection, *and* no dispatch, just the delimited adapter and two helpers. `-io` gains `detect`, which identifies XLSX, ODS and delimited text from the bytes and **needs no format crate to do it**: a zip's first local file header names its first entry, and ODF requires `mimetype` to be first and *stored uncompressed* precisely so a reader can identify a document without decompressing it. Dispatch stays in `casual-calc-sdk`, which is the entry point hosts actually call. Giving `-io` the whole OOXML stack so the graph matched the sentence was rejected: a CSV-only consumer would carry all of it. **This closed a real gap, not just a documentation one.** Format came from the *filename extension* and never from the bytes, so a mislabelled `.xlsx` that was really an `.ods` opened wrong and an upload with no filename could not be opened at all — while `foreign_format` in `casual-calc-render` had been refusing to believe a file's claim about itself, for the pictures *inside* those same workbooks, all along. `SessionFormat::for_bytes` is the way in. Detection returns `None` rather than guessing: "probably a CSV" over a binary file is a sheet of mojibake. **Seen red without the fix**: reducing detection to "starts with PK, call it XLSX" fails 4 of 26 — the ODS, the ODF *text* document, the unrelated zip, and the truncated package. **Fixtures from two directions**, which is the pairing that matters: Python's `zipfile` writes the packages `-io` parses, so the header offsets are a real writer's; and the SDK tests detect packages **our own writers** produced. Either alone is half a test. **One correction to the row itself**: `36` does *not* repeat the claim — it says "the `casual-calc-io` adapters", which is accurate — so it was left alone. Correct prose is not a defect. **A defect this turned up in my own tooling**: `check-all.py` globs `check-*.py`, so the `check-rust.py` added last round was swept into the fast runner — a four-minute build inside the two-second check, which is the exact thing its docstring forbids. Excluded by name, with the reason. | The crate graph matches `19`'s DAG, or `19` is amended by an ADR that says why it changed |
| ODS-02 | Nothing fuzzes ODS, and the fidelity harness has never seen one | Done | P2 | Built, and it paid for itself before it was merged. An `ods` fuzz target runs in `fuzz-build`, and `tools/casual-calc-fidelity` gained an `--ods` mode with a **LibreOffice-authored** corpus wired into `oracle-diff`. The target makes two passes: the raw bytes as a package, and the same bytes wrapped as `content.xml` inside a zip the harness builds — because arbitrary bytes never form a ZIP, and the element walk is where every parser-shaped defect lived. Refusal passes; only a panic, a hang or an OOM is a defect. **It found three more defects immediately** — `ODS-04`, `ODS-05` and `ODS-06`, two of them P0, one of which this project had shipped two hours earlier. The corpus records divergences with a reason and they are **self-expiring**: when the two sides agree the run fails as a *stale marker*, so an excuse cannot outlive the defect it excuses. That is how both P0 fixes were confirmed — nine markers went stale in one run, and LibreOffice agreement went from 70 of 80 formulas to 79 of 80. **What made all of it invisible before:** every ODS test fed the reader a document this repository had written. | `cargo fuzz run ods` clean over 682k executions; `--ods` agrees with LibreOffice or names the divergence with a reason that expires |
| ODS-03 | Document metadata is dropped, and it is the one loss that is silent | Done | P2 | `import_ods` opened `content.xml` and nothing else, so author, title and timestamps vanished on a round trip **with an empty report** — silent for a structural reason: the report is assembled while reading `content.xml`, and nothing can count what was never opened. `meta.xml` is now read into a new format-neutral `Workbook::properties` and written back, so the metadata **survives** rather than merely being counted; whatever `<office:meta>` held that the model does not is named per element, and `styles.xml`/`settings.xml` are named from the zip directory without decompressing them. Additive by ADR-010 — defaulted in, skipped out when empty — so `SCHEMA_VERSION` does not move and an untouched workbook serializes to the bytes it always did. **`retained_parts` was rejected deliberately**, though it is where the `.xlsx` importer parks `docProps/core.xml`: the OOXML writer emits every retained part unconditionally, so an ODS to XLSX save would have leaked a stray `/meta.xml` into the package — a new defect in place of an old one. Every part is now bounded **twice**, on the declared size and on the read, because the declared size is the archive's own claim; `content.xml` gained the stronger bound too. **The mutation worth keeping:** transposing `dc:creator` and `meta:initial-creator` — which mean opposite things in ODF and OOXML — passes a round-trip test perfectly, because reader and writer are symmetric. Only a test that names the two roles apart catches it, and the real fixture records neither name. | A LibreOffice document's metadata is read, survives a round trip, and what is not modelled is named per element; seen red reading `content.xml` alone, and under a creator/last-saver swap |
| ODS-04 | LibreOffice's padding was silently trimmed away | Done | P1 | LibreOffice does not write leading, trailing or repeated spaces as characters — ODF collapses whitespace inside `<text:p>`, so it writes them as `<text:s text:c="N"/>` **elements**, which this reader walked past because it only collected text events. `"  padded  "` arrived as `"padded"`, and a cell holding a single space arrived empty: a silent change to somebody's data, on the most ordinary content there is. `<text:tab/>` had the same hole. Found by the LibreOffice corpus of `ODS-02`, and gated against a **LibreOffice-written fixture**, because nothing this repository writes emits `<text:s>` — a round trip through our own writer could never have exercised it. Same circularity that hid `ODS-06`. The repeat count is bounded like any other attacker-controlled repeat. | A LibreOffice document's padding survives import; seen red with the `<text:s>` arm disabled — `"padded "` against `"  padded  "` |
| ODS-05 | A 329-byte file materialised 16.7 million cells | Done | P0 | `MAX_REPEAT` bounded each repeat attribute and **nothing bounded their product**. One `<table:table-cell>` carrying `number-columns-repeated` and `number-rows-repeated` together materialised 4096 x 4096 = **16,777,216 cells**; measured here at **1.50 GB of resident memory and 16.4 seconds from 329 bytes**, and ten such rows fit in two kilobytes. The ODF reader is reachable from a WOPI host's upload, so this was a denial of service behind a friendly file extension. Bounded now on the **product**, at the `max_populated_cells` the OOXML reader has enforced since `SEC-023` — deliberately the same number rather than a fresh judgement, because two readers admitting different amounts of the same workbook is its own defect. The document is **refused, not truncated**: keeping the first eight million cells of a file that claims sixteen would be the silent loss this project refuses. The cursor is untouched, so a document may still legally *span* a huge range. **What made it invisible:** every ODS test fed the reader a document this repository had written, and no such document contains an amplifier. It was found by the fuzz target of `ODS-04` — the first thing ever to hand this reader an input nobody wrote. | A tiny package declaring the maximum repeat product is refused with `TooManyCells`, and an ordinary wide fill still reaches its far corner |
| ODS-06 | LibreOffice could not read a single formula this engine wrote | Done | P0 | `WOPI-07` taught `export_ods` to write formulas, as `table:formula="of:=…"`, and **never declared the `of:` prefix**. A prefix used and not bound is not untidy XML, it is not well-formed namespace XML: **LibreOffice answers `Err:510` for every formula cell in a document OpenCalc saved**. Confirmed against a real `soffice` — `=SUM(A1:A3)` came back as `Err:510` before the declaration and `10` after. Arguably worse than the defect `WOPI-07` fixed: formulas used to be dropped, and now they arrived as errors, so the file looked like it had formulas and every one was broken. **Why nothing caught it, and this is the lesson:** `import_ods` matches attributes by *local* name, so it read the broken file back perfectly and every round-trip test passed. **A round trip through your own reader cannot see a namespace defect, by construction** — only a real LibreOffice round trip could, which is exactly what `ODS-04` built. The gate is mechanical rather than a re-test of this one prefix: any prefix used, including one inside an attribute *value*, must be declared. The first version of that gate passed with the declaration deleted — it only scanned element and attribute *names*, and `of:` lives in a value — so it was fixed before it was trusted. | Every prefix the writer uses is declared, value-prefixes included; seen red with `xmlns:of` removed, and against real LibreOffice |
| WOPI-06 | No proof-key validation | Done | P2 | WOPI's request-signing scheme lets a host prove a request came from the editor it registered. Optional, and no host currently requires it, but it is the next hardening step for SharePoint Online. **Built.** The access token is a bearer credential — anyone holding one can make the same calls — so every outgoing request now carries an RSA-SHA256 signature over the token, the URL as sent, and a .NET-tick timestamp, with the public half published as `<proof-key>` in discovery. **The row's acceptance was written the wrong way round**: this service is the WOPI *client*, so it signs and never verifies, and there is no incoming request here to refuse. What is testable, and what the gate does, is close the loop — verify our own signature exactly as a host would, using the modulus and exponent read out of the discovery document rather than the key pair in hand. No new dependency: `ring` was already in the tree via rustls, which also avoids the `rsa` crate's advisory history. Off unless `OPENCALC_WOPI_PROOF_KEY` names a PKCS#8 key, and a configured key that will not load is **fatal at boot** rather than a warning — starting anyway would advertise nothing and sign nothing while looking healthy. Eleven mutations, all seen red: URL not upper-cased, length prefixes dropped, DER leading zero kept, modulus and exponent swapped, Unix epoch, seconds instead of 100ns, the signed URL missing the access token, the token not percent-encoded, discovery publishing the wrong value, a proof key advertised with none configured, and the headers dropped. **The encoding one is the lesson**: it passed until the test token was changed from `host-token` to something base64-shaped, because a token of plain letters makes percent-encoding a no-op and hides whether the URL signed is the URL sent. | A host verifies the proof on a request this service actually made |
| BRAND-01 | No white-labelling | Done | P1 | Product name, logo and colours were hard-coded. One name now flows from `OPENCALC_BRAND_NAME` into the discovery document, the session chrome and the editor's own toolbar and About dialog, carried to the editor bundle on its iframe URL. Escaped at all three edges: XML attribute, query string, and DOM text — a brand arrives on a URL, so anybody who can send a link chooses it. | `a_brand_name_is_escaped_into_the_markup`, `the_brand_is_appended_to_the_editor_url_safely`, `editor.branding.spec.mjs` |
| DEP-03 | Redis op log never trimmed | Done | P1 | `RPUSH` with no `LTRIM`, `EXPIRE` or `DEL`, read back with `LRANGE 0 -1` every lease tick per document per node — while `since` carried a comment saying *"the log is compacted, which is what keeps this bounded"*. Nothing compacted it. Trim and expiry now happen **inside the append script**, the only moment the log is known consistent and already locked. The in-memory coordinator is bounded to the same window, which `contract!` caught. | `the_log_is_bounded…` (both backends) and `a_log_nobody_returns…`; mutation gives `kept 10250 against a window of 10000` and `no expiry (-1)` |
| DEP-04 | A lost coordinator is invisible to clients and to k8s | Done | P1 | `order()` failed its append and returned without acking, so clients resent forever, while `/healthz` — unconditional, and the only probe — kept saying `ok`. Now the append's refusal is sent back as `Refused{NotSaving}` and `/readyz` is a separate probe that 503s when `peers()` fails, leaving liveness alone so the node is drained rather than restarted. | `a_refused_append_is_reported_to_the_client_rather_than_swallowed`, `a_node_cut_off_from_the_coordinator_reports_itself_unready` |
| DEP-05 | Drain cannot finish inside a stop grace period | Done | P1 | Sequential saves, 10s each, no global deadline: thirty documents needed five minutes against Docker's 10s default, and the remainder was SIGKILLed mid-drain — the loss the drain exists to prevent. Now bounded-concurrent under a **global** deadline, keeping the per-document timeout so one hanging callback cannot eat the budget. Compose sets `stop_grace_period: 40s` against a 25s deadline. | `the_drain_stops_inside_its_deadline…`; mutation to the sequential shape gives *"the process never stopped"* at 5.04s |
| DEP-06 | No metrics | Done | P1 | `/stats` returned two integers, so "are saves failing?" could only be answered by tailing logs — which no alert can do. `/metrics` now serves ten counters and two gauges in Prometheus text, hand-written rather than adding a dependency to a server that reads untrusted files. | `metrics_count_a_save_that_really_happened` asserts the **delta**; mutation gives `0 -> 0` |
| DEP-07 | CI never built an image | Done | P1 | Both images now build in CI and the standalone stack is brought up and probed, so a Dockerfile that stops building fails here rather than at an integrator. **Published.** **Release shape decided (owner's call), and executed as `server-v0.0.0`.** **Two images** (owner's decision, after working the architecture through): `casualoffice/calc` and `casualoffice/wopi`. The split follows the one the code already makes — ADR-012/014 keep the collaboration server unable to mint tokens and holding no per-document state, which is what lets one node own a document and the cluster scale by adding nodes; WOPI needs both a minting key and a per-file lock, so folding it in would trade that property away. Collabora and ONLYOFFICE ship a single image because their daemon *is* both; this project chose otherwise, deliberately and on the record. `casual-calc-host` says in its own first paragraph that it is the demo integrator, so it ships as a role inside `calc` rather than as a third public name. One name because that is what was asked for; three containers because the admission budget reads the *container's* cgroup ceiling and this *process's* RSS, so co-tenanting the three would have the collab server measure only itself against a limit all three spend from, believe it had headroom it did not, and admit work until the OOM killer took every document on the node including unsaved work — the precise failure the 85% mark exists to prevent. Published on a **`server-v*` tag**, which `release-sdk.yml` already reserved for exactly this reason: once two release workflows watch one tag namespace, tagging either fires both. So image and SDK versions move independently, as required, and **nothing can publish until such a tag is pushed** — the hold is enforced by the trigger rather than by anybody remembering it. Credentials are in place as `DOCKER_USERNAME` / `DOCKER_SECRET`. Two things were owed at release and not before: a registry **overview**, which did not publish and is now `DEP-18`, and the **marketing site**, which is updated. Sequenced after the remaining P0/P1 work and immediately before `TAURI-001`. **The publishing half is now built, and deliberately dormant.** `release-images.yml` publishes `casualoffice/calc` (the collaboration server) and `casualoffice/wopi` (the adapter) on a **`server-v*`** tag — the namespace `release-sdk` had already reserved for it, precisely so image and SDK releases cannot fire each other. Multi-arch (amd64 and arm64), with an SBOM and a build-provenance attestation naming the repository, workflow and commit, on the same argument `CI-005` makes for npm. A prerelease never moves `latest`, and a tag that is not a semantic version is refused before anything is built. `fail-fast` is off across the matrix because a half-published release is worse than a failed one: the half that landed is already public. **The hold is enforced by the trigger, not by memory** — there is no branch trigger, and the manual path defaults to a dry run — and `tools/check-release-hold.py` asserts exactly that for every `release-*.yml`, so a future workflow cannot quietly acquire one. Registry pages live in `docs/registry/` and are published with the image, so an image cannot land undescribed and the text is reviewed like anything else. **Still owed at release, and not before:** the marketing site pointing at the published images. **A consequence worth naming:** `casual-calc-host` is not published. It calls itself the demo integrator, which is why — but it is also what serves the editor and stores documents in the standalone quick start, so that path still needs a `docker compose build`. Say the word and it becomes a third image. **Owner's decision: `casual-calc-host` is published after all**, as `casualoffice/calc-host`. It is the demo integrator and it is also what serves the editor and keeps the documents, so leaving it out meant the standalone quick start needed a `docker compose build` rather than a `docker run` — a worse first five minutes than the smaller public surface was worth. Three images now, each with a registry page the release gate insists on.  **Released as `server-v0.0.0`.** Main was verified green at `d2b9755` first — publishing three public images from a commit whose CI had not been seen pass is not a thing to do quickly. The tag fired `release-images.yml`, which published `casualoffice/calc`, `casualoffice/wopi` and `casualoffice/calc-host`. The marketing site followed publication rather than preceding it: `deploy.html` claimed *"No published image ... not yet pushed to a registry"*, and changing that before the images existed would have made the page assert something untrue — the same failure this repository files rows about, pointed outward at users. | `docker-build` job; images published on `server-v0.0.0` |
| DEP-13 | Redis itself is still a single unreplicated box | Done | P1 | Three things, unequal, and the row's framing was wrong: **"Redis is a single box" was hiding three defects in *this* repository**, not in Redis. **(1) The link never re-dialled.** It was a `MultiplexedConnection`, which does not: when the socket died the driving task ended and every later command — on it and on every clone, which is what the coordinator hands out — failed for the life of the process. **A Redis that merely restarted cost a restart of every node**, which is most of what "a single failure stops ordering cluster-wide" meant in practice. Now a `ConnectionManager` with a bounded budget, and subscriptions re-subscribe: the same defect had a quieter symptom there, because a dead message stream simply *ends*, indistinguishable from "nothing was published", so a document silently stopped renewing its lease while the node went on serving it. **(2) The epoch fence was `<` where it must be `!=`.** An appender *ahead* of the store looks impossible and is exactly what a failover produces — the promoted replica is missing the claim that raised the epoch. Under `<` that leader was believed, **and so was whoever the rewound store thinks holds the lease**: two live leaders, each committing locally before appending, and the one that loses the revision CAS is permanently diverged with no resync built. Verified by reverting it: the append returns `Ok(1)` where it must return `Fenced`. The change refuses strictly more and accepts nothing new, so it cannot introduce divergence. **(3) TLS.** The `redis` crate had no TLS feature compiled, so **every `rediss://` URL was refused at parse time** and no deployment could encrypt the coordinator link at all. Now `rediss://` with `OPENCALC_REDIS_CA` for a private CA and optional mutual TLS; certificates against a plaintext URL, and `#insecure`, are refused at startup. **Not done: replication and failover** — Sentinel over Cluster mode is `ADR-020` in `77`, and it stops there on purpose: Redis replication is asynchronous, so a failover can lose an append that was already acknowledged, which contradicts ADR-014 §4's durability promise. Shipping Sentinel without deciding that would make this row *look* finished while leaving a silent-loss path open. **What made it invisible:** the client-side half looked like infrastructure rather than code, and `docs/65` asserted nodes "recover by re-claiming" — describing the design, not what was built. **Replication and failover are now built (`ADR-020`, Accepted).** Sentinel over Cluster mode, with `min-replicas-to-write` as the decision rather than a detail: a collapsed in-sync set makes the coordinator **refuse** a write instead of accepting one it will lose, which turns the silent loss into a shape `ADR-012` already defines and clients already handle. Verified by starting a coordinator with the floor at zero and watching the append be accepted — *"this is the write a failover loses, acknowledged to a client that will stop resending it"*. The node **checks** the floor at startup *and after every re-resolution*, because the setting is per server and the standard mistake is configuring the original primary and nothing else. **What the design note did not foresee, and the reason this needed building rather than reasoning about:** `SENTINEL FAILOVER` demotes the old primary *in place*. The socket stays healthy, reads keep working, every write returns `READONLY`, and the driver classifies that as not worth retrying — so `DEP-13`'s re-dial reaches nothing and the node refuses every edit for the rest of its life. Re-dialling cannot fix it; **re-resolving** can, which is why the trigger is the error rather than the socket. A private CA is **refused** against a `+sentinel` URL rather than accepted and ignored, because this driver builds the resolved primary through a path that cannot carry one. | Two real failovers — a demotion and a kill — with ordering resuming and every acknowledged revision still in the log; a write with the in-sync set collapsed refused and the log not grown |
| CI-013 | A presence test's margin was below the platform's clock | Done | P2 | `platform (windows-latest)` failed on `answering_keeps_a_participant_in_the_roster_rather_than_expiring_their_cursor` — 270 passed, 1 failed — while every other platform was green. **Not a defect and not a retry.** The test asserted a participant who *is* answering is **not** expired, using a 60 ms presence TTL against a 20 ms ping: three pings of headroom. Windows' default timer granularity is about 16 ms, so two late scheduler slots on a loaded runner expire somebody who answered, and the build fails for a property that held. **A margin below the platform's own resolution is not a tight test, it is a coin flip** — and a suite that fails at random is one people learn to re-run, which is how a real failure gets waved through. Five pings of TTL (400 ms) and a wait of three whole TTLs, so the scheduler may be late and the assertion still means what it says. **Widening did not blunt it**: removing the `roster.heartbeat` that keeps a connected-but-quiet participant alive still fails it, `left: 0, right: 1`. **Its siblings were checked and left alone.** Two other helpers use 10 ms ticks, and both assert an expiry *happens* — a polarity a slow runner makes more likely, not less. Only this one asserted the negative, which is the direction that flakes. | A responsive participant stays in the roster on a runner whose clock ticks every 16 ms |
| CI-012 | The failover behaviour ADR-020 exists for is unexercised in CI | Done | P1 | Both halves of the coordinator work skip on the runner: the TLS tests need `redis-server`, and the whole `ADR-020` failover harness needs `redis-server` **and** `redis-sentinel`. They run locally — two servers, three sentinels, quorum two, a demotion failover and a kill failover — and in CI they print a skip and pass. So the property this project just decided to depend on is asserted nowhere that gates a merge, which is the same shape as a metric that is scraped and always zero. **Fixed, and the fix is two things.** The runner gets `redis-server` — which brings `redis-sentinel` with it — because the service container was never enough: it gives a Redis on 6379, and these tests start their *own*, since a failover needs two primaries and three sentinels it can promote and kill. **And the skips are now the assertion.** Installing the binaries fixes it today; nothing stops a runner image changing, or a test growing a new precondition, and going quiet again — and a green job would say nothing either time. So the job greps its own output and fails if a coordinator test announces it was skipped, naming which. That is the general shape of the defect this row is about, and it is worth stating: **a test that skips and passes is indistinguishable from a test that ran**, exactly as a counter that is scraped and always zero is indistinguishable from nothing happening. Both were live in this repository this week. | `redis-server --version` and `redis-sentinel --version` on the runner, and the job fails if any coordinator test prints its skip line |
| SRV-07 | A log-bound test is wall-clock flaky | Done | P2 | `the_log_is_bounded_rather_than_growing_forever::in_redis` takes a lease with a one-second TTL and then issues 10 250 appends; under full-suite load that outruns the expiry and it fails `Unled`. **Reproduced on unmodified main** before being blamed on the change that found it, which is the right order. It passes in isolation. A test whose result depends on how busy the machine is teaches people to re-run rather than to read, and this project has already paid for that habit once. **Measured rather than guessed at.** The appends take **0.64 s** against a key expiry of **4 s** — a six-fold margin, which is exactly the size a loaded runner eats, and why it passed alone and failed in the suite. The bound being asserted has nothing to do with time, so the lease is now long enough that no amount of load can reach it: 0.64 s against 2 400 s, a **3 750-fold** margin. Still an expiry rather than none, so a test that leaks a lease does not leave one behind for ever. Every other test in the file keeps the shared one-second TTL, because a handful of appends inside a second is fine — this one does ten thousand two hundred and fifty. The mechanism was confirmed by shrinking the lease until it reproduced the exact `Unled` the original failure showed, rather than by assuming the diagnosis. **Why it matters more now:** `CI-012` just made the runner execute these tests for real, so a wall-clock flake here would red main intermittently — and an intermittent red teaches people to press re-run instead of to read, which is the habit this project has already paid for. | 10 250 appends complete against a lease that cannot expire; shrinking it reproduces `Unled`, so the diagnosis is demonstrated and not asserted |
| DEP-15 | Four RUSTSEC advisories, one a live vulnerability | Done | P0 | `dependency-policy` went red on every branch at once, which is the signature of a newly published advisory rather than of anything a PR did. Four: `RUSTSEC-2026-0258` (**h2 unbounded empty DATA frames** -- a real denial-of-service, reachable on the HTTP/2 stack all three servers expose), plus three `unmaintained` notices (`rustls-pemfile`, `rustybuzz`, `ttf-parser`). **Fixed by upgrade, not by suppression, wherever an upgrade existed.** h2 -> 0.4.16 clears the vulnerability. `rustls-pemfile` is *gone from the tree*: `axum-server` 0.8 dropped it, and our own direct use moved to `rustls-pki-types`. Only the two with no published successor are ignored, individually and with reasons, tracked as `DEP-14`. | `cargo deny check advisories` passes with no vulnerability and no blanket relaxation |
| DEP-16 | The fuzz tree resolves its own lockfile, and nothing audited it | Done | P1 | `cargo audit` reads one lockfile. `fuzz/` resolves its own, and CI **compiles** it -- so a second dependency tree was outside the supply-chain gate entirely. It was still pinning `h2` 0.4.15, the version `RUSTSEC-2026-0258` is against, and linking it, while `dependency-policy` reported no vulnerabilities. Found because `DEP-15`'s workspace-lock update made `cargo fuzz build` re-resolve, which tripped the existing `git diff --exit-code Cargo.lock` assertion and turned `fuzz-build` red on main. That assertion is the only reason this surfaced at all: it checks that the lock is *stable*, not that it is *safe*, and it caught this by accident. **Fixed**: the fuzz lock is resolved to match (h2 0.4.16, axum-server 0.8.0, rustls-pemfile gone), and `cargo audit --file fuzz/Cargo.lock` now runs beside the workspace one, because a separately-resolved tree will drift again. Verified with the job's own pinned toolchain rather than an approximation of it -- `cargo +nightly-2026-07-20 fuzz build` then the diff -- and the new audit step is RED against the lockfile main carried an hour earlier. | Both lockfiles are audited, and the fuzz build leaves its lock unchanged |
| DEP-14 | The text-shaping stack is unmaintained | Done | P2 | `rustybuzz` and `ttf-parser` are both unmaintained with no published successor (`RUSTSEC-2026-0206`, `RUSTSEC-2026-0192`); ttf-parser is reached only through rustybuzz, so it leaves when the shaper does. Neither is a vulnerability -- they are "the author has stopped", not "this is exploitable" -- which is why they are ignored in `deny.toml` by id, with reasons, rather than by relaxing the category. **The destination is already half-built:** `casual-calc-render` ships `skrifa` (the crate ttf-parser's own advisory recommends) for outlines and uses rustybuzz only for glyph ids, so moving the shaper to the same `fontations` family makes the two agree by construction instead of by a hand-kept mapping. Amends ADR-018. **Done.** The shaper is `harfrust` — the same HarfBuzz algorithm, maintained under the harfbuzz organisation, built on `read-fonts`. Neither `rustybuzz` nor `ttf-parser` is in the lock file, and **both `deny.toml` ignores are deleted**, along with their `.cargo/audit.toml` entries. `cargo audit --deny warnings` exits clean; `cargo deny check bans licenses sources` passes. **`skrifa` moved 0.43 → 0.46, and that was the point rather than housekeeping.** The old argument for handing shaped glyph ids straight to `skrifa` was that a glyph id indexes the font's own tables, so the same bytes give the same ids to both — true, and true of *two different parsers that happened to agree*: rustybuzz read with `ttf-parser`, skrifa with `read-fonts`. On skrifa 0.43 the tree still held **two** `read-fonts` versions (0.40 beside harfrust's 0.43), which would have left exactly that two-parser argument in place under a new name. At 0.46 both resolve to one. The agreement is structural now. **The bundle shrank**: 7,553,505 → 7,547,191 bytes, and `cargo tree -p casual-calc-wasm` shows no shaper at all, so ADR-018's "native only" decision still holds rather than being assumed. **Behaviour held by the test that was already there**: Hebrew still comes back in visual order — the reverse of memory order — asserted against a naive `cmap` lookup rather than fixed glyph ids, so it tests the *relationship* and survived the shaper being swapped underneath it. That is what made this a dependency change rather than a rewrite. | Neither crate is in the lock file, and the two `deny.toml` ignores are deleted |
| CI-008 | The wasm gate's exclusions were a hand-kept copy of the host list | Done | P1 | `cargo check --target wasm32` excluded the two host crates by name. Adding a third broke the job on `main`, because the list was a copy of a fact the manifest already states. It is now derived from `server/` — the same boundary `dependency-policy` reads. | `cargo check --target wasm32-unknown-unknown --workspace $(derived)` exits 0 where the hand-listed form exits 101 |
| CI-009 | The ADR-012 boundary gate only checked one of the three hosts | Done | P1 | It grepped `crates/` for `casual-calc-collab-server` alone, so a crate depending on `casual-calc-host` or `casual-calc-wopi` was told the boundary was intact — while the workspace manifest states the rule for all three. Found by looking for the *class* of CI-008 rather than fixing only the job that went red. The host list is now derived, so a fourth host is covered on the day it is added. | A crate mentioning any host fails the gate; verified against all three |
| CI-007 | The fuzz workspace's audit could not read the project's advisory decisions | Done | P1 | `cargo-audit` reads `.cargo/audit.toml` relative to its working directory, and the step ran `cd fuzz`, where no such file exists — so the fuzz tree was audited against no decisions at all and failed on `RUSTSEC-2025-0134`, an entry already sitting in the root config. It now runs from the root with `--file fuzz/Cargo.lock`, so there is one list read from one place; a copy in `fuzz/.cargo/` would fix today and drift tomorrow. | `cargo audit --deny warnings --file fuzz/Cargo.lock` exits 0 where `cd fuzz && cargo audit` exits 1 |
| CI-005 | Supply-chain and provenance gates | Done | P1 | Workflow permissions turned out to be done already — all four are least-privilege — so the remainder was two inputs and one attestation. **Container bases were floating tags**: `debian:bookworm-slim` is retagged whenever Debian rebuilds it, so the same Dockerfile produced a different image with nothing in the repository changing — the hole the action SHA-pinning already closed, on the larger input. Pinned by digest, with a gate. **npm publish had no provenance**, so a consumer's only evidence a package came from this repository was the package saying so; `--provenance` plus `id-token: write` now mints an attestation naming repository, workflow and commit. And the `sdk-types` job I added last week used `npm install` rather than `npm ci`, so its result depended on the day it ran. | The digest gate fails on an unpinned base, verified both ways |
| DOC-025 | Live docs and ADRs reconciled | Done | P1 | This consolidation is its deliverable. **Its gate now exists and passes**: `tools/check-doc-claims.py` (`DOC-032`) enforces "no doc claims a gate CI does not run" mechanically, over 55 documents. Remaining, and both smaller than they looked: the pending-ADR list, now tracked as `ADR-019` for the overdue capability-trait decision; and the "142 stale claims", which **has no list, no tool and no artifact** — the string occurs once in the whole repository and `git log -S` shows it was introduced by the consolidation commit itself, not by any audit. A backlog nobody can enumerate cannot be worked down, only carried, so the honest next step is to re-run an audit that produces a list rather than to chase a number. **The "142" is now replaced by a number that is checked.** A mechanical audit of every code path the documents name — the one class of claim that *is* enumerable — found **53 paths and 3 wrong**, not 142. Two were name drift, where the thing exists under another name and the document was corrected — the view-only sample is `sdk/examples/viewer`, and the marketing documentation page is `webapp/docs.html`; `55` named both under names that were never on disk. One was a promise nothing keeps — a host-toolbar sample listed as shipping and never built — which is a row (`SDK-010`) and not a doc edit, because deleting the line is how a promise to integrators disappears without anybody deciding to drop it. `tools/check-doc-paths.py` runs in CI so it cannot drift again. **What the first version got wrong is worth keeping:** it treated every backticked path as a claim, and flagged `fuzz/.cargo/` — which `CI-007` names as the fix it *rejected* — and every `docs/65`-style citation. A gate that cries wolf is a gate somebody switches off, so citations are excluded and deliberate absences are listed with their reason, self-expiring: if the path ever appears, the exemption fails. **Closed, and the last item was one nothing was looking for.** The two remaining threads are both settled: the pending-ADR list emptied when `ADR-019` was decided (the capability trait, rejected — `RND-11` was built the other way), and the unenumerable "142 stale claims" was replaced by a measured 53 paths of which 3 were wrong, all fixed, with `check-doc-paths.py` in CI so it cannot drift. **What was left was a status.** `ADR-021` still read `Proposed` — while `RND-11` had been built to it, shipped and closed, its `Polyline`/`Polygon`/`Wedge` variants sitting in the display list with an arm each in the renderer. `RND-11`'s own row said "Proposed" in the same sentence that recorded the work as done. A decision record's status is load-bearing: it is the first thing a reader consults before following the argument, so a register that says `Proposed` about a decision the codebase is built on says the opposite of the truth about exactly the decisions that mattered enough to build. **Review cannot catch it** — the register and the row are different files and neither diff is wrong on its own — so `tools/check-adr-status.py` does: a `Done` row naming an ADR must find it settled. One direction only, deliberately; an `Accepted` ADR with no finished row is ordinary, since deciding without building is allowed. `Rejected` counts as settled, because a row can be closed *by* rejecting an approach, which is exactly what `ADR-019` was. **Seen red**: putting `ADR-021` back to `Proposed` fails with the row named. Passing: 22 decisions, 95 finished rows, none left proposed. | No doc claims a gate CI does not run, and no doc names code that is not there |
| DOC-027 | No self-hosting page on the public site | Done | P2 | `docs/65` is a thorough operator reference and lives in the repository, where somebody deciding whether they *can* self-host never sees it. The site had an SDK guide and nothing for the deployment half, so the answer to "what does running this actually involve" was a clone away. **Added `webapp/deploy.html`**, reusing the SDK page's shell so the two cannot drift apart visually: the two stacks, the host contract's four endpoints, the two addresses that get confused, every environment variable an operator changes across all three servers, tokens and JWKS, reverse proxying, TLS, health and metrics, storage and backup, WOPI, and a symptom-to-cause table. **Written from the code, not from memory** — every claim was checked against the source before it was written down, and the numbers came out of the binaries that read them. Gated by `tools/check-deploy-docs.py` in CI: every `OPENCALC_*` and every `opencalc_*` metric the page names must exist in a server. One direction only, because the page is deliberately what an operator changes rather than an exhaustive dump. Seen red four ways — a variable no server reads, a metric nothing emits, a rename *in the code* (the drift that actually happens), and the page gutted, which every other check would have passed. Says plainly what is missing: no Helm chart, single Redis, no version history, no published image. | The page names only settings that exist, checked in CI |
| DOC-028 | The homepage shipped two identical nav entries | Done | P2 | `DOC-027` linked the new self-hosting page into every page's navigation with a script that guarded against a repeated **link** and not a repeated **label** — and `index.html` already carried a `Self-host` anchor to its own section, so the homepage went out with two identical-looking entries pointing at different places. Reported by the user, not by anything here: it is not a broken link, not invalid HTML, and invisible to any test that renders the page successfully. It only looks careless. **Fixed** by giving the guide one name wherever it is linked — `Deploy docs`, parallel to `SDK docs` — leaving `Self-host` to mean the homepage section alone, and adding the guide to the homepage nav beside the SDK one so the two documentation pages are reachable the same way. Two stale links found alongside: the `#selfhost` section and the SDK page both still sent readers to `docs/65` on GitHub as "the deployment guide", which is the page `DOC-027` built. Gated by `tools/check-site-nav.py` in CI — no two entries in one nav share a label, every relative target exists, every `#anchor` is on the page. Seen red four ways, the first reproducing the exact mistake and its exact message. | No nav has two entries with the same label |
| DOC-030 | No published performance or sizing figures | Done | P2 | Asked for directly, and the honest first answer was that only *limits* were written down — `max_documents`, `max_participants` — which are configuration, not capacity. `PERF-10` landing gave the missing half: **83.64 bytes a cell**, measured as the peak resident delta on the CI runner. The self-hosting page now carries two matrices: what each engine operation costs against its budget, and what a node of a given size holds at three editors a sheet. Every figure re-derived from the benchmark report before publishing. **The useful finding is that the engine is not what runs out**: 3 000 editors at two edits a second is 6 000 edits a second, which at 0.25 µs each is about a sixth of one percent of a core — so sizing is memory and sockets, not calculation. Marked throughout which machine each number came from, because memory is the Linux runner and the timings are an Apple laptop, and conflating them would make the page confidently wrong. | The site answers "how big a box do I need" with measured numbers |
| PERF-12 | No server-side load harness | Done | P1 | **Measured.** `tools/casual-calc-loadtest` drives N documents by three clients over real WebSockets and reads the node's own `opencalc_resident_bytes`. Against a Linux container: idle 6.6 MB; 10 docs/30 editors 11.6 MB; 25/75 12.4 MB; 50/150 15.9 MB — **10 200 of 10 200 edits acknowledged**, p50 16-27 us, p99 1.1-2.8 ms with no degradation from 50 to 150 concurrent editors. Marginal cost between the 25- and 50-document runs is **143 KB per open document**; 190 KB is the figure published. **The harness was the defect.** Run against no server at all it printed a report of zeroes and exited 0, so wiring it into CI would have produced a job that goes green whether the server works, is broken, or was never started — the same shape as a submission silently dropped, which this repo has paid for before. It now exits non-zero when nothing was acknowledged, and when acknowledged falls short of sent, because the percentiles are computed only over edits that came back and so are blind to dropped work. That guard caught a real failure within a minute of existing: the first scaled run reported zeroes because every join was refused by the URL allowlist. **What made it invisible:** a load test's output is a wall of plausible numbers, and zero is a plausible number. **Correction it forced:** `webapp/deploy.html` sized from cells alone, which under-sizes a many-small-document deployment by 3.3x - at 1 000 cells the collaboration overhead is 70% of the cost. The rule of thumb now adds the measured 190 KB instead of hiding it in a x1.4 fudge. | `--documents N --clients 3` reports RSS and p99, and **fails** when nothing was acknowledged or the server dropped work |
| CI-011 | A registry blip reds the whole run, and nothing retries | Done | P2 | A `lint` job failed with `download of config.json failed -> curl failed -> [35] SSL connect error` **0.3 seconds** after starting, having compiled nothing, and passed unchanged on re-run. There was no `.cargo/config.toml` and no `CARGO_NET_RETRY` anywhere, so all 13 cargo jobs red the run on any registry hiccup. The gap between the fetch starting and the error is far too short for any backoff, so cargo never classified it as worth retrying - which means **raising `net.retry` would have been an inert fix**, configuration that looks like a remedy and changes nothing, and stops anybody looking again. The retry is therefore in the shell, in `.github/actions/cargo-warmup`, where it does not depend on how cargo classifies a curl code. **Why it is worth a row:** a red main that means nothing is what teaches everyone to ignore a red main that means something. **What makes it rot:** not breaking - a fourteenth job added later that quietly lacks the step, and a flake nobody can explain because the other twelve are fine. Hence a gate rather than a convention. | `tools/check-ci-retry.py`: every cargo job warms the registry, exemptions named with their reason |
| SRV-05 | A join that cannot fetch its document fails silently | Done | P2 | A join whose document fetch failed was refused with `.map_err(\|_\| Refusal::NotSaving)` — **the `_` was the entire defect**, discarding the origin's own explanation, which the HTTP transport had already built. Fifteen clients failed to join a URL nothing was serving and the log recorded not one line, while the neighbouring allow-list refusal logs once per attempt. **The metrics were worse than missing.** Four counters were declared, exposed on `/metrics` and documented in `65` while being incremented **nowhere**: `fetches_ok`, `fetches_failed`, `refused_capacity` and `revisions`. So the number an operator alerts on read a hard zero *through a total outage in which every join failed* — the dashboard was not absent, it was lying, which is strictly worse because it answers the question wrongly instead of not at all. The first three are now wired; `revisions` is `SRV-06`. A fetch failure logs WARN with the reason (connection refused, timeout, status code); bytes that arrive and are not a workbook — an origin answering a login page with 200 — log ERROR and move a new `opencalc_documents_unreadable_total`. **The URL is redacted at the query string**, because a host's download URL carries the credential that authorises the download and this path fires exactly when somebody is about to paste their log into an issue. Second half: `OPENCALC_ALLOWED_HOSTS` is compared with the port stripped, so `127.0.0.1:8080` is not a stricter rule but a **dead** one; the refusal now names the entry and says the port can never match, conditionally so a healthy allow-list is never blamed. **What made it invisible:** the failure sat on the server's side of a client-visible refusal, so the only symptom was an edit that never arrived — and the counter that existed to catch exactly that had never been connected, which no test asked about because the exposition format parsed cleanly. | Six tests, each seen red first — the strongest asserting on an **empty log buffer**; five mutations red, including a URL logged whole and a hint that fires unconditionally |
| SRV-06 | `opencalc_revisions_total` is a permanently-zero metric | Done | P2 | `opencalc_revisions_total` was declared, exposed and documented, and incremented nowhere, so a cluster ordering edits reported itself idle. Wired at **both** points a revision is actually ordered — the standalone commit, and the clustered leader *after* a successful log append, so a fenced leader counts as `appends_refused` rather than as throughput. Counted in **operations, not submissions**, because a revision number counts operations; a per-call increment would report a rate the revision numbers themselves contradict. **The gate is the real deliverable and it closes the class.** It reads the `AtomicU64` fields off the `Metrics` struct declaration and asserts each is both exposed and incremented by shipping code, with comments and string literals stripped first — so a comment cannot stand in for an increment, which was proved by mutating the increments into comments *describing themselves* and watching it still fail. Driven from the struct rather than a hard-coded list, because a list in a test is the same artefact as the list in the docs: right the day it is written and silently short the day somebody adds a counter, which is exactly how four of them died. Its honest limit, stated rather than discovered: it proves coverage, not reachability — a counter incremented on dead code would pass — which is why the scenario tests remain. **What made the whole class invisible:** a Prometheus exposition of zeroes parses exactly as well as one of real numbers, so every test that scraped `/metrics` passed. | `every_counter_on_metrics_is_exposed_and_incremented_somewhere`, plus a standalone and a clustered scenario; seen red by declaring an unwired counter and by commenting out an increment |
| SEC-018 | A bracketed IPv6 host can never match the allow-list | Done | P2 | **Worse than the row said, and the row was mine.** It called this a usability defect on the grounds that the parser fails closed. It does not always: `check_url` stripped the port with `rsplit_once(':')`, which cuts `[::1]` at the last colon *of the address*, and the same sloppiness **admitted** `https://[::1]:8443x/…` and `https://[host.example]/…` by misreading a malformed authority as a host on the list. Verified here by reverting the parser and watching `should refuse https://[::1]:8443x/files/1` fail — it was allowed. Replaced with a real authority parser covering `host`, `host:port`, `[literal]` and `[literal]:port`, where the bracketed form must parse as an `Ipv6Addr` and anything trailing the brackets can only be a port. Allow-list matching now compares as an `IpAddr` as well as textually, so `::1`, `0:0:0:0:0:0:0:1` and `[::1]` are one host — an entry that *is* the address being refused, spelled differently, is the same trap as the port one. **What made it invisible:** the failure presented as a refusal, and refusals look exactly like the allow-list working. | Twelve adversarial authorities refused, a bracketed literal matched by any spelling, and the port hint diagnosing `[::1]:8443`; seen red against the old parser |
| SRV-03 | Admission was a guessed count, not the resource it was protecting | Done | P1 | A node refused the thousand-and-first document and the two-hundred-and-first participant whatever it was actually using. Those numbers guessed at what a machine could hold, so they were wrong in **both directions at once**: a 64 GB node turned work away at 8 GB, and a 2 GB node accepted a thousand documents it could not hold. **Both counts now default to 0, meaning unlimited**, and admission reads the node's own memory — the **cgroup** limit rather than the host's, because a 2 GB container on a 64 GB machine is a 2 GB node, and sizing from the host is how a container is killed while `free` looks healthy. **It refuses at 85%, not at 100%, and that is the one thing not taken literally**: reaching the ceiling is an OOM kill, which takes every document on the node including everybody's unsaved work, where refusing one document costs one person a click. `VmRSS` rather than `VmHWM` — the opposite of `PERF-10`'s choice and for the opposite reason: a peak never falls, so a node that spiked once would refuse for ever, including after everything was evicted. An unmeasurable node **admits**, since a machine with no cgroup and no `/proc` must not go from unprotected to useless. Four mutations red: refusing only at the ceiling, an unmeasurable node refusing everything, cgroup v1's unlimited sentinel taken literally, and the peak read for the current size. **Two defects of my own reached main with it, and both are now pinned.** The admission check locked the document registry to count, and one caller already held that lock — so the server **deadlocked on the first join**. It did not crash and logged nothing: the socket went quiet for twenty seconds and then for ever, which is the worst way for a server to fail because nothing anywhere says what happened. Caught by `PROD-13`'s end-to-end gate, which had passed on the run immediately before. The locking and non-locking checks are now separate functions, and a test asserts with a *timeout* that the one called under a held lock answers — a deadlock is invisible to any test that waits for a result. Second: the **participant** check asked the same question, which counts *documents*, so once a node reached its document cap every arrival at an already-open document was refused. Joining costs a connection, not a document. Third, and the one to remember: `0` used to mean *no capacity* and now means *no limit* — a silent inversion of a safety setting, in the dangerous direction, pinned by its own test. Verified end to end: nine clients across three documents, 54 edits sent and 54 acknowledged, p50 15 µs and p99 3.5 ms, where before the fix it hung for ever. | A node admits until its memory is short, and never because of a count |
| SDK-008 | `WorkbookSession` escapes | Done | P1 | Checked all three the row names; only one was a hole. `apply_raw` already refuses in read-only and clears the untouched original; `config_mut` changes settings that are read at open, and toggling read-only mid-session is the host's prerogative. `workbook_mut` is the real one: it hands out the right to change anything, including into a state `validate` rejects. It cannot be supervised — the session does not see what happens through the reference, and validating per call is not affordable because `validate` walks every cell and hosts take that reference per keystroke. So the check is where being wrong stops being recoverable: `save` validates before writing. A corrupt workbook in memory is a bug; one that became a file is data loss. | `an_invalid_workbook_is_refused_rather_than_written` (`OC-MDL-0001`), `taking_a_mutable_reference_gives_up_the_untouched_original` |
| SDK-009 | npm packages ship no types | Done | P1 | `@opencalc/engine` had declarations (wasm-bindgen writes them); `@opencalc/sheet` and `@opencalc/react` had none and no `types` field, so every TypeScript host embedding this got an error and a reason to use something else. Declarations are now hand-written beside the hand-written JavaScript they describe, shipped by the build, and **compiled against**: a consumer written the way the docs tell an integrator to write one, under `strict`. A second check reads both files, because `tsc` proves the surface composes and cannot prove it is real — a `.d.ts` naming a method nobody implemented type-checks perfectly. | CI `sdk-types`: removing the tag map, an event, or adding a method that does not exist each fail it |
| SDK-010 | An SDK sample promised to integrators was never built | Done | P2 | `55` §What ships where lists five runnable samples; four existed. `sdk/examples/host-toolbar` — *our* commands on *their* buttons with all our chrome off — was never written, and **the row said it was assembly rather than design, which was wrong.** Building it found why nobody had: the element could `listCommands()` and `commands({hidden, disabled})` — **discover** them and **suppress** them — and there was no way to *run* one. A host with its own toolbar could learn our command ids, hide the ones its product has no use for, and could not put a single one on a button. The missing third of the surface. `run(id)` now exists on the element, on the editor, and in `embed.d.ts` — declared in all three because `SDK-009` established that a declaration nothing compiles against drifts. **A command *is* its control**, so running one activates that control rather than a second dispatch table beside the first; two tables drift, and the drift is a menu item that works and a host button that does nothing. An unknown or disabled id is **refused at the call**, because a host that mistypes should learn there and not from a user months later. **What made it invisible:** the sample was listed as shipping, so nobody built it and found out it could not be. \| Three browser tests: a command runs by id, an unknown id is refused with the way to find the real ones, and **every id `listCommands()` returns has a control behind it** — the two halves agreeing is the property, and a list that lies is exactly what a spot check misses | `sdk/examples/host-toolbar` runs, drives the editor through `commands()` with the chrome off, and is built by whatever builds the other four |
| COL-38 | A parse failure is reported as `CannotMerge` | Done | P2 | A message the server could not read was answered `CannotMerge`, which names the **transform** — the one part that was working. The socket stayed open, the heartbeat was answered, the roster showed the participant present, and every edit went nowhere; it cost a live debugging session to find. `Refusal::Malformed` now says what happened, and a client seeing it knows not to retry — the same message will not parse the second time either. Shipped on the 5→6 bump it shared with COL-40. | `a_message_that_cannot_be_parsed_is_not_called_a_merge_failure` |
| DEP-09 | Load-aware placement never consumed | Done | P2 | `announce` publishes load and `elect()` picks the least-loaded peer, but `elect` is called only from tests. **Done.** `place()` reads the load back: least-loaded peer, **never this node** (a client that obeys a redirect to the node that just refused it never stops), **only one with a public address**, and **only one under the cap**. Ties break on id through `elect`, so two nodes refusing at the same moment send their clients to the same place rather than splitting them. A **new setting** was unavoidable: `Peer.advertise` is the *internal* address — a service name on the cluster network — which is precisely the address a browser cannot use. `OPENCALC_PUBLIC_URL` is per node and optional; unset, placement does nothing, which is right behind a single load balancer where a redirect would return through the balancer to an arbitrary node. **On the wire, a field and not a `Refusal` variant.** ADR-016 says to move `PROTOCOL_VERSION` when old and new read the same message *differently*; a new variant is worse than that — an unknown tag makes the whole message unreadable, so a client that used to show "this node is full" would show nothing. `Stopped.elsewhere` is `#[serde(default, skip_serializing_if)]`, verified against the shape an old client actually has, and absent from the JSON entirely when there is nowhere to name. The version does not move. Redis carries it in a **sibling key**, not a third tuple element: `peers` reads the body with `from_str::<(String, u32)>` and `continue`s on error, so a longer tuple would make a previous-build node *silently drop* new nodes from its peer list rather than fail loudly. The client follows **one** redirect per session — two full nodes would otherwise name each other and bounce a client forever — and `redirectTarget` refuses anything but `ws:`/`wss:` and any downgrade off TLS, since the value decides where a token gets sent. **Seen red without the fix**: dropping the filters fails 4 of 6 placement tests; making the lookup return nothing fails the end-to-end with `left: None`; removing the URL guards fails 2 of 5 client tests. **A test that could not fail, found on the way.** `a_full_node_turns_a_new_document_away_rather_than_degrading` used `connect(addr)` — which asks for `DOC` while its token names `other-document` — so the join was refused as `NotAuthorised` and the node's cap was never reached. Its assertion was `matches!(answer, Stopped { .. })` with no reason in it, so it passed on the wrong refusal for its whole life: a capacity test that never tested capacity. It only surfaced because `DEP-09` needed that exact scenario and got `NotAuthorised` back. Now asserts the reason, as do both new tests. | A full node redirects instead of refusing |
| DEP-10 | Multi-tenancy documented, not implemented | Done | P2 | One `Verifier`, one key set, and `iss` is never checked — so one tenant's minter can mint for another's document. **P0 for anyone multi-tenanting.** **Done, and not the way the row implied.** Checking `iss` against a policy would have satisfied the acceptance and fixed nothing: with one shared key set the claim is a field the minter fills in, so any tenant holding a trusted key can still sign a token naming another. The boundary has to be the *binding* — `Verifier` now holds one `Trust` per issuer, each with its own key set, and a token is verified against **the keys of the issuer it names**. `OPENCALC_ISSUERS=iss=jwks_url,…` configures it; `OPENCALC_ISSUER` pins the issuer for a single-tenant deployment, where the verified `iss` (never the unverified one used to select the key) does the refusing. The refresh loop iterates every trust, or a second tenant's keys would be frozen at boot — `SRV-02` reintroduced one tenant along. **Seen red without the fix**: routing to the first key set and dropping the binding returns `Ok(Claims { iss: "https://b.example", … })` for a token tenant A signed naming tenant B — a live session on another tenant's document. **What made it invisible**: the acceptance criterion was satisfiable by a check that does not hold. A test asserting "a token from the wrong issuer is refused" passes against a policy comparison; only a test with *two key sets* can tell the two designs apart. | A token from the wrong issuer is refused |
| DEP-11 | Secrets are env-vars only | Done | P2 | No `*_FILE` fallback, so the signing key is visible in `docker inspect` and `/proc/1/environ`. **Done.** New `casual-calc-secrets` crate (std only, no dependencies) reading `NAME_FILE` in place of `NAME`, wired to `OPENCALC_SHARED_SECRET`, `OPENCALC_ADMIN_TOKEN` and `OPENCALC_REDIS_URL` — the last because `redis://user:pass@host` carries a password. Both forms set is **refused, not ranked**: a precedence rule is how a deployment that believes it moved to files keeps running on the variable it forgot to delete. An empty file is refused too, being a mount that did not happen. `unknown_secret_files` names any `OPENCALC_*_FILE` no server reads, because the plural typo leaves a mount that is present, correct and ignored — indistinguishable from a missing secret. **Seen red without the fix**: ignoring the `_FILE` branch fails 6 of 7 tests, and the one that stays green is exactly `the_environment_variable_still_works`. **Two things this turned up.** The environment cannot be written in a test — edition 2024 makes `set_var` `unsafe` and the workspace forbids unsafe — so the rule was split into `resolve(name, inline, file)`, which a test can reach; a rule reachable only through a mutation no test may perform is a rule with no test. And `check-deploy-docs` refused the first draft: the `_FILE` names were synthesised at runtime, so no server named them and the page documented strings that did not exist. Making them literal is what made the misspelling warning possible. A third: the published-secrets test matched `OPENCALC_SHARED_SECRET` as a *prefix*, so it read the `_FILE` path `/run/secrets/…` as a published signing key. Narrowed to require a separator after the name, and **checked that narrowing it did not blunt it** — a real value planted in `.env.example` still fails. | A mounted secret file is read |
| DEP-12 | No backup/restore, and partly non-atomic writes | Done | P2 | No stated RPO; `create`, `upload` and `settings.json` use plain `fs::write`, so a crash mid-upload leaves a truncated file. **The non-atomic half is fixed; backup/restore is still open, hence Partial.** `create`, `upload`, the kept versions and `settings.json` all wrote with `fs::write`, which truncates first — so a crash, a full disk or a killed container in between left a file that exists, has the right name and is **half a document**. That is worse than no document, because a backup taken afterwards copies the truncated version over the good one. All of them now write beside and rename. Two details that carry the weight: the temporary is a **sibling**, because `rename` is only atomic within a filesystem and across one it silently degrades to copy-then-delete — reopening the very window this closes, in the place nobody would look; and a failed rename removes its temporary, because a directory filling with `.part` files is how a disk fills up for reasons nobody can explain. Document is written before metadata, so a crash between them leaves an invisible document rather than a listed one whose bytes are missing. **Honest about the gap:** whether a crash mid-write leaves the old bytes cannot be asserted without crashing, so the sibling property is what the test pins — and a mutation putting the temporary in `/tmp` reds it. **The backup half is now closed too, and the honest part is what it does not promise.** The recovery point is the backup interval and nothing shortens it — no continuous archive, no point-in-time recovery — which `65` now states before offering any procedure. The insight worth keeping: **a backup of a running store is a set of atomic files, not an atomic set of files.** Each write is atomic since the first half of this row, so no single file is ever half-written; but a document is three files written one at a time, so a live copy can hold the bytes without the metadata. Neither is corruption and both are recoverable — the defect was that **nothing detected it**, so a restore looked complete and was quietly short.
