The host now scans its store at startup and names what does not line up: `invisible` (bytes nothing lists), `dangling` (a listing whose bytes are gone), `unfinished` (a `.part` from a write interrupted before its rename) and `stranded` (versions of a deleted document, costing space nobody has accounted for). **Reported, never repaired**, deliberately: an invisible document may be somebody's only copy and a dangling entry may be the record of one that should be hunted down — opposite treatments, and only an operator knows which. | A crash mid-write leaves the previous file intact; a store missing a document, a listing, or carrying a leftover `.part` is named at startup — each seen red by neutralising the scan |
| PERF-06 | Range precedents scanned linearly | Done | P2 | One edge per range, scanned per popped cell: `O(dirty × ranges)`. 8.98x for 10x the sheet — headroom, not viability. **Fixed** with the row-band buckets the code's own comment had already named as step four: edges are indexed by the rows they cover, so a popped cell looks only in its own band. Ranges too tall to bucket (a whole column spans every band, so filing it everywhere costs more than reading it once) go to a `tall` list that is always scanned — bounded by how many *open* ranges a workbook has rather than how many ranges. Measured, same `dirty_total` both ways so the answer is unchanged: per-edit cost goes 4→7→13→26 µs across 200/400/800/1600 ranges before, and 7→7→9→8 µs after; the 1600-range pass drops 42.2 ms to 12.8 ms. **Below ~300 ranges the index is slightly slower** — the bookkeeping does not pay for itself until there is something to skip. **On the gate, which took three attempts to make real.** Being wrong here is silent — a missed bucket leaves a formula un-dirtied, so it keeps a stale value and nothing errors. The first test compared two copies of the lookup written in the test itself, so mutating the production loop changed nothing it read; the second called `attach` after clearing a cell to simulate churn, but `attach` returns early for a cell with no formula and never detaches, so it churned nothing. Only the third — driving `dirty_from` itself, against a linear-scan twin, plus a direct assertion of the index's own invariant — caught all of: tall edges never scanned, one band indexed instead of all, the sheet dropped from the key, the moved edge not reindexed after `swap_remove`, its old entries not removed, and stale slots left behind by a detach (which answer correctly until `ranges` shrinks past them, then panic). | Per-edit cost flat in the number of ranges |
| PIV-05 | A pivot's fields named columns its source does not have, and saving produced a file Excel refuses | Done | P0 | Switching-blocker #8, and the measurement found a **third case neither of the two the brief offered**. Not `CF-01` (the bytes are preserved — open-and-save is byte-faithful) and not `CHT-05` (the features are not "retained but not honoured"). **The importer wrote an OOXML cache-field index into `source_column`, a slot the model documents as a zero-based offset into `PivotTable::source`.** Those two index spaces coincide only while every cache field is a source column — and a calculated field or a group field ends that immediately, because Excel appends a cache field for each. So a three-column source got `fld="3"` and `x="4"`. **Three consequences, none of them announced.** On refresh, an out-of-range column reads as `None` without complaint: the group row field rendered `(blank)` on every line, the calculated measure rendered an entirely empty column, and a field captioned *Pct of total* showed raw sums. On save, `<field x="4"/>` against `<cacheFields count="3"/>` is an out-of-range index — **a repair prompt on the whole workbook, not merely a lost pivot**. And the compatibility report said `pivotTable Mapped / Preserved`: an active claim of full mapping over a pivot that had just lost two fields and a display semantic. Database fields are now **counted rather than assumed to be a prefix** — an identity map would shift every field after a mid-list derived one onto its *neighbour's* column, which is a pivot quietly summarising the wrong measure. A field that cannot be resolved is left out and named in the report. Strictly a narrowing: nothing that used to resolve to an in-range column stops resolving. **No model type changed, so `PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays 7.** `RND-05` is a non-issue here and the worker checked rather than assumed: `grep -ril pivot` returns **0 files** in both renderers — a pivot is materialised as ordinary cells, so there is one path and no divergence risk. | Six mutations, each red, including a deliberate **over-drop guard** — without it, "drop everything" would have passed every loss-counting assertion. **One mutation proved less than it looks and was reported**: removing the `@formula` detection changed the model not at all, because the field falls past the source width either way; it was caught only by the report test. Honest reading — that detection buys report *precision*, not correctness |
| CI-022 | Main still had no CI signal on the commits between two close merges | Done | P1 | `CI-013` fixed this once and it stayed half-broken, which is the interesting part. That row made `cancel-in-progress` asymmetric — `false` on main, `true` elsewhere — after 19 of 40 main runs came back `cancelled`. The rate fell to **5 of 30** and did not reach zero, and nobody looked again because the gate written alongside it passed. **`cancel-in-progress: false` protects a run that has already started. It does not protect one that is still queued**: when a third request joins the group, GitHub discards the pending one. Two merges landing minutes apart therefore left the commit between them with no CI at all. Established rather than reasoned — the cancelled run inspected had **zero jobs**, so it never ran. Main now gets a concurrency group **per commit**, so nothing can supersede it, queued or otherwise. A pull request keeps the per-ref group, where superseding is the point. **The durable finding is about the gate, not the config.** `check-workflow-concurrency` asserted the *expression* and the *outcome* stayed broken — a gate green over exactly the thing it was written to catch, which is `CI-017` and `CI-018`'s shape a third time. It now checks the group as well. | Seen red by reverting the group to one per branch: `gates main, but its concurrency group is the same for every commit on main`. Measured before: 5 of the last 30 `ci` runs on main cancelled, none of which had started a job |
| DOC-047 | Nine open rows were already fixed, and `docs/12` was wrong in eleven places | Done | P1 | A sweep of all 44 open rows against the code, prompted by finding **two stale-`Open` rows within ten minutes** — `A11Y-01` and `DOC-035`, both shipped, both still claiming Open. A tracker that says a P1 is open when it is closed sends the next round of work at something already done, which is the cost `UX-MAP-03` paid twice. **Nine were already fixed.** Seven more keep their status but had a stale *mechanism* corrected, including two whose citation named the wrong method. **`docs/12` §4.1 was wrong about six chords, not five, and its tally understated the product by nine rows** — the corrected count is 21 of 23 landing, not twelve. Five further claims were wrong: the `?doc=` route that never existed, the pagination that does not exist, the op log that is not a history, seven citations of a document number that was retired, and four of eight figures in its own summary table drifted within a single day. The worker also marked what it **could not** verify — editor command counts, cold-boot timing, the frame numbers — as not re-measured rather than silently carried forward, which is the distinction the document had lost. Superseded claims are **struck rather than deleted**, each naming the row that closed it. | Verified row by row with a citation each; the non-obvious ones are named in the report — `PIV-02` genuinely needs Excel and cannot be settled here |
| CI-023 | The protocol-version gate was defeated by one word | Done | P2 | Proved by running, by the `DOC-047` worker, against a gate written two days earlier: *"`PROTOCOL_VERSION` is **at** 5"* exits 0, and deleting `at` reds the identical sentence. `docs/08` had sat two bumps stale behind exactly that phrasing. **A gate a synonym can walk around is not checking the claim, it is checking a phrasing** — and it read as an answer, which is why nobody re-measured. Now the first number within a short window, whatever prose sits between. A **transition** is still excluded, because *"6 → 7"*, *"moves to 8"* and `docs/61`'s *"goes to 2 … it has since moved on"* are records of a change rather than claims about now — and the exclusion had to be widened too, since the original only knew about arrows. | Seen red across four phrasings (`is 5`, `is at 5`, `sits at 5`, `currently 5`) and green across both transition forms. The widening immediately caught the real stale claim in `docs/08` that the narrow pattern had been missing |
| DOC-048 | A document path with the right number and the wrong name passes every doc gate | Done | P2 | Proved by running: a path naming **document 68** with the visual-audit filename — the exact collision `DOC-035` closed — passed **all four** documentation gates with exit 0. (The bad path is described rather than written here: a blanket correction of stale citations rewrote this very row's example into a live one, so the row documenting the defect stopped containing it. The same shape as the row about unescaped pipes containing an unescaped pipe.) **Closed by extending `check-doc-references`:** a citation that spells out a filename must name a file that exists. It immediately found two live stale citations in this tracker. `check-doc-references` checks that a cited *number* resolves and nothing checks the *name* after it, so a rename leaves every citation pointing at a file that no longer exists while every gate stays green. Proposed rule: a backticked `docs/…` path ending in `.md` must exist. Named without a proposal, because it needs a design rather than a pattern: **nothing compares a status stated in prose against the row's real status**. `check-doc-references` already parses both halves — what is missing is a decision about what counts as a status claim in prose. That is the class that produced `DOC-046`. | Open |
| UX-SEL-01 | A right-click threw away the selection it was aimed at, and every verb in the menu followed | Done | P0 | Reported from a running editor: `Ctrl+A` took the block, a right-click **inside** it left a single cell selected, and the menu that opened then acted on that cell rather than on what the user was looking at — **Delete included**. **The cause was ordering, not policy.** `mousedown` fires *before* `contextmenu`, so the right button reached the ordinary select path and collapsed the selection before the menu opened. The `contextmenu` handler already had the correct rule and never got the chance to apply it: keep a selection you clicked **inside**, move one you clicked **outside**, which is what Excel and Sheets both do. Its header branches even carry a comment about this exact hazard — *"every verb in it then acted on the wrong target, including Delete"* — so the rule was known and only partly enforced. The fix is one button guard on the `mousedown` selection path, leaving `contextmenu` as the single owner of the policy. **Not a blanket "ignore the right button"**, which would have broken the commoner gesture: right-clicking a cell you have not selected yet. | Four browser tests, both halves asserted. Seen red by removing the guard: `the selection survived the right-click` and `the dragged range survived` go red while the *outside* case stays green, which is what shows the fix is targeted rather than a disable. Column and row selections are covered too — they take different branches, and the report only exercised two of the four kinds. `SEC-022` is what this class costs uncaught: *"Remove Duplicates destroyed data the user never selected"* |
| DOC-046 | Three files cite `COL-46` as an open P0, and one of them guards a safety default with it | Done | P1 | Found by the `DOC-047` sweep. `COL-46` is **Done**; `AGENTS.md` §"Current state" and `CLAUDE.md` both still call it *"a live P0"*, and `AGENTS.md` says fifteen crates where there are sixteen. **The sharp one is `webapp/editor.presence.js`**, which justifies the `canShare` safety default with *"`COL-46` is an open P0"*. A reader can correctly verify that citation, find the row closed, and change a safety default **on a true observation about a stale comment**. The default has since been flipped for other reasons and the remaining justification is `COL-50`, so the comment is now wrong twice over. Outside the sweep's `docs/`-only boundary. **Corrected in all three.** `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` now name `COL-50` as what replaced it — an insert meeting a delete not converging for a formula *range*, where each answer is the one Excel gives for its own order, so it is a semantics decision rather than a defect to patch. The crate count is sixteen. **`webapp/editor.presence.js` was the one that mattered**, because its stale sentence was load-bearing: it justified a *safety default* with a row a reader could verify as closed. It now records that `COL-46` is Done, that `canShare` is `true` in `standalone` and `desktop`, and that the capability remains for two reasons that do **not** expire — a host owns its own document in `embedded`/`wopi`, and `COL-50` is still open and named in the dialog before it will connect. | Corrected in three files; `check-doc-references` now catches a citation naming a file that does not exist |
| PIV-06 | Deleting a column inside a pivot's source silently re-aims its fields | Done | P1 | Found by the `PIV-05` worker looking for other producers of the same fault rather than stopping at the one it fixed. `structural.rs` shrinks `pivot.source` on a column delete and **never renumbers `source_column`**. Delete column A of a three-column source and the pivot silently groups by a different field; delete B and a measure goes blank. Excel renumbers. Same root cause as `PIV-05`, different producer — and it means the corrupt-file path is **narrowed, not closed**: import can no longer produce an out-of-range index, but a structural edit still can. The worker deliberately did **not** add a clamp in the writer, because `write_workbook` has no compatibility-report seam, so the clamp would be a silent drop *and* would mask this. | Open |
| CI-014 | A signature change broke the one consumer outside the workspace | Done | P1 | `FID-28` added an argument to `transform`. Every local gate passed — `cargo build --workspace`, `cargo test --workspace`, clippy, docs, the oracle — and `fuzz-build` broke on `main`. `fuzz/` is its **own Cargo workspace**, so `--workspace` never reaches it; the local `check-rust.py` runner did not build it either. CI's authoritative gate for this is the `fuzz-build` job, which is what went red. The whole tree was green while a consumer no longer compiled, and the only thing that knew was a CI job that runs after the merge. `check-rust.py` gained a `fuzz targets` gate. `cargo check`, not `build`: the failure mode is a signature that no longer matches, which type-checking catches in seconds where a sanitizer build takes minutes. The fuzz target now derives its sheet identities from its own base workbook the way the real callers do — passing an empty slice would compile and exercise a path no client takes. | Restoring the break makes the new gate fail with `error[E0061]: this function takes 4 arguments but 3 arguments were supplied` |
| CI-015 | `cancel-in-progress` treated a push to main like a superseded branch, so main's own CI was cancelled 19 times in 40 | Done | P0 | Reported as *"main is red"*. Main was not red — **main had no result at all**, which is worse, because a missing answer reads as a failing one and neither is true. `1aca07f` merged, its `ci` run was cancelled at 47 seconds, and nothing re-ran it. `concurrency: {group: ci-${{ github.ref }}, cancel-in-progress: true}` applied one rule to every ref. **For a pull request that rule is right** — the run is superseded the instant the branch moves, and cancelling it destroys nothing anyone wanted. **For a push to main it is backwards**: there is one run per merge, nobody is queued behind it, and its result *is* the record of whether main is releasable. The config spent the record to save runner-minutes that were not scarce. Counting the last forty main runs: **19 cancelled, 18 success, 2 failure** — so for months the honest answer to "does main build?" was unavailable about half the time, and #129's merge run had died the same way at 3m26s. **What made it invisible: the failure mode of a cancellation is that it looks like a decision somebody made.** A red job invites a look; a grey one reads as housekeeping, and nineteen of them in a row never once prompted the question. Fix is the asymmetry the original expression missed — main is excluded by name, branches still cancel. The gate found a **second** instance on its first run, `pages.yml`, which is exempted on the record because it is a deploy rather than evidence: the run that cancels it publishes newer content to the same place. | `tools/check-workflow-concurrency.py`, wired into `repository-policy` and seen red — restoring `cancel-in-progress: true` gives ``ci.yml: gates main but cancel-in-progress is `true`.`` and exit 1 |
| CI-016 | The gate against reusing a tracker id could not see the ids most likely to be reused | Done | P1 | Found by walking into it. Adding `SEC-01` for the row above, then renaming it to `SEC-004` to match the three-digit convention, **collided with the zip-bomb row already holding `SEC-004`** — and `check-tracker-ids.py` passed anyway. Its logic was never wrong; its input was. `TRACKERS` listed three documents and not `docs/14a-ARCHIVE-CLOSED-WORK.md`, so **246 ids were invisible to it** — and an archived id is exactly the id a person reads as free, because it is no longer in the tracker they are looking at. CLAUDE.md's standing warning is that five rows have been added with an id already in use and that `FID-13` named two unrelated changes for weeks; this is why that kept happening despite a gate built to stop it. **Widening it immediately surfaced two live collisions**, both genuinely different work under one id: `SRV-03` is *admission decided by memory* in the tracker and *standalone broadcast ordering* in the archive; `WASM-01` is *the editor cannot open anything but .xlsx* and *case-insensitive replace trapping on non-ASCII*. The **archive** rows were renumbered to `SRV-08` and `WASM-03`, not the tracker ones, because the tracker meanings are cited from code — `SRV-03` in the collab server's admission tests and the loadtest, `WASM-01` in `io.rs` and three places in the editor — and the archived meanings are cited nowhere. | `tools/check-tracker-ids.py` now reads 4 documents and 440 ids; it reports `exit=1` with both collisions named before the renumbering, and `440 unique` after |
| PROD-08 | Fuzzing reaches some parsers | Done | P2 | The gate asked for every untrusted parser fuzzed and a corpus from several producers. Three were unfuzzed and all three were reachable from an upload: **`casual-calc-import`** — the *primary format*, and the largest parser in the project — **`casual-calc-io`**'s delimited reader, and **`Workbook::from_snapshot`**, which a resume, a cluster hand-off and a peer all feed. **They found three defects immediately, two of them P0.** `MODEL-01`: serde_json reads floats approximately unless told otherwise, so a tenth of every document's numbers changed across a snapshot — fixed. `IO-01`: CSV export writes `0` for anything under 5e-16 — filed with a reproducer. `IO-02`: `write_delimited` walks the bounding box rather than the populated cells, so 16 KB of input exhausted 2 GiB — filed. Corpus now spans **five producers** (Excel, LibreOffice including its OOXML *export* filter, openpyxl, XlsxWriter and this repo), read from `fixtures/` in place so seeds cannot drift from checksummed fixtures. **The snapshot hold became a proof**, as the ODS amplifier did: it used to skip drifting inputs, and now asserts they do not drift — so losing `float_roundtrip`, which is one word in a manifest that no other test would miss, fails the fuzzer by name. **What made all of it invisible:** every existing test compared this code to itself. The `xlsx` budget assertion nearly repeated that mistake — the shipped cell limit is 8 000 000 and no fuzzer input can reach it, so the check would have been an unreachable branch; each pass also imports under a tight budget, forced red to prove that path is entered. **Still uncovered, named rather than implied:** the JWKS keyset parser, and clipboard `text/html`, which is genuinely untrusted and needs a JS instrument cargo-fuzz cannot provide. | `cargo fuzz run` clean on xlsx (147k), delimited (587k) and snapshot (8.35M) executions; `tools/check-fuzz-lock.py` keeps the separate lockfile honest |
| MODEL-01 | A snapshot round trip changed a tenth of every document's numbers | Done | P0 | `serde_json` writes with `ryu` — exact and shortest — and **by default reads with a fast approximation**, so `from_snapshot(to_snapshot(w))` was not `w`. Measured independently here at **10.97%** of values at ordinary spreadsheet magnitudes coming back one ULP out, and **278 of 2001** through the real snapshot path; `12.805579049246651` is one of them. `f64::from_str` on the *same string* is correct every time, so neither the writer nor the format was ever at fault — only the reader. A snapshot crosses a **resume, a cluster hand-off and a save back to the host**, so a tenth of a document's numbers changed in the last bit the first time it took any of those paths. That breaks the first two priorities outright: never produce a wrong cell value, and identical input gives an identical model. It converges after one step rather than compounding, which is the only mercy in it. Fixed by enabling `float_roundtrip`, declared on each crate that parses rather than relied on through feature unification — unification does not apply to a crate built alone, and `cargo test -p casual-calc-model` is exactly where the drift would be tested for. **What made it invisible:** every test compared a workbook to itself *within one process*, and the defect only exists across the serialize boundary. Found by the `snapshot` fuzz target of `PROD-08`, the first thing that ever compared a workbook to itself across it. | 2 000 generated values and the known one survive a snapshot bit-for-bit; seen red without the feature at 278 of 2001 |
| IO-01 | CSV export erases any number smaller than 5e-16 | Done | P0 | `format_number` derived its decimal places from the value's magnitude and clamped them to 15, so for `abs(n) < 5e-16` the clamp refused the digits the value needed: `format!("{n:.15}")` rendered `0.000000000000000` and the cell was written **`0`**. `1e-16`, `1e-300` and `5e-324` all vanished on one save; `-1e-300` became `-0` and then `0`, so the text was not even a fixed point and the **sign** went on the second save. Rounding happens in exponential form now — `{n:.14e}` is 15 significant digits *at any magnitude* — which fixes both ends: the clamp had also been silently denying the promised 15-digit rounding to anything above `1e15`. Notation follows the exponent, positional inside `[1e-6, 1e21)` and exponential outside, so `1e300` is not written as 301 digits. **One trap found in the building:** rounding `f64::MAX` to 15 digits rounds it *out of* `f64`, and a naive version wrote `inf` — which the reader refuses to type, turning a number into text. Guarded, and the guard is fuzz-reachable. Found by the `delimited` target of `PROD-08`; its hold for this defect is gone and is now an amplification assertion instead. | A value below the old threshold survives a round trip **as text and as bits**, and a second save is a fixed point; seen red as `left "0,0,0,0.000000000000001"` against the real values |
| IO-02 | A 63 KiB CSV makes a save allocate a gigabyte | Done | P1 | `write_delimited` walked the sheet's **bounding box**: three cells at `A1`, `(0, n-1)` and `(n, 0)` needed `2n+3` bytes of input and cost `n^2` cell visits, so 16 KB exhausted 2 GiB and a 63 KiB upload made a save allocate a gigabyte — reachable from the file picker and from a WOPI host. **The reasoning matters more than the fix.** A field's position *is* its address, so the two candidate answers were "compact towards A1" and "write cells where they are". Compaction is wrong: it moves every cell and breaks the parse-write-parse fixed point the crate's whole conservative typing policy rests on. What the bounding box added on top of writing cells where they are was **trailing empty fields**, and those are the one thing provably not data — `a,,,` and `a` read back as the same row with the same cell. So dropping them is **lossless**, and needs no cap, no truncation and no compatibility report. Residual stated rather than hidden: an empty row still costs its 2 bytes, so a lone cell at the last grid row writes about 2 MiB of line breaks — linear in the address and bounded by the grid, not quadratic. | A three-cell sparse sheet saves in proportion to its cells; seen red at 4 004 004 bytes for three cells, and the fuzz target now asserts the amplification bound directly |
| WASM-02 | The editor stores an infinity the file format cannot spell | Done | P2 | Found by the worker fixing `IO-01`. `casual-calc-wasm` types a typed-in cell with `trimmed.parse::<f64>()` and **no `is_finite()` check**, unlike `casual_calc_io::type_field`, which requires finite. So typing `1e400` in the editor stores `Number(inf)`; a CSV export writes `inf`, and reopening that file reads it back as **text** — a number that left as a number and returned as something else. The fix belongs in the typing path, not the writer: there is no CSV spelling of infinity that reads back as a number, and the model should not hold one. **Unreachable by the fuzzer**, which is why it needed a person: the reader can never produce this input, so no round-trip target can generate it. | **Done.** The typing path in `casual-calc-wasm` now requires `n.is_finite()`, so `1e400` is kept as the `InlineString` `1e400` — the person sees their own text back rather than a number they never wrote. Gated by `tests/browser/editor.infinite-input.spec.mjs`, which asserts both that the cell holds no infinity and that it survives a save-and-reopen unchanged, with an ordinary `42` beside it so the test cannot pass against a build that mangles everything. **Seen red without the fix**: dropping `n.is_finite()` and rebuilding the bundle failed both tests with `Received string: "inf"`. **What made it invisible**: the defect is in the *typing* path, and every fuzz target we have starts from a reader. No reader can produce an infinity, so no round-trip target could ever generate the input — the same 'comparing our code to itself' blind spot as the ODS reader fed our own documents. A browser test, not a Rust one, because the browser is the only place the typing path runs. |
| MNT-001 | Empty catches swallow command failures | Done | P2 | 105 swallowing catches, audited and classified rather than mass-rewritten. Thirteen were real: a user-invoked command whose refusal left a control that did nothing — the Delete key, Edit ▸ Clear, autofit, comment and defined-name deletion, View ▸ Gridlines. Five were worse than silent: the UI proceeded anyway, so a refused comment delete emptied the textarea and a refused name delete removed the row while the workbook kept the name. The rest are read-only probes whose fallback *is* the answer, and were left alone. | `editor.errors.spec.mjs` — a protected sheet's refused Delete is reported *and* the cell still holds its formula |
| MNT-002 | Integration modules are review monoliths | Done | P2 | Three files, 34k lines: `editor.js` (13.2k), the WASM bridge (11.5k) and formula dispatch (9.8k). **All three are done: formula dispatch here, the WASM bridge as `MNT-004`, `editor.js` as `MNT-005`** — because they are different jobs with different gates rather than more of the same. **The seams were not chosen — they were already written down.** `functions.rs` carried 18 `// --- Statistics ---`-style headings, and the split makes the module system enforce the taxonomy those comments had only been asserting: `aggregate`, `datetime`, `engineering`, `financial`, `info`, `lookup`, `math`, `matrix`, `special`, `stats`, `text`, with `mod.rs` keeping the name catalogue and the dispatch. Nothing exceeds 1.6k lines. One heading was **not** a seam and was distributed instead: *M6-2 built-ins* named a release milestone, so `MEDIAN` sat beside `TEXTJOIN` for no reason other than shipping in the same sprint. Its eighteen members went to their families. Re-exported flat (`pub(crate) use math::*`) so every call site names a function exactly as before: the dispatch is unchanged, and the diff is a move rather than a rewrite. **Proved to be a move, not a rewrite**: all **299** functions present before and after, none lost, none added, and every body identical once whitespace and visibility are normalised — the only textual changes are `rustfmt` rewrapping signatures that grew past the line limit from `pub(crate)`. Two real finds fell out of it: the `ODD*` bond family had been filed under engineering by proximity, and its family prose was a **doc** comment butted against `OddBond`'s own with no blank line, so rustdoc rendered a paragraph about nine functions as the documentation of one struct. | 205 eval tests, all 8 Rust gates including the three oracle steps; the catalogue test now scans every module and both its guards were seen red — an unlisted module (`left: 11, right: 12`) and a submodule arm missing from the catalogue |
| MNT-004 | The WASM bridge is one 11.5k-line module | Done | P2 | The second of `MNT-002`'s three. `casual-calc-wasm/src/lib.rs` was the whole editor API — 11,519 lines, 361 items, 261 exports — in one file. Now fifteen modules beside a thin root: `axis`, `calc`, `cells`, `clipboard`, `collab`, `data`, `formula`, `history`, `io`, `objects`, `sheet`, `structural`, `style`, `view`. **This row previously said the file had "no section headings". That was asserted, not checked, and it is wrong** — it has four. Four, in 11.5k lines, is close enough to none to have made the point, but the row stated a fact about the code that the code did not support, which is the thing this project files rows about. Corrected here rather than quietly. So the seams really were read off the exports rather than enforced from comments, and doing that turned up why the file resisted review: **topics recur**. Charts are defined at 2459 *and* 4297, comments at 1478 *and* 3281, formatting in four separate stretches. The split is therefore a reorder, not a slice — which is why the equivalence proof below matters more here than it did for `MNT-002`. Re-exported flat and **`pub`, not `pub(crate)`**: every one of these was a `pub fn` at the crate root, so a narrower re-export would have quietly removed items from the public API as a side effect of tidying. The modules stay private, so the root is still the only path in. Two items were misfiled by position and moved to where they belong: the `COLLAB` thread-local had landed in `history`, and `ChartWire` in `data`. | **341 functions before and after, none lost, none added, no body differing**; and the JS contract identical — **261 exports before, 261 after, none added or removed**. All 8 Rust gates including the three oracle steps, and 131/131 browser tests against a rebuilt bundle |
| MNT-005 | `editor.js` is one 13.2k-line script | Done | P2 | The last of `MNT-002`'s three, and the only one without a compiler. **No section headings at all**, so the seams had to be derived — and the measurement is what made the job tractable: 409 functions over **118 shared mutable bindings**; 102 functions (967 lines) touch no shared state, **233 (5,155 lines) only *read* it**, and 74 (5,105 lines) write it. ES modules give a **live read-only binding**, so everything in the first two groups moves with the shared state left where it is — no body edited, no assignment site rewritten. That is the whole design: the writers and the 118 bindings stay in `editor.core.js`; 6,098 lines went to twelve topic modules. Owner chose this scope over a full `state.js`-with-setters rewrite, which had no parser available to make the rename scope-aware. **Proved a move**: 409 definitions before and after, 409 *distinct* (counted as a list, not a set — an earlier version of this check keyed by name and would have hidden duplication), none lost, none added, no body differing; and 191 top-level non-function declarations, none of which left the core. | 131/131 browser tests |
| MNT-006 | Two defects the split introduced, and what let them hide | Done | P2 | Recorded separately because both are about *verification*, not about the editor. **A module's sub-imports carry no query.** The page loads `editor.js?v=<mtime>`; the new topic modules imported `./editor.js`. Two URLs are two module instances — every top-level listener registered twice, one keystroke committed two edits, and a single undo popped a phantom and looked like it had done nothing. That is *exactly* the defect `editor.smoke.spec.mjs:126` was written for, and its comment describes the original occurrence; it caught the same thing twice. The fix keeps `editor.js` as a thin entry owning the tag and re-exporting `editor.core.js`, so the core has one URL — and the entry keeps the public **name**, because the gates find the module by `script[src*="editor.js"]` and renaming it would have meant editing the tests to fit the change. **A green probe against an unwired build.** The generator wrote the twelve modules, then threw before rewriting `editor.js` — the banner anchor `let init, wasm;` had already been rewritten to `export let init, wasm;` by an earlier pass — and stderr was going to `/dev/null`. The modules sat as dead code, nothing imported them, and the load probe passed. What caught it was a *number*: `editor.js` was still 13,206 lines when it should have been ~7,800. A third, found the same way: a top-level `const geo = {…}` between two functions was swallowed into a moved function, because the extractor scanned back to the last line starting with `}` instead of counting braces. The equivalence proof compared *functions* only and was structurally blind to a `const` leaving the file — which is why it now checks top-level declarations too. | The three checks that would have caught each: a duplicate-aware definition count, a top-level-declaration census, and a line count on the file that was supposed to shrink |
| DEP-18 | The registry overview cannot be published with an access token | Done | P3 | Found by releasing. `server-v0.0.0` built and pushed all three images — multi-arch, SBOM, provenance, `0.0.0` and `latest` — and then **reported the release as failed** because the last step, syncing the Docker Hub page from `docs/registry/*.md`, got `Forbidden`. **This row's original reason was wrong and is corrected here rather than quietly.** It said Docker Hub's description endpoint refuses an access token and wants account credentials. It does not: `peter-evans/dockerhub-description` accepts a Personal Access Token, but the description endpoint requires the **`Read, Write, Delete`** scope, while pushing an image needs only `Read, Write`. `DOCKER_SECRET` has read and write, which is why it pushes three images successfully and then gets `Forbidden` on one README. So this is a scope decision, not a credential conflict. **And the decision is not obviously "widen it".** The push token would gain the power to delete published images in order to edit a web page — a strictly worse blast radius for a strictly cosmetic gain, in a workflow that runs on every tag. The narrower options are a *second* secret used only by this step, so the delete-capable token never touches the build, or leaving the overview to be edited by hand, which is a page that changes about once a release. `DOCKER_SECRET` is a PAT, which is the right thing for it to be — a password in CI is worse than a token that can only push — so this is a real conflict rather than a misconfiguration to correct. **Made non-fatal, not hidden.** The step still runs and still fails visibly, and the run summary now says whether the page synced. The reason is that a release reporting failure when the artifact is public and correct is the more dangerous of the two wrong reports: it invites a re-run of a workflow whose work is already done. `DEP-07`'s promise of a registry overview is therefore *owed and unmet*, which is what this row records rather than the workflow quietly succeeding. Routes: a scoped Docker Hub org token if one can update descriptions, or publishing the page some other way. Not decided. **Closed 2026-08-30 by the decision the row was waiting on: the scope was widened.** `DOCKER_SECRET` now carries `Read, Write, Delete`, and `87dea52` removed the `continue-on-error` the previous release had added — *"a step that can succeed must be allowed to fail; `continue-on-error` earns its place only while a failure is expected, and left on afterwards it is a step nobody is watching, which is how a page silently stops syncing"*. The narrower routes (a second secret, or editing the page by hand) were therefore not taken, and that is a decision on the record rather than a default. | The `Publish the registry overview` step in `.github/workflows/release-images.yml` is fatal again, so a page that stops syncing fails the release rather than being reported in a summary line |
| DEP-19 | A yanked `chacha20` reddened every branch at once, and no branch had changed | Done | P1 | `cargo audit --deny warnings` failed `dependency-policy` with *1 denied warning found: chacha20 0.10.1, yanked*. Nothing in this repository changed — the crate was yanked upstream, which turns a green lockfile red everywhere simultaneously and on a PR whose diff is JavaScript and CSS. Bumped to `0.10.2`; audit clean, workspace builds. **The reason `--deny warnings` is right here even though it costs a red day like this one:** a yanked version is the registry saying *do not use this*, and the alternative — treating it as advisory — means shipping a dependency its own author withdrew and finding out why later. The cost of the strict setting is an occasional unrelated red; the cost of the loose one is unbounded. **Fixed incompletely the first time, and the tracker said otherwise.** The row claimed *audit clean* on the strength of one workspace: `fuzz/` resolves its own lockfile, still held `chacha20 0.10.1`, and main stayed red through two merges. That is `CI-014`'s trap — the separate workspace `--workspace` does not reach — walked into again, by me, in a row whose own subject was a dependency. The CI step for it already existed and was already commented with this exact warning. A second confusion followed: running `cargo audit` **from inside** `fuzz/` reports `RUSTSEC-2025-0134` because `.cargo/audit.toml` — where that advisory is already decided, with a reason — is not found from there. CI runs `--file fuzz/Cargo.lock` from the repository root and does see it. Verified by reproducing both CI invocations exactly: exit 0 and exit 0. | `cargo audit --deny warnings` **and** `cargo audit --deny warnings --file fuzz/Cargo.lock`, both from the repository root as CI runs them |
| DEP-17 | A deployed build can serve a stale editor module | Done | P2 | A consequence of `MNT-005` that the dev server hid. A fresh entry tag over a cached module is the exact stale-module failure the `?v=` was introduced to stop, one level down. `Cache-Control: no-cache` on the editor's static files — cache it, but ask before using it. A 304 per module, and the question is gone. The wasm blob carries its own `?b=` and is unaffected. The service is built by `editor_service`, which `main` nests and the test calls, so the assertion cannot pass against a router nothing wired up. | `editor_modules_are_served_with_revalidation`; without the layer it fails `left: None, right: Some("no-cache")` |
| MNT-003 | The TLS test fixture raced itself, and broke main | Done | P1 | `certificate_files()` guarded on `!cert_path.exists()` and wrote the certificate **before** the key, so a second test thread arriving in that window saw the certificate, concluded the pair was ready, and was handed a key file that did not exist yet. Tests in one binary share a process and run concurrently, so they share the pid-named directory. It surfaced as `a_configured_certificate_means_the_socket_speaks_tls` panicking on `tls_config(...).unwrap()` — **on one platform, sometimes**, which is the shape that gets called flaky and re-run rather than fixed. The race was latent for as long as two callers existed; adding a third (`DEP-15`'s key-error test) made it likely enough to turn main red. **Fixed** with `OnceLock`, which closes the window rather than narrowing it: there is no observable half-written state left to race with. Proved by widening the window to 300ms — which reproduces CI's failure exactly, same test and same line — and then re-running with that widening still in place after the fix. | The suite passes with a 300ms delay planted between the two writes |
| UX-GRID-02 | `ensureVisible` ignores zoom | Done | P2 | The viewport rect is compared against grid units without dividing by `state.zoom`, so scroll-into-view overshoots at 200%. **The code was already fixed** — on 16 Aug in `0b5e08d`, with no test and without the row being updated, so it has been an ungated fix able to regress silently ever since. The gate is the work. It has to derive the viewport itself from three raw inputs (the rectangle, the zoom, the frozen origin), because the defect lives *inside* that calculation and a hook returning `ensureVisible`'s own answer would agree with the bug and assert nothing. Three cases: a cell reached at 200% sits wholly inside the real viewport, the same at 50% where the error reverses, and reaching a cell needs more scrolling at 200% than at 100% — the last stops a fix that ignores zoom entirely from passing the first two. Seen red under the original defect and under dividing the wrong way. | Scroll-into-view lands correctly at 200%, and is gated |

Three gates, on computed style rather than on the class being applied: the roster survives, the menus do not, and a single-user embed is left with no empty strip. | TAURI-003 | How the desktop webview reaches the engine — **decided: swap the binding** | Done | P2 | **Decision.** Option three: the editor keeps one codebase and the desktop swaps what its single `wasm` handle points at, so the shell calculates natively. The objection to option three was that nothing guaranteed the two implementations answer alike, and a divergence would be invisible until a user found it. **That objection is now answered rather than accepted**: `engine-trace.mjs` records every call and answer through the one handle and compares traces, and it was proved against a second engine making the exact `PERF-11` mistake — a formula printed absolutely instead of at its cell's origin — catching 1 divergence in 18 calls. A decision with a safety net rather than a hope. \|  The decision `TAURI-001` cannot proceed past. The browser editor makes **229 distinct engine calls** (101 mutating, ~129 querying) through a single `wasm` binding — so reusing it on the desktop means answering those 229 from native Rust, and the three ways of doing that differ by more than style. **Bundle the wasm build**: the editor works unchanged and the desktop app calculates in WebAssembly, which is the property `ADR-023` and [44](44-TAURI-DESKTOP-SHELL-DESIGN.md) exist to protect. **Reimplement as Tauri commands**: native calc, and a second surface the size of `casual-calc-wasm` to keep in step — `TAURI-002` is the evidence for that cost, where *one* 113-line function in one host stopped a second host typing into a cell. **Swap the binding**: the editor already funnels everything through one `wasm` handle and a `wasmApi()` accessor, so the shape exists; what does not exist is any guarantee the two implementations answer alike, and a divergence would be invisible until a user found it. Worth noting the third has a gate that could exist: run the same call sequence through both and compare, the way `oracle-diff` compares this engine against LibreOffice. That is a real design, not a hand-wave, but it is the sort of thing to decide deliberately. **The gate that made the third option checkable now exists**, built before choosing rather than after: `engine-trace.mjs` wraps the single `wasm` handle the editor already funnels everything through, drives one scripted session, and records every call and every answer. Run the same script against a second engine and compare the traces — the shape `oracle-diff` uses against LibreOffice. A `Proxy` rather than 229 wrappers, for the reason `UX-SITE-03` made the hard way: a rule that enumerates its subjects is one omission from being wrong. Answers are *summarised* rather than hashed, so a disagreement reads as `"=A2*2"` versus `"=A1*2"` and says where to look, rather than as two hashes that say only that you are in trouble. **Proved to detect what it exists for**: a second engine that gets one call subtly wrong — a formula printed absolutely rather than at its cell's origin, which is the exact `PERF-11` mistake the desktop shell itself made — is caught, 1 divergence in 18 calls. Without that evidence the option is a hope; with it, it is a decision with a safety net. | The desktop editor and the browser editor answer the same 229 calls identically |
| TAURI-004 | The desktop had no way to draw a native menu without a second copy of the menu | Done | P2 | *"If it is a desktop app it should behave like one"* — which means **the operating system draws the menu bar**, not an HTML strip inside a window. The obstacle was never Tauri; it was that a native menu appeared to need its own File/Edit/View definition, and **two definitions of one menu drift, with the drift always in the copy nobody looks at.** It turned out not to need one. The editor already funnels every menu item and toolbar button through a single command id — `data-oc-command`, `listCommands()`, `runCommand(id)` — so the native side can hold **ids and nothing else**: no closures, no duplicated labels, no second dispatch path. `menuModel()` returns that tree, and is derived from the **live DOM rather than the `MENUS` literal it is built from**, deliberately: the DOM is what `runCommand` dispatches against and what `applyCommandRules()` hides in read-only mode, so a literal-derived model would describe a menu the app does not currently have and every disabled entry would be wrong. Same reasoning as the engine trace preferring a `Proxy` to 229 wrappers. `?chrome=native` hands the bar over **before first paint** — hiding it afterwards shows it for a frame and then takes the space back under the user — and its nodes stay in the document, because `runCommand` dispatches by clicking them and a detached bar would give the native menu entries that throw. On the Rust side, `desktop/src/menu.rs` parses the model and translates shortcuts to `CmdOrCtrl+…`, so ⌘S on macOS and Ctrl+S elsewhere fall out of the platform's own resolution rather than out of this code guessing which machine it is on. **A comment that contradicted its own assertion, caught before it shipped.** The accelerator test claimed *a modifier with no key is not a shortcut* while asserting `Ctrl+` produced `CmdOrCtrl+Plus`. `Ctrl++` genuinely is Ctrl-and-plus and leaves an empty segment when split; `Ctrl+` leaves the same empty segment and means nothing. Now there is — a window, and **the operating system's own menu bar**. The menu is not defined here: `main.rs` asks the editor for `menuModel()` over the invoke bridge and holds ids and nothing else, so a click sends the id straight back through `runCommand(id)`. **Not one of `File`/`Edit`/`View`/`Insert`/`Format`/`Data`/`Tools`/`Help` appears anywhere in the Rust** — grep returns 0 — which is the proof there is one menu definition and two presentations rather than two definitions that drift. macOS's application menu and its `Quit` are added here rather than taught to the editor, because that is a platform convention and not a document one. | Builds and runs; the screenshot shows the eight menus at the top of the screen and the HTML bar gone from inside the window, with a 6MB `.xlsx` rendering. `check-rust.py`'s `desktop shell` gate compiles it. **Not yet verified: that a menu click dispatches** — macOS refused `osascript` assistive access, so the round trip is unproven rather than proven, and is written here as unproven |

Locked to the dominant axis by ratio, generously enough that a genuinely diagonal gesture keeps both components, because locking those would make the grid fight the hand. **A first test measured 1px of drift and nearly buried this.** The sheet was blank, so `clampScroll` held the view at the left edge and there was nowhere to drift *to*; with content to scroll through, one up-and-down gesture drifts **85.8px**. A performance or motion test on an empty document measures the clamp, not the behaviour. | `editor.scroll-axis.spec.mjs` (3): a noisy vertical gesture ends at `scrollX == 0`; a deliberate sideways swipe still scrolls; a diagonal keeps both axes. Seen red at `Received: 85.7999999999999` |

The accessibility mirror is hundreds of DOM nodes and its guard against a redundant rebuild hashes `JSON.stringify(geoItems)` — every visible cell, every frame — so while scrolling the hash changed each frame, the guard never fired, and the tree was rebuilt continuously. `updateScrollbars` then read `clientHeight`, forcing a synchronous reflow of everything that rebuild had just dirtied, to learn a number that only changes when the window does. **66.6ms → 16.7ms at 40 columns: 15fps to 60.** All three sizes measured now sit at the frame budget. **The first version of this fix was wrong and a test caught it.** Deferring the mirror unconditionally broke *a copied value pastes as itself*, which reads `#a11y-7-0` — the mirror is the only DOM a canvas grid has, so it is what a screen reader reads *and* what the tests read, and delaying it delays the announcement of a paste. It now defers only while the view is actually moving. **Still ahead, and larger:** `draw()` calls `JSON.parse(session_cells(...))` over the whole viewport every frame — `session_display_list` exists and has no caller outside a test. That is the remaining structural cost. | `editor.scroll-budget.spec.mjs` (3), asserting the mechanism rather than a frame time, because a timing assertion on CI measures the runner: zero mirror rebuilds across 40 scroll frames, a rebuild once the view settles, and a populated tree when it does |

| MOVE-01 | There was no move primitive, so every drag-to-reorder gesture was impossible | Done | P1 | Four gestures failed together — drag a column header, drag a row header, drag the selection border, and the multi-range that depends on the same machinery — and it was never a UI oversight: `grep` for a move operation returned `MoveSheet` and nothing else. `MoveColumns`, `MoveRows` and `MoveRange`, with `before` in **pre-move coordinates** because that is where the drop indicator the user is looking at actually sits. All three `guard_protected` first, and `session_move_range` guards **both** rectangles — the source is emptied and the destination overwritten, and guarding one is the `SEC-006` shape of a guard that measures less than the edit. All three are `RecalcPlan::Full`: `CALC-01` one level down, since the precedent graph is keyed by `(sheet, CellRef)` and a move renumbers every address. **The reference rule.** A line move is a permutation. If a span's image is contiguous, use it — so a reference to a moved column *follows* it, and a span a column is merely reordered *within* is left alone. Otherwise delete-then-insert, which is Excel. **One deliberate divergence, documented at the code:** Excel's naive delete-then-insert drifts `=SUM(A1:C1)` to `=SUM(B1:D1)` on an in-range reorder, off cells it still fully covers; this returns it unchanged, which is what keeps a table's shape when a column is dragged inside it. A property test sweeps 5×2×6×15 combinations proving **a move never produces `#REF!`**. | 30 new transaction tests and 3 wasm tests. Seven mutations, each caught: removing the fixup gives `left: Some("SUM(B1:B3)") right: Some("SUM(D1:D3)")` and `S!B1*2` against `S!D1*2` across sheets — re-verified by the orchestrator, whose own first mutation was a no-op (`let _ = (f(x))` still calls `f`) and proved nothing until it was done properly |
| MODE-01 | Every mode presented the same chrome, so an embedded editor offered the host's own File menu | Done | P1 | *"We have multiple modes — each should be thought of independently."* Measured first: the same editor booted standalone, as a desktop app and embedded gave **identical** chrome — 195 commands, `File ▸ Open`, six Download entries, Print, in every one. Not cosmetic: an editor inside somebody's page offered `File ▸ Open`, so a user could **replace the host's document from inside the host's page**, and six Download entries, so they could take a copy the host never authorised. Under WOPI the host owns save and versioning and the editor's own Save disagrees with it. Modes are now composed **capabilities** — `canOpen`, `canSaveAs`, `canPrint`, `ownsFile`, `chrome`, `readOnly` — and `applyCommandRules()` reads them, with **no name check anywhere**. `ownsFile` hard-forces `canOpen: false` and cannot be overridden back on, because no host means *the document is mine, and also let the user swap it*; `canSaveAs` is not forced, because *download a copy* is a real permission a host grants. An unknown `?mode=` falls back to **standalone**, never to something restrictive — a typo in a deployment URL must not take a user's Save away. `listCommands()` and `menuModel()` drop what is hidden and `runCommand` refuses it, so the API and the menu cannot disagree; and the gate sits on the file input itself, because that is where a file becomes the document. **Three latent defects surfaced the moment `applyCommandRules()` ran at boot for the first time.** It wrote `node.hidden = on` outright, which asserts every command starts visible and enabled — three do not — so a change that hides *nothing* would have revealed a bare "Choose file" control, revealed the toolbar overflow, and **enabled Undo over an empty history**. Worse on a narrow window: the collapse *moves* controls into a flyout, so every group is genuinely empty, and marking those hidden is unrecoverable — the groups would have gone for good on the next resize and taken the whole toolbar with them. A date is identified by the engine's own number format rather than by sniffing text, and `m` is excluded from the day predicate so `hh:mm` is not mistaken for a month. Zero padding survives (`Q01 → Q02`), a number too long for `i64` tiles instead of wrapping, and overflow is `checked_`. **A mutation that only a formula could catch:** treating a time format as a day renders *identically* on screen, because a day-shifted serial displays the same clock time — the test reads the serial back through `=A3-A1`. | `cargo test -p casual-calc-wasm` 63 pass; both seen red as `left: "2024-01-01" right: "2024-01-02"` and `left: "Item 1" right: "Item 2"`, and four mutations each caught |
| COL-44 | A concurrent move has no operational transform | Done | P1 | Raised by the work that created the gap, which is the right time. `Band` cannot express a permutation, and the usual fall-through — *"`against` moves nothing, so `subject`'s coordinates still mean what they meant"* — would be **false** for a move: two participants would diverge silently and never heal. `transform` therefore returns `Unsupported` in both directions rather than guessing, so a contended move is refused instead of corrupting a shared document. Uncontended moves never reach `transform`, so the feature works for a single editor today. Refusing is the safe half; the missing half is a transform that lets two people reorder columns at once. | `a_concurrent_move_is_refused_by_the_transform` |
| CI-018 | `assert_round_trip` cannot see relative-reference drift, in every test that uses it | Done | P1 | Found by a worker checking its own inverse rather than trusting it, and it is the `CI-017` shape again: a gate that is green over the defect it exists to catch. `observable()` in `casual-calc-transaction`'s tests compares `wb.formula(h).to_string()` — the **absolute** rendering of a tree whose references are stored as offsets (`PERF-11`). Two different offset sets can render identically, so the comparison passes over a real difference. Demonstrated: with a degraded `MoveColumns` inverse the whole `observable()` round-trip stayed green while `SUM(B1:C1)` had silently become `SUM(C1:C1)`. `assert_round_trip` is built on `observable()`, so **every** existing round-trip test carries the blind spot. The immediate work spelled its formulas out with `formula_text` and said why; the general fix is to compare what is stored, not what it prints. **Fixed by comparing the tree.** `Expr` already derives `PartialEq` and, since `PERF-11`, holds `StoredRef` — the offsets themselves — so the stored form *is* directly comparable and no second rendering had to be invented. `CellSnap.formula` became a `StoredFormula { text, tree }`: equality is decided on `tree`, and `text` (printed at the cell's **own** origin) exists only so a failure reads `SUM(C1:C1)` against `SUM(B1:C1)` instead of two pages of `StoredRef` debug. Tree equality strictly implies the old string equality, so the change can only tighten. **The exact mechanism, which the finding above did not name:** `Display for Expr` is `At(self, ABSOLUTE)`, and at origin `(0,0)` every *negative* offset resolves off-sheet and prints `#REF!` — so all left-of-cell and above-cell references collapse onto one string. **A move op cannot hide a forward defect from a round trip at all** — `move_lines` snapshots before mutating and restores the original formula handle, so the tree returns by construction; the only thing a move round trip can catch is a missing snapshot, which is exactly the demonstrated case. Insert/delete round trips *do* run the real rewrite both ways, and that region was swept: no defect. | Both halves re-verified by the orchestrator, by running rather than by reading the report. Degrading `move_lines`' inverse (`ops.extend(restores)` → `let _ = restores`, at line 395 of three identical sites — the first attempt matched all three and proved nothing) makes the new gate red with `col: -5` against `col: -6`; restoring `observable()` to the absolute `format!("{tree}")` **with the same degradation still in place** turns it green again. That green over a live defect is the finding. A text-only comparison at the cell's own origin would also have caught this particular case, so part of the value is the origin and part is the tree; the tree is what closes the residual below. 213 pass (211 + 2) |

| UX-FLT-01 | The filter dropdown could not sort, and listed numbers in byte order | Done | P1 | Two defects, both of them the first thing anybody does with a filter. Excel and Sheets both put Sort A→Z at the top of that menu; this had only conditions, so sorting meant leaving the dropdown for Data ▸ Sort. And the checklist was a `BTreeSet<String>`, so it read `10, 100, 2, 9` — byte order presented as a value list. Ordering is now numbers (dates included, since a date is a number wearing a format, so serials give chronological order for free) → text, case-insensitively with a raw-byte tie-break → blanks, which is Excel's order **and** the order the engine's own sort already used, so the checklist now reads in the order Sort A→Z would produce. A total order means no flicker between openings. One deliberate divergence from the brief, and it is the right one: the brief said consult `looksLikeHeader`, but that heuristic returns false unless a column has text over a number — which is exactly the shape of a filter on a single text column. A filter range's first row is a header *by construction* (`toggleFilter` always includes it), so the heuristic would have sorted the heading into the data. | Both halves seen red by the orchestrator by reverting and running: reverting `editor.dialogs.js` reds the sort row, and reverting `data.rs` and rebuilding the wasm reds the ordering row. The worker's four Rust mutations each land, including one on a date test that only discriminates once a `dd/mm/yyyy` format is applied — under the default ISO rendering a byte sort is *accidentally* chronological and the old code passed |
| DATA-SORT-01 | Sorting a filtered range leaves `filter_hidden` stale, so the grid hides the wrong rows | Done | P1 | Found by the `UX-FLT-01` worker against its own change and reported rather than worked around — the right call, twice over. `session_sort_range_multi` neither skips hidden rows nor recomputes `filter_hidden`, and that set is rebuilt only by `commit_filter` and on load. Measured: with a filter keeping `West`, `filter_hidden` was `{2}` (the East row); after sorting by another column East moved to row 1 and the set still said row 2 — so the filter now hides a `West` row and **shows** the `East` one it exists to hide. Rows silently misrepresented is the `no silent data loss` bound in its display form. **Pre-existing and already reachable** through Data ▸ Sort with a filter on; `UX-FLT-01` makes it one click nearer. The worker declined the only in-boundary mitigation (a no-op `session_set_filter_values` to force a recompute) because it routes through `session.edit` and would add a second undo step, so Ctrl+Z would undo the recompute instead of the sort — a workaround that trades a visible defect for a stranger one. **Fixed by excluding hidden rows from the sort** — Excel's documented behaviour, and the option that needs no derived state at all: no hidden row's data moves and no visible row lands on a hidden index, so `filter_hidden` and `hidden_rows` go on naming exactly the rows they named. The predicate is `is_row_hidden`, not `filter_hidden`, so a row hidden by hand is pinned too. The recompute alternative was **built properly and measured**, not waved away: sorting every row and recomputing afterwards as a second edit reds both tests, and one Ctrl+Z undoes the recompute while leaving the data sorted — the two-step undo the analysis predicted. | Seen red by the orchestrator by reverting `slots[i]` to `r0 + i`: `row 2 holds "West" and hidden=true; the filter keeps West`. The gate asserts **forward state** — `is_row_hidden(r) == (region != "West")` read off the sheet after the sort — because a round trip is symmetric and structurally cannot catch stale forward state, and `session_filter_info`'s `hidden` is a *length*, which is exactly what this defect leaves correct. **Two mutations that proved nothing, both recorded:** recomputing `filter_hidden` while keeping the exclusion passed 5/5, because the recompute was a no-op against an already-correct set; and the first attempt at narrowing the predicate was a compile error rather than a red test. Also worth stating plainly — the undo test does **not** discriminate the original defect, only the design choice |
| UX-DESK-02 | The desktop shell was a web page in a window: webview file picker, no document in the title | Done | P1 | The two things that give a desktop application away first. `File ▸ Open` raised the *webview's* picker, and the title bar said the application's name rather than the document's — so nothing on screen told you which file you had open or whether it was saved. Now `native_open`/`native_save` raise the platform's panels, and the title reads `figures.xlsx — OpenCalc`, gaining a `•` while there is unsaved work. **Bytes cross the bridge, never paths.** No command accepts or returns a path, so a webview cannot ask the host process to read or write a place of its choosing; large payloads go over the raw IPC channel rather than a JSON array of bytes, and `stage_save_bytes` also accepts the JSON fallback because Tauri silently switches to `postMessage` when the custom protocol is blocked. `Session` is **default-deny**: until the webview reports its capabilities both Open and Save refuse, and `ownsFile` forces `canOpen` off on the Rust side too, because a report can be stale or forged. **Two defects the worker found while reviewing its own area**, both real: the bootstrap was `eval`'d once in `setup()`, so a reload left a native menu bar dispatching into nothing — it is now injected on every `PageLoadEvent::Finished`; and `native_save` kept the staged workbook bytes on the refusal path, so a refused save left the document's bytes resident. **A build gate, because `frontendDist` embeds `webapp/` at compile time and `webapp/pkg/` is generated**: a missing wasm build failed nothing and produced a green build, a green bundle, and a blank window. A release build now panics with the exact `wasm-pack` command. The `•` is a leading marker rather than macOS's own document-edited dot: `tao` has `set_is_document_edited` but Tauri 2.11 does not re-export it, and reaching `NSWindow` needs a raw pointer and a message send, which `unsafe_code = "forbid"` rules out. Host wiring is at the choke points, not the callers: `download()` for all five save formats, a capture-phase listener on `#tb-open` for every route to Open, and `applyCommandRules()` for the menu republish — so a route added later is covered without anybody remembering. The title is **polled**, not pushed, for the reason `isDirty()` is derived: a push from every write path is a list of write paths, and the omission is always the one added last. | 28 desktop tests, 18 of them seen red as stubs. Two re-verified by the orchestrator by mutation: dropping the dirty marker reds 4, and making `guard_open` always allow reds `a_shell_that_has_not_been_told_refuses_everything` and `a_host_that_owns_the_file_cannot_be_talked_into_an_open`. The first mutation attempt produced a type error, not a red test — a compile failure proves nothing, and it was redone type-correct. 256 browser tests still pass, all 24 gates green. **Not done, and tracked at `TAURI-001`:** no installer is produced (`cargo-tauri` is not installed here, and a distributable additionally needs an Apple Developer ID plus notarisation, and a Windows signing certificate — none of which exist); no CI job builds the desktop app at all; the whole of `webapp/` is embedded including `serve.py` and the site pages; and file associations are unconfigured. On macOS `rfd` concatenates all filters into one list, so there is no format popup and the `All Files` entry matches nothing — a `.csv` named `.txt` is unreachable from the panel there, though Windows and GTK behave as written |
| UX-DESK-03 | The desktop Open panel greyed out `.ods`, which the engine has read for six closed rows | Done | P1 | Raised by the user — *"we still only support xlsx, csv, tsv and psv.. nothing else"* — and half right in a way worth recording. The **engine** reads `xlsx, ods, csv, tsv, tab, psv`: `casual-calc-ods` is its own crate with its own fuzz target and six closed rows (`ODS-01`..`ODS-06`). The **web editor** offers all of them, because it asks `openable_extensions()`, which interrogates the SDK about candidates rather than reciting a list. The **desktop shell** kept its own `const SPREADSHEET_EXTENSIONS = ["xlsx", "csv", "tsv", "psv"]` — so a LibreOffice file was unselectable in a panel raised by an application that opens it perfectly. The doc comment above that constant said the formats were *"the ones the engine reads and writes"*, which had stopped being true; that is the shape this repository has a rule about, and the fix is the code, not the comment. The shell now learns the list from the engine over the existing capability report, read under **one lock** with `guard_open` so a `set_capabilities` cannot land between the permission and the filters. The old constant survives only as a floor for the moment before the webview reports — deliberately a *subset* of the truth, because offering less for an instant costs a cancelled panel while offering more costs a file that opens and then fails. | 29 desktop tests. Two existing tests had to change and both were themselves the evidence: `the_open_panel_offers_every_format_the_engine_reads` asserted the four-item list its own name contradicted, and `an_unknown_format_is_still_named` used **`ods` as its example of an unknown format** — a test and its code agreeing about something they were both wrong about. New test `no_two_entries_in_the_open_panel_share_a_name`, seen red: the engine's full list gives `.tsv` and `.tab` the same label, and two identical rows is a picker nobody can choose from |
| CI-021 | `observable()` compared no sheet metadata at all, so hidden rows had zero round-trip coverage | Done | P1 | `CI-018` closed the formula half of this helper's blindness and the metadata half turned out to be wider: `observable()` compared sheet name and cells only — no `filter_hidden`, no `hidden_rows`, no merges, no autofilter, no tables. **Verbatim proof it was a hole:** with `snapshot_metadata` degraded to drop `filter_hidden` from every structural inverse — i.e. undo for the hidden set deliberately destroyed — the suite was `213 passed; 0 failed`. What made it invisible: `assert_round_trip_strict` *does* compare everything, but it is unusable once a formula is rewritten because the arena grows, so every formula-carrying test fell back to the metadata-blind helper. The fix uses `SheetMetadata::capture` rather than a hand-listed subset, so a field added later is compared without anyone remembering to come back. | Re-verified by the orchestrator by running: restoring `observable()` to `SheetMetadata::default()` reds `observable_distinguishes_sheets_that_differ_only_in_which_rows_are_hidden` and nothing else, 214 passed 1 failed. Worth recording alongside: the worker's first attempt at the undo test used `MoveRows` and **would not go red under any mutation** — a round trip is symmetric and a move is a permutation, so the reverse move reindexes the hidden set whether or not anything snapshotted it. It only discriminates with `DeleteRows`, where re-inserting an empty band cannot invent a dropped row |
| COL-45 | Every commit failure was reported as a failing save, including the ones that were not | Done | P1 | `COL-38` inverted, and found by the `COL-44` work. `net.rs` mapped every `ServerError` to `Refusal::NotSaving` — *"the document is not accepting edits — its saves are failing"* — so the entire user-visible surface of a contended move arrived as `not saved: notSaving`. `Refusal::CannotMerge` has existed since the protocol was written and **nothing in the server ever sent it**. The two ask the user for opposite things: copy your work out of a document that is losing it, versus that one action did not take. Blaming the half that was working is the more expensive error of the two. | Seen red before the fix (`left: NotSaving, right: CannotMerge`). The worker then mutated the `_ => NotSaving` fall-through **to** `CannotMerge` and all 274 tests passed — the fall-through was unpinned, so the bug could have returned inverted — and added a test of both arms. Orchestrator re-verified by running: reverting the `Transform(_)` arm reds both tests, restored gives 275 |
| COL-46 | A `$`-anchored formula silently diverges across a concurrent insert | Done | P0 | Found by the `COL-44` worker against a pair that has nothing to do with moves, and verified rather than assumed. A `SetCell` carrying `=$D$1` rebased across a concurrent `InsertColumns{at:0}` gives `col: 4, col_absolute: true` on one replica and `col: 3` on the other — `$E$1` against `$D$1`. `rebase_onto_band` shifts the **cell address** and carries the formula verbatim; relative references survive because `PERF-11` stores them relative to their own cell, and **absolute ones do not**. This is two replicas of a shared document holding different formulas with no error raised anywhere, which is the worst class this system has: divergence that looks like success. It is P0 above `COL-44` itself, because a contended move is at least *refused* while this one commits. **What made it invisible:** the in-tree TP1 property test's seed *"deliberately contains no formulas"*, and `observe()` prints `cell.value` and never the formula — the same shape as `CI-018` and `CI-021`, a gate that cannot see the field the defect lives in. Fixing the seed is part of the fix. **This row understated the defect.** Widening the probe showed **relative references diverge too**: the claim that they survive because `PERF-11` stores them relative to their own cell holds only while the band lies *outside* the span between reference and cell. `=A1` in `B2` across `InsertColumns{at:1}` gives `col: -2` against `col: -1`. Only the one case the original probe happened to pick converged. **Fixed by calling the pass `apply` already runs**, not a second implementation: `apply` resolves a stored tree to absolute, rewrites it and re-stores at the new origin, and the transform did the address and not the tree. `structural::CarriedShift` now exposes that pass and the transform calls it. A formula lives behind an arena handle, so rewriting needs read *and* intern — `transform::FormulaTable` is that capability, and plain `transform(…)` keeps its signature and **refuses** rather than carrying a formula verbatim, so the out-of-boundary fuzz target still compiles. | Reproduced verbatim on the untouched tree first. Six tests red under `rebase_cell_formula` returning the cell untouched, including both TP1 properties and the session-level one — re-verified by the orchestrator. On the **pristine** tree that same mutation left `tp1_holds_for_every_supported_pair` green: the blind gate demonstrated rather than asserted |
| COL-47 | A refused chunk leaves the client permanently deaf, and the page says nothing | Done | P1 | `ClientSession` never drops a refused chunk, so one unmergeable submission blocks **every later arrival** — measured: resending gets the identical refusal for ever, and meanwhile the user stops receiving everybody else's edits. `webapp/collab.js`'s `socket.onmessage → receive(message)` has no `catch`, so a throwing `collab_receive` stops applying remote edits with no status change at all: the document quietly stops being shared and looks fine. **This should land regardless of `COL-44`.** Any transform, however complete, still refuses some pairs — `COL-44`'s own candidate leaves move-vs-move at 80% answered — so this is what makes a refusal survivable rather than terminal. **Re-measured 2026-08-30: the editor half is closed and the engine half reproduces.** `webapp/collab.js` now wraps `receive(message)` in a `catch` that reports `status("desynced", …)`, with the reasoning written beside it — *"this is the one failure where carrying on silently is worse than stopping, because the user goes on typing into a document they believe is shared"*. So *"the page says nothing"* is no longer true. `ClientSession::receive` (`crates/casual-calc-transaction/src/session.rs:451`) still returns `Err` on a transform failure without dropping the chunk, and nothing removes it from `self.sent`, so the deafness is unchanged. **The row keeps its P1**: a reported desync is better than a silent one and is not a recovery. | Open |
| COL-55 | The row was wrong: a refused arrival did not stop the client, it let it diverge silently | Done | P0 | `COL-47` said one unmergeable submission *blocks every later arrival*. Measured on the untouched tree, **a later non-contending arrival went straight through**: `LATER ARRIVAL: Ok(())`. So a client applied revision 2 without ever applying revision 1, with nothing raised anywhere. **Deafness would have been the honest failure** — this was `COL-46`'s class wearing a different name, and the row described the safer half of the truth. Worse, `receive` was **not atomic**. The outstanding edit was rebased past an arrival the workbook never received — `SetCell` col 2 → col 5 — and `resend` would have carried it to the server, turning a private divergence into everybody's. **Chosen: the client stops being a client, and both routes to the network close behind it.** Dropping the chunk diverges; stopping in name only is what shipped and it drifted; re-fetching is right and the engine has no transport to do it. So `receive` latches, every later `receive` answers `Desynced`, and `flush`/`resend` seal. `edit` and `record` deliberately keep working — **the line is drawn at the network, not at the keyboard**. The cost is everything unacknowledged, but *nameably*: `has_unacknowledged()` still answers, which is the question the editor already asks before replacing a document. The server needs to know nothing; recovery is an ordinary join. | Seen red by the orchestrator: unenforcing the latch gives `left: Ok(()) right: Err(Desynced)` — the divergence itself, asserted. **The worker reported that its first mutation proved less than it looked**: removing the latch short-circuited *before* the assertion that reproduces the defect, so it sharpened it to latch-set-guard-removed. It also stated plainly that the atomicity fix is unobservable through the public API once the seal is in place, so that test lives inside the module |

Only the delete produces the loud `<field x>` overflow that `PIV-05` was about. **And the delete's inverse never restored the pivot at all.** `snapshot_metadata` captures only the sheet the delete ran on; a pivot reading `Data` lives on `Report`, so undo left its source a column short, permanently. Dropping is also what every other position-indexed thing already does when its band goes. | Seen red by the orchestrator by suppressing the renumber: three tests, including the insert/drag case the brief never mentioned and the cross-sheet undo |

It stays `false` in `embedded` and `wopi` for a different and permanent reason: starting a session for a document somebody else owns is the host's decision. Consequently `.xlsx`, `.ods` and `.csv` **remove** the project and name the loss, rather than smuggling executable content into a container that declares itself macro-free. **The security bound, stated rather than assumed:** `vbaProject.bin` is a retained part — never parsed, interpreted, executed, or read by the calc engine. Its size is bounded by the ordinary package admission limits, not a special case.
