| CHT-06 | `push_bars` is uncapped, and charts already spend frame budget nobody measured | Done | P1 | Measured for `docs/84`: six series over 1,000 rows is **6,007 display-list items and 722 KB of JSON across the wasm boundary per frame**; 5,000 rows is 16.7% of the 16.7 ms budget in the engine alone, before any painting. `PERF-D-01` fought for that budget and `editor.frame-budget.spec.mjs` pins it — **and mentions charts nowhere**, so this is invisible to the gate that exists to catch it. The plot has no cap; a chart over a large range is bounded only by the range. **The measurement that decided the fix:** at a 400px frame with 1,000 rows and 6 series the bar width computes to **exactly zero** — all 6,000 polygons drew nothing. The plot had stopped showing anything long before the bound; it was only still paying for it. So this is not a trade between fidelity and cost the way it looked. Points above the bound are **bucketed, not dropped**: each bucket draws one rectangle per series from its **minimum to its maximum**, so no outlier is hidden, and `value_extent` still sees every point so the axis is unchanged. What is given up is horizontal resolution below one bar width. With `groups == points` the arithmetic is byte-identical and every pre-existing geometry test passed unmodified. | Measured before → after at 1,000 rows × 6 series: **6,006 items → 126**, 722 KB → 14.8 KB, 4.2% → 1.5% of the frame; at 10,000 rows, 64.7% → 18.9%. Structural test, not wall-clock. Seen red by the orchestrator uncapping it: `1000 rows x 6 series emitted 6000 bar polygons`. **A mutation that proved nothing, and what it caught:** reviewing its own bucketing the worker found a series shorter than the longest silently lost its leading points — and the first test for that *passed with the bug reinstated*, because blanked cells still yield one entry per cell so nothing was ragged. Rewritten with ranges of genuinely different sizes, it discriminates |
| CHT-08 | A chart series naming a deleted sheet re-resolves against whatever is created next | Done | P1 | Measured, not speculated. A series whose sheet is deleted **keeps the sheet name** — no `#REF!` — vanishes from the picture with nothing reported, and then **re-resolves against unrelated data if a sheet of that name is recreated**. A chart that silently starts plotting somebody else's numbers is worse than a chart that breaks. **The convention was argued, not assumed.** The worker measured the *formula* path first: `apply(RemoveSheet)` does not rewrite formula text either, and `eval_reference` prints `#REF!` when the sheet is missing — so a formula also keeps its text and also re-resolves. A chart cannot follow that convention because it has nowhere to print the error; the series simply leaves the picture. It follows the convention **chart series already have**: `DeleteRows`/`DeleteColumns` already rewrite a series whose data is deleted to `#REF!`, and removing the sheet deletes the same data at a coarser grain. That is one convention, not two. A broken series is now kept and labelled `Name (#REF!)` rather than vanishing. | Seen red by the orchestrator by suppressing the break: `left: ("Other!$B$1:$B$3", …) right: ("#REF!", …)`, and the recreated-sheet test with it. Case-insensitive, matching how references resolve |

Stacking makes it worse, because a stacked chart is read *by* colour. A test asserting the defect, which is the shape `FID-35` and the colour byte-order bug both had. **The old assertion carried two claims in one line** — that the palette is the *file's theme* (right, kept, still asserted) and that it repeats at 7 (wrong). Split rather than overridden. The accent now varies past slot 6 using Excel's own theme-colour variants, giving 36 distinct series before it cycles, and an unreadable theme colour is returned unchanged rather than guessed at. | Seen red: `series 1 and series 7 share 4472C4` |

| SAVE-01 | A save that did not happen said it did | Done | P1 | Found by the `docs/83` design work while measuring, not by testing — and it is a defect I introduced with the desktop shell. `doSaveNative` called `download()` and then `markSaved()`, and `download()`'s native branch is **deliberately un-awaited**. My own comment gave the reasoning: *"making it async would change five call sites into a shape where a forgotten `await` silently drops the save"*. That had it backwards — the caller does not merely report the save, it **clears the dirty bullet and disarms the close warning**. So a cancelled panel, a failed write, or the boot window where the shell still refuses everything left the document marked saved with nothing written and the error only in the console. The user was told their work was safe and could then close the window. `download()` now returns a promise resolving to the written file's name, or `null` when the user cancelled, so a caller can tell *did not happen* from *went wrong*; one that ignores it behaves exactly as before. The three outcomes are deliberately not alike — written marks saved and names the file, cancelled stays dirty and says nothing because the user knows what they did, failed stays dirty and is named. | Three tests, all seen red by restoring the original ordering, including `cancelling is not saving`. **The full suite then caught a second thing**: my status text called a browser download a *save*. In a tab the file goes to the downloads folder and the document has no home to go back to — only a host that owns a file has saved anything, which is the distinction `docs/83` turns on |
| SAVE-02 | Phase A — desktop in-place save | Done | P1 | `docs/83` §8. `Ctrl+S` writes back to the file that was opened, rather than producing a download beside it. Delivers on its own: a desktop user stops accumulating `opencalc (1).xlsx`. **The write is a temporary file in the target's own directory, `sync_all`, permissions carried across, then a rename over** — not a truncate-and-write, because a save that fails partway must never leave the user with neither the old file nor the new one. **Read-only is checked explicitly**, which is not belt-and-braces: on Unix a rename over a read-only file *succeeds*, so an atomic write would silently defeat the flag the user set. Also covered: a missing directory, a deleted target re-created, the file changed underneath us, two windows on one file, and `SAVE-01`'s rule that the document is marked saved only when bytes have landed. | 41 desktop tests and 12 browser tests, each mutation red on exactly its own test. **The orchestrator's first atomicity mutation proved nothing** — writing straight over the target *succeeded*, so it showed the test discriminates the path and not the hazard; the worker had already hit and documented the same trap. Redone as *the temp file is the target*, it reds with `left: "new bytes" right: "a year of work"` — a save that reported failure having replaced the document |

A user who saw the bar and hesitated lost the thing the bar was for. A slot holding an offered, unanswered draft is now reserved as well as the ones live tabs hold, and only **Discard** — a user saying so, on an entry that named the document — puts one back. | UX-DLG-01 | A hidden child collapsed a dialog's control column, and every table-style swatch was blank | Done | P0 | Reported by the user — *"who adds a scroller in dialogs or popups... have you even seen table in google sheets or microsoft excel"* — and it was not a scroller, it was worse. Measured rather than eyeballed: `.oc-table-form` declares `auto minmax(0, 1fr)` and **computed `450px 0px`**. `err.hidden` is `display: none`, a `display: none` child occupies **no grid cell**, so auto-placement shifted every later control one place: the full-width checkbox landed in the `auto` label column and swelled it to the whole form, collapsing the control column to zero. The range input rendered as an 18px pill against the right edge, detached from its label, with "Style" clipped off the edge. **It looked broken in the default state**, because the error is hidden until the range is refused — so every user who ever opened Create Table saw this. The error now lives in a `.oc-form-cell` beside the input it belongs to, which is also where a refused range should be answered. That cell is **`flex`, not `grid`**: flex has no cells to fall out of, so a `display: none` child shortens the stack and shifts nothing — making it a grid reproduces the trap one level down, which is what the first attempt at this fix did. **The swatches, separately:** all eight were blank boxes. Chrome's UA stylesheet gives every `button` `align-items: flex-start`, which start-aligned each band to its own empty content height — four 6px rows reserved, nothing drawn, `childHeights: [2, 0, 0, 0]` where the 2 is a border. The colours had been arriving correctly the whole time. And both swatch builders ignored `bodyFill`, so rows 2 and 4 were transparent and showed the panel through them — a light style and a dark one differed by one stripe, in a picker whose only job is to show the difference. One painter now serves both. | Four tests, all seen red by the orchestrator by reverting: putting the hidden child back gives `control column collapsed: tracks were 450px 0px`, `the range input is a pill`, and the general gate; removing `align-items: stretch` gives `band heights [{h:2},{h:0},{h:0},{h:0}]`. Test 4 is the **general** gate — it sweeps every dialog for a `display: none` child in an auto-placed grid and asserts it measured more than 8 grids first, so a sweep that looked at nothing cannot read as a pass. `editor.css?v` bumped: Pages serves the literal, so without it users keep the old stylesheet |
| UX-TABLE-02 | A table's header label is drawn underneath its own filter arrow | Done | P1 | Raised by the user for the third time — *"have you seen tables on google sheet"* — and the first two times the answer looked at the **dialog** rather than the table. Measured on the canvas this time. What is **right**: the header fill paints `#4472c4`, the bands `#dae3f3` and the body `#ffffff`, each exactly what `session_table_at` resolves, so the style pipeline works end to end. What is wrong: **the header cell reserves no room for the filter arrow**, so the label and the control occupy the same pixels. Scanning the rightmost 16px of each header for ink that is not the header fill: `Revenue` puts 5 pixel-columns of glyph inside the arrow's own zone, `Region` 6 in the reserved strip beside it. On screen `Revenue` reads `Revenu` with the arrow sitting on the last letter. Google and Excel both shorten the label with an ellipsis *before* the control and never overlap them. Second, smaller: `session_table_at` returns `border: "85A3D9"` and **no outline is drawn at all**. A table with no boundary does not read as an object, which is most of why it does not look like a table. **Both fixed.** The guard at `editor.paint.js:532` was `if (cw < 22) continue` under a comment claiming it avoided *"covering its label"* — a contract the code did not keep, since it never measured the label at all. It was also the wrong lever: Excel and Sheets do not drop the control on a narrow column, they shorten the label. The header text now gets `cw - 16` of room, and the table draws the outline the engine already resolved. | Both seen red by the orchestrator: removing the outline gives `0 of 9 rows carry the border colour on the table's left edge`, and `ux-visual-audit.mjs` independently goes 5 findings → 6. | PERF-D-03 | The grid trapped the WASM module at row 74,566 and took the document with it | Done | P0 | Found by the competitive-analysis agent by **running** the editor, and it is the most serious defect this project has had. `session_row_offset_px` was `rows.offset(row) as i32 * 96 / 1440`. `offset` already returns `i64`; narrowing to `i32` **before** multiplying by 96 threw away the width it had been handed, and the product passes `i32::MAX` at an axis offset of 1,491,308 px — row 74,566 at the default 20px height. **`overflow-checks = true` in `[profile.release]` makes this a panic in the shipped build, not a wrong number.** A panicking `wasm_bindgen` export traps the module: the editor stops and the document goes with it, with no panic hook, so the console is empty. **Two routes a user reaches by accident:** `Ctrl+End` on a deep sheet, and typing `A1048576` into the Name Box. Both now go through one `px_from_twips`, computing in `i64` and clamping — a value past what the boundary type can carry has to come back as *something*, and a clamp is the only answer that keeps the module alive. | Four tests, all seen red with `attempt to multiply with overflow`. **Two mutations proved nothing first and are worth recording**: narrowing to `i32` and then multiplying in `i64` is not what the original did and reproduced nothing — only multiplying *in* `i32` reproduces it. And the saturation test caught a hole in the **fix**: plain `i64` multiplication overflows too, just further out, so the first version moved the boundary rather than removing it. `saturating_mul` closes it. Verified in the browser: row 1,048,575 returns 20,971,500 and the session survives |
| IO-05 | The printout was not the sheet: no widths, no merges, no borders, and Page Setup collected three settings nothing read | Done | P1 | Fifth on `docs/12`'s switching-blocker list, because for many users the printout *is* the deliverable. `session_print_html` carried bold, fill and number format and dropped column widths, merges, cell borders and all three scale settings; `&P` was stripped rather than substituted. **A control stating a contract the code does not keep** — the dialog kept its controls and the code was fixed. **The brief's premise was false and the worker said so, which is the most useful thing in the report.** `docs/12` §6 and §3.17 both assert that `casual-calc-layout` and `casual-calc-render` already paginate, and `IO-03` (PDF export) is scoped from that claim as *"a writer over existing layout"*. `grep -rn "paginat" crates/` returns one hit, in a comment. There are no page breaks, no headers/footers and no print scaling in either crate. None of this was wiring. **`IO-03` is mis-scoped and `docs/12` is wrong** — see `IO-06`. **The pagination decision, argued rather than assumed:** the browser paginates and the engine supplies the numbers. There is nothing to disagree with, because the engine does not paginate and there is no preview, so the only thing a user compares paper against is the screen; a paginator here would be a second layout engine for the same grid, which is the fault `RND-10` removed; and it would still print *through* the browser. The one thing CSS cannot express is fit-to-page, so the engine computes that percentage, in the new `casual-calc-layout::print` so the `IO-03` writer gets the same answer. `zoom` rather than `transform: scale()`, because a transform does not reflow and the browser would paginate the unscaled box. | Eight mutations, each seen red. **One proved nothing and was reported**: the first version of the hidden-column span test failed against *correct* code — the expectation was wrong, not the behaviour. Also found en route: a merged banner reaching past the last populated column printed as one narrow cell |
| SEC-023 | A workbook's header text could close the `<style>` element and run script at the editor's origin | Done | P0 | Introduced and closed inside `IO-05`, and worth its own row because the class outlives the change. Moving headers and footers into `@page` margin boxes puts workbook-controlled text inside a **stylesheet**, where `push_html_escaped` is useless — `&lt;` inside CSS is four literal characters, not an escape. A header reading `</style><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>` closes the sheet and runs script in a print popup carrying the editor's origin, from any `.xlsx` a user opens. Verbatim, with the escaping removed: `@top-center{content:"</style><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>"` — the tag intact in the output. Escaped as CSS `\3c `, and `<`/`>`/`&` are all hex-escaped rather than reasoning about which sequences can terminate the element. **The standing sink check could not have caught it:** `SEC-001` greps `webapp/*.js` and the host's HTML, so markup assembled in Rust is outside it entirely — the same blind spot `clipboard.rs:443` already records in a comment. That gate hole is the durable finding. | Seen red by the orchestrator by reverting the escape: the `</style>` tag appears intact in the emitted document |
| IO-06 | `docs/12` and `IO-03` both rest on a pagination that does not exist | Done | P2 | Found by the `IO-05` worker checking its own brief rather than accepting it. `docs/12` §6 row 4 lists print layout under *"engine capable, editor cannot reach it"*, and §3.17 says the fix is *"a writer over existing layout, not new layout"*. Both are false: `casual-calc-layout` and `casual-calc-render` contain no pagination at all. `IO-03`'s cost was estimated from that claim, so **the estimate is wrong in the expensive direction** — PDF export needs a paginator, not a writer. `IO-05` builds the first piece of one (`casual-calc-layout::print`: paper, printable box, fit-to-page scale). Correct both documents and re-scope `IO-03`. **Closed: both documents corrected and `IO-03` re-scoped.** `docs/12` §3.17 now quotes `crates/casual-calc-layout/src/print.rs`'s own opening — *"Nothing here paginates. That is deliberate"* — records the decision `IO-05` took (the browser paginates, the engine supplies the numbers, because a paginator here would be a second layout engine for the same grid, the fault `RND-10` removed), and states that PDF cannot take that route. §6 row 4 and §10.1's PDF bullet carry the same correction, and §6's blanket "host-side wiring" claim is withdrawn. | Open |
| FID-35 | An eight-digit colour was emitted as OOXML and read as CSS, turning opaque black into transparent red | Done | P2 | Reported by the `IO-05` worker as out of scope and fixed here. An eight-digit OOXML colour is `AARRGGBB`; an eight-digit CSS colour is `RRGGBBAA`. `html_cell_css` accepted `len == 8` and emitted it unchanged, so an opaque black `FF000000` reads in CSS as `#FF0000` at zero alpha — **fully transparent red**. Reordered rather than dropped: the alpha is real and the receiving application can honour it. Hits both the printout and the clipboard HTML, so a cell pasted into another application lost its colour. **Nothing reported it**, because the string is a valid colour in both notations and only means different things — the shape of a defect no error path can see. | Seen red: with the alpha left first, `color:#FF000000;background-color:#80FF0000;` |
| UX-AUDIT-01 | Nothing looked at the chrome, so the user was the only visual gate | Done | P0 | The user, on being asked to keep reporting defects: *"do you want me to examine all cases and show you"*. No. Four visual defects in a row were found by a person looking at the screen — a dialog's control column collapsed to `0px`, eight table-style swatches that rendered blank, a table label drawn under its own filter arrow, a reorder gesture with no feedback — and **`ux-sweep.mjs` was green through every one of them**, because it asks whether a *behaviour* exists and none of these break one. A behaviour sweep is structurally blind to everything a person sees first. `ux-visual-audit.mjs` walks the real chrome and measures geometry: collapsed or mis-placed grid tracks, text clipped by its own box, content given no area to draw in, controls drawn outside their surface, pointer targets under 24px, plus two canvas checks the DOM cannot see — glyph ink inside a table header's filter-arrow zone, and border ink along a table's edge. Generates `docs/68`. **Its first run returned 153 findings and that was a failure, not a success**: roughly 140 were `<option>` elements, which have no box because the select paints them, and a closed panel measuring zero throughout. A report that cries wolf is ignored, which costs the same as no report. Scoped to the open surface and to elements that genuinely want area: **7 findings, all real**. | Verified by the defects it re-finds without being told: the table header ink and the missing outline, both of which had been found by hand hours earlier. It also found one nobody had — see `UX-DLG-02` |
| DATA-DUP-01 | Remove Duplicates deletes the rows a filter is showing and keeps the ones it is hiding | Done | P1 | Found by the `DATA-SORT-01` worker checking a sibling it was told to confirm or refute, and it is worse than the guess that prompted it. `session_remove_duplicates` scans `first_row..=r1` with no `is_row_hidden`, so an active filter is invisible to it. Measured with a throwaway probe: rows `East/1`(hidden), `West/1`, `West/1`, `East/2`(hidden), filter keeping `West` — `removed = 2`, and the survivors are `East/1` and `East/2`. **Both visible rows were deleted and both hidden ones survived.** The hidden `East/1` acted as the first occurrence, so each visible `West/1` was removed as a duplicate of a row the user cannot see: the grid empties with no visible cause. In the other ordering a hidden row is deleted silently instead. Distinct from `DATA-SORT-01`: `filter_hidden` is *not* stale here — `DeleteRows` reindexes it correctly — so this is purely about which rows are considered. **Not fixed deliberately.** The one-line candidate is `if sh.is_row_hidden(r) { continue; }` in pass 1, but whether hidden rows should be non-candidates, non-keys, or both is a product decision that changes deletion semantics, and this is the `no silent data loss` bound in its deleting form — the wrong choice destroys data quietly. **Decided and fixed: a hidden row is neither a candidate nor a key.** The product question was real but it has a defensible answer — this application already excludes hidden rows everywhere else it acts on a filtered range (`clip_capture` when copying, and `DATA-SORT-01` when sorting, both on the same `is_row_hidden` predicate), so a filtered view deletes what it shows. Deciding it the other way would have made three neighbouring operations disagree about what a filter means. **Excluding them only from deletion would not have fixed it** — the invisible row still claims the first occurrence and still takes a visible one with it, which is exactly the measured case. That is the half that is easy to get wrong. | Test seen red: `removed` is `2` where it must be `1`, both visible rows deleted. **The first version of that test proved nothing and passed under the mutation** — it keyed on both columns, where `East` and `West` can never collide, so the hidden rows could not affect the result. Keyed on the number column alone it discriminates. A test whose scenario cannot express the defect is the commonest way a fix here has been believed without evidence |

| DATA-STAT-01 | No column statistics | Done | P1 | Asked for by name. `session_column_stats` returns counts, the empty count, unique values, a numeric block (sum, average, median, min, max, sample and population deviation) and a **type distribution** — which is how a user spots the one text cell wrecking a SUM. The decisions that make it correct rather than plausible: a blank is not a zero and never divides the average; a number stored as text is counted as text and **named** in `numberAsText` rather than silently coerced; errors are counted and are not numbers; frequency is bounded to a top ten with every unlisted occurrence still accounted for; ordering is a total order, because `HashMap` order is randomised per process and without a tie-break the panel flickers between identical runs. Deviation is computed in two passes, since `E[x²] − mean²` loses its digits on columns of large near-equal numbers — timestamps and prices, which is most of them. **A non-finite guard found by asking what would have to be true:** `format!("{}", f64::NAN)` is `NaN`, which is not JSON and would throw in the host's parse, taking the panel out over one imported cell. The UI panel is not built yet. | 11 tests, all 8 originals seen red against a stub, and eight separate mutations each caught — blanks-as-zeroes gives `left: 6.0, right: 15.0`; dropping the frequency tie-break makes two calls in one process disagree |
| A11Y-01 | The accessibility mirror does not follow the viewport | Done | P1 | Found while gating {perf} and **not caused by it** — verified against an unmodified tree. After scrolling 1600px the mirror's first `aria-rowindex` is still `1`, so a screen reader is being read a screen that is no longer there. The mirror is otherwise unusually good — a real DOM tree with absolute indices, better than the comparable products — which is what makes this worth fixing rather than rebuilding. Deliberately **not** asserted in the scroll-budget gate: that gate would then fail for a different defect and quietly stop guarding the frame budget it exists for. **The reported symptom was a mis-measurement, and the real defect is a different one.** *"After scrolling 1600px the mirror's first `aria-rowindex` is still 1"* reads `document.querySelector("[aria-rowindex]")`, which finds the mirror's **column-header row** — `rebuildA11yGrid` gives that `aria-rowindex="1"` deliberately, at every scroll position, by construction. Measured on an unmodified tree at `scrollY 3460`: `{firstVisibleRow: 173, mirrorFirstDataRow: 173}`. **The settled mirror was correct all along.** What is real is *when*: `scheduleA11yGrid` cleared and re-armed its 120 ms settle timer on every frame, so a scroll that never settles never rebuilt. Staleness was bounded by the length of the gesture and nothing else — measured over 1.5 s of continuous wheel scrolling, the view was at row 380 and the mirror at row 160. **220 rows behind, and unbounded in principle.** A screen-reader user scrolling continuously reads a document that has moved on without them. Fixed with a staleness *ceiling* (`A11Y_MAX_STALE_MS = 250`), the settle wait becoming `min(120, ceiling − age)` — one expression that is both the settle timer and the ceiling. Always through a timer and never inline, so the rebuild's layout dirtying lands in its own task rather than inside the animation frame. 250 ms is argued against what a reader can consume: announcing one row takes well over a second, so a quarter second cannot let a reader *finish* a row that had already left the screen. Rebuilding per frame was rejected — that is the 43% of frame time `PERF-D-01` removed. **And a second defect found in the same function:** `startGlide` never called `viewIsMoving()`. A glide is the throw *after* the finger has gone, so neither `wheel` nor `touchmove` fires and the deferral never saw it — one fling rebuilt the mirror on all 40 of its frames, the entire pre-`PERF-D-01` cost still live on the platform with the least frame budget to spare. One line. \| Both seen red by the orchestrator: reverting the mechanism to a fixed settle gives `the mirror was 1513ms out of date … the ceiling is 250ms`, and removing the glide line gives `38 mirror rebuilds during one glide`. **A mutation the worker reported as proving nothing:** it first reverted by setting the ceiling to `Infinity`, and the test *passed* — `toBeLessThan(Infinity + 150)` is vacuous. It pinned the constant with a second assertion and reverted the mechanism instead. Cost measured rather than asserted: ~4 slightly-long frames per second **while the wheel or a finger is moving**, and nothing at all while reading, editing or keyboard-navigating | |
| CF-01 | Formula conditional formats did not exist, and were deleted from files that had them | Done | P1 | Fourth on `docs/12`'s switching-blocker list, and two defects under one name. **The data loss first:** `expression` rules were dropped on import, so an Excel workbook highlighting whole rows with `=$D2>100` arrived without it — and a user who opened and saved that file lost the rule from the file too. **Then the feature:** no `expression` rule means no whole-row highlighting, the commonest conditional format in real workbooks. Preservation was built before evaluation, deliberately: that is the half costing users something they cannot get back. `CfRule::Expression` stores the formula **as the file writes it** — A1 text anchored to the top-left of the applied range — which is what makes the writer a byte passthrough rather than a re-anchoring that can drift. **The per-cell shift is `PERF-11` doing the work, not arithmetic**: parse once, `restore_at(ABSOLUTE → Origin::at(range.start))`, then evaluate at each cell. Moving the origin *is* the shift, so there is no second place the offsets could be written down differently. Wired at **both** renderers so they cannot diverge (`RND-05`). `timePeriod` was built because it turned out nearly free — Excel writes an equivalent `<formula>` beside it, and this engine's `TODAY()` reads a host-supplied clock so it stays deterministic. It is recorded **`Degraded`**, not silently reshaped: it re-exports as `expression`, so Excel offers it as "Use a formula" rather than "Yesterday", and the cells it paints are unchanged. `iconSet` remains an honest omission — it needs a model variant, a new channel on `CellEffect`, glyph drawing in both renderers and cell-space reservation. | Seven mutations, each seen red — including one anchoring at `A1` instead of the range's top-left, on a range deliberately **not** starting at `A1`, because at `A1:H10` the two anchorings agree and the test would prove nothing. The report also stays honest in the other direction: `expression` no longer appears as a loss, asserted explicitly, because naming a loss that did not happen misleads as much as missing one that did. **A mutation that proved nothing, reported:** testing `session_add_cf`'s refusal aborted rather than answered — `JsError::new` is a wasm import and cannot run on a non-wasm target — so the decision was split into a pure function and tested there |
| CF-02 | The editor could not reach the rule the engine had just gained | Done | P1 | **The eighth time in this repository that the engine has had a capability the editor could not reach**, and `docs/12` §6 now names it as a category. `CF-01` built formula conditional formatting end to end — model, import, export, evaluation at both renderers — and the panel offered no way to author one, so whole-row highlighting was reachable by the engine and not by a user. The panel now offers *"custom formula is"*, and **the anchoring is made visible rather than assumed known**: a line under the field names the anchor cell and says why `=$D2>100` paints a whole row where `=D2>100` paints one column. That is the thing users get wrong, and it is invisible in a formula box. A refusal lands **beneath the field it came from** with `aria-invalid` on the field — `docs/82` found a dialog whose validation appeared nowhere useful and this does not add a second. **The panel did not read rules back at all** — the worker checked rather than assumed; only the separate Manage-rules modal listed them. Two further defects found by looking at the running panel: an Apply that silently rewrote a loaded rule, and a refusal left standing in the status bar over a rule that had since applied cleanly |
| CF-03 | A whole-row highlight came out striped wherever a column was blank | Done | P1 | Raised by the `CF-02` worker against the feature it was exposing, and it defeated the point of `CF-01`. `session_cells` walks `row_band`, which is **stored** cells — so a cell nobody has typed in was never offered to `effect_for` and never got a fill. Harmless for a rule about a cell's own value, which a blank does not have; **wrong for a rule about another cell**, and `=$D2>100` is precisely a rule that paints blanks. Measured: with B2 and D2 written, `session_cells` returned a fill for `1,1` and `1,3` and nothing for C2 or E2. On any real table with a blank column the highlight came out striped. Empty positions inside a conditional format's range are now evaluated too, **bounded by the window rather than by the rule** — a format over a whole column is a million cells and the caller has already asked for the few hundred it can draw. Duplicates are dropped where two rules cover one position, or the painter would draw the loser over the winner. | Seen red by dropping the emission: `row 2 has D over a hundred, so every cell of it paints — including column 2, which holds nothing`, `left: "" right: "FFD166"` |
| UX-MOB-05 | Every submenu opened off the right edge of a phone, and tapping one could delete data | Done | P0 | Switching-blocker #9, and the first measurement is the finding: **`positionSub` measured a `display: none` element**. `whileHidden {offsetWidth: 0, offsetHeight: 0}` against `whileShown {197, 185}` — so `left + sw > innerWidth` could never be true and both the edge-flip and the bottom clamp were **dead code from the day they were written**. Invisible on a desktop, fatal at 390px: 12 of 13 submenus off-screen, three also off the bottom. `anchorMenu` had the order right; only `positionSub` did not. **Fixing it exposed a data-loss bug, stated as introduced-and-fixed inside one change rather than claimed as a pre-existing find.** With the submenu now on screen it lands over the row that opened it, and a tap replays the full mouse sequence — `mouseenter` opened the submenu and the synthesised `click` then ran whichever item was under the finger. Tapping `Clear ▸` wiped the cell. Hover now opens a submenu only under `(hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)`, checked per event so a device that gains or loses a mouse is answered as it is now. **Nothing listened to `visualViewport`** — `grep` returned zero hits — so the on-screen keyboard covered the cell being edited on both iOS and Android geometries. Written as *keep the edited cell inside what is visible*, not as keyboard handling, because a keyboard and a page zoom arrive through the same event and want the same answer. 44px targets via `(pointer: coarse)`, not a width breakpoint: a 390px *window* on a desktop still has a mouse. Phone targets under 24px went 8 → 3, desktop provably unchanged, and grid height unchanged at 629px. | Five mutations, each seen red — including `13 submenus opened; these landed off the screen` and `one tap on a submenu row wiped the cell`, both re-verified by the orchestrator. **A regression the worker introduced and caught by measuring rather than reading**: its first coarse-pointer draft grew the status bar and rescaled the viewport, which `editor.narrow-screens.spec.mjs` forbids but cannot see, because that spec runs with a mouse |

| PERF-D-01 | The grid drops frames while scrolling, and the cost tracks visible columns | Done | P1 | Reported as *"not that fluid"*, and measured rather than accepted or dismissed. Driving wheel events over a populated sheet and timing `requestAnimationFrame` intervals: **100×12 → 33.3ms median; 3000×12 → 33.3ms; 3000×40 → 66.6ms.** The reliable signal is the *scaling*, not the absolute figures — the probe awaits a frame per wheel event while `scheduleDraw` coalesces onto its own `rAF`, so it inflates the baseline, and that caveat is recorded here rather than left for the next reader to discover. What it does establish: **cost tracks the number of visible columns, not the size of the sheet** — 12 to 40 columns doubles the frame — so this is per-cell draw work and not a whole-sheet scan. **Profiled, and the assumed cause was wrong.** The brief said `draw()` JSON-serialises the viewport every frame and `session_display_list` has no caller — both true, and neither is the bottleneck: the whole viewport crosses the boundary in ~5 ms of a 26 ms frame and `JSON.parse` is under 1 ms of that. Wiring the display list would have replaced the cheap half. **The actual cause: the frame fetched about eight times the cells it drew.** `colCap`/`rowCap` are floored by `MIN_LINE = 8`, because a line *can* be 8px and one engine call has to be enough — so a 1280×800 view of 100px columns asked for **157 columns × 73 rows = 11 461 cells to draw 13 × 29**, and everything downstream measured and mapped all of it. That is exactly why the cost tracked *visible columns* rather than sheet size: narrower columns mean more of the over-fetch is real. Three fixes. **(a)** The cap stays — it is the safe over-estimate — and the line lists are trimmed to the window afterwards, keeping one line past the edge because that is where a spilling label clips and what `colAtX` falls back to. **(b)** The spill gather reduces to the *nearest* populated cell per row instead of pushing the whole band, and applies the spillable-text filter **after** reducing rather than before — which was also a **correctness** bug: a blocking number between a far label and the window never entered the set, so the scan ran through the column that should have stopped it and drew the label into a window Excel would not show it in. **(c)** `ctx.measureText` is cached on `font + text`; it was 17% of frame time, re-measuring the same strings at 60fps. | **Measured on the harness, before and after, by the orchestrator:** 102px columns `34.2 ms → 16.7 ms`, 30px columns `38.4 ms → 17.1 ms`; cells fetched `11 461 → 420 / 1260`. Five structural tests, each seen red by reverting its own fix — removing the trim gives `157 columns fetched to draw 13`, the unbounded gather gives `1040 cells held for a 21×26 window`, bypassing the cache gives `9360 re-measurements`. Structural rather than wall-clock deliberately: a timing assertion on a shared machine is a flaky test that gets deleted. **The harness itself had to be fixed twice, and both failures are the same class as the defect.** The original probe awaited a frame per wheel event while `scheduleDraw` coalesces onto its own rAF, inflating the baseline. The replacement awaited its timing promise *before* starting the scroll, so it measured 1.5 s of idle frames and reported a flat `16.7 ms` both before and after — a clean-looking number that proved nothing. It is kept in the tree as `frame-profile.mjs` with both mistakes written down |
| TAURI-002 | Typing into a cell was a rule only one host knew | Done | P1 | **Found by building the second host, which is the only thing that could have found it.** `build_set_op` — what typed text *means*: when it is a formula, when a leading apostrophe forces text, which number format `007` implies, when an entry keeps the style already there — was `pub(crate)` in `casual-calc-wasm`. 113 lines of engine semantics living in one host. A desktop shell could not type into a cell without reimplementing all of it, and the parts it would get wrong are exactly the ones nobody remembers. Nothing about it was ever WebAssembly-specific: it takes a session and returns an `Operation`. It is now `WorkbookSession::input_edit`, with its inverse `cell_input`, and **128 lines are gone from the wasm bridge**, which calls the shared one. Two hosts, one implementation — the same fault `RND-10` fixed for drawing, caught here *before* the duplicate existed rather than after. | `desktop/` compiles against the SDK alone and its shell test passes; the browser's 151 tests pass against the moved implementation |
| SDK-011 | `blank()` returns a workbook with no sheets | Done | P2 | Found while building the desktop shell, and fixed rather than mirrored. `WorkbookSession::blank()` produces a workbook of **zero** sheets — right for building one up programmatically, wrong for every interactive host: a window whose workbook has no sheets has nothing to draw, no tab strip and no cell to put a caret in. **Both hosts had already worked that out separately.** The browser pushed a `Sheet1` in `session_new`, the desktop shell pushed one in its own constructor — a rule written down nowhere, kept in two places, and a third host would have rediscovered it or shipped a blank window. `with_sheet()` sits **beside** `blank` rather than replacing it. Thirty-six callers use `blank` and most add sheets of their own, so changing what it returns would hand them two — a name that says which one you want costs less than a subtle change to what an existing one means. Both hosts now call it and their duplicated fixes are gone. **A mutation that proved nothing, caught before it was believed.** The first check ran `cargo test -p casual-calc-sdk "a\\|b"` — which is not a cargo filter, so *neither* test ran and the mutation reported "ok, 0 passed, 77 filtered out". Read quickly that looks like a pass. Running each test by name shows both failing `left: 0, right: 1`. A test count of zero is not a green result, and the alternation syntax that works in `grep` does not work here. | `a_new_session_can_be_asked_for_a_sheet_to_open_on` asserts both constructors together, so merging them would fail it; `the_opening_sheet_is_a_sheet_you_can_type_into` types a formula into the sheet a host is handed and checks its id is its own |
| DOC-029 | docs/44 describes a host-capability trait that does not exist | Done | P3 | `docs/44` described a host-capability trait the engine depends on. **No such type exists** anywhere in `crates/`, `server/` or `sdk/` — the name appears only in prose, in `08`, `19`, `44` and the changelog. What was actually built is two unrelated seams and a hole: `Environment` (a value, installed onto `Workbook::volatile_now`), `Cancel` (a real trait, in `casual-calc-model/src/cancel.rs`), and **no threading seam at all** — one `std::thread` hit in the whole workspace, in a stack-depth test. The doc now names the seams that exist, with citations, and says plainly that the trait is not built. **The promise is not lowered**: `19` invariant 7, `02` and `40` still state it, and the overdue decision is tracked as `ADR-019` rather than edited away. Two smaller fictions went with it — an `Engine` type that was never built, and a diagram claiming `casual-calc-io` opens `xlsx/ods/csv` when it depends only on `-model` and `-layout`. **What made it invisible:** `44` claims no CI gate at all, so nothing could have caught it; it was found by reading the design before building on it. | `docs/44` names only seams that exist, and each real gap is a row instead of a prose edit |
| SEC-017 | The only shipped host cannot stop a long job | Done | P1 | `SEC-012` made admission and full recalculation stoppable, and **the only shipped host took none of it**: `session_open` called plain `WorkbookSession::open`, `session_recalculate` plain `recalculate()`, and both landing-page helpers plain `import_package`. So the scenario `cancel.rs`'s own header describes — a workbook inside every limit and simply enormous holding the tab's one thread — was still what a browser user got. Fixed with a `performance.now()` deadline behind `session_set_time_budget_ms`, wired to all four, with the editor bounding an open at 10 s and F9 at 5 s. **A deadline, not a flag, and that is the design**: JS and wasm share one thread, so a `stop()` handler cannot run until the job it would stop has already returned — a settable flag would look like cancellation and never once fire. A **Keep waiting** button re-runs with no limit, because a limit the user cannot overrule is a refusal. `session_recalculate` now *returns* its outcome; it returned nothing, so a partial pass was indistinguishable from a complete one. **What made it invisible:** `SEC-012`'s tests exercised the SDK, where the seam is, not the bridge, where it went unused — and a seam that is cancellable looks taken. **Still open:** `collab_receive` recalculates uncancellably; left deliberately, because what an acknowledged-but-stale revision means to a peer is ADR-012's territory, and no test could be made to fail without deciding it first. | Three browser tests; seen red with the budget token neutralised — "a stopped open reported success", "F9 reported a finished calculation on a pass that was stopped part-way" |
| COL-43 | A relayed edit recalculates uncancellably | Done | P2 | The third instance of `SEC-017`'s class, and the one that could not be fixed with it. `collab_receive` calls plain `session.recalculate()`, so a peer's edit that triggers an expensive pass holds the tab exactly as an oversized open used to. Three lines to change and deliberately not changed: with no budget set the cancellable call is bit-identical, so **no test could be made to fail**, and making it meaningfully cancellable means deciding what an acknowledged-but-stale revision means to a peer — which is ADR-012's territory. A convergence question, not a plumbing one. Filed rather than fixed because it needs the decision first, which is the only reason this session accepts for filing. **Done, to the owner's decision: apply the edit, mark values stale.** `collab_receive` uses `recalculate_cancellable(&budget_token())` and returns the outcome. Convergence is defined on the **model**: the operation is applied and acknowledged before the recalculation starts, so the document converges on cell content whatever happens next, and only derived values are left behind — the session is already marked stale by the SDK, and the host is told so it can finish rather than present a half-fresh sheet as final. The budget is **per batch, not per operation**: twenty relayed edits at 50 ms each is a second of frozen tab, which is the thing being prevented rather than a smaller version of it. The editor passes the same budget a local F9 gets — a peer's edit is not entitled to more of this tab than the person sitting at it. **Two test-design traps, both of which produced a green test against unfixed code.** First, `CANCEL_CHECK_INTERVAL` is **4096**: the engine asks the cancel token every few thousand evaluations, not every cell, so the first draft's 400 formulas finished without ever asking and a zero budget changed nothing. The workload is 6000 formulas now, pasted as **one** operation because 6000 separate edits would be 6000 recalculations of a growing sheet. Second, the client guarded the budget on `recalcBudgetMs > 0`, which silently turned the test's zero budget into an unbounded one — the engine's own convention is that **negative** means no limit and `0` is a real budget of no time. Inventing a second convention next to an existing one is how a test stops testing. **Seen red without the fix**: restoring the plain `recalculate()` fails with *"no relayed batch reported stale values, so the recalculation was never cancellable"*, while the convergence half stays green — which is the point, since convergence was never the thing at risk. | A peer's edit that exceeds the budget leaves the tab responsive and the document convergent, asserted with two browser clients |
| DOC-032 | DOC-025's own gate is a gate CI does not run | Done | P1 | `DOC-025`'s acceptance is *No doc claims a gate CI does not run*, and `67` states its gate as *a documentation consistency check rejects the known contradictory phrases and validates generated facts*. **No such check exists.** CI runs exactly six `tools/check-*.py` scripts — tracker-ids, deploy-docs, site-nav, env-example, theme-tokens and ci-retry — and no doc-consistency check. The row that exists to catch unbacked gate claims is itself one. Worse, its headline number is unbacked too: **the "142 stale claims" has no list, no tool and no artifact**. The string appears exactly once in the repository, and `git log -S` shows it was introduced by the consolidation commit itself rather than by any audit; the pre-consolidation row named ten documents and no count. A backlog nobody can enumerate cannot be worked down, only carried. **Built.** `tools/check-doc-claims.py` runs in CI over 55 documents and 19 job names, asserting two mechanical things: every `tools/check-*.py` a document names exists *and* is run by a workflow, and every name a document calls a CI job is one. It caught its own row on the first run — `DOC-032` named the script before CI ran it. What it deliberately does **not** check is prose with no identifier in it: "this is covered by a test" cannot be verified by grep, and a gate that pretended to would be the very thing it exists to prevent. The unbacked "142 stale claims" figure is untouched by this and remains `DOC-025`'s problem: the string appears once in the repository and `git log -S` shows the consolidation commit introduced it, so there is no audit behind it. | `tools/check-doc-claims.py` runs in CI and fails on a doc naming a gate absent from the workflows |
| ADR-019 | The dual-host capability trait is three phases past its deadline | Done | P2 | `08` lists the capability-trait ADR as Pending, *to be Accepted at Phase 0*. Phases 0-1E, 2 and 3 have all shipped without it, and what got built instead is two unrelated seams (`Environment`, `Cancel`) and no threading seam. Meanwhile `19` invariant 7, `02` and `40` each state the trait in the **present tense**, so four documents describe as existing a thing that was never decided. The decision to make is whether it is one trait or the shape the code actually grew into. `TAURI-001` is the first work that cannot start without it, which is why this surfaces now. Two sibling ADRs are also overdue on the same register. **Decided by the owner: the single trait is rejected.** What grew instead — a value for the clock, a predicate for cancellation, and no threading seam because nothing is parallel — is accepted as the design, on the grounds that the three share only the property that the engine cannot supply them, which is not enough to make them one interface. Boundary invariant 7's wording becomes "no engine crate names a platform", and `02`, `19` and `40` now say that instead of promising a type nobody wrote; they were left promising it until the decision was made, because editing them first is how a decision gets made by nobody. `tools/check-host-seams.py` enforces it. `TAURI-001` is unblocked: a desktop shell supplies values and has nothing to implement. | The ADR register has no Pending entry whose stated deadline has already passed |
| DOC-031 | ADR-018 says an unshaped build reports it, and nothing can be asked | Done | P2 | `64` promises a build without shaping "reports that it lacks it rather than silently producing wrong output — a caller rendering a thumbnail can then decide, rather than discovering it from a customer", and **nothing could be asked**. `casual-calc-render` exposed the render entry points, the data-bar style and its constants; a caller rendering Arabic on a WebAssembly build got the glyphs in the wrong order and no indication of it. Now `shaping_available()` — `const`, so it costs nothing to branch on — and `needs_shaping()`, which is a *script* question answerable from the string alone, so a host can ask before rendering anything. Neither decides anything alone: an unshaped build is fine until the document contains Arabic, and Arabic is fine on a build that shapes; the pair is what identifies a thumbnail that will be silently wrong. **The gate was inert when first written, and that is the part worth keeping.** Asserting `shaping_available() == cfg!(feature = "shaping")` passes on the default build even when the function is hardcoded `true`, because both sides are then true — the mutation did not bite. It is only meaningful where the feature is **off**, which is the browser profile, and CI never built it. The `wasm` job now runs `cargo test -p casual-calc-render --no-default-features`, and the mutation reds two tests there. **What made the original invisible:** the promise was about the build nobody tested. | `shaping_available()` matches the feature, and CI runs the unshaped profile; seen red there with the answer hardcoded, and with Arabic removed from the script ranges |

## Closed after 2026-09-01

Moved verbatim from [14](14-EXECUTION-TRACKER.md) when the live tracker
crossed half closed rows and `tools/check-tracker-freshness.py` said so.
Ids are unchanged, because other documents and code cite them.

| PIV-10 | Every pivot part written since `PIV-02` fails schema validation | Done | P1 | Found by the slice-B worker while adding attributes next to it, and fixed there because a part that does not validate is a bad place to add more. `<pivotTableDefinition>` was written without `@dataCaption`, which `schemas/ooxml/sml.xsd:1096` marks `use="required"` — verified by the orchestrator at that line. **What made it invisible:** LibreOffice reads the part happily either way, and LibreOffice is the only oracle this repository has; the schema was vendored for the fidelity audit and had never been pointed at anything the writer produces. So the one tool that could have seen it was never asked, and the one that was asked cannot see it. | `xmllint` against the vendored schema, which nothing had done before — `pivotCacheDefinition1.xml` and `pivotTable1.xml` both validate, exit 0 |
| CI-030 | CI queues for hours, and the ceiling it hits belongs to the organisation and not to this repository | Done | P1 | **The first version of this row blamed this repository's merge rate, and that was wrong.** The mechanism is right and the attribution was not. Measured across the organisation: the account is on **GitHub Free, which is 20 concurrent jobs shared across all 17 repositories** — not per repository. At the time of writing 28 runs were queued or running org-wide, of which **`enclave` held 26 and this repository held 2**. So the org-wide stall is mostly not ours, and trimming our matrix would not have fixed it — which is exactly the wrong remedy the first diagnosis pointed at. What *is* ours is the shape of a single run: **a `ci` run here is 19 jobs against a ceiling of 20**, where `enclave`'s CI is 8. One run of ours therefore occupies about 95% of the organisation's entire allowance, so while it executes nothing else in any repository can start, and our own runs cannot overlap each other at all. That is why a merge here is followed by roughly ten minutes of single-file queue, and why the effect is felt in repositories that have nothing to do with this one. The consequence that makes it P1 is unchanged: a green tick arriving two hours after a merge cannot stop a bad merge, so `ci` is a record rather than a gate. Remedies, in order of leverage and none yet chosen: raise the ceiling (a Team plan is 60 concurrent jobs, and at one seat it is the cheapest lever by a wide margin); cut jobs per run here with path filters, since a docs-only change currently spends all 19 including three desktop bundle builds and the 3x2 platform matrix; and look at `enclave`'s volume, which is the binding constraint right now. **How this was missed the first time is the lesson**: the queue was read with `gh run list` inside one repository, which cannot show a limit that is shared across seventeen. The instrument was scoped more narrowly than the thing it was measuring — the same shape as every other entry in this table | Reduced rather than eliminated, and the residue is stated in `docs/15` rather than left to be found: `benchmark-smoke`, `fuzz-build` and `docker-build` are skipped on a pull request and `desktop` drops to Linux alone, taking a pull-request run from **19 jobs to 14** while main keeps all 19. The org's own volume was the larger half and the owner cut `enclave` from its own side in the same session. **No gate asserts the job count**, because the number that matters is the organisation's concurrent ceiling and no check inside this repository can see it — which is the same blind spot that produced the row's first, wrong diagnosis |
| CI-032 | A gate is counted as wired when its name appears in a workflow *comment* | Done | P2 | Found while wiring `TAURI-013`'s gate. `check-doc-claims.py` asserts that every `tools/check-*.py` a document names is actually run by CI, and it does that by searching the concatenated text of every workflow for the string. **A mention inside a `#` comment satisfies it.** `check-workflow-bashisms.py` was in exactly that state: `REL-01`'s row named it, `release-desktop.yml` mentioned it in a comment explaining what it asserts, the gate went green, and **the check never ran in CI at all** — it was only ever executed by `check-all.py` locally. So the promise "CI runs this" was kept by a sentence saying CI runs it. The fix is to parse the workflow and look for the script in a `run:` step, the way `check-workflow-bashisms.py` already resolves jobs rather than matching file text — the same lesson, one gate over. Both gates are wired as real steps now, so nothing is currently mis-reported; what is open is the hole that let it happen. | The workflow is parsed and only `run:` bodies are read, with comment lines inside a block scalar dropped — a comment inside a `run:` is no more executed than one beside it. Demonstrated on the same tree both ways: against a workflow whose only mention of a gate is `# tools/check-openable-extensions.py`, the old substring rule reports **WIRED (passes)** and the new rule reports **NOT WIRED (fails, correctly)**. Kept honest by `check-gate-selftest.py`, which now exercises two fixture workflows — one where the gate is only commented and one where a step runs it — because the rule depends on the YAML structure rather than on a line. **The stricter rule immediately found a second case**: `tools/check-rust.py`, named in `docs/14` and run by no workflow. That one is not a broken promise — it is an aggregate *runner* whose constituents CI runs as its own jobs — so it and `check-all.py` are exempted **by name, with the reason recorded**, rather than by a rule like "a script that runs other scripts is exempt", which would exempt any gate that grew a subprocess. That their constituents are all present is rule 3's job, which asserts `ci.yml`'s job list against the table in `docs/15`
| CI-033 | A gate crashed on a matrix it could not have seen, and main went red | Done | P1 | **Two changes of mine, an hour apart, each verified alone.** `REL-01` added `check-workflow-bashisms.py`, which resolves a job's matrix to decide whether the job can land on macOS, and read `include` as a list of dicts — which it was. `CI-030` then rewrote `ci.yml`'s `desktop` matrix as `fromJSON(...)` so a pull request builds one platform instead of three, and YAML hands an expression back as a **string**. The gate walked it and raised `'str' object has no attribute 'values'`. Main was red, and the pull request that exposed it was failing on a step neither of its own commits touched. Neither change was wrong alone, and neither could have been tested against the other, because the second did not exist when the first was written. Fixed by reading an expression **conservatively**: the raw text counts as a runner label, so a `fromJSON` literal naming `macos-latest` puts the job in scope. That is the safe direction — the cost of being wrong is checking a job that never runs on macOS, against missing one that does, and `desktop` is still in scope after the fix. The general lesson is the one worth keeping: **a gate that raises reports nothing, not nothing-wrong**, and there is no shape of green that distinguishes the two. | The three real matrix shapes are exercised in `check-gate-selftest.py`, the gate for gates, seen red against the old resolver: "`check-workflow-bashisms.runners_for` raises on an expression-valued matrix: AttributeError(\"'str' object has no attribute 'items'\")" |
| CI-034 | Two Cargo workspaces were never format-checked or linted by anything CI runs | Done | P1 | **Found by running `cargo fmt` in `desktop/` and watching it rewrite files nobody had touched.** `desktop/` and `fuzz/` are separate Cargo workspaces (`ADR-023`), and `cargo fmt --all` / `cargo clippy --workspace` reach only the workspace they run in — so **main was unformatted in both while every CI job was green**: two diffs in `desktop/src/close.rs`, five across `fuzz/`. This is `CI-014`'s shape a fourth time, and it compounds two separate holes. `tools/check-rust.py` had already grown desktop format and clippy gates when this gap was found once before, and **CI has never called that runner** — it is named in a comment in `ci.yml` and in no `run:` step, which is exactly `CI-032`'s hole in a second place, so the gates existed and nothing ran them. And the earlier fix grew a *desktop* format gate without growing the *fuzz* one, because it was written against the workspace that had just failed rather than against the rule. Both workspaces are now format-checked in the `format` job — `cargo fmt` parses rather than compiles, so neither needs the webview or GTK packages — and the desktop shell is linted inside the `desktop` job, which is where the packages clippy needs already are. | `cargo fmt --manifest-path desktop/Cargo.toml -- --check` and the same for `fuzz/` were **red on main before anything in this branch changed** — 2 and 5 files respectively — and are green after. `check-rust.py` grew the fuzz gate its desktop sibling has had since the last time this happened |
| TAURI-009 | The desktop File menu speaks the web page's vocabulary | Done | P1 | Asked for: open from disk, save/update, save-as or export, metadata, and being asked whether to become the default for supported extensions. **Four of the five already exist** — `native_open` raises the platform panel, `file.save` with `write_in_place` updates the opened file, `Properties…` opens a document-properties dialog with its own browser spec, and file associations are designed in [86](86-DESKTOP-RELEASE-IDENTITY-SETTINGS-AND-UPDATES.md) §6. What is wrong is the **naming**: the desktop menu is derived from the editor's own `MENUS`, so a desktop application offers **"Download ▸ Excel"** where every desktop application on every platform says *Save As*. Downloading is what a browser does; a desktop app writes a file. This is the same complaint as the branding strip (`UX-CHR-01`) one layer in — the web vocabulary surviving into a native shell. | Closed by the `UX-CHR-01/02` change and verified in the tree rather than from the commit message: `NATIVE_LABELS` at `webapp/editor.core.js:1212` with the web wording parked in `data-oc-label-web:1227`, so the *label* changes and the command id does not — `file.download.excel-xlsx` is still matched by `/^file\.download/` elsewhere |
| TAURI-011 | Closing the desktop window discards unsaved work without asking | Done | P1 | **The window-close route is closed; `Cmd+Q` is not, and that is stated rather than implied.** Nothing handled `CloseRequested`, and the editor's `beforeunload` is a web affordance neither the native close button nor the menu goes through — so the only warning the product had was one the shell could not reach. **`CloseRequested` cannot wait**: it is a synchronous callback on the event loop, and the question is answered in the webview, asynchronously, because only the editor knows whether the document is dirty and only it has a dialog that matches the application. So the first request is always refused, the webview is asked, and the answer returns as a command that latches and closes again; the second request sees the latch and passes. **A clean document agrees without a dialog** — the ordinary close costs one round trip and no question, because a dialog on every close is one people learn to dismiss without reading. And a shell with no editor closes anyway: a window that cannot be shut is a worse failure than one that shuts without asking. | 3 tests in a `close` module, seen red both ways: letting every close through -> "a close nobody has agreed to must be refused, or the question is never asked"; removing the clean-document path -> "the clean-document path must agree before the dialog is reached, or every close asks". `main.rs` uses that module rather than holding a second copy of the script. 53 desktop tests, the workspace, clippy, fmt and 20 gates all pass. **What no test here covers**: the dialog itself needs a running window, and macOS `Cmd+Q` arrives as `RunEvent::ExitRequested` rather than here — a separate route, closed by `TAURI-014` in the same branch |
| TAURI-014 | Quitting the application still discards unsaved work without asking | Done | P1 | The other half of `TAURI-011`, and it does not arrive through the window: macOS `Cmd+Q` terminates the *application* as `RunEvent::ExitRequested`, never `WindowEvent::CloseRequested`. Reuses the same latch and the same script rather than growing a second pair, so there is one question and one answer that cannot disagree. **The two ways out are told apart, and that is the substance rather than a nicety**: answering a quit by closing the window leaves the process running after the user asked it to stop, and answering a close by quitting takes away more than was asked for. The script carries which one it is and says so **in the words the user reads** — a dialog saying "Close" when they pressed `Cmd+Q` describes something that is not about to happen. `quit` is a parameter rather than two scripts because the question is identical and only the consequence differs; two scripts would be two places for the wording to drift, and the wording is the only thing between a user and lost work. | 4 tests, both directions seen red: one verb for both routes -> "a quit must say quit: a dialog naming the wrong verb describes something that is not about to happen"; not telling the shell which was asked -> "the shell must be told which way out was asked for, or it answers the wrong one". 54 desktop tests, the workspace, clippy, fmt and 20 gates pass. **Still not observed in a running app** — the dialog needs a window, and `TAURI-010` showed what that evidence looks like |
| TAURI-012 | A native accelerator is consumed before the webview sees the key | Done | P1 | From [87](87-THE-DESKTOP-DIMENSION.md), and it is worse in the desktop shell than in the browser rather than neutral. Microsoft's own Mac table gives **Cmd+T for both** "cycle absolute/relative references" and "create Table", disambiguated by edit mode. We took the Table half and none of the disambiguation — and because a native menu accelerator is consumed *before the webview sees the keystroke*, Cmd+T fires mid-edit and opens a modal over a half-typed formula, where the browser build would have let the key through. The chords genuinely differ per platform too: insert-date is `Control+;` while insert-time is `Cmd+;`, and AutoSum is `Shift+Cmd+T` against Windows' `Alt+=` — so folding `Cmd` into `Ctrl` is not sufficient, and **there is no `isMac` test in `webapp/` at all**. Also unbound: `Ctrl+O` and `Ctrl+N`; and `Ctrl+S` works but `showShortcuts()` — the only in-app shortcut documentation — does not mention it. | Rust: `an_accelerator_that_collides_with_editing_is_released_while_editing` (red: `releases_during_edit` did not exist) and `a_chord_that_differs_by_platform_is_not_folded`, seen red on its own with `left: Some("CmdOrCtrl+;")  right: Some("Ctrl+;")` after the fold was restored — checked separately, because the first run failed to compile and a compile error is not evidence that an assertion would have failed. Browser: `Ctrl+O opens the file picker rather than the browser's`, `Ctrl+N starts a new workbook` and `the shortcut list names the file chords`, all red. **`Ctrl+S` was documented by nothing and my first version of that test said it was**, because `toContain("Ctrl+S")` is satisfied by `Ctrl+Shift+Z`, which is already in the list — matched to the end of the chord now, and read from the dialog's own rows rather than from `document.body`, which contains the File menu and would have reported the list as complete either way. The existing `every chord the shortcuts panel advertises does something` then failed on the new row and demanded it be pressed, which is the gate working: Open raises the OS picker and leaves no mark on the page, so the mark is made by the editor's own click reaching the real `#tb-open` input  |
| TAURI-010 | A double-clicked spreadsheet would open a blank window | Done | P1 | The opening half built and **proven before** the associations were declared, which is the order this row existed to enforce. `argv` on Windows and Linux, `RunEvent::Opened` on macOS — which is `cfg`-gated to macOS in Tauri itself, so it really is the only route there — plus a second activation on a running app, and a path arriving *before* the window exists, held until there is something to open it into. **The evidence is observation, not documentation**: the worker built the shell, hand-assembled an `.app`, registered it with `lsregister`, launched with no arguments and then `open -a`'d two files into the running process, tracing the whole loop. Reverting the feature and repeating that produced one line of log and no document — the blank window this row names, reproduced. `path_from_args` asks the UTF-8 question of the `-` and `file:` rules only and **never of the path**, because a Linux filename is bytes and `to_str()` silently refused a whole class of real files; `take_pending_open` is `async` because a synchronous command would read the whole workbook on the drawing thread, on the first frame a user sees. | 8 tests, each mutated and watched fail. **Verified by the orchestrator**: shrinking the associations to the four `docs/86` claimed -> "bundle.fileAssociations and SessionFormat::for_extension disagree", left 5 against right 7. That doc said four and the engine opens **seven** — missing `.ods`, `.xlsm` and `.tab`, which is the second-list-that-drifts mistake the same section warns about two paragraphs earlier. Corrected here, and the shell now asks the SDK rather than holding a list |
| TAURI-013 | Three copies of the openable-extension list, and nothing compares two of them | Done | P3 | Found by the `TAURI-010` worker. `desktop/src/launch.rs` and `crates/casual-calc-wasm/src/io.rs` now hold identical `CANDIDATE_EXTENSIONS` arrays, because `desktop/` is a separate cargo workspace (`ADR-023`) and cannot depend on the wasm crate; `desktop/src/dialog.rs:26` holds a third, documented as a deliberate pre-report floor. Both candidate lists filter through `SessionFormat::for_extension`, so **neither can over-claim** — a format in one and not the other is merely absent from either the panel or the associations, never wrongly offered. That bound is why this is P3 and not P1. Nothing compares the two today. | `tools/check-openable-extensions.py`, red in both directions before it was kept: dropping `psv` from the desktop half alone prints "offered by the picker, refused on double-click: ['psv']", and adding `numbers` to the association list prints "registers file associations we cannot open: ['numbers']". The third list is checked as a **subset** rather than for equality, because it is a deliberate pre-report floor — narrowing it is a decision, and claiming a file type from another application and then failing to open it is not |
| TAURI-007 | The desktop app has no release, only per-PR bundles | Done | P1 | A `desktop-v*` tag now promotes what CI already builds. **This built nothing new** — `ci.yml`'s `desktop:` job already compiles all three platforms on every push; what was missing was a way to *get* the bundles. The release swaps CI's cheap-to-build formats for the ones a person downloads (`.dmg`, AppImage) and attaches a SHA-256 per file, because unsigned builds leave a checksum as the only verifiable thing. **Three of my own mistakes, each caught before it could fail at release time:** I invented the `download-artifact` action pin, since no workflow here used that action and there was nothing to copy — resolved the real one through the API and confirmed the method against `upload-artifact`'s existing pin; the checksum step assumed `shasum` on all three runners, where `sha256sum` is absent on macOS and Windows bash was unverified; and **`--prerelease` would have killed the update channel silently** — `releases/latest/download` skips prereleases, so the manifest an updater fetches would 404 on every machine with nothing in any log we own. `release-sdk.yml` is right to use the flag and this is right not to: an npm package is found by version, a desktop updater follows a "latest" URL. | The collect step exercised locally against a fake bundle tree — two Linux bundles with checksums, and **exit 1 when the bundler emitted nothing**, which is the failure `ci.yml` already knows (`tauri build` exiting 0 having produced nothing). My first harness for that was itself wrong, passing the glob through a shell variable where bash cannot brace-expand it |
| DATA-NT-01 | A number stored as text makes SUM return zero, and nothing says so | Done | P1 | **From "no formulas are working, SUM resulted zero" — where the engine was right and the report was fair.** `SUM` over text returns 0, which is exactly what Excel returns, and the engine is correct to keep `"10"` as text: coercing on the way in would silently change somebody's data, and `="10"<"9"` is `TRUE` for a string. What was missing is that **nothing said so**. A person reading that zero cannot tell a correct empty sum from a column an importer turned into strings. `session_column_stats` already counted `numberAsText`, so the knowledge existed — in a panel nobody opens when a sum looks wrong. Now `session_cells` emits `nt` for a literal string that parses as a finite number (a formula is excluded: Excel flags constants, and a formula's result is its author's choice), the grid draws Excel's green corner in the **top right** so it cannot hide the red error marker in the top left, hovering names the consequence *and* the fix, and `Data ▸ Convert text to numbers` does what Excel's *Convert to Number* does. The arithmetic is unchanged, and a test pins that first — making `SUM` coerce would be a far worse bug than the one being fixed. | Five tests, and **two bugs in my own fix were caught by them rather than by review**. Converting a `@`-formatted cell re-typed it as text, so the sum went 0 to 20 instead of 60 — Excel moves such a cell to General and this did not. Then, with the format cleared, it was **still** 20: `input_edit` decides how to type its argument from the cell's *current* state, and every operation in a batch is built before any is applied, so it still saw the format this same batch was clearing. `SetValue` with an already-parsed `f64` is the fix for that. The seeding was wrong at first too — `="10"` is a *formula*, which the flag correctly ignores, so the test proved nothing; the two routes that really produce this are a Text-formatted column and a leading apostrophe, and both are exercised now. 458 browser tests, workspace tests, clippy, fmt and 22 gates pass
| REL-03 | The `.deb` cannot install on Ubuntu 22.04, and the AppImage looks like it failed to download | Done | P1 | **Reported from a real download on Ubuntu 22.04: "tried to download, it's not opening".** Two separate causes, both verified against the published artefacts rather than guessed. (1) The `.deb` declares `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-0` — read out of its own `control` file — and **that package does not exist before Ubuntu 23.04**; 22.04 LTS has `4.0`. Tauri v2 requires 4.1, so this is a platform constraint rather than a packaging mistake, and the install fails outright with an unmet dependency. Nothing said so anywhere. (2) The AppImage is **type 2** (`AI\x02` in the ELF header), which mounts itself with **FUSE 2** — Ubuntu 24.04 ships only FUSE 3, and the failure is *silent*: a double-click does nothing at all, with no message, which reads as a broken download. `--appimage-extract-and-run` needs nothing installed and is now the first thing offered. **The AppImage is the answer for 22.04, and that was checked rather than assumed**: its squashfs was extracted and it carries `usr/lib/libwebkit2gtk-4.1.so.0`, `libjavascriptcoregtk-4.1.so.0` and `libgtk-3.so.0` inside it — which is what the 84 MB is — so it does not use the system's webkit at all. The download page now leads with the AppImage on Linux and marks the `.deb` as 23.04-or-later. **It also separates the two macOS dialogs**, which the previous version conflated: "Apple could not verify" is the ordinary quarantine block and `xattr` alone fixes it, while "is damaged" is the unvalidatable-signature case from `REL-02` that also needs `codesign`. Telling someone to re-sign an application when all they needed was to clear a flag is the wrong instruction. | Verified on the published release: `ar x` on the `.deb` for its `Depends` line, the ELF magic for the AppImage type, and `unsquashfs -o 944632 -l` for the bundled libraries — the offset found by scanning for a superblock whose block size is a plausible power of two, because the first `hsqs` match in the file is a coincidence and unsquashfs refuses it
| REL-02 | The macOS build says it is damaged, and it is not | Done | P1 | **Reported from a real download of `desktop-v0.0.0`**: macOS says *"OpenCalc is damaged and can't be opened. You should move it to the Bin."* It is not damaged, and `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine` **does not fix it** — which is what made this worth diagnosing rather than filing under `TAURI-008`'s "unsigned builds warn". `codesign -dv` on the published bundle: `flags=0x20002(adhoc,linker-signed)`, `Sealed Resources=none`, and `codesign --verify` says *"code has no resources but signature indicates they must be present"*. That is the **linker's** ad-hoc signature, not `codesign`'s. Every arm64 binary must be signed to run at all, so the linker supplies one — but it seals no resource directory, and Gatekeeper validating a bundle that claims resources and has none reports the whole application as damaged. **The failure is not "unsigned", it is "signed in a way that cannot be validated"**, and the two want different fixes: the first is a certificate nobody has bought, the second is free. Fixed with `bundle.macOS.signingIdentity: "-"`, which is Tauri's own ad-hoc signing, applied **during** bundling — it cannot be a workflow step after the build, because `--bundles dmg` produces the `.app` and wraps it in one command, so signing afterwards would sign a copy the `.dmg` does not contain. This does not replace code signing and does not remove the quarantine prompt (`TAURI-008` stands); it removes the *second*, wrong error, the one that tells a user to move a working application to the bin. The site also gained `download.html` — three platform cards and the one-time step each operating system needs, because an unsigned build a user cannot open is not a download. It states the Apple Silicon limit too: the 0.0.0 `.dmg` is `arm64` only and will not run on an Intel Mac, which nothing said anywhere. | Diagnosed and fixed on the published artefact itself, not on a rebuild: the released `.dmg` was downloaded and mounted, `codesign --verify --deep --strict` failed with the message above, `codesign --force --deep --sign -` was applied to a copy, and the same command then exited 0 with `Sealed Resources version=2 rules=13`. The release job asserts it from now on — `codesign --verify --deep --strict` on the built `.app`, because `--force` exits 0 whether or not the result validates, and a signature that exists and does not verify is the entire defect |
| REL-01 | The desktop release drops the macOS build, and only a release could have shown it | Done | P1 | **Found by running the dry run, which is the only thing that runs this code.** The `desktop-v*` pipeline collected its Linux and Windows bundles and failed on macOS with `shopt: globstar: invalid shell option name`. macOS ships **bash 3.2** — the last GPLv2 release, and unchanged for fifteen years — where `shopt -s globstar` is an error rather than a no-op, and under `set -e` it takes the step with it. The `.dmg` was never collected. No glob in the file uses `**`, so the option was never needed. **What makes this a gate rather than a one-line fix is where it happened**: the same step already carried a hand-written portable `sum256()`, written precisely because `sha256sum` is absent on macOS — so the platform difference was known, reasoned about, and then walked into two lines above. Knowing a rule does not catch the next instance of it.
