It was also invisible until release: `ci.yml` builds a macOS `.app` and never runs the collection step, so no pull request could have failed. `tools/check-workflow-bashisms.py` resolves the matrix and reads only steps that can actually land on macOS — **its first version matched per file and immediately produced two false positives** (`mapfile` in two `ubuntu-latest` jobs), which is the same too-wide-instrument failure the rest of this table keeps recording. | Reproduced locally under the same bash the runner has: `/bin/bash -c 'set -euo pipefail; shopt -s nullglob globstar'` exits 1 with the runner's exact message, and exits 0 without `globstar`. The gate is red on the unfixed workflow (`release-desktop.yml:124: job \`bundle\` uses shopt -s globstar`) and green on the fixed one, with no false positive among the three macOS-capable jobs |
| FID-45 | A script no bundled face covers draws nothing, and nothing says so | Done | P1 | **The claim needed narrowing, and checking the citation is what narrowed it.** `missing_scripts` is not unused: `WorkbookSession::missing_font_coverage()` exists and is carefully documented — *"the difference between a support ticket and a sentence naming what to install"*. What did not exist was any path that **asks** it. So a caller who exported a PDF and read the compatibility report — the thing whose whole job is to say what could not be carried — was told nothing, and had to know to make a second call it had no reason to suspect. Worse than the undrawn-picture case beside it in the same report: a missing picture leaves an obvious hole, where missing text leaves a cell that **merely looks empty**, indistinguishable from one that is. Named per script rather than per character, because "this sheet contains Devanagari and no face covering it is installed" is actionable and a list of code points is not. | `a_script_no_face_covers_is_named_in_the_export_report`, asserted **through the export path** rather than by calling `missing_font_coverage` directly — calling it directly passes whether or not anything folds it in, which is precisely the gap. Seen red: `exported without the report naming that script: []`. **My first assertion was wrong**, looking for "font" or "script" against an entry reading "no installed face covers it" — the fix was working and the test was not; it now pins the script's own name, which is what a reader needs to know what to install |
| HIST-01 | No version history: the document has no past a user can reach | Done | P1 | Raised by the user, and it is the largest single feature gap against every competitor named — Google Sheets keeps named versions and per-cell edit provenance, Excel has AutoRecover plus SharePoint history, OnlyOffice keeps revisions. Here undo is the only route backwards and it dies with the tab. ~~Most of the machinery already exists and is pointed the wrong way: the collaboration server's append-only op log *is* a history and nothing reads it as one.~~ **That premise was wrong, and `SAVE-09` measured it.** The op log is a *resume buffer*: no timestamps anywhere, no per-revision author, roughly 400-600 ops retained, Redis capped and TTL'd at 10k and one hour, the session evicted 30 seconds after the last participant leaves, nothing persisted. It is correctly scoped for what it does and simply is not a history — which `docs/57` already stated as a boundary, that the server is "not a file store, a version history". So this was built on **snapshots** with storage of their own (`SAVE-08`, `HIST-03`), and the premise is struck through here rather than left to read as though the log had ever been an option. The open questions are policy, not mechanism: how far back, what a named version costs to store, what a user without the collab server gets (local-only is the common case today), and whether restoring is a new op or a rewrite. Design note before code. Note also that history and `COL-44` interact: a log containing moves it cannot transform cannot be replayed. **One sentence above is false and is corrected here rather than left to brief the work** (`SAVE-09`, measured): *"that log **is** a history; nothing reads it as one"*. It is not a history. There are no timestamps anywhere, no per-revision author, roughly 400–600 ops retained, Redis capped and TTL'd at 10k and one hour, the whole session evicted 30 seconds after the last participant leaves, and nothing persisted — a correctly-scoped **resume buffer** that looks like a history from outside. This makes the row **larger** than it was written, not smaller: history needs its own storage, which is what `docs/83` §6 designs, and `SAVE-08` is the build. The row's open questions are unchanged and still policy rather than mechanism. | `a version can be saved and the document brought back to it`, `a restore is one undo step, and undo puts the document back`, and `saving a version twice with no edit between does not store two`. The undo one is the load-bearing assertion: a restore arrives as one `Operation::Batch` of ordinary edits and a batch has one combined inverse, so undoing it must bring back **both** changed cells — a per-cell restore would leave half of it applied, and that is what the test looks for rather than merely counting undo steps. All three were red before the bindings existed, because `runCommand("file.version-history")` had no id to run and the panel could not open. 450 browser tests, workspace tests, clippy, fmt, doc and 22 gates pass
| HIST-03 | A saved version does not survive a reload | Done | P1 | **`SAVE-08` built the store host-agnostic for exactly this and `HIST-01` reached it from the editor, and neither carried it across a reload** — a version lived until the tab closed and no longer. A history you lose by pressing F5 is not a history, and the feature read as working right up until somebody refreshed. Three bindings rather than one blob, because the host stores it in two pieces and should not take a megabyte apart to read a date: `session_versions_manifest` (metadata, **including hidden entries** — hiding is a display choice the user made, and losing it on reload would un-hide everything they had tidied away), `session_version_bytes`, and `session_version_add`. The same `meta`/`bytes` split `editor.drafts.js` already keeps, so opening the panel costs kilobytes and only a restore costs megabytes. Ids come back unchanged, because a restored history whose ids were renumbered is a different history and the id is what `restore_version` addresses. Bytes are written before the manifest: if the tab dies between them, storage holds bytes no manifest names — wasted space, cleaned on the next write — rather than a manifest naming bytes that are gone, which would be a version the panel offers and cannot restore. Keyed per document, and `File ▸ New` forgets *after* `newDocument()` clears the name, so it removes the bucket the new document will use rather than the old one's. | `a saved version is still there after a reload, and still restores` — which restores as well as counts, because a row in a list whose bytes did not come back is worse than no row. Plus `versions are kept per document`. Two errors of my own on the way: `reloadVersionsForTest` was added to an import list *and* declared locally, which broke boot entirely so every test in the file timed out on "loading engine…" rather than failing an assertion; and the compression test seeded 400 cells in a loop and hit the 60s timeout — the cost was 400 wasm round-trips, not bytes, and thirty long repeated strings prove the same thing in thirteen seconds |
| HIST-02 | No change tracking and no per-edit attribution | Done | P2 | Distinct from `HIST-01` and often confused with it: history is *what the document was*, tracking is *who changed what and whether it is accepted*. Excel's Track Changes and Sheets' edit history both answer the second, and neither is reachable here — `COL-35` broadcasts an in-progress edit to peers but nothing is retained once it lands. Depends on `HIST-01`'s storage decision; filed now so it is not silently folded into it and quietly dropped. **Designed in [89](89-CHANGE-ATTRIBUTION-AND-TRACKING.md).** The finding that shapes everything: the model carries **no authorship at all** — an `Operation` has no author and neither does the wire — and `undo_would_discard` already says so in `crates/casual-calc-transaction/src/lib.rs:437`, where the absence is relied on. Identity exists at the connection (`ADR-014`'s JWT, `presence.rs:47`) and is thrown away one layer later, so the gap is exact: **it is known at the moment of the edit and not kept**. The note proposes one slice — an interned `AuthorId` on the cell plus a side table, an `author` on `WireOperation` rather than on `Operation`, and one line added to the hover `DATA-NT-01` already built — and **refuses three things with reasons**: Excel's Track Changes, because accept/reject is a workflow with its own storage and conflict rules against `ADR-011`'s OT rather than a display; Sheets' every-change-forever, because that is `SAVE-09`'s op-log idea again and is unbounded where last-writer-per-cell is bounded by the sheet; and a per-cell timestamp, because eight bytes on every cell answers a question asked about one cell occasionally, when the version already carries the time. The one open question is the product owner's: whether an author is a display name or a stable identity, since the second puts something identifying a *person* into the model and that is a privacy surface a spreadsheet did not previously have. | Built in four slices, each with the test that would fail under the alternative. **Identity**: two people called Alex are two authors and a rename does not split one, because `AuthorTable::intern` matches on the host's opaque id and on the name only when there is none — the product owner's answer, and it would be the opposite under name-matching. **Cost**: `a_session_with_no_author_attributes_nothing`, because "free unless shared" is the property the side-map shape was chosen for. **Narrowness**: `a_dependent_formula_is_not_attributed_to_whoever_moved_its_precedent` — Excel does not credit the author of `A1` with every cell that sums it, and `written_cells` is deliberately narrower than the recalculation plan. **Refusal**: `a_refused_edit_attributes_nothing`, since a name left for an edit that did not happen is a lie. **Wire**: `an_edit_arrives_attributed_to_the_person_who_made_it`, against a receiver that already knows a different person so the ids cannot line up by accident — `COL-12`'s failure in a new field — and `a_peer_that_predates_attribution_still_reads_a_message_carrying_it`, which decodes through a struct without the field, because the no-bump claim is worth a test rather than a paragraph. **Visible**: the hover names the person *and* says "since this file was opened", which is not a hedge — attribution does not survive a round-trip, and unlike an image that visibly vanishes it *looks* present in a reopened file because the cells are all still there. 465 browser tests, 26 collaboration tests in real browsers, workspace tests, clippy, fmt, `cargo doc` and 22 gates
| COL-56 | `collab.js` never turns `desynced` into a rejoin | Done | P1 | **The status was the only trace, and this editor has no `desynced` branch at all** — `editor.core.js` handles `lost`, `live`, `reconnecting`, `refused` and `stopped`, so `status.textContent` was never written. Measured on two real browsers against the real server with a genuine move-vs-move refusal: the bar read **"collaborating"** over a document that had stopped being shared, `sockets: 0`, revision frozen at 0 while the peer committed 1. **A plain `reconnect()` is not the recovery**: the server recognises the resume key, answers `resumed`, and `collab_resume` deliberately keeps the engine's latch (`session_tests.rs:1664` asserts it), so the next arrival throws again — rotating the key is the fix, because an unrecognised key is an ordinary join and an ordinary join comes back with a snapshot. Found on the way: the `resumed` branch said `status("live")` with no `lostUnsent` guard, so **any** reconnect after **any** loss wiped the notice — the same disappearing-notice failure one socket later. Unacknowledged work is named, never offered: it was written against a revision the client has abandoned. | `collab.spec.mjs:609`, two browsers and a real refusal rather than an injected frame. **Verified by the orchestrator, not the worker**: M1 whole fix reverted → `["joined"]` alone, "a desynced client never rejoined"; M3 reconnect kept and only the key rotation removed → same failure, which is what proves the key is the fix; M2 the `lostUnsent` guard removed → bar reads "collaborating" against `/were not saved/`. 31 collab tests pass |
| CI-025 | `serve.py` cache-busts only `editor*.js`, so one agent's browser run can silently test another's tree | Done | P1 | **Reproduced green before it was fixed**, which is the whole point of the row: a second checkout was served on the suite's port, the pre-fix tree was restored, and `browser-smoke` reported **9 passed, exit 0** while that other checkout's access log showed **165 requests** — the run tested a tree that was not its own and said nothing. **The mechanism was worse than the row stated.** `BUILD` was not falling back to `"dev"` because the query never changed; it was `"dev"` *even when the server stamped correctly*, because `editor.js` is `export * from "./editor.core.js"` and a static import evaluates its dependency **before** the importing module's own body — so `editor.core.js` read `globalThis.__opencalcBuild` before `editor.js` had assigned it. `?b=dev` was therefore the URL for every build this dev server has ever served, in every context. That is why the fix stamps each module's own specifier rather than broadening the HTML rewrite: it removes the dependence on evaluation order. **18 of 20 module URLs carried no useful tag** before. Half two is enforcement: `reuseExistingServer: false` plus a config-load preflight that names the occupant — at config load, because Playwright starts `webServer` *before* `globalSetup`, so a `globalSetup` check runs after the run has already attached. | `gate.served-tree.spec.mjs` — a browser spec rather than a `tools/check-*.py`, because the failure is "the *page* loaded the wrong bytes" and only a served run reaches it. Hashes the served modules **from inside the page** against the working tree. **Verified by the orchestrator**: with both halves reverted and a foreign checkout on the port, `editor.modes.spec.mjs` reported **14 passed, exit 0, while 323 requests were served from the foreign tree**; with the fix, the preflight refuses before the run starts and names the other checkout by path |
| CI-027 | A stale `webapp/pkg` silently outlives the source it was built from | Done | P1 | Bit the orchestrator **twice in one session** and a worker once. `webapp/pkg` is gitignored, so the build on disk survives a branch switch, a `git checkout --`, a stash and a revert, then fails in whatever way its *old* source did — a plausible failure, not an obviously impossible one. Once it failed three `editor.drafts` tests on a branch whose source was **correct** (a leftover from a mutation experiment), which read exactly like a regression in the change under test. `CI-025` proved the served *JavaScript* is the working tree's; this is the input it did not cover. The suite's preflight now refuses a build whose sources do not match, and `tools/build-wasm.py` records the hash CI and a laptop both compare against — CI calls the script rather than `wasm-pack`, so the hash path is exercised there instead of waiting to break on somebody's machine. | **Two of my own drafts were wrong, and both were caught by running it rather than reading it.** The first compared *mtimes*, on the stated reasoning that "was this built after the source moved" is the one question a timestamp answers — it is, and it is the wrong question: a branch switch rewrites every source mtime without changing a byte, so it fired on a build that was perfectly current, and a gate that cries wolf after every checkout is one people route around. The second hashed content on both sides and still disagreed on a clean tree: Python sorts `Path` by segments and JavaScript sorts strings, so a directory whose name contains a hyphen sorts before or after its sibling depending on which language you ask — a hyphen is below `/` as a character and above it as a segment boundary — and a different order is a different digest. Verified in three states — clean (passes), mtimes touched but bytes unchanged (passes, which the first draft failed), and one byte appended (refused, naming the mismatch) |
| COL-61 | The transform refused a case its own module docs did not name, and nothing counted the refusals | Done | P2 | `transform`'s "What is not handled yet" listed two cases. There are **three**: two concurrent line moves whose source bands *partially overlap* also refuse, and that is the only one of the three a user reaches by ordinary dragging — it is what latched the session behind `COL-55` and `COL-56`. Measured, not read: disjoint, identical, crossing and adjacent bands all transform; only partial overlap has no answer, because the two moves disagree about which lines they are carrying. **What made it invisible:** every test here asked whether *one pair* behaves, and `overlapping_but_unequal_move_bands_are_refused` had asserted this very refusal for three weeks — but nothing asked what the whole surface *is*, so the docs could go short without any test disagreeing. My own reading was wrong first: I inferred from `grep` that `MoveColumns` had no transform arm at all, and running it refuted that in one command — the arm is in the `transform/` submodule. | `the_refusal_surface_is_exactly_these_cases` pins the exact set over *geometries*, not just variant pairs — a matrix over pairs alone reports the surface as one case wide, because move-vs-move only refuses on overlap. Seen red by widening the transform to refuse `MoveColumns x InsertColumns`: the test names the new pair on both sides. 240 crate tests, clippy, fmt and `cargo doc -D warnings` all exit 0 |
| CHT-07 | A chart's protocol break is the quiet kind | Done | P1 | `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 7 -> 8, landed **with** `CHT-05` rather than before it: a bump with nothing behind it, followed by a wire change with no bump, is two half-measures where the point is that the two must arrive together. `SheetMetadata` field 19 `CHARTS` is a **whole-vector replace**, so an old client that cannot see `grouping` does not ignore it — it writes the vector back without it on its next submit, and the sender's stacked chart becomes clustered for everyone. That is the *quiet* break `docs/62`'s rule names: the two peers read the same message differently and the one that loses says nothing. `COL-54`'s new enum variant was the loud kind, and loud is better. The reasoning is written beside the constant, next to the paragraph explaining why `Draft` did **not** move it — the two cases sit together so the rule reads as a rule rather than as a habit. | `check-doc-claims` caught all three documents that still stated 7 (`docs/08`, `AGENTS.md`, `docs/84`); the browser suite's own `PROTOCOL` constant is asserted against the engine's by a CI step |
| CHT-13 | The host resolves every chart's data every frame and uses eight integers of it | Done | P1 | **The brief's own numbers were wrong in two ways, and the worst case is worse and differently shaped.** At 10,000 rows x 6 series the payload is 592,530 bytes, not 1.4 MB — but `chart_data::read_strip` allocates `vec![blank; strip.len()]`, one entry per *named* cell whether populated or not, so after `SEC-024`'s cap a chart whose series name whole columns costs 65,536 points per series **on an empty sheet**: 2,162,988 bytes and 14 ms a frame, for a chart `session_chart_items` draws in 510 bytes. Reachable by opening a file, not by having a big sheet — `SEC-024` turned an OOM kill into 2 MB a frame and did not remove the per-frame cost. It is also *eight* integers, not four: `anchoredRect` reads `fx,fy,tx,ty` too, and narrowing to four would have snapped every chart to a column boundary. Exactly one consumer, so this narrowed in place rather than splitting the wire. Measured in Chromium: **8.80 ms of a 16.7 ms budget, every frame, for a rectangle** -> 0.00 ms. `RND-05` was never at risk: both renderers go through `chart::push_chart`, and the removed payload fed neither. | `chart_frame_payload` (3 native) and `editor.frame-budget.spec.mjs`. **Verified by the orchestrator**: restoring the per-point cost fails two of the three on a **one-byte** difference (73 against 74), because each asserts an *equality between two payloads differing only in the range the series name* rather than a byte ceiling — so it catches a leak of any size and needs no re-tuning when a field is added. `no_charts_is_an_empty_list` passed under the mutation and the worker said so |
| RND-15 | `rowAtPx` is not an inverse: scrolling a sheet with wrapped rows paints a blank grid | Done | P1 | **Reported from a running editor against the user's own file**, and the report was exact: "while scrolling .. upper rows become empty and flickers", "when data exceeds visual page limit .. before that there is no scroll so it's good". The engine stores one height per row and knows nothing about wrapped text, so the editor grows rows itself and folds the growth into `rowOffsetPx`/`rowAtPx`. The **forward** direction was right. The inverse iterated `session_row_at_px(px - growthBefore(guess))` four times on the stated reasoning that growth is monotonic so it converges — **it does not converge, it is a 2-cycle**: for a guess above the answer it subtracts the growth of every grown row above it, drives the pixel argument **negative**, the engine clamps that to row 0, and the next step lands back on the original guess. Measured: `rowAtPx(200)` answered row 10, whose top edge is at 896 px; at a scroll of 800 it answered a row past the last, so the frame held `rows: 0, geoItems: 0` and the grid painted blank — no numbers, no lines, nothing. The flicker is that every scroll step lands somewhere different. Now a binary search over the growth segments: `growthBefore` is a step function, so **within one segment the inverse is the engine's own** — one call with a fixed offset — and which segment is a monotone predicate. log2(grown rows) calls, exact, against an unbounded guess. **What made it invisible:** it needs both grown rows *and* a scrollable sheet, so the default document cannot show it; nine synthetic scroll probes (vertical, horizontal, diagonal, fast, bursts, retina, deep) all passed because none of them wrapped text. The user's file did, on every row, because it carries no `<cols>` at all. | `editor.row-at-px.spec.mjs` asserts the **defining property** — `rowOffsetPx(r) <= px < rowOffsetPx(r+1)` — not remembered numbers, since pinning "row 2 at 200 px" passes for any implementation that happens to hit that one case; it fails at essentially every sampled pixel without the fix. A second test asserts the frame is never empty while the sheet is still scrollable. **The first draft of the fixture grew no rows and the test passed green proving nothing** — caught only by a guard asserting the fixture actually grows |
| PERF-13 | `frame-profile.mjs` cannot see the cost it exists to measure | Done | P1 | It reported **the same numbers with 1.96 MB crossing per frame as with 61 bytes**, so `CHT-13`'s per-frame cost was invisible to the repository's own frame profiler. Two structural reasons: rAF-interval timing cannot see work that fits in the slack under vsync — it measures whether frames are *late*, not what they *cost* — and it **seeded no charts at all**, so it could not have exercised the path in principle. Now three modes: `calls` times the work itself (build + cross the wasm boundary + parse) and reports payload characters, `charts` seeds the pathological case (six series naming whole columns on an empty sheet), and `frames` is untouched because "are frames late" is a real question — just not the only one. **Reading order is documented and matters:** bytes first (deterministic, any movement is real), then the *minimum*, because noise here is one-sided and only the minimum moves when the work does; `0.00ms` means under Chrome's ~0.1 ms clock clamp, not free. | `frame-profile.selfcheck.spec.mjs` plus `--mode=calibrate`, which injects a known cost and **needs no engine mutation at all**. **Verified by the orchestrator on his own machine**: an 8 ms burn -> calls +7.90 ms, frames -0.10 ms — `PERF-13` in one run. Call timing floored at the display period -> "an empty probe measured 16.70ms"; the chart seed emptied -> "the `rows` seed built no chart". Each mutation reds one test and leaves the other green. 11 tests across selfcheck, frame-budget and scroll-budget pass |
| FID-39 | `edits_applied` does not count an undo, contradicting its own documentation | Done | P1 | `History::undo` did not increment `applied`; only `apply` and `redo` did. The counter's own doc comment reasons *from* the opposite — *"undo counts as an edit too… This can only err the other way"* — so the mistake the comment rules out was the one the code made. **The failing case is not the obvious one.** Undoing an edit made *since* the save errs safely, reporting a needless difference; undoing one made **before** it moves the document away from disk while the number the host compares stands still, so no close warning is shown and the work goes without a prompt. The SDK's `apply_raw` and `workbook_mut` had the same hole against the same promise — they bypass the undo *stack* on purpose, and bypassing the *dirty signal* was never part of it; both already drop `source` on exactly that reasoning, so the counter is now conservative on the same grounds. `note_foreign_change` deliberately touches neither stack: a change nobody recorded an inverse for is not undoable, and a stack entry for it would let Ctrl+Z walk into a state the history cannot reconstruct. | `undoing_a_saved_edit_is_visible_to_a_host_watching_edits_applied` and `the_history_bypasses_still_move_the_dirty_counter`, both seen red first — the SDK one initially failed for the *wrong* reason (`blank()` has no sheet, so `apply_raw` returned `SheetNotFound`) and was fixed before it counted as evidence. Workspace tests, clippy, fmt and `cargo doc -D warnings` all exit 0 |
| FID-44 | `FID-39` made `workbook_mut` count, and it is a plain accessor — so every styled edit counted twice | Done | P1 | **Turned `main` red for five merges, and I shipped it.** `FID-39` moved the dirty counter in `workbook_mut` on the reasoning that it cannot see whether a write follows, so it should assume one — the same conservatism that makes it drop `source`. That was wrong about *who calls it*: the wasm layer uses `workbook_mut` as an ordinary accessor **inside** an edit, to intern a style (`style.rs:1038`) or store a formula (`structural.rs:559`), so such an edit counted twice. The editor's draft bar reads that number and **states it to the user**: it read "2 edits ahead of the last save" after one edit, and a test building a deliberate count collision saw 91 where it expected 45 — a doubling, which is what named the cause. `source` may be conservative because a needless re-serialize costs only time; a count shown to a person has no safe direction, an over-count is simply a wrong statement. `undo` and `apply_raw` still count — those were the actual holes. **What made it invisible:** I ran the workspace tests and 19 gates and neither reads this number the way the draft bar does. Nothing in Rust does. The browser suite is the only place it is observable, and I did not run it for a change to a counter a browser feature displays. | `the_history_bypasses_still_move_the_dirty_counter` now asserts `workbook_mut` leaves the counter **alone**, with the reason in place so it is not re-added. Seen red by reinstating the call: `editor.drafts.spec.mjs` reproduces CI exactly — "the collision was not set up, Expected 45, Received 91" and "2 edits ahead" against "1 edit ahead". 18 drafts tests pass with it removed |
| UX-EMBED-02 | An embed defaults to the all-permissions preset | Done | P1 | A framed `editor.html` and an `<opencalc-sheet>` both resolved to `standalone`, making every embed **byte-identical to our own page**: `canOpen` true, so `File ▸ New`/`Open` were listed *and runnable* and a visitor could replace the host's document from inside the host's own page; `canSaveAs` true, so eight download entries the host never authorised; `canShare` true, when the preset comment says that must be the host's decision; `chrome: "web"`, so our branding sat in their product. A frame that says nothing is now an embed — the **default** moves, not the ceiling, and an explicit `?mode=` still wins. Two sites, because a shadow root cannot be seen at module-eval time where `askedMode()` runs. The cross-origin `window.top` access **throws, and the throw is itself the answer "yes"**, so the catch returns `true`. One knock-on the fix forced: `applyModeChrome` stamped `document.documentElement` unconditionally, harmless only while a shadow mount could never reach `chrome: "embedded"` — it now would hide the *host page's* header, the shadow boundary crossed in the one direction it exists to prevent. | Five tests in `editor.modes.spec.mjs` asserting the **mechanism** — `listed`, `capabilityForbids`, and a real `runCommand("file.open")` — so a hardcoded `canOpen = false` would not satisfy them. **Verified by the orchestrator**: fix reverted -> 3 red naming mode `standalone`; the `explicitMode() === null` guard dropped -> the precedence test goes red. 14 modes tests and a 57-test sweep over every frame/shadow-root spec pass |
| UX-EMBED-03 | Three shipped callers inherited the moved default, and one lost its chrome | Done | P1 | Found by the `UX-EMBED-02` worker against its own change — the right instinct, since a default that moves under a shipped product is the cost of moving it. **WOPI was a real regression**: `wopi` and `embedded` differ on exactly one axis, `chrome`, which `wopi` keeps as `"web"` on purpose because the editor *is* the frame and draws its own chrome the way Office Online does — so an unmarked WOPI frame silently lost its header. Our landing page demo lost the branding strip and downloads it exists to show. The host server's frame was already *correct* under the new default and is now explicit anyway, because a shipped product should not change behaviour because a default moved underneath it. **What makes it invisible:** each caller stayed perfectly valid — it asked for nothing and got a working editor, just not the one it meant, so the defect exists only relative to an intent nothing had written down. | **`tools/check-editor-mount-mode.py`, new** — an in-page mount must name its mode; a plain `<a href>` navigation need not. Seen red on all three callers. **Version one of the gate did not catch the WOPI case it was written for**: it matched `editor_url` and a quoted string on one line, and the real default wraps across two, so it caught three test fixtures and printed green over the regression — the same narrower-question fault one level up. Now matched over a 3-line window; a first repair that flagged any URL at all reported 18 sites, almost all a bare filename in an asset table |
| SAVE-08 | Phase C — snapshot-based version history | Done | P1 | **The engine and SDK half is built**; the wasm bindings and the editor are not. Capture, list, name, hide, `plan_restore` and `restore_version`, with the store host-agnostic (`into_parts`/`from_parts`) and the clock the caller's. **A restore is one `Operation::Batch` of ordinary edits**, so it travels to collaborators as edits and costs exactly **one** undo step — a batch has one combined inverse. **The present is captured as a `Saved` version before the restore lands, and if that capture cannot be stored the restore is refused** rather than proceeding with no way back. **`docs/83` §6.3 is wrong about what a version is**: read literally, "`session_save()` bytes" is 424 ms per capture at 300k cells (`SAVE-06`); the model snapshot is the same state at **27-39 ms**, about 15x cheaper, and is already the collaboration transport. **Neither version constant moves, argued rather than asserted**: no new `Operation` or message variant (not `ADR-024`'s loud break), and no new field inside a whole-vector replace (not `CHT-07`'s quiet one) — the store is engine-local and never on the wire. **The two missing halves are built** (`HIST-01`): `crates/casual-calc-wasm/src/versions.rs` binds capture, list, name, hide, plan and restore, and `File ▸ Version history…` opens a panel that lists versions and restores one behind a plan the user reads first. | 31 tests and eleven mutations. **Two were real bugs the worker found in its own code**: eviction that discarded autosaves for a capture it then refused, and a report that suppressed a chart warning while `InsertSheet` carries no `restore` payload — so the sheet came back, its XML did not, and the report said nothing. **Verified by the orchestrator**: reordering eviction to discard before judging -> 2 red; making every version evictable -> 4 red, "six autosaves went by and the named version is still here". One of my own mutations did not compile and proved nothing, which is why it is not counted. Workspace, clippy, fmt and `cargo doc -D warnings` all exit 0 |
| SAVE-13 | A version store's snapshots are uncompressed, and gzip is 11x | Done | P2 | Measured during `SAVE-08`: 17.82 MiB at 300k cells, 1.61 MiB gzipped at -6 — so a 50 MiB budget holds **two** versions uncompressed and 31 compressed. The byte ceiling, not the count ceiling, is what binds on any real workbook. The worker deliberately did not build it: putting a compressor into a core wasm-bound crate to spend ~300 ms per capture is a design decision rather than a worker's call, and the host has cheaper options — a browser gets `CompressionStream` off the main thread, an object store often gets it free. `byte_len` is uncompressed, so the budget is conservative rather than wrong. | Compressed in the **host**, which is what this row argued for and why the worker was right not to build it: `CompressionStream` off the main thread costs nothing, where a compressor inside a core wasm-bound crate would spend ~300 ms per capture. The engine's `byte_len` stays **uncompressed**, so the retention arithmetic remains conservative rather than wrong — a store budgeting against a codec would hold more than it could restore if the codec changed. `stored snapshots are compressed` asserts the codec is recorded and the stored size is under half the engine's own figure. A "compression" that made the bytes larger is refused and stored raw, since tiny snapshots do exactly that and spending a user's quota to be able to say the word gzip is not a saving
| COL-62 | The wire flattens rich text, so two collaborators converge on plain characters | Done | P2 | `WireOperation` stored each string as a plain `String` and `localise` re-interned with `intern_string`, so `StringTable::runs` was dropped in transit: two people editing a cell of mixed bold and plain **converged on the flattened text, silently**. `COL-12` fixed the *identity* half of this class — an id meaning different things to two participants — and this is the *content* half, where the id resolved correctly and what it resolved to had been made poorer. `restore.rs` already had the right shape, re-interning through `intern_rich_text` precisely so a restore does not flatten, which is why the bug surfaced there rather than being fixed there. **`PROTOCOL_VERSION` does not move, and that is asserted rather than argued.** This is the `Draft` case, not `CHT-07`'s: `WireOperation` does not `deny_unknown_fields`, so an old peer skips the new key and reads the rest exactly as before — flattened, which is the behaviour it already had — and a new peer seeing no runs concludes "not rich text", which is what such a sender means. Nobody is misled, so nobody is refused, and a bump would cost every unupgraded tab its session to deliver formatting it cannot show. | `a_rich_string_crosses_with_its_formatting`, seen red with the receiver holding `None` for the runs while the characters survived — the exact shape of the silence. Plus `a_peer_that_has_never_heard_of_runs_still_reads_a_message_carrying_them`, which decodes the message through a struct that has no `runs` field, because the no-bump claim is worth a test rather than a paragraph. 262 crate tests, 48 workspace suites, clippy, fmt and doc all pass |
| SAVE-09 | The op log is a resume buffer that two documents call a version history | Done | P2 | Measured while writing `docs/83`, and it corrects both `HIST-01` and `docs/12` §3.19, which each say the collaboration server already keeps a history that nothing reads as one. It does not. There are **no timestamps anywhere**, no per-revision author, roughly 400–600 ops retained, Redis capped and TTL'd at 10k and one hour, the whole session evicted 30 seconds after the last participant leaves, and nothing persisted. It is a correctly-scoped resume buffer that *looks* like a history from the outside. Both documents need correcting, and history needs its own storage — which is what `docs/83` designs. | All three statements are corrected. `docs/12` §3.19 and its summary tables strike the claim through and put the measurement in its place; `HIST-01`'s note now does the same, which mattered most because a **closed** row is what a reader trusts. `docs/57` already carried the right answer as a boundary — the server is "not a file store, a version history" — and `docs/83` carries the numbers, so each correction now sits in the document that owns the claim rather than only in this table. **No gate, deliberately**: a check for "op log" near "history" would fire on every sentence that correctly explains the difference, including all of these, and a gate that has to be argued with is a gate that gets muted
| FID-36 | Text from an undone edit is written into the saved file | Done | P1 | Severity raised from P2 on measurement: this is disclosure, not tidiness. `set_cell("typo")` then `undo` left `sheetData` empty and a whole `sharedStrings.xml` part whose **only reason to exist was the text the user took back** — and typed text is written *inline*, so that `<si>` had no reference at all. A curiosity for one download; under autosave a schedule, so every discarded draft rides along in the file a user hands to somebody else. **The whole leak is one table.** `StringId` is the only id type appearing in cell values and `CellStore` exists only as `Sheet.cells`, so the reference surface is exactly "cell values" and a complete scan was possible: rich-text runs, formula *result* strings and inline strings all leak through the same table; defined names, comments, hyperlinks and sheet names do not, because their ops restore a whole vector. **The crux is telling the two directions apart** — dropping strings that came from the user's own file would be the data loss this project forbids. A provenance watermark records how many entries the document arrived with; the table is append-only, so provenance is a *prefix*, not a flag per entry, and the prefix is emitted whole and in order so **preserved indices never move** and anything retained verbatim that names a string by index still resolves. Pruning happens at write, never in the model: a `StringId` **is** an index, and the undo stack, the clipboard and a live collab session all hold ids — renumbering under them would make redo restore a cell reading somebody else's text. | `crates/casual-calc-sdk/tests/discarded_strings.rs`, 7 tests. **Verified by the orchestrator, both directions:** the pre-fix writer -> 6 red, reproducing `<t>typo</t>` in the package; provenance ignored (prune everything unreferenced) -> **exactly the two fidelity tests** red, "an entry from the author's own file was dropped", with the leak tests still green. `check-rust.py`: all gates pass, 1627 workspace tests |
| DOC-034 | `55` gates the SDK build on nine decisions, and the packages shipped without them | Done | P1 | All nine of `docs/55` §12 are answered in writing, and **seven of them were answered by the release happening rather than by anybody choosing** — which is the finding, so §12 now says so rather than reading as though the decisions were open. Each answer records what shipped: one transport (the direct same-origin mount; the iframe one is unbuilt and nobody can be relying on it), change events per **range** (`onChanged` carries `e.range`, and a per-cell event would make a thousand-cell paste a thousand callbacks), custom JS functions unbuilt with the determinism objection intact (a workbook whose values depend on host JavaScript cannot be recalculated by anything but that host, which takes the file out of the format), `preview` a **preset** and not a mode (the shipped vocabulary is `standalone`/`embedded`/`wopi`, and `examples/viewer` already states the distinction — `view` is an access level, `preview` a presentation), the Web Worker not started, and localized function names unbuilt. **Decision 3 is the costly one and is now taken deliberately: the `--oc-*` names stay.** §1 called the rename "cheap now and impossible after the first release" and the first release has happened — three packages published, 32 names set by hosts in their own stylesheets — so renaming would break every consumer that themed an embed, to buy a naming convention. **The rule §12 set was unenforceable and was not enforced**; what is enforceable is the part that costs money to get wrong, so the published names are a manifest with a gate rather than a sentence. [55](55-SDK-EMBEDDING-AND-INTEGRATION-DESIGN.md) §12 closes with *"Nothing beyond the prototype in §10 gets built until these are answered"*. Three npm packages have since published (`SDK-009`, `SDK-010`) with seven of the nine still open. The costly one is decision 3: §1 says renaming the `--oc-*` theme tokens to AG Grid-style typed names is *"cheap now and impossible after the first release"*, and the first release has happened — so the decision is being taken by the calendar rather than by anybody. The document keeps its text; this row is the promise it made. **Answer or withdraw each of §12's open decisions, and say in writing what the token names now are.** | `sdk/theme-tokens.json` is the written answer to "what are the token names now?", and `check-theme-tokens.py` asserts `webapp/embed.js` advertises exactly that set **in that order** — order included, because a host builds a theme picker from it. Seen red by renaming one token: "advertised and not published: ['bgColor'] / published and not advertised: ['backgroundColor']". Before this, the gate could say every advertised token was *honoured* and nothing could say they were the names anybody meant to publish |
| UX-DLG-02 | Row height and column width are asked for with a raw `window.prompt` | Done | P1 | `sizeDialog` called `window.prompt`, which cannot be styled, does not look like the application, blocks the page, and is unreachable from the embed and the desktop shell. Now a modal built the way `tableDialog` and `confirmModal` build theirs — Enter accepts, Escape cancels, focus lands in the field with the value selected and returns to the grid, Cancel touches no size. `window.prompt`, `confirm` and `alert` are now **absent from `webapp/` entirely**. Three of the brief's premises were wrong and the worker said so: the line number was stale (`:2634`, not `:2351` — the row carried the same stale number); **"0 hides the line" is false** — `resize_px_to_twips` floors every size at 8 px, so the old prompt's accepted `0` silently became 8 px and the message now says "use Hide"; and `sizeDialog()` with no arguments was **already broken**, reading `Row NaN height (px)` because `session_row_px(sheet, undefined, 1)` threw into an empty catch. Field is `type="text"` with `inputmode="numeric"` on purpose: a `number` input reports `""` for unparseable text, which is the destroys-what-you-typed behaviour being removed. | Ten tests in `editor.resize-live.spec.mjs`. **Verified by the orchestrator**: fix reverted -> 9 red on `.oc-modal:not([hidden])` never appearing; and the CSS assertion proved separately by removing only `align-self: start` -> "the label drifted away from its field", 10.09 against a bound of 4 — a CSS-only line is the easiest kind to write green. 23 tests across four dialog specs pass |
| UX-DLG-03 | The size dialog cost one undo per line, where the drag costs one | Done | P1 | Found by the `UX-DLG-02` worker against its own change and correctly left alone — the brief said keep the multi-selection behaviour, and the *prompt* had the same fault. It is still a defect: the dialog looped `session_set_col_width` per column inside one `tryEdit`, and each call is its own `session.edit`, so resizing B:D took three Ctrl+Z to put back. The **drag** path already used `session_set_col_width_range` (`editor.core.js`), so **the menu and the drag disagreed about what one undo means** — and the person who resizes three columns is the one who finds out. What was open was the **header** and the **control metrics**. The branding strip is gone from a desktop window — the OS title bar already carries the document — and the grid gains **114 px, +19%** (toolbar 49->37, formula bar 41->33, status bar 41->31). **The trap this row names is real**: `#settings-panel` lived inside `.app-header`, so hiding the header took Settings with it; it moved to the overlay host and gained a second form — anchored under the gear when the gear is on screen, a centred `aria-modal` dialog when it is not, which is what Excel, LibreOffice and OnlyOffice all do. `#tb-status` and the collaborator roster moved into the status bar rather than vanishing with the header — `desktop` has `canShare: true`, so that was a session whose participants the desktop user could not see. | Six tests in `editor.native-chrome.spec.mjs`, every one asserting computed style and geometry rather than a class. **Verified by the orchestrator**: putting the panel back inside `.app-header` -> "Settings opened somewhere the user can see it", expected true received false. **392 browser tests pass, exit 0** — the worker could only run 26 specs because the repo's config wanted ports it did not own, which `CI-025` has since fixed |
| UX-CHR-10 | The strip shows a name that cannot be changed, and lost the only mark identifying the application | Done | P1 | **Reported from a running editor, and both halves are corrections to `UX-CHR-03`.** (1) The document's name was rendered and not editable. Excel, Sheets, OnlyOffice and LibreOffice all rename from the title area; the only route left here was Save As, which is a different operation with different consequences because it writes a file. A name you can read and not change is a caption. (2) The logo went with the wordmark, and that was one item too far: what `docs/88` measured is that no competitor spends a strip **naming the product in words** — the wordmark, the `Alpha` pill, `engine v0.0.0` — and a mark is not a name. A browser tab has no chrome of its own, so the mark is the only thing on screen saying which application is running, which is why every web application keeps one and why the desktop shell, whose OS title bar says it already, still does not. The wordmark and version pill stay out, and the assertion that actually holds that line is the *text* one: the product's name may not appear in any region of the chrome, however it got there. Rename is a button swapped for an input rather than a `contenteditable` — an always-live text field in the title area is something a stray keystroke edits, and this string reaches a native window title. | `the document can be renamed from the strip`, red as "clicking the name did not open an editable field", plus `Escape abandons a rename`, `clearing the name leaves the document as it was` (an empty name is refused rather than stored, since a document called "" reads as untitled everywhere and cannot be seen to be wrong), `a name containing markup is shown, not executed` — the name reaches the strip, the tab title and a native window title, so `textContent` gets a test rather than a comment — and `the strip carries a logo and still names no product in words`, red as "there is no logo in the strip". 436 browser tests pass |
| UX-SEL-06 | Selecting a column highlights the empty cells and none of the filled ones | Done | P1 | **Reported from a running editor, and the worst of the three because it makes a table unusable**: "on selecting a whole column it only highlights the first row … in case of table we can't see those colour change or highlight, we can only see the blue box created on the first row of that column". That blue box is the active cell's outline — the only mark that survived. The cause is paint order. The selection tint was drawn early, and everything opaque drawn afterwards erased it: table shading (`session_tables`, opaque per cell), banded rows, conditional formatting, any cell with a background. The tint now paints **after** the fills and **before** the text, which is what `--oc-selection-color` being translucent (`rgba(…, .10)`) was always for — over white it composites to exactly the tint it drew before, so nothing changes for an empty cell, and over a table's fill it reads as the same selection instead of being replaced by it. | `selecting a column tints the cells that have a fill of their own`, seen red as "a filled cell looks identical selected and unselected (255,230,153), so the selection is invisible on it". **Two earlier versions of that test passed against the bug.** `session_set_fill` writes the model and schedules no repaint, so the first sampled a frame drawn before the fill existed — comparing a stale white pixel against a painted one, which differs whichever way the paint order goes. The test now forces a frame through the Name Box and asserts the unselected pixel *is* `255,230,153` first, so a fill that never landed cannot be mistaken for a fix |
| UX-CHR-11 | The rename field opens empty, and does not look like the rest of the chrome | Done | P1 | Reported: "still rename is not working, I can't click, no input box, nothing — and it's hideous, doesn't match the application design system". Two faults. The field was seeded from `documentName()`, which is **`null` until a file has been opened** — so on a new workbook clicking the name replaced "Untitled workbook" with an empty box: the label vanished and nothing legible took its place, which reads as a control that does not work rather than as a rename waiting for input. And it was styled with a heavy accent border and a 3px focus ring, louder than anything else in the chrome, at a fixed `24ch` that made the strip jump sideways under the pointer that had just clicked it. Seeded from the label now, sized to the label's own footprint, and drawn with the hairline and faint ground the Name Box and toolbar inputs already use. | `the rename field opens with the name already in it`, which asserts the field's value equals the text the strip was showing, and bounds its width so the strip cannot jump |
| UX-CHR-12 | The formula text sits against the top of the bar, beside a Name Box that is centred | Done | P2 | Reported as part of the same round. `UX-CHR-08` made `#formula-input` a `<textarea>` and left it `content-box`, so a 26px height plus 4px padding made a **34px control inside a 36px bar** and the text sat hard against the top. Next to a Name Box that is centred, that is what made the region look broken rather than merely tight. `border-box` and a 28px height: one 18px line with 5px above and below it. | `the formula text is centred in the bar, like the Name Box beside it`, which measures the input's centre against the bar's *and* against the Name Box's — a single-sided check would pass on a bar that is itself misaligned |
| UX-CHR-03 | The branding strip names the product where every competitor names the document | Done | P1 | `docs/88` §1.2, measured against Excel, LibreOffice Calc, OnlyOffice, Google Sheets and Numbers: **none of the five has a region naming the product.** They name the *document*, in the title bar. Ours spends 53px on a logo, "OpenCalc", an `Alpha` pill and `engine v0.0.0` — and it is the single largest structural reason the editor reads as a web page rather than an application. **The product-owner question this raises is answered here rather than deferred**: the strip is not deleted uniformly, because web and desktop do not have the same problem. A desktop window has an OS title bar carrying document identity, so the strip goes entirely; a browser tab has none, so the region survives carrying *document* identity — file name and save state — and never product identity. That keeps `UX-DESK-01`'s generalisation while refusing the version of it that would leave a web user unable to see which file they have open. Version and build move to `Help ▸ About`, where five of five put them. | `no region of the chrome names a product` and `the web strip carries the document's name and save state, not the product's`, both seen red with a wordmark put back: "a wordmark, logo or version pill is back in the chrome / Expected: 0 / Received: 2" and "the product mark is still in the strip: OpenCalcAlpha Untitled workbook Saved". Plus `the engine status line is in the status bar` in both chromes and `the version left the strip for Help ▸ About` |
| UX-CHR-04 | The selection summary floats over the cells it describes while the status bar below it is empty | Done | P1 | `docs/88` §5: a 595x33px frosted panel (`backdrop-filter: blur(14px)`, 11px radius) showing six statistics, floating over the grid — while the status bar 49px beneath it is empty in the middle. Excel, LibreOffice, OnlyOffice and Sheets all put this in the bottom strip and **none floats it**. It covers the data the user selected in order to describe it. Moves to the status bar at Excel's default three — Average, Count, Sum — the remaining question in `docs/88` §10.2 answered in favour of Excel's default rather than Sheets' one-at-a-time menu, because the bar has room for three and parity is the stated bar. | `the selection summary is text in the status bar, not a panel over the cells`, seen red by floating it back: "it still covers the cells it describes / Expected: false / Received: true". Asserts the box is inside `.bottom-bar`, is `position: static`, carries no `backdrop-filter` and no shadow, and reads exactly `Average: 2 Count: 3 Sum: 6` |
| UX-CHR-05 | Toolbar grouping is stated twice and both statements are too weak to read | Done | P2 | `docs/88` §3.1-3.2: each group is a filled 10px-radius capsule (a 3% luminance step, below the threshold at which a boundary reads) **and** a 1px rule. All four desktop competitors use a 1px rule alone; LibreOffice and OnlyOffice declare separators as first-class elements. Saying it twice weakly is worse than saying it once clearly, and the capsules are what make the bar read as a row of segmented controls. Rule only, 26px buttons, 3px radius, 6px gaps, and the `.oc-chrome-native` metric block collapses into the base so there is **one** metric set rather than two that drift. Enables `UX-CHR-06`; must not run concurrently with it. | `a toolbar group is stated by a rule, not by a capsule`, seen red with the capsule restored: "the capsule is still filled / Expected pattern: /rgba(0, 0, 0, 0)\|transparent/ / Received string: \"rgb(246, 247, 249)\"". Plus `the desktop and the page draw one toolbar, at one set of metrics`, which is what stops the two metric sets drifting apart again |
| UX-CHR-06 | The toolbar does not fit a 1440px laptop, so the overflow chips are the phone UI firing on a desktop | Done | P2 | `docs/88` §3.4, and it **refutes the diagnosis it was sent to confirm**: the `Data ▾`/`Tools ▾` chips are not a web idiom chosen for looks, they are `reflowToolbar()`'s mobile overflow behaving exactly as designed, on hardware it was never meant to fire on. Measured: the bar needs **1463px** and collapses at **1461px**; at 1280px a third of it is chips. Restyling the chips would therefore have been wasted work. The fix is a width contract — fit 1280px uncollapsed — which forces governing the *inventory*, and the inventory question is `docs/88` §10.3: **composition parity, not inventory parity**. Excel's inventory is a ribbon's; ours is a toolbar's. So `tbg-tools` goes and a number-format readout arrives, which is the question our chrome currently cannot answer at all. Depends on `UX-CHR-05`. | `no toolbar group is collapsed at 1280px` (and at 1366/1440/1600/1920), plus `the toolbar reports the active cell's number format` and `the tools commands survive the group leaving the toolbar`. The width test was **green before the readout and red after it** — "the toolbar overflowed at 1280px, so a desktop user is being shown the phone's overflow / Expected 0 / Received 3" — which is the design's arithmetic reproducing itself, and the removal of `tbg-tools` is what turns it green again. Measured collapse threshold 1269px before, 1211px after, so the bar gained a 96px control and 58px of headroom at once. 431 browser tests pass |
| UX-CHR-07 | At twelve sheets the add-sheet and all-sheets buttons scroll out of reach | Done | P1 | `docs/88` §5, found by running rather than reading: the sheet strip puts `+` and the all-sheets `☰` **inside** the scroller, so at 12 sheets 897px of content sits in a 796px viewport and both controls are unreachable — and there are no nav arrows, so there is no way to reach them at all. A reachability defect, not a cosmetic one. All four competitors pin these outside the scroller. Moves left, outside, with tab-scroll arrows. | `twelve sheets put the add-sheet and all-sheets buttons out of reach`, seen red against the unmodified `editor.sheets.js`: "the add-sheet + is not the element under its own centre — it scrolled out of the tab strip / Expected: true / Received: false". The assertion is `elementFromPoint` at the button's own centre rather than mere visibility, because a control scrolled under a sibling is *visible* to a bounding-box check and still unclickable — the weaker check would have passed on the broken build. `syncSheetNav()` adopts whatever `renderTabs()` appended and re-homes it into a pinned rail, so the rail cannot grow a duplicate `+` per rebuild, and it reads the strip's presence from layout rather than from the `.oc-hide-tabs` class, which sits on an ancestor this module does not own  |
| UX-CHR-08 | Expanding the formula bar spends 58px of grid to show one line | Done | P2 | `docs/88` §4, found by running: the expand chevron produces an **86px-tall box containing a single vertically-centred line**, because `#formula-input` is an `<input type="text">` and an input cannot wrap. 58px of grid is surrendered for nothing. The chevron also sits at x=160, where all four competitors put it at the far right. One flat bar with a 1px seam, chevron far right, `<textarea>`. **Sequence this alone** — the tint mirror positions against `.formula-bar` and the `<input>`→`<textarea>` change touches the editing path; and it shares CSS with `UX-CHR-04`. | `expanding the formula bar shows more than one line`, seen red as "the INPUT scrolled 1439px sideways to reach the end of the formula, so the 86px box is showing one line". **Two earlier versions of that test passed against the exact defect they were written for**: `scrollHeight / lineHeight` reports the *box*, so an `<input>` in an 86px box divides into four lines that do not exist, and `scrollWidth > clientWidth` does not discriminate on an `<input>` either — both measured the container while claiming to measure the text. A third was vacuous because the sample formula fitted on one line at 1280px. What works is asking what the *user* has to do: put the caret at the end and see whether the control had to scroll sideways to get there. Plus `Alt+Enter breaks a line in the formula bar, and Enter still commits` (the regression this change most easily causes), `the bar is one surface with a seam`, and `the expand control is at the right end of the bar`, red at 1111px from the edge |
| UX-CHR-09 | Row and column header bands are sized by constant rather than by what they must contain | Done | P3 | `docs/88` §6: the column band is 24px where LibreOffice derives the row band from its own widest label; ours is a constant, and `measureText("1048576")` is 51px against a 46px clip, so the last rows' labels are **derived to clip** — the note is explicit that this one is derived from source and was not observed, because three attempts to drive the viewport to row 1,048,576 stopped at the data edge. Confirm the clip before fixing it. Also adds the select-all triangle every competitor draws in the corner. | **The clip was confirmed before it was fixed, which the row asked for.** `docs/88` derived it from source and could not observe it — three attempts stopped at the data edge. The Name Box is the route that jumps past the data: `A1048576` lands there, and a screenshot shows the leading digit cut off at the band's edge. `the widest row label fits inside the row band`, red as "the band is 46px and the label needs 50px, so the last rows draw a truncated number". Plus `an ordinary sheet does not pay for seven digits` (red at 46px), `the column band is 20px` (red at 24), and `the corner draws a select-all mark, and clicking it selects the sheet` (red as "cornerMark is not a function") — the click already called `selectAll()`, so what was missing was anything on screen saying so. The band is **stepped**, not continuous: one that tracked the exact widest label would reflow the whole grid while scrolling, and sizing from `"8888"` rather than from the label on screen is what stops the band moving when 1999 scrolls past 8888. Two of my own errors on the way: a dangling `HEADER_W` after the constant was replaced, which broke boot entirely (`ReferenceError`, engine never loaded), and a first version of the corner test that asserted on the Name Box — which keeps the anchor `A1` for a whole-sheet selection, as Excel does, so it failed against a correct implementation. 444 browser tests pass |
| UX-CHR-02 | Native chrome hides the menu bar without checking that anything else draws one | Done | P1 | **A regression I introduced in `UX-DESK-01`, reported from a running editor**: "it doesn't have header as well as file menus .. and no way on screen to change theme". `.oc-chrome-native #menubar { display: none }` is written under the comment *"The operating system is drawing the menu"* — and that is true only inside the Tauri shell. **There is no Tauri detection anywhere in `webapp/`** (verified: no `__TAURI__`, no `__TAURI_INTERNALS__`, no `isTauri`), so `?chrome=native` in an ordinary browser hides the header *and* the menu bar and puts nothing in their place. What is left has no File menu, no View menu, and — because theme is a `<select>` inside the gear that lived in the header — **no route to the theme at all**. The `TAURI-006` design worker flagged the same seam from the other side: `editor.native-chrome.spec.mjs` runs in a browser where `?chrome=native` only changes CSS, so no test here can observe the native bar it assumes. **Hiding a menu must require evidence that another one exists**, not a query string that asks for it. | `the menu bar survives a chrome request that nothing answered`, `a browser keeps the bar`, and `the theme is reachable in desktop chrome` — all three seen red against the single-class version, with "the menus went and nothing drew any / Expected: visible / Received: hidden" and "there is no route to the theme in this chrome" |
| UX-CHR-01 | Theme is buried in a gear popover, and the branding strip carries a file action | Done | P1 | Reported from a running editor: the dark/light option and the open-file button are "not the right place or icon for that". Both are placement defects with a correct answer already in the product. **Theme is a display option and the View menu is where display options live** — it already holds Gridlines, Cell markings, Formulas instead of results and Zero values (`editor.core.js:8894`). Theme is instead a `<select id="set-theme">` inside the settings popover (`editor.html:62`), which is two clicks and a different mental model from every sibling setting. Excel and Sheets both put display toggles in a View menu and neither hides the appearance control behind a gear in the title area. **`#hdr-open` (`editor.html:9`) is a folder icon in the branding strip**, which duplicates File ▸ Open and puts an action in the one region that should carry identity and document state only — it is also the control `UX-DESK-05` found still *listed* in desktop chrome with nothing to click. A branding strip is not a toolbar. | `the theme is reachable in desktop chrome` (red: `Expected: not "nowhere"`), plus the settings panel no longer owning a theme select and `header.open` leaving `listCommands()` with the button |
| UX-DESK-05 | `header.open` stays listed in desktop chrome with no control to reach it | Done | P3 | `webapp/editor.html:19`'s `#hdr-open` is hidden with the header, but `header.open` remains in `listCommands()`. File ▸ Open in the OS menu covers the *capability*, so nothing is lost — but if "listed implies reachable by pointer" is meant to be an invariant, this breaks it, and `UX-EMBED-02` leaned on exactly that reading when it asserted a command was gone by checking the list. Decide which the list means. | Closed by the `UX-CHR-01` change and verified in the tree: `#hdr-open` and `header.open` survive only as comments at `webapp/editor.core.js:1324` and `:10348`. Because `listCommands()` is derived from the live DOM, deleting the button deleted the id, which is what restores "listed implies reachable by pointer" |
